The brand portal returns only a raw lifetime count (lib/brands.ts:194 `impressions: [...impBy.values()].reduce(...)`) with nothing to compare it against. All seven required ad-serving changes at docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:256-267 — including item 1 'Delivery counting' and item 6 'Billable-impression cap per gamer' — are absent from lib/ads.ts (104 lines, pure weighted selection) and lib/cards/ads.ts.
  - *Consequence:* The company cannot prove delivery of the thing it sells, cannot stop over-delivery, cannot stop under-delivery, and cannot detect either. A brand that is short-delivered has no in-product evidence and will find out at month end; a brand that is over-delivered gets free inventory that CP was paid out against (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:19 puts CP cost at $1,500/mo per 1,000 daily gamers against $2,000 revenue — a 25% margin that a 30% over-delivery erases entirely). Because there is no per-gamer billable cap (doc item 6), the abuse in findings 1 and 4 flows straight through to the brand-facing count with nothing between it and the invoice. Doc-vs-code contradiction on the four most load-bearing commercial claims in the document.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:49, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:51, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:256, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:267`
- **[SEVERE]** The duration beacon is a client-supplied number with no auth and no ownership check — 'average view time' is 100% attacker-controlled, up to a forged 1 hour per impression.
  - *Mechanism:* The `duration` branch (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:40-46) accepts any `{id, ms}` and does `db.update(adImpressions).set({durationViewedMs: Math.min(Math.round(ms), 3_600_000)}).where(eq(id, payload.id))` with NO session check, NO check that the impression belongs to the caller, and NO lower bound (a negative `ms` is stored verbatim, since Math.min(-500, 3600000) = -500). The legitimate value is itself just `Date.now() - viewStart.current` computed on the client (components/AdSlot.tsx:87) and shipped by navigator.sendBeacon (line 81) — a wall-clock delta from the user's own machine with no server-side cross-check. Because the impression branch returns the new row id (line 37), an attacker who created the impression already holds the key needed to set its duration. That column is then averaged and presented as a metric: `avgViewMs: AVG(duration_viewed_ms)` (app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx:25).
  - *Consequence:* The only 'quality of view' number in the entire system is a self-reported integer from an unauthenticated caller. It carries zero evidentiary weight in an audit, and it can be tuned to whatever a seller wants a brand to see — e.g. 3,600,000 ms per impression, or a plausible 4,000 ms. If it is ever put in front of a brand as proof of attention it becomes an affirmative misrepresentation rather than a weak metric.
  - *Exploit:* curl -s -X POST https://clustergg.com/api/ads/beacon -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"type":"duration","id":"<any impression id you hold>","ms":3600000}' — repeat per id harvested from step 3 of the previous finding. Result: avgViewMs for that creative reads 3,600,000.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:40, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:42, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:44, components/AdSlot.tsx:81`
- **[SEVERE]** There is no viewability standard anywhere: no dwell-time requirement, no visibility/occlusion check, and an impression fires even when the creative image never loads.
  - *Mechanism:* The web impression fires from an IntersectionObserver with `{threshold: 0.5}` (components/AdSlot.tsx:76) the instant `e.isIntersecting` is true (line 66). That satisfies the 50%-of-pixels half of the IAB display standard and NONE of the one-continuous-second half — the beacon is sent on the first intersection callback, with no timer, no re-check, and no cancellation if the element scrolls away 20 ms later. Worse, the observed element is the host `<div ref={hostRef}>` (lines 61, 77, 112), not the `<img>`/`<video>`. The div has an explicit `aspectRatio` style (line 120) so it occupies its box regardless of whether the creative's `fileUrl` resolves — a 404'd creative, a dead Blob URL, or an image blocked by an ad blocker still counts a full impression. IntersectionObserver also does not model occlusion, `opacity:0`, `visibility:hidden`, z-index cover, CSS transform scaling to 1px, or background/minimised tabs: all of those produce counted 'views'. Finally app/loading.tsx:60 renders `<AdSlot placement="loading_screen"/>` inside the Next.js route-transition skeleton — an ad whose entire on-screen lifetime can be a few hundred milliseconds is counted the same as any other.
  - *Consequence:* 100% of web impressions are non-viewable by MRC/IAB definition — not 'partly measured', but measured against a standard that was never implemented. A brand buying at a $5 CPM (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:50) is buying an industry-priced viewable impression and receiving an untimed intersection event. On a $500/100,000-view contract the entire $500 is priced against a delivery definition the buyer's own standard would score at 0% viewable.
  - *Cites:* `components/AdSlot.tsx:64, components/AdSlot.tsx:66, components/AdSlot.tsx:76, components/AdSlot.tsx:77`
- **[SEVERE]** There is no deduplication of any kind on ad_impressions — same user, same creative, same second is two billable rows, and the web client is architected to emit one impression per 5-second rotation tick.
  - *Mechanism:* The ad_impressions table (lib/db/schema.ts:542-558) carries exactly one index, `imp_cc_idx` on (campaign_creative_id, created_at) (line 558) — a plain btree, not unique. There is no unique constraint, no onConflictDoNothing, and no dedup window on any of the three write paths (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23, lib/cards/ads.ts:207, lib/discord/ads.ts:80). On the client, the rotation interval fires every `rotationIntervalSeconds` (default 5 — lib/db/schema.ts:475) whenever a placement has >= 2 creatives (components/AdSlot.tsx:48-55); each `idx` change re-runs the impression effect (line 58), whose cleanup resets `impressionId.current = null` (line 91) so the very next intersection logs a fresh impression. An idle browser tab therefore emits one impression per slot per 5 s indefinitely.
  - *Consequence:* A single browser tab left open on a page with two ad slots produces 2 impressions / 5 s = 1,440/hour = 34,560/day — about 35% of a $500/100,000-view monthly contract from one idle tab, with no human present. Combined with the missing dedup this makes 'impressions' a measure of tab-seconds, not of people. It also means the per-brand totals in the brand portal (lib/brands.ts:194) and the CTR denominator (lib/brands.ts:200) are structurally inflated by an unknown, unmeasurable multiple — the single most damaging thing an auditor can establish, because it means no correction factor can be computed after the fact.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:542, lib/db/schema.ts:558, components/AdSlot.tsx:48, components/AdSlot.tsx:52`
- **[SEVERE]** A Discord impression is logged at PNG-resolution time, before the message is delivered — failed replies, expired interaction tokens and staff previews all count as delivered impressions, and the replies are ephemeral by default.
  - *Mechanism:* cardRef (lib/discord/cards.ts:101-127) resolves the card image and then calls logCardAdImpression at line 121 — inside the render pipeline, fire-and-forget by design (lib/cards/ads.ts:198 'an impression that fails to write is a number slightly low'). The interaction handler renders first and delivers second: `const payload = await renderScreen(...)` (app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:369) then `await editOriginal(i.token, ...)` (line 370), with a catch that replaces the message with an error string (line 371). Every failure mode after render — expired 15-minute interaction token, missing channel permission, Discord 429/5xx, user closed the client — leaves the impression row committed and the message never seen. The reply flags default to `MessageFlags.Ephemeral` (line 360), so the 'view' is a message visible to exactly one person, in one client session, that Discord discards. Separately, app/api/discord/preview/route.ts:32 calls the same renderScreen, so every internal staff preview of a screen mints a billable impression against whichever brand the rotation picked.
  - *Consequence:* 'Impression' in this system means 'the server drew a PNG', not 'a human was shown an ad' — and never means the second thing, because the image bytes are fetched by Discord's CDN, not by the viewer, so no viewer-side signal exists at any point. There is no delivery confirmation and no way to construct one. Combined with ephemeral replies, the sellable unit is 'one PNG rendered, possibly to nobody, possibly never delivered'. An auditor comparing the reported count against Discord's own message-delivery reality has no reconciliation path, which is the finding that ends the audit.
  - *Cites:* `lib/discord/cards.ts:118, lib/discord/cards.ts:121, lib/cards/ads.ts:198, app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:360`
- **[SEVERE]** Web ad clicks are unsigned and unauthenticated on the same open beacon, while the Discord click path is signed — a competitor can drive any brand's CTR arbitrarily in either direction.
  - *Mechanism:* The click branch of the open beacon (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48-57) inserts into schema.adClicks (line 50) with no signature, no session requirement and no rate limit — the same anonymous curl that forges impressions forges clicks. This is the exact threat lib/cards/ad-click.ts:11-14 documents and defends against for Discord: `verifyClick` (line 38) is enforced at app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33 so that nobody can 'run that brand's click count — and their cost-per-click — through the floor'. Both paths write to the same ad_clicks table and both feed the same brand-facing CTR (lib/brands.ts:200 `ctr: imps.length ? clicks.length / imps.length : 0`). Separately, lib/quests.ts:118 still pays ad_click at defaultWeight 25 / defaultCap 3 against ad_impression at weight 1 / cap 25 (line 117), so a gamer maximising the Signal quest performs 3 clicks per 25 impressions — a structural 12% CTR.
  - *Consequence:* The one performance number a brand actually reads is forgeable from both ends. A competitor can push a rival's CTR to ~0% by injecting impressions, or inflate it to an absurd figure by injecting clicks and then watching the brand's own analytics report zero landing-page sessions. Meanwhile the honest, un-attacked baseline is already ~12% against a real display range of 0.05-0.35% — the doc itself names this 'the signature of incentivised fraud' that 'gets us discounted to nothing by buyers and delisted by networks' (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:232-234) and claims ad_click was dropped to 0 CP, which lib/quests.ts:118 disproves. A 12% CTR with zero downstream conversion is the first number an agency's fraud team looks at, and it is visible before any audit is commissioned.
  - *Exploit:* curl -s -X POST https://clustergg.com/api/ads/beacon -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"type":"click","ccId":"<victim ccId from /api/ads/serve>"}' — loop. No signature required, unlike /api/ads/go which rejects unsigned requests at app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:50, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:11, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:38`
- **[MATERIAL]** The IP hash that backs the only 'unique reach' figure uses a salt published in the repo, making it reversible — and it counts NAT'd households and carrier-grade-NAT mobile users as one person.
  - *Mechanism:* hashIp (lib/ads.ts:6-9) is `sha256(salt + ":" + ip).slice(0,32)` with `const salt = process.env.AD_ANALYTICS_SALT ?? "cluster-salt"` (line 7). The fallback constant and the shipped .env.example value are the SAME string — .env.example:70 reads `AD_ANALYTICS_SALT=cluster-salt` — so any deployment that copies the example (the default action) uses a salt that is public. The entire IPv4 space is 2^32 preimages; a full rainbow table over it against a known salt is minutes of GPU time, so the 'hashed' IP is de-anonymisable and the route comment at app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:13-14 ('raw IPs never touch the database') is materially misleading as a privacy control. Independently, x-forwarded-for is attacker-supplied at any hop before the trusted proxy, and IP is not a person: one household NAT or one mobile carrier CGNAT collapses hundreds of distinct humans into one 'unique', while a phone moving between cells inflates uniques for a single human.
  - *Consequence:* Two separate exposures. (1) Measurement: the 'Unique reach' column (app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx:26, surfaced at line 78) is not a count of people in either direction and has no known error bound — it cannot be defended in an audit and cannot be corrected retroactively. (2) Privacy/regulatory: a reversible pseudonym derived from an IP is personal data under GDPR Art.4(1) and Recital 26; the code's own comment asserts it is not. With the doc's target geographies including EU/UK traffic (seed countries include GB/DE — lib/db/seed-activity.ts:524), this is a live GDPR finding attached to the ad system rather than a hypothetical one.
  - *Cites:* `lib/ads.ts:6, lib/ads.ts:7, lib/ads.ts:8, .env.example:70`
- **[MATERIAL]** The investor-facing 'impressions30d' metric includes ClusterGG's own house-brand ads, in a function that painstakingly excludes the house brand from every other number it returns.
  - *Mechanism:* commercialStats (lib/dataroom/index.ts) filters HOUSE_BRAND_ID out of `paying` (line 275), out of `mrr` (line 277), out of `cardCampaigns` (line 280, with the comment 'counting it here would report an advertiser that is us'), out of `brands` (line 294) and out of `activeCampaigns` (line 295) — but `impressions30d` (line 301) is the unfiltered `count()` from line 267 with no brand predicate at all. The house creative is not incidental: HOUSE_AD (lib/cards/ads.ts:164-181) fills every unsold card slot by design ('There is no such thing as an unsold card'), and ensureTopBannerAd (lib/db/seed.ts:1284-1303) plants a house creative on the site-wide `top_banner` placement. At the launch fill rates the doc itself assumes (A5: 'Fill rate above 56%' — docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md, section 10), close to half of all logged impressions are Cluster advertising Cluster.
  - *Consequence:* The headline delivery number in the investor data room is inflated by unsold inventory at an unstated and unbounded rate — on a pre-revenue platform it could be ~100% house. Every derived figure an investor computes from it (implied CPM, cost per view, inventory headroom, the 'views available / sold / unsold' cockpit the doc promises at docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:294-301) is wrong by that factor. That the same function correctly excludes the house brand five times and not the sixth makes this hard to characterise as an oversight in a diligence conversation.
  - *Cites:* `lib/dataroom/index.ts:267, lib/dataroom/index.ts:275, lib/dataroom/index.ts:277, lib/dataroom/index.ts:280`
- **[MATERIAL]** The bot tells every server 'this server earns a share of Cluster's ad revenue' — no ad-revenue share exists in code; server earnings are computed solely from sponsored-challenge fees.
  - *Mechanism:* Every sponsored post the bot drops into a community carries the footer 'Sponsored — this server earns a share of Cluster's ad revenue.' (lib/discord/ads.ts:64), and the bot's own screens repeat it as 'your server's growth toward ad revenue' (lib/discord/screens.ts:268, 638, 1475; lib/discord/guilds.ts:13). lib/server-earnings.ts is the module that computes what an owner is actually paid, and its own header states the rule: a sponsored challenge costs `challengePrice`, 70% is the prize pool, and 'The remaining 30% is the platform fee, and THAT is what this module divides' (lines 4-12). EARN_TIERS (line 42) and platformFeePct (line 50) are functions of the challenge price only. Grepping lib/, app/ and components/ for adRevenue or any apportionment of ad_impressions to guild payouts returns nothing — impressions carry a guildId (lib/db/schema.ts:556) and are counted per guild for a display stat (lib/discord/guilds.ts:431), but that number is never multiplied by anything or paid out.
  - *Consequence:* Server owners are recruited on a revenue-share that does not exist. This matters to the impression question specifically because it is the stated reason owners should tolerate ads in their communities and drive bot usage — the demand-side engine for the whole impression supply. When owners discover their 'ad revenue' is $0 regardless of impressions delivered, the supply of Discord inventory (the entire product per docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:220) is at risk, and the platform has a documented pattern of paying-claim-without-paying-code that a plaintiff or a regulator would read as inducement. Separately it caps the fraud risk in one direction — inflating guild impressions pays an owner nothing today — but only because the promised mechanism was never built.
  - *Cites:* `lib/discord/ads.ts:64, lib/discord/guilds.ts:13, lib/discord/screens.ts:268, lib/discord/screens.ts:638`


#### A3 · CP ledger soundness (13)

- **[FATAL]** The 500 CP/day ceiling is advisory, not enforced: awardQuestAction is a read-then-write with zero transactions, zero row locks, and zero post-write reconciliation, so N concurrent requests each mint the full action weight.
  - *Mechanism:* awardQuestAction reads the ceiling and the day's total in one round trip (`const [ceiling, already] = await Promise.all([dailyCpCeiling(db), cpEarnedToday(db, userId)])`, lib/quests.ts:490), computes `room = Math.max(0, ceiling - already)` (491), and only later writes the row (519). `room` is a local JS variable — `room -= cp` at 518 mutates process memory, not the database. There is no `BEGIN`, no `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, no advisory lock, no CHECK constraint, and no unique-per-day constraint anywhere. A grep for `.transaction(`, `FOR UPDATE`, and `BEGIN` across all of lib/ and app/ returns nothing. The driver makes it worse: production uses `drizzle(neon(process.env.DATABASE_URL))` over neon-http (lib/db/index.ts:911-913), where every single query is an independent stateless HTTPS round trip — transactions are not merely unused, they are unavailable on this driver. The gap between the read at line 490 and the insert at line 519 is a minimum of two Neon HTTP round trips (the per-action cap count at 502 and the dedup select at 511), i.e. roughly 20-60ms of wall clock. Every request that arrives inside that window reads `already` as the same stale number and independently concludes it has the full 500 CP of room.
  - *Consequence:* Unbounded in N. With ad_click at weight 25 (lib/quests.ts:118), 100 concurrent POSTs inside the window each write 25 CP: 2,500 CP against a 500 CP ceiling and a 75 CP per-action cap — a 5x ceiling overrun and a 33x action-cap overrun in one burst. At DEFAULT_CP_PER_DOLLAR = 10000 (lib/marketplace.ts:55) that is $0.25 per burst; twenty bursts is 50,000 CP, which is exactly the price of a $5 bronze trophy that is redeemable for real cash. Nothing ever re-sums the day and claws back, so the mint is permanent. 1,000 sock accounts running this once a day is $5,000/day of genuine cash liability against a model whose entire thesis is 'our worst case and our best case are the same event' (lib/quests.ts:43-44).
  - *Exploit:* 1. Sign in (any free account). 2. Load any page with an ad slot and read the `ccId` out of the DOM — it is public. 3. Fire 100 simultaneous `POST /api/ads/beacon` with body `{"type":"click","ccId":"<ccId>"}` and NO `id` field. Because `refId: payload.id ?? clickId` falls back to a freshly generated `uid()` (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:56), all 100 get distinct refIds, so the dedup select at lib/quests.ts:511 misses on every one. 4. All 100 read `count(*) = 0 < cap 3` at line 502 and `already = 0` at line 490. 5. All 100 insert 25 CP. Ledger now holds 2,500 CP for the day. 6. Sleep 2s, repeat. 7. At 50,000 CP buy a $5 trophy and redeem it for cash via app/actions/trophies.ts:150-163, which checks only that the trophy is `held` and never checks where the CP came from.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:490, lib/quests.ts:491, lib/quests.ts:516, lib/quests.ts:518`
- **[FATAL]** buyTrophy double-spends: balance is read, then two inserts fire with no transaction, and the resulting overdraft is silently hidden by Math.max(0, ...).
  - *Mechanism:* `const wallet = await cpWallet(db, buyerId); if (wallet.balance < price) return {...}` at lib/marketplace.ts:223-224, then an unconditional `db.insert(schema.marketplaceOrders)` at 234 and `db.insert(schema.userTrophies)` at 244. Three separate round trips, no transaction, no lock on the buyer's row, no idempotency key on the purchase. cpWallet (138-141) is itself two more independent reads (getTotalCp over quest_events + cpSpent over marketplace_orders). N concurrent buyTrophy calls all read the same balance, all pass the check, and all insert. The overdraft is then made invisible: `balance: Math.max(0, earned - spent)` at line 140 clamps a negative wallet to zero, so neither the gamer's wallet page nor the admin ledger ever shows that more CP was spent than was ever earned. The userTrophies insert uses `.onConflictDoNothing()` (247) against unique index ut_user_trophy_chal_idx on (user_id, trophy_id, challenge_id) (lib/db/index.ts:128) — challenge_id is NULL for every marketplace purchase and Postgres treats NULLs as distinct in a unique index without NULLS NOT DISTINCT, so that index provides no protection here at all; and in the case where it ever did fire, the order row (and therefore the CP charge) is written at 234 regardless, charging the gamer for a trophy they never receive.
  - *Consequence:* Each extra concurrent request yields one extra `held` trophy at zero marginal CP cost. Every held trophy is redeemable for its full cash `value` through app/actions/trophies.ts:145-153, which sums `trophies.value` with no provenance check whatsoever. One 50,000 CP balance fired 10-wide produces 10 x $5 = $50 of real cash liability against $5 of CP. This is the single line in the codebase where a ledger bug converts directly into money leaving the company. marketplaceWallet's `liability` figure (lib/marketplace.ts:363) will show the full obligation, so the loss is real and booked — it is only the *balance* that lies.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Accumulate exactly one trophy's worth of CP (or mint it via finding 1). 2. Issue 10 simultaneous calls to the buyTrophy server action for the same trophyId. 3. All 10 read balance >= price and pass. 4. 10 marketplace_orders rows and 10 user_trophies rows are written; each user_trophies row has a distinct uid so onConflictDoNothing never fires. 5. Wallet now reads balance 0 (clamped) instead of -450,000 CP. 6. Select all 10 award ids and submit one redeem request via requestRedeem — `awards.length !== ids.length` passes because all 10 are genuinely held — for 10x the cash.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:223, lib/marketplace.ts:224, lib/marketplace.ts:234, lib/marketplace.ts:244`
- **[SEVERE]** The per-action daily cap is raced by the identical read-then-write pattern, and is checked with a COUNT that a concurrent writer has not yet contributed to.
  - *Mechanism:* `const [{ c }] = await db.select({ c: sql`count(*)` }).from(schema.questEvents).where(...)` at lib/quests.ts:502-504, then `if (Number(c) >= cap) continue;` at 505. The row that would make the count exceed the cap is not inserted until line 519, two round trips later. Concurrent callers all observe the pre-burst count. There is no partial unique index on (user_id, quest_id, action_key, date_trunc('day', created_at)) that would let the database refuse the (cap+1)th row — the only unique index on quest_events is qe_dedup_idx on (user_id, quest_id, action_key, ref_type, ref_id) (lib/db/index.ts:102), which has no date component and is trivially defeated by supplying a fresh ref.
  - *Consequence:* Every per-action cap in ACTION_CATALOG is defeatable by concurrency, not just the global ceiling. The comment at lib/quests.ts:29-33 — 'Every action has a cap... an uncapped action is not a generous action, it is an unpriced one' — describes an intent the code does not implement under load. ACTION_CAP_SUM (lib/quests.ts:149) is asserted as a fixture in tests, but the fixture is meaningless because the caps it sums are not enforced.
  - *Exploit:* Same burst as finding 1. The cap check and the ceiling check are the same defect at two scopes; one burst defeats both simultaneously.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:501, lib/quests.ts:502, lib/quests.ts:505, lib/quests.ts:519`
- **[SEVERE]** tests/db/marketplace.mts asserts 'the balance never went negative' against a value that is mathematically incapable of being negative — the one invariant that guards real money is covered by a vacuous test.
  - *Mechanism:* The assertion is `ok("the balance never went negative", (await cpWallet(db, nova.id)).balance >= 0)`. cpWallet returns `balance: Math.max(0, earned - spent)` (lib/marketplace.ts:140). The expression `Math.max(0, x) >= 0` is a tautology for every finite x. The test passes identically whether the gamer is 500 CP in credit or 5,000,000 CP overdrawn.
  - *Consequence:* An investor reading the test suite sees an explicit assertion that the wallet cannot go negative and reasonably concludes the double-spend case is covered. It is not covered; it is masked. The correct assertion (`earned - spent >= 0`, on the raw difference) would fail the moment finding 3 is exercised. This is the difference between an untested invariant and a falsely-attested one.
  - *Cites:* `tests/db/marketplace.mts:148, lib/marketplace.ts:140`
- **[SEVERE]** CP can be minted for something that did not happen: /api/ads/beacon awards ad_impression and ad_click on a self-asserted POST with a user-controlled ccId, no verification that any ad was ever rendered, and no rate limit.
  - *Mechanism:* The route accepts an arbitrary JSON body. For `type: "impression"` it inserts an ad_impressions row and awards ad_impression keyed on the server-generated `id` (36). For `type: "click"` it inserts an ad_clicks row and awards ad_click keyed on `payload.id ?? clickId` (56). The only gate is a session cookie. Nothing verifies that a creative was ever rendered, that the ccId corresponds to a placement the gamer could have seen, that an impression preceded the click, or that the click is human. `ccId` is a foreign key to ad_campaign_creatives (lib/db/schema.ts:544, 562) so it must exist — but it is emitted into the page HTML for every ad slot, so obtaining a valid one is a view-source. There is no rate limiter on the route (grep for rateLimit/throttle across lib/ and app/ finds hits only in discord/audience.ts, discord/post-queue.ts, portal-auth.ts, api/gamers/search, api/portal/unlock — never on the beacon).
  - *Consequence:* Two harms compound. (a) CP: ad_click at 25 CP x cap 3 = 75 CP/day fabricated with zero ads served, which is 15% of the entire daily ceiling — and this is the action every one of the four shipped mission templates requires x3 (lib/missions.ts:61, 76, 91, 106). (b) Brand fraud: the same POSTs write ad_impressions and ad_clicks rows, which are the rows brands are billed and reported on. A gamer optimising their own CP is simultaneously fabricating the delivery numbers sold to advertisers. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md itself names ad fraud 'the single most reputation-damaging line in the old table' — the endpoint that makes it a one-liner is shipped and open.
  - *Exploit:* `curl -X POST https://<host>/api/ads/beacon -H 'Cookie: cluster_session=<yours>' -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"type":"click","ccId":"<any ccId from page source>"}'` — repeat. Each call books a click against a brand and pays 25 CP up to the (raceable) cap of 3.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:18, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:36, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:56`
- **[SEVERE]** profile_views_25 is credited from an unauthenticated page render, and the same 25-view crossing can be paid twice because the two live code paths write into disjoint refId namespaces that the dedup index cannot unify.
  - *Mechanism:* Two independent implementations of 'record a profile view' are both live and both award the same action with incompatible dedup keys. Path A, the public `force-dynamic` profile page (app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:41), increments the counter and awards with `refId: `${Math.floor(viewCount / 25)}`` — values "1", "2", "3" (line 103). Path B, recordProfileView, reached from the Discord screens (lib/discord/screens.ts:449), awards with `refId: String(u.views)` — values "25", "50", "75" (lib/identity.ts:179). qe_dedup_idx keys on (user_id, quest_id, action_key, ref_type, ref_id) (lib/db/index.ts:102); "1" and "25" are different ref_ids, so the same 25-view boundary pays twice, once per path. Separately, path A is reachable by any anonymous HTTP GET — the only guard is `if (!isOwner)` (line 98), and `viewer` is null for logged-out requests. There is no viewer dedup, no IP throttle, no bot filter. Path A also writes the counter as an absolute value read earlier in the same render (`set({ profileViews: viewCount })`, line 99) rather than `+1` SQL, so it clobbers concurrent increments from path B and loses updates under concurrent views. recordProfileView is imported at line 16 of the page and never called — dead evidence of a refactor that left two competing implementations in production.
  - *Consequence:* 75 CP/day (25 CP x cap 3, lib/quests.ts:91) mintable by an unauthenticated `for i in $(seq 1 75); do curl https://<host>/u/<slug>; done` — no account, no session, no CAPTCHA. That is 15% of the daily ceiling with no human involved. It also means the profile-view number shown to gamers and, by extension, any reach figure derived from it, is an open counter that anyone on the internet can drive. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §2.1 classifies 'every 25 profile views' as PASSIVE — i.e. explicitly counted as revenue-generating attention that produces no screen. It produces no screen because no human ever looked.
  - *Exploit:* `for i in $(seq 1 100); do curl -s -o /dev/null https://<host>/u/<target-slug>; done` from any machine, logged out. Each 25th request awards the profile owner 25 CP. Works against your own profile from an incognito window or any VPS.
  - *Cites:* `app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:41, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:99, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:102, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:103`
- **[SEVERE]** Four of the nineteen live actions have no emitter anywhere in the product, so three of the four shipped mission templates contain tasks no gamer can ever complete.
  - *Mechanism:* `challenge_progress` (lib/quests.ts:72), `share_card` (90), `stat_levelup` (103) and `play_session` (107) carry non-zero weights and caps in ACTION_CATALOG but are never passed to awardQuestAction anywhere in app/ or lib/. A grep for each string across the whole tree returns hits only in lib/quests.ts (the catalog), lib/missions.ts (the templates), lib/cp-economics.ts (a model), and lib/db/seed-activity.ts (demo data). `best_profile_award` appears only in seed-activity.ts:135 — demo only. Yet every one of the four MISSION_TEMPLATES is built on them: 'Show up' requires challenge_progress x4 (100 CP), share_card x3 (75 CP) and stat_levelup x4 (100 CP) — 275 of its 500 CP, 55%, is unearnable. 'Get seen' requires challenge_progress x3, play_session x3, stat_levelup x2 — 200 CP, 40%. 'Give back' requires challenge_progress x4, share_card x3, stat_levelup x3 — 250 CP, 50%. 'Bring people' requires challenge_progress x3, play_session x3, stat_levelup x2 — 200 CP, 40%.
  - *Consequence:* The 500 CP daily ceiling is not merely unreachable through the missions — the missions are the product's central daily loop and between 40% and 55% of every one of them is dead. lib/missions.ts:44-48 states the arithmetic is 'asserted in tests/db/missions.mts against the LIVE prices, not against these numbers, so the table and the missions can never drift apart again.' The test asserts prices; it does not and cannot assert that an action is ever emitted. The commit message for the repricing work is 'B61: measured before building — 500 is not reachable from eight actions'; the fix repriced the catalog rather than building the emitters, so 500 is still not reachable, now for a different reason. Every retention, ARPU and CP-cost projection built on 'a gamer who never misses earns $0.05 a day' (lib/quests.ts:62) is modelling a day that cannot occur.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:72, lib/quests.ts:90, lib/quests.ts:103, lib/quests.ts:107`
- **[SEVERE]** awardQuestAction's error handling is a bare catch that discards the error object, so every failure in the money path — a dropped Neon connection, a constraint violation, a bad settings row — is indistinguishable from success and leaves no trace.
  - *Mechanism:* `try { ... } catch { /* gamification is non-fatal — never block the underlying action */ }` at lib/quests.ts:482 and 533. The catch binds no variable, logs nothing, and emits no metric. Because there is no transaction, any throw between line 519 (ledger insert) and line 531 leaves the ledger and the progress table permanently disagreeing with no record that it happened. The same pattern repeats in the two reads the award depends on: dailyCpCeiling returns DEFAULT_DAILY_CP_CEILING = 500 on ANY error (441) and cpEarnedToday returns 0 on ANY error (451). Those two defaults compose into the worst possible failure mode: if the platform_settings read fails while an admin has the ceiling set to 100, the ceiling silently reverts to 500 — a 5x cost increase from a transient network blip. And if the cpEarnedToday read fails, `already = 0` and `room = ceiling`, so every award for the rest of that request behaves as if the gamer has earned nothing all day. Both are fail-OPEN in the direction that costs money, in a codebase whose own abuse test (tests/db/abuse.mts:43-45) states the principle 'The dangerous case... Fail CLOSED.'
  - *Consequence:* Under Neon degradation — the single most likely production incident for a serverless app on an HTTP-per-query driver — the daily ceiling silently stops existing for the duration. Every gamer active during the incident can earn up to the full ceiling per request rather than per day. There is no alert, no log, and no post-hoc way to identify which rows were minted during the window, because the catch discarded the evidence. Compare capsToday at line 759, which uses the identical bare catch and returns `empty` with ceiling 500 — so the disclosure screen also lies during the same incident.
  - *Exploit:* Not attacker-triggerable directly, but observable: any Neon 5xx or timeout on the platform_settings query during a burst removes the ceiling for that request.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:482, lib/quests.ts:533, lib/quests.ts:441, lib/quests.ts:451`
- **[MATERIAL]** quest_events rows are written and user_quest_progress is incremented as two independent unbatched statements, so a unique-index conflict silently inflates QP, tiers and completions with no ledger row behind them.
  - *Mechanism:* The ledger insert at 519-521 ends in `.onConflictDoNothing()`. The very next statement, unconditionally and without inspecting the insert's row count, runs `db.insert(schema.userQuestProgress)...onConflictDoUpdate({ set: { qp: sql`${qp} + ${weight}` } })` at 524-528. When the dedup select at 511 loses a race and the unique index qe_dedup_idx (lib/db/index.ts:102) rejects the row, the ledger correctly records nothing — and QP is incremented anyway. unlockTiers (530) and maybeCompleteQuest (531) then run off that phantom QP, minting tier badges, incrementing `completions`, and adding `maxThreshold * completed` to `lifetimeQp` (lib/quests.ts:551-553). The same non-atomicity works in reverse: if the process dies or the Neon HTTP call fails between line 521 and line 524, the ledger holds a paid row that no quest progress reflects. Neither direction is recoverable, because the whole body is wrapped in a bare `catch { }` at 533 that discards the error object entirely — there is no log line, no metric, no dead-letter row. A partial write is indistinguishable from a no-op.
  - *Consequence:* Tier badges and quest completions — which gate challenge entry via `gate_quest_id` / `gate_min_badges` (lib/db/index.ts:151-152) — can be obtained without the CP that is supposed to buy them. lifetimeQp inflates without bound. And because the swallow at 533 is total, none of it is observable: an operator has no way to know how often this fires.
  - *Exploit:* Fire two identical awards concurrently with the same (userId, questId, actionKey, refType, refId) — e.g. two simultaneous requests for the same challenge join. One wins the ledger insert; the loser's insert is swallowed by onConflictDoNothing but still adds `weight` QP at line 524. Repeat to farm tier badges at half price.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:519, lib/quests.ts:521, lib/quests.ts:524, lib/quests.ts:527`
- **[MATERIAL]** docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §5.2 describes a ledger row, a cp:0 record and a stored 'daily_cap_reached' reason that do not exist — and the code does the opposite, deleting zero-CP rows from the ledger view.
  - *Mechanism:* The doc states: 'Past 500 CP, an action is still logged with cp: 0 and a stored reason of daily_cap_reached. The quest ledger and the wallet page render it as "Clicked an ad — daily maximum reached, no points added". The row is what proves to a gamer that we saw what they did.' Three separate falsehoods. (1) quest_events has no reason column — the table is id, user_id, quest_id, action_key, qp_awarded, cp_awarded, ref_type, ref_id, created_at (lib/db/schema.ts:843-864, mirrored at lib/db/index.ts:85-100); a repo-wide grep for the string 'daily_cap_reached' returns zero hits in any .ts or .tsx file. (2) Past the PER-ACTION cap no row is written at all — lib/quests.ts:505 `continue`s before reaching the insert at 519. (3) Past the global ceiling a cp:0 row is written, and getCpLedger then explicitly filters it out: `return rows.filter((r) => Number(r.qp) > 0)` (lib/quests.ts:783), with a comment justifying the removal. The ledger renders the exact opposite of what the doc promises.
  - *Consequence:* The 'full disclosure' half of the stated B17 policy — 'no interruption, full disclosure' (lib/quests.ts:700) — is not implemented on the path the doc names. A gamer who hits a cap sees nothing in the CP log; the row that the doc calls 'what proves to a gamer that we saw what they did' either does not exist or is deliberately hidden. This is a third independently verified false claim in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md, alongside the passive/active flag and the ad_click repricing already established. The document should be treated as a wish list, not a specification, and any diligence figure sourced from it re-derived from code.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:843, lib/db/schema.ts:864, lib/quests.ts:505, lib/quests.ts:783`
- **[MATERIAL]** There are two live definitions of a gamer's total CP and they disagree by the exact multiplier the ledger split was built to remove; the inflated one is what the dashboard labels 'Total earned'.
  - *Mechanism:* lib/quests.ts:429-430 asserts CP_PAID_SQL is the single definition: 'Every read of "how much CP" goes through this expression; there is no second definition anywhere.' getTotalCp (684-695) honours that, summing COALESCE(cp_awarded, qp_awarded) over the ledger, and its own comment (686-691) explains precisely why progress cannot be used: 'Progress is now credited to every listening quest while CP is credited once, so qp + lifetimeQp across quests double-counts by exactly the multiplier this item removed.' Forty lines earlier, QuestView.totalCp is computed as exactly that forbidden expression: `totalCp: (p?.lifetimeQp ?? 0) + qp` (line 668) — read from user_quest_progress, not the ledger. app/feed/page.tsx:116 pipes it into DashQuest.totalCp and components/FeedDashboard.tsx:146 renders it beside a CP icon as 'Total earned:'.
  - *Consequence:* A gamer sees a 'Total earned' figure on the feed dashboard that exceeds their spendable wallet balance by the number of quests listening to their actions. That gap is a support ticket per user and, worse, reads externally as withheld money — the precise failure lib/missions.ts:301-304 says the single-ledger design exists to prevent. It also means any internal CP-outstanding metric read from user_quest_progress rather than quest_events overstates the liability, so the two numbers management might quote can differ by an integer multiple depending on which one was pulled.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:429, lib/quests.ts:431, lib/quests.ts:668, lib/quests.ts:684`
- **[MATERIAL]** The money tables have no migration history: drizzle/0000_mighty_fenris.sql and its snapshot contain no quest_events, quests, trophies or marketplace_orders — the real schema is 219 hand-written raw SQL strings replayed on every cold boot with 'does not exist' errors swallowed.
  - *Mechanism:* drizzle/ contains exactly one migration (0000_mighty_fenris.sql, 454 lines, journal has a single entry). `grep -n 'quest_events' drizzle/0000_mighty_fenris.sql` returns nothing; `grep -c 'quest_events' drizzle/meta/0000_snapshot.json` returns 0. The tables that hold the CP ledger, the quests, the trophies and the marketplace orders exist only as raw template-literal strings in a 219-element COLUMN_MIGRATIONS array (lib/db/index.ts:26-842), replayed by runColumnMigrations on every cold start (called at 870 and 899). Its catch at 848 swallows both `already exists` AND `does not exist` — so a CREATE TABLE that fails for a reason matching either pattern leaves the table missing with only a console.warn that nobody reads in a serverless log. The unique dedup index that the entire double-pay defence rests on (qe_dedup_idx, line 102) is created with IF NOT EXISTS inside that same swallowing loop: if it ever fails to create, the dedup select at lib/quests.ts:511 becomes the only protection and the onConflictDoNothing at 521 becomes a no-op, silently.
  - *Consequence:* Drizzle Kit is disconnected from the production schema. Anyone running `drizzle-kit generate` against schema.ts produces a diff that believes half the database does not exist — a destructive migration waiting to be applied. There is no reviewable, versioned history for any schema change touching money, so 'when did the dedup index arrive and against which data' is unanswerable. Operationally, 219 sequential DDL statements over neon-http (one HTTPS round trip each) run on every cold lambda, adding seconds to cold start and creating a wide window where two racing cold boots run DDL against each other — a race the code acknowledges at lib/db/index.ts:895 but only for the DDL_STATEMENTS path, not this one.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:26, lib/db/index.ts:85, lib/db/index.ts:102, lib/db/index.ts:848`
- **[MATERIAL]** The redeem_trophy award is granted at request time and is reversible by the gamer, so a redeem/cancel loop mints CP and spams the admin payout queue — and two shipped missions instruct gamers to do it twice a day.
  - *Mechanism:* requestRedeem awards `redeem_trophy` keyed on the freshly minted `redeemId` (app/actions/trophies.ts:163) and its own comment states the award is deliberate at REQUEST rather than at payment. cancelRedeem (171-181) then sets the request to `cancelled` and returns every trophy to `held` (176) — with no reversal of the CP and no record on the quest side. Because each new request generates a new `redeemId`, the dedup key is different every time, so the only thing standing between a gamer and unlimited redeem_trophy CP is the per-action daily cap of 2 — which is itself raceable (finding 2). Each request also fires notifyAdmins (164).
  - *Consequence:* 50 CP/day free per account for zero real activity, plus two admin notifications per account per day for payout requests that are immediately cancelled. Two of the four shipped mission templates make this an instruction rather than an exploit: 'Show up' asks for redeem_trophy x1 (lib/missions.ts:60) and 'Give back' asks for redeem_trophy x2 (lib/missions.ts:90). The product is telling every gamer, every day, to file cash-out requests as a points-farming activity. At any meaningful DAU that is a payout-review queue built out of noise, and it structurally destroys the signal staff would use to spot a genuine fraudulent redemption.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Hold any trophy with value > 0. 2. Call requestRedeem on it — earn 25 CP, admins get pinged. 3. Call cancelRedeem — the trophy returns to `held`. 4. Repeat. The cap of 2/day is the only limit, and finding 2 removes it.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/trophies.ts:163, app/actions/trophies.ts:171, app/actions/trophies.ts:176, lib/missions.ts:60`


#### A4 · Cash-out path safety (10)

- **[FATAL]** buyTrophy has no transaction and no atomic debit — N concurrent purchases mint N trophies from one balance, and the driver in use physically cannot fix it with a transaction.
  - *Mechanism:* buyTrophy re-reads the balance at lib/marketplace.ts:223 (`cpWallet` = getTotalCp − SUM(marketplace_orders.cpSpent WHERE status='complete')), checks it at :224, then writes the order at :234 and the award at :244. There is no transaction, no row lock, no unique constraint, and no conditional UPDATE. `marketplace_orders` carries only two non-unique indexes (lib/db/schema.ts:698-701), so nothing at the DB layer serialises this. Balance is a derived SUM, never a stored decrementable column, so there is not even a `WHERE balance >= price` to race against. Fire K concurrent POSTs to `purchaseTrophy` with a balance of exactly one trophy: all K executions read the same pre-insert SUM, all K pass the check at :224, all K insert. Result: K `user_trophies` rows at status 'held', each carrying `trophies.value` dollars, for one trophy's worth of CP. `cpWallet` then clamps with `Math.max(0, earned - spent)` at :140, so the overdraft is invisible — the balance reads 0 and the ledger reconciles to itself. This is not patchable with `db.transaction()`: lib/db/index.ts:911-913 constructs the client with `drizzle-orm/neon-http`, the HTTP driver, which has no interactive transaction support. `grep -rn '\.transaction('` over lib/ and app/ returns zero hits — the entire money layer is non-atomic by construction, not by oversight.
  - *Consequence:* A gamer who has legitimately earned 50,000 CP (100 days at the 500 CP/day ceiling — nominally $5 of value) and fires 100 parallel requests walks away with 100 × 'Bronze Ember Cup' = $500 of redeemable liability. 100× multiplier on a single account, repeatable every time the balance refills, bounded only by HTTP concurrency. At 1,000 exploiting accounts that is $500,000 of redeemable trophies against $5,000 of earned CP. The existing test at tests/db/marketplace.mts:144-148 asserts 'a second purchase is refused on the SERVER, not by the page' — it is a sequential call and proves nothing about the concurrent case; there is no `Promise.all` or concurrency assertion in any of tests/db/marketplace.mts, wallet.mts, gifting.mts or cp-economics.mts.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Sign in; accumulate (or mint, see the beacon finding) CP equal to the cheapest trophy — 50,000 CP for the $5 bronze. 2. Capture the `purchaseTrophy` server-action POST from /marketplace with DevTools. 3. Replay it 100× in parallel with the same session cookie (`for i in $(seq 100); do curl ... & done`). 4. All 100 read the same balance, all 100 insert an order and a 'held' award. 5. Open /profile — 100 bronze cups. 6. Select all and submit one redeem request for $500.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:222-247, lib/marketplace.ts:117-141, lib/db/schema.ts:683-701, lib/db/index.ts:911-913`
- **[FATAL]** requestRedeem is a read-then-write race: the same trophy can be locked into unlimited simultaneous redemption requests, each carrying its full cash value, and the provider's idempotency key cannot catch it.
  - *Mechanism:* app/actions/trophies.ts:141-144 SELECTs the awards with `status = 'held'`, :145 checks the count matches, :150 INSERTs a `trophy_redeems` row with `amount` = the summed live trophy value, and :154 UPDATEs the awards to 'pending'. Four separate statements, no transaction, and critically the UPDATE at :154 is `WHERE id IN (ids)` with **no `AND status = 'held'` guard** — so it cannot fail on a losing racer either. `awardIds` is a `jsonb` array (lib/db/schema.ts:733), which means no foreign key and no unique constraint can ever prevent the same award id appearing in two redeem rows. Fire K concurrent requestRedeem calls naming the same award: all K see 'held', all K insert a redeem row for the full amount, all K set 'pending'. The admin queue at /admin/redeems then shows K legitimate-looking requests. Tremendous's `external_id: req.payoutRef` (lib/payments/tremendous.ts:103) — described in-comment as 'the single most valuable line in this file' — keys on `trophy_redeems.id`, and the K rows have K distinct ids, so the provider sees K distinct orders and funds all of them. The only defence shipped is the client-side `disabled={pending}` on the submit button at components/TrophyCase.tsx:330-332, which is a UI affordance, not a server guard.
  - *Consequence:* One $50 Supernova Crystal becomes K × $50 of approved payouts. The gate is a human clicking Approve on K rows that look identical and correct — and since `approveRedeem` (app/actions/trophies.ts:217) writes no audit row, there is no artefact afterwards showing the same award was paid twice. Compounded with the buyTrophy race the multiplier is multiplicative: 100 trophies × 10 concurrent redeems each = 1,000 payout rows from one gamer's legitimate 50,000 CP. Money leaving Tremendous cannot be recalled — the codebase states this principle itself at lib/abuse.ts:151 ('Money that has left a payout provider cannot be brought back') and then applies the 30-day hold it recommends only to server payouts (app/actions/payouts.ts:201), never to gamer redemptions.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Hold one redeemable trophy. 2. Capture the `requestRedeem` server-action POST from the trophy case. 3. Replay it 10× in parallel with the same awardIds. 4. Ten `trophy_redeems` rows now exist, each $50, all pointing at the same award. 5. Wait for staff to work the queue.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/trophies.ts:139-154, app/actions/trophies.ts:170-181, lib/db/schema.ts:730-763, lib/payments/tremendous.ts:100-107`
- **[FATAL]** CP itself is mintable: /api/ads/beacon awards CP on an unauthenticated request body with a server-generated dedup key, and the cap/ceiling checks in awardQuestAction are read-then-write.
  - *Mechanism:* Two independent defects compound. (a) app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts accepts an arbitrary JSON body, requires only a session cookie, has no CSRF token, no rate limit, no verification that a creative was ever rendered, and no check that the caller has not already reported this impression. The dedup key passed to `awardQuestAction` is `refId: id` where `id = uid()` generated fresh on line 22 of that same request (:36) — so the `qe_dedup_idx` unique index at lib/db/index.ts:102 can never fire. The click path at :56 uses `payload.id ?? clickId`, also attacker-supplied or fresh. (b) `awardQuestAction` reads the daily ceiling and today's total at lib/quests.ts:490, reads the per-action count at :502, checks `>= cap` at :505, and only then inserts at :519. Under concurrency every parallel call reads `already = 0` and `c = 0`, so every one of them pays out. With ad_click at weight 25 / cap 3 (lib/quests.ts:116), 100 parallel click beacons all pass `count >= 3` and all insert: 2,500 CP in one burst against a documented 500 CP/day hard ceiling. `onConflictDoNothing()` at :521 offers no protection because the ref is unique per request.
  - *Consequence:* The '500 CP/day = $0.05 per gamer' figure that docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:32-42 calls 'the number to plan against' is not enforced. Even without the race, a script earns the full 500 CP/day ceiling in under a second with no gameplay — turning the per-gamer cost model into a pure function of how many accounts an attacker creates. lib/abuse.ts:1-22 explicitly reasons that 'fifty fake accounts at the new numbers is $2.50 a day rather than $63' and concludes the gamer side is de-risked; that conclusion assumes the ceiling holds, and it does not. With the race, one account can mint CP at whatever rate its connection sustains — and CP converts to redeemable trophies through buyTrophy. Secondary: the same endpoint writes `ad_impressions` rows that brands are billed against, so it is also a self-serve ad-fraud endpoint against the revenue side.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Sign in normally. 2. Read any `ccId` out of the ad markup on any page. 3. `for i in $(seq 200); do curl -X POST /api/ads/beacon -H 'Cookie: cluster_session=…' -d '{"type":"click","ccId":"<id>"}' & done`. 4. All 200 read count=0 and already=0; all insert quest_events with cpAwarded=25. 5. Check /wallet — thousands of CP. 6. Spend it in the marketplace.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:12-59, lib/quests.ts:490-521, lib/quests.ts:113-116, lib/db/index.ts:102`
- **[SEVERE]** One staff account is a complete unaudited mint-to-cash pipeline: create a trophy of any dollar value at any CP price, buy it, approve it, send it — no second approver, no area check, no audit row.
  - *Mechanism:* `saveTrophy` (app/actions/admin.ts:1110) is guarded by `requireStaff()` — the lowest privileged admin role — not by `requireArea('trophies')`. It accepts `value: Math.max(0, Number(...))` with no upper bound (:1120) and `cpPrice: Math.max(0, Math.round(...))` with no lower bound other than 0 (:1127). `priceOf` honours any explicit cpPrice unconditionally: `if (t.cpPrice && t.cpPrice > 0) return t.cpPrice;` (lib/marketplace.ts:96). So a staff account can publish a trophy worth $1,000,000 priced at 1 CP. It then buys it from its own gamer account for 1 CP, submits a redeem for $1,000,000, and — because `approveRedeem` (:217), `sendRedeem` (:247) and `markRedeemPaid` (:328) are *also* only `requireStaff()` — approves and sends it itself. There is no maker-checker anywhere on this path and no value threshold that escalates. The area-grant system exists (lib/permissions.ts:27-36) but `grep requireArea( app/actions/*.ts` shows it is applied only to 'storage' and 'ads'; none of the trophy or redemption actions use it. The department boundary is enforced only inside `app/admin/layout.tsx:73`, which is a *render-time* check — a server action executes before the layout renders, so a staff member whose department excludes trophies can still POST these actions directly. Blast radius is compounded by the audit gap: `audit()` is called for `badge.update`, `post.pin`, `partner.delete` and forty other trivia (app/actions/admin.ts:159-903), but app/actions/trophies.ts contains **zero** `audit()` calls — approving, sending and marking-paid a cash payout leave no admin-attributed record at all.
  - *Consequence:* Blast radius of one compromised staff credential is the company's entire ability to pay, in a single session, with the only forensic trace being the `trophy.create`/`trophy.update` audit row from saveTrophy (:1147/:1150) and no record of who approved or sent the money. Because `sendRedeem` calls the live Tremendous adapter and Tremendous delivery is LINK-based and instant (lib/payments/tremendous.ts:95), funds are gone before any reconciliation. The 30-day anti-abuse hold the codebase itself designed (lib/abuse.ts:34, 'the ONLY reversal mechanism that exists') is wired to server payouts only (app/actions/payouts.ts:201) and never to this path.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Compromise any account with role='staff'. 2. POST saveTrophy with value=250000, cpPrice=1, inMarketplace=on. 3. From a gamer account with ≥1 CP, buy it. 4. requestRedeem for $250,000. 5. approveRedeem(id). 6. sendRedeem(id). 7. Collect the Tremendous link.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/admin.ts:1110-1128, lib/marketplace.ts:96, app/actions/trophies.ts:217-236, app/actions/trophies.ts:247-308`
- **[SEVERE]** There is no KYC, no identity verification, no sanctions screening and no tax reporting on the cash-out path — the only gate is a self-declared date of birth and a self-declared country typed into the same form that requests the money.
  - *Mechanism:* `requestRedeem` takes `input.birthDate` and `input.country` straight from the client (app/actions/trophies.ts:91-95), writes them to the users row unvalidated, and then evaluates `eligibilityOf(age, country)` against those very values (:99-105). Nothing checks a document, a payment instrument, an IP, or a third-party identity provider. The 18+ rule (lib/eligibility.ts:26) and the sanctions block (lib/eligibility.ts:38-42, six countries, self-described as 'a placeholder for counsel') are both defeated by typing a different string. A grep of the entire repository for `kyc`, `sanction`, `ofac`, `1099`, `w-8ben`, `aml` and `identity verif` returns hits only in comments, a vendor blurb and the eligibility placeholders — there is no screening implementation anywhere. `US_REPORT_THRESHOLD` (lib/eligibility.ts:56) and `annualRecipients` produce a list for a human; nothing files anything, and the aggregation is per `userId`, so any number of sybil accounts each stay under $600 by construction. Separately, `payer('rewards')` falls back to `manualPayout` whenever TREMENDOUS_API_KEY is absent (lib/payments/index.ts:92-103), and `manualPayout.send()` returns `{ok:true}` having done nothing (lib/payments/manual.ts:43-48) — the redeem row moves to 'sent' with no link and staff are expected to wire money by hand.
  - *Consequence:* The marketing claim that trophies are 'redeemable for real cash' is backed by, in the default configuration, a human transferring money out-of-band after reading a self-attested birth date. For a prize-and-promotion scheme paying strangers worldwide this is not a control environment an acquirer or a payments partner will accept: minors can be paid, sanctioned-jurisdiction residents can be paid, the same natural person can be paid through N accounts, and no information return is generated. Regulatory exposure here is not quantifiable from code but it is the kind that ends a banking relationship, and remediating it means building the identity layer that does not exist rather than tuning one that does.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Create an account from a sanctioned jurisdiction or as a 15-year-old. 2. Farm or mint CP. 3. Buy a trophy. 4. On the redeem form, type a birth date in 1990 and select 'US'. 5. eligibilityOf returns ok. 6. Repeat across N accounts, each staying under $600/yr so annualRecipients never flags anyone.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/trophies.ts:85-106, lib/eligibility.ts:26-42, lib/eligibility.ts:105-113, lib/eligibility.ts:44-56`
- **[SEVERE]** `marketplaceWallet.liability` — the one number the code calls 'what we owe' — is wrong in both directions and is displayed on exactly one admin page that nothing forces anyone to open.
  - *Mechanism:* `liability` is `SUM(marketplace_orders.value) WHERE status='complete'` (lib/marketplace.ts:363). That is gross lifetime sales value of BOUGHT trophies. It is understated because it counts nothing awarded through `awardChallengeTrophies` (lib/trophies.ts:89-114) or Profile of the Week (lib/profile-week.ts:475-486) — and lib/trophy-admin.ts:28 states in-repo that 'won, never bought … is most trophies'. It is simultaneously overstated because there is no join to `user_trophies.status`, so every trophy already redeemed and paid is still counted as outstanding. The correct figure — SUM(trophies.value) over `user_trophies WHERE status='held'` — is computed nowhere in the product. `stuckTotal` (lib/stuck-money.ts:222-230) deliberately excludes held-trophy value. `lib/admin-metrics.ts:99-100` counts trophy rows and pending redeems as integers, not dollars. `lib/finance.ts` — the module that models the company's economics — contains no reference to trophyRedeems, userTrophies or trophies.value at all. The only surface is the `Redeemable liability` stat tile at app/admin/marketplace/page.tsx:42.
  - *Consequence:* The company cannot answer 'what do we owe if every held trophy is redeemed today' from any screen, and the number it does show is not that number. On a shelf where a legendary is $50 and every challenge and every weekly vote mints more, the true outstanding obligation is a growing figure that appears in no financial model, no dashboard, and no alert. Combined with the two race conditions above — which mint held trophies without minting matching `marketplace_orders` rows in the buyTrophy case, and mint redeem rows with no matching trophy consumption in the requestRedeem case — the reported liability drifts further from reality precisely as the exploit runs. The assertion that supposedly protects this is `ok('and what we OWE if every trophy is cashed out', mw.liability >= 0 && typeof mw.liability === 'number')` (tests/db/marketplace.mts:194-196): a tautology that passes for any value.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:349-376, app/admin/marketplace/page.tsx:42, lib/trophies.ts:89-114, lib/profile-week.ts:475-486`
- **[MATERIAL]** docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md's stated worst case — '100% redemption at full value' bounded by $0.05/gamer/day — is false: challenge and Profile-of-the-Week trophies mint redeemable dollars entirely outside the CP economy.
  - *Mechanism:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:41-43 asserts 'CP is a liability, not a cash cost, until it is redeemed … Worst case — 100% redemption at full value — is the number above', where the number above is the 500 CP/day ceiling = $0.05/gamer/day. That bound only covers trophies bought with CP. `awardChallengeTrophies` (lib/trophies.ts:89-114) inserts `user_trophies` rows at status 'held' carrying the trophy's full `trophies.value`, consuming zero CP. The seeded catalogue is $5/$10/$25/$50 (lib/db/seed.ts:73-76), so a single challenge win is $50 — equal to 1,000 days at the CP ceiling. Profile of the Week does the same weekly (lib/profile-week.ts:475-486). Worse, on the server-owner path the prize pool is a number the owner types with no upper bound (`prizeValue: Math.max(0, Math.floor(input.prizeValue ?? 0))`, lib/challenge-requests.ts:74), `podiumFor` then picks the highest-value trophies at or under 60/30/10 of it (lib/challenge-requests.ts:310-322), and `sponsorPrice` is set to 0 for any non-brand request (lib/challenge-requests.ts:224). An owner who types 1000000 gets the top trophy in the catalogue on all three places for $0 of revenue.
  - *Consequence:* The per-gamer cost model that the whole revenue equation in docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.3 is derived from ('minimum views per gamer per day = 50 ÷ CPM') understates the true cost of a gamer by however much trophy prize value the platform hands out — a figure that is independent of the ceiling and appears in no model. One unsponsored server challenge approved with an inflated prizeValue mints up to $150 of redeemable liability against $0 of revenue, repeatable per guild. Staff approval and a separate publish click are in the loop, which is the only thing standing between this and unbounded issuance — a human reviewing a number they have no tooling to price.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:41-43, lib/trophies.ts:89-114, lib/profile-week.ts:475-486, lib/db/seed.ts:73-76`
- **[MATERIAL]** Gifting is an unrestricted transfer of redeemable dollar value between accounts, so the per-account eligibility gate can be routed around by anyone.
  - *Mechanism:* buyTrophy resolves any recipient by public profile slug (lib/marketplace.ts:210-217) and writes the award directly to that recipient at status 'held' (lib/marketplace.ts:244-247). The only checks are that the slug exists and the account is active. The recipient's eligibility is never consulted at purchase, and eligibility is only ever evaluated against the *redeemer* at redemption time (app/actions/trophies.ts:99-105). So the 18+ rule and the sanctions block bind the account that presses Redeem, not the account that generated the value.
  - *Consequence:* Any control keyed to the individual account is decorative: a farm of N accounts (or an under-18 or sanctioned-jurisdiction gamer) converts CP to trophies and gifts them to one collector account that passes the self-attested gate. The collector is also the only account that appears in `annualRecipients`, which is fine for them — but it means the sanctions and age controls provide no coverage at all over who actually earned the value. There is no cap on gifts received, no velocity limit, and no flag raised anywhere when one account accumulates trophies from many strangers.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Farm CP across N accounts (trivially, via the beacon). 2. Each buys the cheapest trophy and gifts it to collector@. 3. Collector holds N trophies, declares an eligible age and country, and redeems the lot in one request.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:207-217, lib/marketplace.ts:244-247, app/actions/trophies.ts:85-106, lib/eligibility.ts:105-113`
- **[MATERIAL]** No refund path exists anywhere, despite the schema and comments both asserting one — a mispriced, mis-gifted or fraudulently-obtained purchase is unwindable.
  - *Mechanism:* `marketplace_orders.awardId` is documented as 'The award row this created, so a refund can find it' (lib/db/schema.ts:689-690) and `status` is documented as 'complete \| refunded' (:696). Both `cpSpent` (lib/marketplace.ts:124) and `marketplaceWallet` (:367) filter on `status='complete'`, so the read side is built for it. Nothing in the codebase ever writes `status='refunded'` — a repo-wide grep for 'refund' returns only comments, a UI blurb, and the two schema comments above.
  - *Consequence:* When the buyTrophy race is exploited, or a staff member fat-fingers `value` on a trophy that 4,000 gamers already bought, or a trophy is gifted to the wrong slug (which the code notes has no undo — lib/gift-search.ts:5, components/TrophyCheckout.tsx:17), there is no in-product remediation. The only lever is a manual SQL edit against a production Neon database, which is both a control failure and a further audit gap. This matters most as the mitigation that does not exist for the fatal findings above: even after detecting the race, the CP cannot be clawed back and the trophies cannot be voided through the product.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:689-690, lib/db/schema.ts:696, lib/marketplace.ts:124, lib/marketplace.ts:367`
- **[MINOR]** The realized redemption rate is 12,500–20,000 CP per dollar on three of the four seeded trophies, not the '10,000 CP = $1' stated on the shelf and in the commercial model.
  - *Mechanism:* `priceOf` multiplies the dollar value by `cpPerDollar` AND by `TIER_MULTIPLIER` (lib/marketplace.ts:98-99), where bronze=1, silver=1.25, gold=1.5, legendary=2 (:64-66). Against the seeded catalogue (lib/db/seed.ts:73-76) the actual CP-per-dollar a gamer achieves is: Bronze Ember Cup $5 → 50,000 CP = 10,000 CP/$; Silver Star Cup $10 → 125,000 CP = 12,500 CP/$; Champion's Nebula Cup $25 → 375,000 CP = 15,000 CP/$; Supernova Crystal $50 → 1,000,000 CP = 20,000 CP/$. Only the bronze hits the advertised rate. The 500 CP floor at :102 makes things worse still for any trophy under $0.05, never better.
  - *Consequence:* The headline exchange rate in docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:32 is the best case available on one of four SKUs; the weighted reality is closer to 14,000 CP/$. This runs in the company's favour financially — the cost model is conservative by roughly 40% — but it is a disclosure problem for a product marketed on 'redeemable for real cash' at a stated rate, and it interacts badly with the fact that the marketplace UI shows a CP price and a dollar value side by side (app/admin/marketplace/page.tsx and the gamer shelf) without stating that the ratio differs by tier.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:55, lib/marketplace.ts:64-66, lib/marketplace.ts:92-103, lib/db/seed.ts:73-76`


#### A5 · The 15-screen guarantee (14)

- **[FATAL]** The 15-screen guarantee is broken outright: a gamer can be credited 525 CP — over the full 500 daily ceiling — without opening a single ad-carrying screen.
  - *Mechanism:* I classified every catalogue action by what actually calls awardQuestAction for it, not by the doc's table. Nine shipped actions are credited to a gamer who opened nothing on Cluster. Full arithmetic from the shipped weights/caps: win_challenge 100x1=100 (lib/quests.ts:77, fired by the cron at lib/sync.ts:204); top3_challenge 50x1=50 (lib/quests.ts:76, same cron line, so ONE challenge result mints 150 CP in one event); finish_challenge 25x2=50 (lib/quests.ts:69, lib/sync.ts:203); profile_views_25 25x3=75 (lib/quests.ts:91 — awarded to the profile OWNER when OTHER people open the page, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:103); follower_gained 25x2=50 (lib/quests.ts:92 — awarded to targetUserId, app/actions/social.ts:83); profile_vote_received 25x2=50 (lib/quests.ts:93, lib/identity.ts:78,124 — awarded to profileUserId); gift_received 25x2=50 (lib/quests.ts:97 — awarded to recipientId, lib/marketplace.ts:275); botlist_vote 25x2=50 (lib/quests.ts:119 — credited from an inbound top.gg webhook, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:111; the vote happens on top.gg, which carries no Cluster creative); bot_added 25x2=50 (lib/quests.ts:123 — credited from the Discord OAuth callback, lib/discord/install-credit.ts:124; the consent screen is Discord's, not ours). Sum = 100+50+50+75+50+50+50+50+50 = 525 CP >= the 500 ceiling. Screens opened by the earner: ZERO. Even if you refuse to call botlist_vote and bot_added passive, the residue is 425 CP, and the remaining 75+ comes from ad_impression (1x25=25) and ad_click (25x3=75) on ONE page left open — see the AdSlot rotation finding. Minimum ad-carrying screens to reach 500 CP: 0 (strictly), 1 (generously).
  - *Consequence:* break-even CPM = 50 / views-per-gamer-per-day. At 0 views the equation has no solution — no CPM at any price clears it. At the 1-screen variant it is $50 CPM, against a $5 sell price (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:1.2), a $4 floor, and the top of their OWN cited market band of $15. At 1,000 daily gamers hitting the ceiling this way the CP liability is $1,500/month against $0 of self-generated impression inventory. The company's stated 'the business is the gap' is inverted: every incremental engaged gamer is pure loss.
  - *Exploit:* No exploit needed — this is the ordinary reward path. A gamer plays their tracked game, wins one challenge (100+50 via the cron), finishes two more (50), and lets three profile views, two follows, two profile votes and two received gifts land while their browser is closed. 425 CP with the client offline. Two bot-list votes on top.gg finish the day at 525.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:77, lib/quests.ts:76, lib/quests.ts:69, lib/quests.ts:91`
- **[FATAL]** Part 2 of the guarantee — the 125 CP/day passive cap — does not exist in code, and there is no active/passive flag anywhere in the codebase.
  - *Mechanism:* The function that would have to contain the passive cap is awardQuestAction (lib/quests.ts:479-534). What it contains instead, in order: one global ceiling read (line 490, dailyCpCeiling), room = max(0, ceiling - already) (line 491), a per-quest per-action occurrence count check (lines 501-506), a (user,quest,action,refType,refId) dedup (lines 511-515), and the payment const cp = paid ? 0 : Math.min(weight, room) (line 516). There is no branch on any action class, no second budget, no passive accumulator. The ACTION_CATALOG element type (lib/quests.ts:46) has exactly five fields — key, label, group, defaultWeight, defaultCap — so the doc's claim at docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:131 that 'Every action in ACTION_CATALOG carries this flag. The mission validator and the CP ledger both read it' describes a field that does not exist and two readers that cannot read it. The nearest structure in the repo, lib/cp-economics.ts:153 SELF_SERVE_SECONDS, is not a passive flag: it is a hand-written effort estimate that still lists the four retired actions (write_post, write_comment, reaction_given) and omits share_card, challenge_progress, play_session, stat_levelup, redeem_trophy, gift_sent, gift_received, follower_gained and profile_views_25 entirely. lib/cp-economics.ts:17-26 ActionConfig also has no flag.
  - *Consequence:* Maximum passive CP under the shipped table is 525 (see finding 1), not 125 — 4.2x the doc's stated bound and above the entire 500-CP day. The mechanism the doc calls 'the mechanism the entire model rests on' is unimplemented, and the specific scenario the doc says 'the passive cap makes impossible' is the default behaviour.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:104, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:131, lib/quests.ts:46, lib/quests.ts:479`
- **[FATAL]** Four of the eight tasks in every shipped mission reference action keys that NOTHING in the product ever emits — the flagship mission is at most 45% completable.
  - *Mechanism:* challenge_progress (lib/quests.ts:72, 25 CP x cap 4), share_card (lib/quests.ts:90, 25 x 3), stat_levelup (lib/quests.ts:103, 25 x 4) and play_session (lib/quests.ts:107, 25 x 3) have ZERO awardQuestAction call sites. I grepped every occurrence of each string across the repo: they appear only in lib/quests.ts (the catalogue), lib/missions.ts (the templates) and tests. There is no dynamic dispatch that could reach them — every awardQuestAction call in the repo passes a string literal (I grepped for a variable action argument and got zero hits), and the only other consumer of QuestActionKey is lib/cp-economics.ts:153, which is a pure lookup table. Per template: 'Show up' (lib/missions.ts:50-64) loses challenge_progress 100 + share_card 75 + stat_levelup 100 = 275 of 500, max reachable 225 = 45%. 'Get seen' loses 200 (60% reachable). 'Give back' loses challenge_progress 100 + share_card 75 + stat_levelup 75 = 250 — exactly half. 'Bring people' loses 200. No shipped mission can be completed by anybody. The test at tests/db/missions.mts:87 asserts only that these keys 'exist and pay' in the table — it never asserts anything emits them — so the suite is green while the product is impossible.
  - *Consequence:* The four templates are the entire behavioural mechanism for extracting 15 screens from a gamer. Every one of them is impossible on the shipped code, and 45-60% of each mission's CP is unreachable by any user action. Combined with finding 1, the only reachable route to 500 CP is the passive one. The mission validator (lib/missions.ts:149-185) checks totals and caps but has no check that an action has an emitter — the exact class of bug it was written to prevent ('a task nobody can finish', lib/missions.ts:147).
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:72, lib/quests.ts:90, lib/quests.ts:103, lib/quests.ts:107`
- **[FATAL]** The Daily Mission is dead code: lib/missions.ts is imported by nothing except its own test — no page, no server action, no admin editor, no schema column.
  - *Mechanism:* I grepped every exported symbol of lib/missions.ts — MISSION_TEMPLATES, parseScheduled, templateForWeek, progressOf, streakFrom, milestonesHit, launchable, missionProblems — and the import path itself ('@/lib/missions'). The only file that imports the module is tests/db/missions.mts:34. progressOf, the function that would render a gamer's mission progress, has zero call sites. There is no stored schedule (no platform_settings key, no column), no streak record, no milestone award path, no admin editor. What the product DOES ship under the word 'missions' is an unrelated four-item onboarding checklist (lib/quest-game.ts:28 MissionConfig — connect an account, join a planet, join a challenge, spot 5 ads), surfaced at lib/quests.ts:670. It is a different type with a different shape and no CP arithmetic.
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md section 2.2 asserts 'The validator refuses to launch a mission that breaks it, exactly as it already refuses one that does not total 500.' The validator exists as a pure function wired to nothing; there is no launch path for it to refuse. The Daily Mission — the framing device the entire pivot document is built on, and the only thing that would direct a gamer to 15 screens — is not in the product at any level a user or an admin can reach.
  - *Cites:* `lib/missions.ts:1, tests/db/missions.mts:34, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:149, lib/quests.ts:670`
- **[FATAL]** /api/ads/beacon is unauthenticated, unrate-limited and unverified: anyone can forge billable impressions for any brand, and any logged-in gamer can mint 100 CP/day with curl and zero page loads.
  - *Mechanism:* The POST handler at app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:12 takes campaignCreativeId straight from the JSON body and inserts an ad_impressions row at line 23 BEFORE any session is consulted (the session read at line 18-19 is .catch(()=>null) and only gates the CP award). There is no origin check, no CSRF token, no signature over the served creative, no check that this ccId was ever served to this session, and no rate limit — middleware.ts:83 matches only ['/admin/:path*','/u/:path*'], so this route has no middleware at all. The ccId values are public: GET /api/ads/serve (app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:17) requires no auth and returns campaignCreativeId for every live creative. On the CP side, ad_impression is written with refId = a freshly generated uid (line 36) and ad_click with refId = payload.id ?? a fresh uid (line 56), so the dedup at lib/quests.ts:511 can never fire and only the daily caps bind: 1 CP x 25 + 25 CP x 3 = 100 CP.
  - *Consequence:* Two separate multi-million-dollar problems. (a) Advertiser fraud: 100,000 forged POSTs — a few MB of traffic, minutes of runtime — delivers a full $500 package to nobody. At any scale this is the entire ad product's integrity, and it is discoverable by any buyer running a log-level audit. (b) CP minting: 100 CP/day per account with zero screens, on top of the 525 passive CP, from a shell script. At 10,000 farmed accounts that is $100/day of liability for $0 of inventory.
  - *Exploit:* 1. curl -s 'https://<site>/api/ads/serve?placement=top_banner' and copy any creatives[].campaignCreativeId. 2. for i in $(seq 1 100000); do curl -s -XPOST https://<site>/api/ads/beacon -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"type":"impression","ccId":"<id>"}'; done — 100,000 billable impressions, one full package. 3. With a session cookie attached, 25 of those plus 3 POSTs of {"type":"click","ccId":"<id>"} credit 100 CP with no page ever loaded.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:12, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:18, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:34`
- **[FATAL]** The stated screen count for the flagship mission's reachable tasks is 2, not 15 — a $25 break-even CPM, 5x the sell price.
  - *Mechanism:* Walking 'Show up' (lib/missions.ts:50-64) task by task against the reachable subset: join_challenge x1 — one screen, the challenge page, which does carry AdSlot placement=challenge_sidebar (app/planets/[slug]/challenges/[challengeId]/page.tsx:498); but joinChallengeFor is equally reachable from a Discord button (app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:202), which opens no web screen at all. challenge_progress x4 — dead, zero screens. share_card x3 — dead, zero screens. follower_gained x2 — passive, credited to the followed user, zero screens. stat_levelup x4 — dead, zero screens. redeem_trophy x1 — one screen, /marketplace, which carries AdSlot placement=marketplace_top (app/marketplace/page.tsx:58). ad_click x3 — fires on a page already counted, zero additional screens. botlist_vote x2 — happens on top.gg, zero Cluster screens.
