Total distinct ad-carrying screens for everything a gamer can actually complete: TWO. The other three templates come out the same way (2-3).
  - *Consequence:* break-even CPM = 50 / 2 = $25.00. They sell at $5 (5x short), their admin floor is $4 (6.25x short), and the top of the market band they cite for community sponsorships is $15 (1.67x short). At the doc's own claimed 15 screens the break-even is $3.33 and the model works; at the shipped 2 it does not, at any price the market will pay. And this is the OPTIMISTIC number — the true worst case (finding 1) is 0 screens, where the equation has no solution.
  - *Cites:* `lib/missions.ts:50, app/planets/[slug]/challenges/[challengeId]/page.tsx:498, app/marketplace/page.tsx:58, app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:202`
- **[SEVERE]** Part 1 of the guarantee is false in the shipped catalogue: four actions pay more than 25 CP, and one cron event mints 150 CP — 30% of the day — in a single write.
  - *Mechanism:* COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md section 2 Part 1 states 'no action pays more than 25 CP. Therefore 500 CP is always 20 actions. There is no shortcut, no single high-value action that empties the day's allowance in one click.' The shipped table contradicts this four times: top3_challenge defaultWeight 50 (lib/quests.ts:76), win_challenge defaultWeight 100 (lib/quests.ts:77), best_profile_award defaultWeight 100 (lib/quests.ts:100), connect_account defaultWeight 50 (lib/quests.ts:109). Worse, lib/sync.ts:204 fires win_challenge AND top3_challenge on the same branch for the same challenge result, so one cron tick credits 150 CP. One win therefore consumes 30% of the 500-CP day; the doc's floor of 20 actions is actually as few as 12 for a gamer who wins once and connects one account (100+50+50 = 200 in three events).
  - *Consequence:* The doc's 'no shortcut' claim is the load-bearing premise of Part 3. With it false, 500 CP is not '20 actions' and the 375-CP active residue the whole model derives from does not exist. This is a claim in the investment materials that the source code falsifies on four lines.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:100, lib/quests.ts:76, lib/quests.ts:77, lib/quests.ts:100`
- **[SEVERE]** AdSlot logs a fresh billable impression on every rotation tick, so 'views' are decoupled from screens entirely — one idle browser tab can consume 8.6% of a brand's $500 package per day.
  - *Mechanism:* The impression effect at components/AdSlot.tsx:58-93 has dependency array [served, idx]. The rotation interval at lines 48-55 increments idx every max(3, rotationIntervalSeconds) seconds whenever the placement has >=2 creatives. On each idx change the cleanup at line 79-92 disconnects the observer and resets impressionId.current to null; the effect then re-runs, creates a new IntersectionObserver on the SAME still-intersecting element, the guard at line 66 (!impressionId.current) passes, and a new impression POST fires. There is no per-session, per-page or per-gamer suppression. /feed renders three or more slots simultaneously (app/feed/page.tsx:176, 259, 265), each rotating independently.
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:1.2 defines a view as 'one screen carrying one brand's creative'. The code bills per rotation tick, not per screen. At 3 slots and a 30s rotation, one tab left open generates 360 impressions/hour = 8,640/day = 8.64% of a $500/100,000-view package, from one person, from zero attention. Twelve such tabs deliver an entire brand package in 24 days against a screensaver. This makes the delivered-views number the brand pays for unauditable and inflates it away from the screen count the entire 15-screen argument is denominated in — the guarantee and the revenue are measuring different things in opposite directions.
  - *Exploit:* Open https://<site>/feed in a browser tab and walk away. No script, no account, no interaction.
  - *Cites:* `components/AdSlot.tsx:48, components/AdSlot.tsx:52, components/AdSlot.tsx:58, components/AdSlot.tsx:64`
- **[SEVERE]** ad_click still pays 25 CP at cap 3 against an ad_impression cap of 25 — a hard-wired 12% incentivised CTR, 34-240x the real display rate the company's own doc says will get them delisted.
  - *Mechanism:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md section 5.1 states 'ad_click drops to 0 CP... Real display is 0.05-0.35%. A 10% CTR with no conversion is the signature of incentivised fraud: it gets us discounted to nothing by buyers and delisted by networks. This is the single most reputation-damaging line in the old table.' lib/quests.ts:118 still carries ad_click at defaultWeight 25, defaultCap 3. lib/quests.ts:117 caps ad_impression at 25. The ceiling ratio the incentive structure therefore targets is 3/25 = 12.0%. All four mission templates still require ad_click x3 = 75 CP, 15% of the daily 500 (lib/missions.ts:61, 76, 91, 106).
  - *Consequence:* 12% CTR against a 0.05-0.35% market baseline is 34x to 240x. Every brand report the platform produces carries a number that reads as fraud to any media buyer who has seen a real campaign, and any programmatic partner (section 4 product 3) will refuse the inventory. The doc identifies this precisely and the code does the opposite — this is not an oversight in an unwritten area, it is a documented, prioritised change that was not made.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:117, lib/quests.ts:118, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:225, lib/missions.ts:61`
- **[SEVERE]** Discord card impressions carry no userId, so 'views per gamer per day' — the denominator of the break-even equation and of the doc's own admin danger-zone alert — is uncomputable for the largest surface.
  - *Mechanism:* logCardAdImpression (lib/cards/ads.ts:200-216) inserts into ad_impressions with id, campaignCreativeId, pagePath, deviceType and guildId — and no userId, despite the column existing and being nullable in the schema. The bot-post path at lib/discord/ads.ts:79-84 does the same. Discord is the surface the doc calls the primary inventory ('the cards are the ad inventory', lib/cards/ads.ts:11). Separately, the bot-post path logs exactly ONE impression per guild post regardless of how many members see it — a 500-member server is billed as 1 view — while the card path logs one per card render regardless of how many people see that message. Neither maps to a person.
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md section 6.1 promises a 'Gamer health' panel showing 'views per gamer per day' and section 6.2 promises a danger zone that goes red at 'Views per gamer below 10 (below break-even at $5)'. Neither exists — I grepped the whole repo for viewsPerGamer / 'views per gamer' / breakEven in app/ and lib/ and found zero hits outside lib/finance.ts, which models fundraising cash flow, not ad delivery. Section 7.3 also promises 'unique gamers reached' in the brand report; with a null userId on every Discord row it cannot be produced. The single metric that would tell the company whether the model is working cannot be computed from the data it stores.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cards/ads.ts:200, lib/cards/ads.ts:207, lib/discord/ads.ts:79, lib/db/schema.ts:0`
- **[SEVERE]** None of the seven required ad-serving changes in section 5.3 exist, and the doc's own admission 'We cannot currently deliver a promised number of views to anybody' is still true.
  - *Mechanism:* serveAds (lib/ads.ts:33-104) selects on placement, retiredAt, campaign status, creative status, brand status and date window (lines 61-71), sorts by priority then weight, and slices to maxCreativesInRotation (lines 75-76). There is no delivered counter, no views target, no pace comparison, no stop-at-target, no per-gamer frequency cap (the function does not receive a userId at all), no queue for brands past the rotation limit — they are silently dropped by the .slice at line 76, exactly the 'silent cutoff' item 4 forbids — and no category exclusivity. pickCardAd (lib/cards/ads.ts:101-124) still computes index = (hash(seed) + floor(at/window)) % live.length (line 111), which is the uniform hash assignment section 5.3 names as the problem. Cache/ad separation (item 5) is partially addressed — the ccId is in the cache key — but the placement payload itself is cached for 60s in a module-level Map keyed only by placement+device (app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:24), with no gamer dimension. Billable-impression cap per gamer (item 6): absent, and unimplementable without a userId (see the Discord finding).
  - *Consequence:* Zero of seven. The company cannot honour a $500/100,000-view contract with any determinism, cannot stop over-delivery, and cannot stop under-delivery. Combined with the rotation-tick inflation and the forgeable beacon, the delivered-views number on a brand invoice is arbitrary in both directions. Selling a guaranteed-volume package on this stack is a contractual exposure on every campaign written.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:232, lib/ads.ts:33, lib/ads.ts:61, lib/ads.ts:73`
- **[MATERIAL]** Setting the ceiling to 0 silently zeroes the entire CP economy while the admin UI says it means 'no ceiling' — and the exposure model on the same screen reports the opposite.
  - *Mechanism:* The save handler at app/actions/cp-calculator.ts:46 tells the admin 'Zero means no ceiling — say so deliberately.' dailyCpCeiling (lib/quests.ts:440) accepts 0 because the guard is n >= 0, and returns 0. awardQuestAction then computes room = max(0, 0 - already) = 0 (line 491) and cp = min(weight, 0) = 0 (line 516): every action in the product pays nothing, forever, silently. The disclosure surface hides it — capsToday returns ceilingHit: ceiling > 0 && ... = false (lib/quests.ts:758), so the screen a gamer opens to check their caps reports nothing wrong. Meanwhile the exposure model rendered on the SAME admin page treats ceiling 0 as unbounded: lib/cp-economics.ts:84-85 returns capped = Infinity and line 133 sets ceilingHolds = false. So the calculator warns of infinite exposure at the exact moment the engine has stopped paying anything at all.
  - *Consequence:* An admin acting on the UI's own instruction turns off all gamer earnings with no error, no log line saying so, and an on-screen model asserting the opposite. Every gamer keeps acting and earns zero; churn is total and the cause is invisible. There is also no upper bound on the ceiling (line 46 only rejects negatives), so a fat-fingered 5000 raises real per-gamer exposure to the full cap sum of 1,330 CP = $0.133/day, 2.66x plan, on one keystroke.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/cp-calculator.ts:46, lib/quests.ts:440, lib/quests.ts:491, lib/quests.ts:516`
- **[MATERIAL]** The 500-CP ceiling is read outside any transaction or lock, so concurrent awards overshoot it — and the beacon endpoint is the perfect concurrency source.
  - *Mechanism:* awardQuestAction reads the ceiling and cpEarnedToday together at lib/quests.ts:490 with a plain SELECT, computes room at line 491, and inserts at line 519. There is no transaction, no SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, no advisory lock, and no unique constraint that would serialise the read-modify-write. N concurrent invocations all read the same `already` and each grants up to its full weight. app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:34 makes this trivially reachable: N parallel HTTP POSTs are N parallel awards.
  - *Consequence:* The 500 ceiling is the doc's stated single guarantee ('one number, not twenty... that is what makes the policy auditable', lib/quests.ts:155-157). It is advisory under concurrency. With the caps summing to 1,330 (asserted at tests/db/cp-economics.mts:48), the true concurrent worst case per gamer per day is 1,330 CP = $0.133 — 2.66x the $0.05 every cost projection in the deck is built on. At 1,000 daily gamers that is $3,990/month against a planned $1,500.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:490, lib/quests.ts:491, lib/quests.ts:519, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:34`
- **[MINOR]** The in-file documentation of the CP table contradicts the table itself: lib/quests.ts states the caps sum to 624 CP/day; the shipped sum is 1,330.
  - *Mechanism:* The doc comment at lib/quests.ts:40-44 reads 'the per-action caps sum to 624 CP/day and the global ceiling allows 500 — five cents a day for a gamer doing everything we want at the maximum. Our worst case and our best case are the same event.' ACTION_CAP_SUM (lib/quests.ts:149) actually evaluates to 1,330 — I recomputed it from lines 68-135 (50+50+100+50+100+75+75+50+50+50+50+30+25+100+100+75+50+50+25+75+50+50) and tests/db/cp-economics.mts:48 asserts exactly 1330. The claim 'our worst case and our best case are the same event' is false by a factor of 2.66.
  - *Consequence:* Minor on its own, but it is the comment an engineer or a diligence reader consults to understand the exposure, sitting directly above the constant that disproves it. It matters because 1,330 is the real per-gamer exposure the moment the ceiling is bypassed (concurrency) or misconfigured — $0.133/gamer/day, 2.66x every cost line in the deck.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:40, lib/quests.ts:149, tests/db/cp-economics.mts:48`


#### A6 · Abuse surface (13)

- **[FATAL]** The 500 CP/day ceiling — the single number the whole cost model rests on — is a non-atomic read-then-write and is bypassable by concurrent requests to a public endpoint.
  - *Mechanism:* awardQuestAction reads the day's spend at lib/quests.ts:490 (`cpEarnedToday`) and the per-quest occurrence count at lib/quests.ts:502-505, then writes at lib/quests.ts:519. There is no transaction, no SELECT ... FOR UPDATE, and no advisory lock anywhere in the function (lib/quests.ts:479-534). The only DB-level protection is the unique index qe_dedup_idx on (user_id, quest_id, action_key, ref_type, ref_id) (lib/db/schema.ts:863), which only stops two rows with an IDENTICAL ref. The ad beacon at app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:22 mints a fresh `uid()` per request and passes it as refId (line 36), so every concurrent request carries a distinct ref and the unique index never fires. N simultaneous POSTs all read `already = X`, all compute `room = 500 - X`, and all insert. The creative id needed is handed out unauthenticated by app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:16.
  - *Consequence:* `exposure().perGamerCp = 500` (lib/cp-economics.ts:118-129) and `maxDailyCost` = $0.05/gamer/day (lib/cp-economics.ts:89-93) are both floors, not ceilings. The entire worst-case table in tests/db/cp-economics.mts:198-200 ($50k/day at 1M gamers) is an assertion about a guarantee the engine does not enforce under load. A single scripted account firing 500 parallel beacon POSTs can credit far more than 500 CP in one day; the amount is bounded by connection concurrency, not by policy. At 1,000 farmed accounts a 10x concurrency overshoot is $500/day rather than $50/day.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Register at /register (no email verification, no captcha — app/actions/auth.ts:11-54). 2. GET /api/ads/serve?placement=<any> — public, returns campaignCreativeId (app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:16-38). 3. Fire N concurrent `POST /api/ads/beacon {"type":"impression","ccId":"<id>"}` with the session cookie. Each request independently reads cpEarnedToday before any of them commits, so each is credited. 4. Repeat with type:"click" (25 CP each, cap 3 — same race).
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:490, lib/quests.ts:491, lib/quests.ts:501, lib/quests.ts:505`
- **[FATAL]** SELF_SERVE_SECONDS omits 775 CP/day of farmable surface and models 305 CP/day when the real binding number is the 500 ceiling — the abuse model describes 7 of 22 live actions.
  - *Mechanism:* SELF_SERVE_SECONDS (lib/cp-economics.ts:153-164) has 10 keys. Three of them — write_post, write_comment, reaction_given — are retired at defaultWeight 0 (lib/quests.ts:132-135) and are silently dropped by the `a.weight <= 0` guard at lib/cp-economics.ts:170. So the surface the model actually computes is 7 rows: join_planet(25), message_new(30), ad_impression(25), ad_click(75), botlist_vote(50), connect_account(50), bot_added(50) = 305 CP/day. The comment at lib/cp-economics.ts:151-152 asserts 'An action absent from this map needs a game, a result, or another person's money, and is not on the free surface.' That is false for every one of: profile_views_25 (25x3=75, minted by anonymous HTTP GETs — app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:96-104), follower_gained (25x2=50, minted by another account clicking Follow — app/actions/social.ts:83), profile_vote_received (25x2=50 — lib/identity.ts:78), gift_sent+gift_received (25x2 each = 100, minted between two colluding accounts — lib/marketplace.ts:274-275), redeem_trophy (25x2=50 — app/actions/trophies.ts:163), join_challenge+finish_challenge (25x2 each = 100 — lib/challenges.ts:247, lib/sync.ts:203). Omitted farmable/collusion surface = 775 CP/day, 2.5x the surface the model does describe. Because 305 + 775 = 1,080 > 500, the real per-account-day figure is the ceiling (500), not the 305 the model computes at lib/cp-economics.ts:204-206.
  - *Consequence:* cpPerAccountDay is understated by 64% (305 vs 500). Combined with the human-seconds error below, the headline 'about six hours per dollar' in the file's own docstring (lib/cp-economics.ts:196-197) and asserted in tests/db/cp-economics.mts:255 is the number an investor and an admin will quote, and it is wrong in the direction that flatters. At the true 500 CP/day and near-zero human time, an account yields $0.05/day at essentially zero marginal cost.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cp-economics.ts:153, lib/cp-economics.ts:164, lib/cp-economics.ts:151, lib/cp-economics.ts:170`
- **[FATAL]** Sybil cost per account is $0.00 — no email verification, no phone, no captcha, no payment method, no device fingerprint, and the IP limit in signupVelocity is dead code that is never passed an IP.
  - *Mechanism:* register() (app/actions/auth.ts:11-54) validates an email with a regex (line 16), checks password length, checks uniqueness, then inserts the user (line 48) and creates a session (line 52). No verification email is ever sent; no `isVerified` gate exists on earning or on requesting a payout. The only friction is signupVelocity (lib/abuse.ts:312-358). Its docstring at lib/abuse.ts:301-306 claims it 'Counts prior signups in the window from the same source — an IP, or an email domain'. `input.ip` is declared in the parameter type at lib/abuse.ts:314 and is NEVER REFERENCED in the function body — the only count executed is the disposable-domain count at lines 328-333, gated on `disposable && domain`. The refusal condition at line 344 is `disposable && seen >= SIGNUP_LIMIT`. DISPOSABLE_DOMAINS is 8 hardcoded strings (lib/abuse.ts:280-283). The caller never passes an IP at all: app/actions/auth.ts:39 is `signupVelocity(db, { email })`, and tests/db/abuse.mts:210 asserts exactly that literal string as correct. The function also fails OPEN on any DB error (lib/abuse.ts:357).
  - *Consequence:* Marginal cost of a fake account is $0 (a gmail `+alias`, or a $1/yr catch-all domain amortised over unlimited addresses, or any of the thousands of disposable domains not in the 8-item list). Revenue per fake account is $0.05/day at the ceiling. Payback period is DAY ZERO — there is no cost to pay back. A 1,000-account farm yields $50/day / $18,250/yr; 10,000 accounts yields $500/day / $182,500/yr. Account creation is a scripted POST with no captcha and no rate limit, so 1,000 accounts is minutes of machine time, not 'work'. The file's own framing at lib/abuse.ts:20-21 — 'Creation-velocity friction... enough that fifty accounts is work' — is not implemented for any address outside those 8 domains.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Script `POST` to the register server action with `user+<n>@gmail.com` (uniqueness is on the exact string, so `+` aliases are unlimited distinct accounts) or any address at a domain not in lib/abuse.ts:280-283. 2. No verification step follows — createSession fires immediately (app/actions/auth.ts:52). 3. Each account immediately earns. 4. At redemption, type any birth date >= 18 years ago and any non-blocked country (lib/eligibility.ts:87-110); nothing checks either.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/auth.ts:11, app/actions/auth.ts:16, app/actions/auth.ts:39, app/actions/auth.ts:48`
- **[FATAL]** Every action the abuse model prices is reachable by a scripted HTTP request; a colluding ring reaches the full 500 CP/day per account at zero human seconds and generates zero ad impressions.
  - *Mechanism:* Enumerating every award reachable without a game or a human: (1) ad_impression 1x25=25 and ad_click 25x3=75 — POST /api/ads/beacon, no session-ownership check on the creative, no proof an ad was ever rendered, no viewability check (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:18-59). (2) profile_views_25 25x3=75 — awarded to the PROFILE OWNER on an anonymous HTTP GET of /u/<slug>; app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:96-104 increments profileViews for any non-owner viewer including logged-out ones, with no per-viewer dedup, no IP throttle and no bot filter, and awards on every 25-crossing. 75 GETs from curl = 75 CP. (3) message_new 10x3=30 — a server action, dedup key is the target user id (app/actions/social.ts:213), so 3 new ring members/day. (4) join_planet 25x1=25 (app/actions/social.ts:107). (5) follower_gained 25x2=50 — paid to the FOLLOWED account, ref = follower id (app/actions/social.ts:83), so a ring supplies unlimited distinct followers. (6) profile_vote_received 25x2=50, ref = voter id (lib/identity.ts:78). (7) gift_sent + gift_received 25x2 each = 100 (lib/marketplace.ts:274-275) — value-neutral inside the ring since the trophy carries its own cash value across, so the 50 CP/day/side is free. (8) redeem_trophy 25x2=50 (app/actions/trophies.ts:163). Sum = 480 CP/day, plus connect_account 50 once, so the 500 ceiling binds. The per-action cap of 2-3 is not a defence at ring scale precisely because it is per-account-per-day while the ring supplies unbounded distinct counterparties: N accounts each hit their own cap every day.
  - *Consequence:* Pure-collusion CP with zero real activity and zero screens opened: follower_gained 50 + profile_vote_received 50 + gift_sent 50 + gift_received 50 + profile_views_25 75 + message_new 30 = 305 CP/day/account = $0.0305/day. Adding the scripted ad beacon reaches the 500 ceiling = $0.05/day/account. At N=1,000 accounts: $50/day, $18,250/yr, against $0 of ad revenue because the ring opens no screens. At N=10,000: $182,500/yr. The model reports this same population as costing 6 hours of human labour per dollar.
  - *Exploit:* Ring of N scripted accounts: (a) each GETs every other member's /u/<slug> 75 times/day → 75 CP each; (b) each follows 2 fresh members → the followed accounts take 50 CP each; (c) each votes on 2 fresh profiles → 50 CP each; (d) pairs gift a floor-priced trophy both ways → 50 CP each side, trophy value conserved; (e) each starts 3 new DM threads → 30 CP; (f) each drives the ad beacon 25 impressions + 3 clicks → 100 CP. Ceiling binds at 500 CP = $0.05/account/day.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:18, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:96, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:103`
- **[SEVERE]** stat_levelup, play_session, challenge_progress and share_card have NO emitter anywhere in the codebase — 350 CP/day of the catalog is unearnable, and all four are load-bearing in all four mission templates.
  - *Mechanism:* ACTION_CATALOG prices challenge_progress 25x4=100 (lib/quests.ts:72), share_card 25x3=75 (lib/quests.ts:90), stat_levelup 25x4=100 (lib/quests.ts:103), play_session 25x3=75 (lib/quests.ts:107). A repo-wide grep for each key returns only lib/quests.ts, lib/missions.ts and tests — there is no awardQuestAction call for any of them. lib/sync.ts writes statSnapshots rows when a metric moves (lib/sync.ts:87-89) and awards finish_challenge/win_challenge/top3_challenge (lib/sync.ts:203-205), but never emits stat_levelup or play_session. This also answers the stat-verification question: stats come from provider adapters via adapter.fetchStats (lib/sync.ts:35), i.e. they are pulled, not self-reported — but no quest award is wired to them at all, so there is nothing to spoof and nothing to earn.
  - *Consequence:* 350 CP of the 1,330 CP table (26%) cannot be earned by anyone. Every mission template requires at least two of these actions (lib/missions.ts:56-59, 71-75, 86-89, 101-105), so no gamer can ever complete a Daily Mission, and the streak mechanic — which requires the FULL 500 ceiling to be earned in a day (lib/missions.ts:346-348) — can never fire. The COMMERCIAL_MODEL claim that 500 CP/day is 'reachable' is false on shipped code. The abuse model is correspondingly wrong in the other direction here: it prices a surface that does not exist.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:72, lib/quests.ts:90, lib/quests.ts:103, lib/quests.ts:107`
- **[SEVERE]** lib/missions.ts is imported by nothing outside its own test — the entire Daily Mission and streak feature, on which the doc's 15-screens guarantee depends, has no consumer in app/, lib/ or components/.
  - *Mechanism:* grep for `from "@/lib/missions"` and `lib/missions` across app/, lib/ and components/ returns zero hits. grep for the module's exported functions — missionProgress, streakFrom, milestonesHit, nextMilestone — returns only their own definitions in lib/missions.ts. The 'missions' referenced in components/QuestGame.tsx:212-219 are a different, unrelated concept (quest.missionsConfig / StarterMissions from lib/quest-game, per lib/db/schema.ts:807 area). MISSION_TEMPLATES, streakFrom (lib/missions.ts:376) and milestonesHit (lib/missions.ts:396) are dead code.
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:414 records '15 screens per gamer per day — Guaranteed by §2' and line 430 restates the arithmetic. The mechanism that would produce those screens is not rendered to any gamer and awards nothing. It is a specification file with a test suite, not a shipped feature. Any revenue projection that assumes 15 brand impressions per gamer per day is built on it.
  - *Cites:* `lib/missions.ts:29, lib/missions.ts:50, lib/missions.ts:376, lib/missions.ts:396`
- **[SEVERE]** The doc's 'passive cap of 125 CP/day' is unimplemented AND unimplementable on the shipped table: the doc's own passive list sums to 400 CP/day, which permits 500 CP at 4 screens — a $12.50 break-even CPM the doc itself calls 'no business'.
  - *Mechanism:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:237 states passive actions are 'capped at 125 CP/day, enforced in awardQuestAction'. awardQuestAction (lib/quests.ts:479-534) has no passive concept: no flag lookup, no second accumulator, no branch on action class. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:139 states 'Every action in ACTION_CATALOG carries this flag' — ACTION_CATALOG entries have exactly four fields (key, label, group, defaultWeight, defaultCap) at lib/quests.ts:46. Worse, taking the doc's own passive column at docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:131-137 and pricing it from the shipped table: follower_gained 50 + profile_vote_received 50 + profile_views_25 75 + gift_received 50 + stat_levelup 100 + play_session 75 = 400 CP/day of passive supply against a claimed 125 cap. Even discounting stat_levelup and play_session (no emitter — see separate finding), real passive supply is 225 CP/day, still 1.8x the claimed cap.
  - *Consequence:* The doc's Part 3 derivation ('at least 375 CP must come from ACTIVE actions ⟹ >=15 screens') fails arithmetically on the shipped table. At 400 passive CP the gamer needs 100 active CP = 4 screens; break-even CPM = $0.05 / (4/1000) = $12.50. At the realistic 225 passive, 275 active CP = 11 screens, break-even CPM = $4.55. The doc itself declares at line 126 that 5 screens ($10 CPM) is 'not a thin business, it is *no* business'. Both shipped outcomes are on the wrong side of that line. For a colluding ring the passive actions are the ONLY ones farmed, so screens = 0 and break-even CPM is infinite.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:111, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:131, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:139, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:237`
- **[SEVERE]** DEFAULT_ASSUMPTIONS.capReach = 0.30 contradicts the product's own design target of 100% and understates expectedDailyCost by 3.33x; the streak's milestone trophies are real cash outside the ceiling and are in no cost model at all.
  - *Mechanism:* expectedDailyCost multiplies the ceiling by dailyActive x capReach (lib/cp-economics.ts:96-100) with capReach fixed at 0.30 (lib/cp-economics.ts:51) — i.e. the model asserts an active gamer reaches 30% of the 500 ceiling. lib/missions.ts:44-48 states every mission is engineered to total exactly 500 ('every quest block is 125 and every mission is 500'), and lib/missions.ts:346-348 defines the streak as 'A day counts when the FULL ceiling was earned' — a mechanic whose whole purpose is to drive capReach to 1.0. The two numbers describe opposite products. Separately, Milestone carries a trophyId (lib/missions.ts:355) and trophies are redeemable for cash at trophy.value (app/actions/trophies.ts:143-149) — so a streak pays real dollars ON TOP of the 500 CP ceiling, and nothing in lib/cp-economics.ts models it. The ceiling is described throughout as 'the guarantee'; the streak is a documented, designed leak past it.
  - *Consequence:* At 1,000,000 gamers: shipped forecast = 50,000 x 0.35 x 0.30 = $5,250/day. At the design target (capReach 1.0, same DAU) = $17,500/day. Understatement = $12,250/day = $4.47M/year. At 100,000 gamers the gap is $1,225/day = $447k/year. Neither figure includes milestone trophy cash, which is unbounded by the model. tests/db/cp-economics.mts:209-210 asserts the forecast is exactly dailyActive x capReach of the worst case — it tests the arithmetic, never the plausibility of the input.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cp-economics.ts:51, lib/cp-economics.ts:96, lib/cp-economics.ts:99, lib/missions.ts:44`
- **[SEVERE]** There is no automated abuse detection on the gamer side at all. The only detector is guild-scoped, needs 50+ linked members to even look, and is explicitly not enforcement.
  - *Mechanism:* grep for ban/suspend/fraud/abuse/velocity/anomaly across app/ and lib/ yields exactly three relevant surfaces: lib/abuse.ts, app/admin/growth-review/page.tsx, and app/actions/admin.ts:165 (setUserStatus — a manual admin button). anomalousGrowth (lib/abuse.ts:220-253) only reads discordGuildMembers, skips any guild with raw < 50 (line 240), and flags on two guild-level heuristics (lines 243, 246). There is no query anywhere over questEvents for per-user CP velocity, no cross-account graph analysis over follows / profileVotes / marketplaceOrders, no impression-per-CP ratio check, no duplicate-IP or duplicate-device clustering. The growth-review page states its own scope at app/admin/growth-review/page.tsx:14: 'This page blocks nothing.'
  - *Consequence:* A 1,000-account gamer-side farm registers on NO dashboard. Nobody would notice it from the product; it would surface only as an unexplained CP/redemption line in finance, or when a human triages the redemption queue account by account. Time to detection is therefore bounded by how often somebody reconciles by hand — days to months, not minutes. The one genuine backstop is that trophy redemptions land as status 'pending' (app/actions/trophies.ts:152) and require manual staff action, which converts the fraud from a cash loss into an unbounded manual-review workload that scales linearly with the farm.
  - *Cites:* `lib/abuse.ts:220, lib/abuse.ts:240, lib/abuse.ts:243, lib/abuse.ts:246`
- **[MATERIAL]** minutesPerDollar's occurrence calculation is an algebraic no-op: it round-trips weight through cpPerMinute and back, so `occurrences` provably equals the cap. The function is arithmetically correct and analytically vacuous.
  - *Mechanism:* At lib/cp-economics.ts:175, cpPerMinute := (weight / secondsEach) * 60. Substituting into line 210: r.cpPerMinute * r.secondsEach / 60 = ((weight/secondsEach)*60) * secondsEach / 60 = weight, exactly. So occurrences = cpPerDay / max(1, weight) = (weight * cap) / max(1, weight) = cap for every integer weight >= 1. Weights are forced to integers by Math.round at app/actions/quests-admin.ts:22, and rows with weight <= 0 are filtered at line 170, so max(1, weight) never binds on shipped data. The expression is therefore identical to `sum(cap * secondsEach)` written the long way. It does NOT, as the docstring at lib/cp-economics.ts:186-197 implies, derive anything the caps did not already state. Two genuine defects survive in it: (a) for an uncapped action (cap <= 0) line 171 hardcodes occurrences = 240 and line 210 recovers 240, so an uncapped action is modelled as exactly 240 occurrences/day with no justification anywhere; (b) if rawCp exceeds the ceiling, line 206 clamps CP to the ceiling while line 209 still charges the FULL time for every action — the two halves of the ratio are then measured on different scenarios. With the shipped table rawCp = 305 < 500 so (b) is latent, but any weight increase trips it.
  - *Consequence:* The number this produces — 356 minutes/dollar, i.e. $0.168/hour — is presented as the platform's abuse-resistance metric and asserted as a gate in tests/db/cp-economics.mts:255 ('a determined faker needs hours per dollar, not minutes'). It measures human keystroke time for 7 hand-picked actions. Every action in that set is reachable by an HTTP request a script controls, so the true human-seconds figure is ~0 and the true rate is unbounded. The test that 'protects' this asserts only `mpd > 60`, which the vacuous arithmetic will always satisfy.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cp-economics.ts:199, lib/cp-economics.ts:209, lib/cp-economics.ts:210, lib/cp-economics.ts:175`
- **[MATERIAL]** A per-action daily cap is stored PER QUEST and accumulates across listening quests: once quest A exhausts its cap the loop falls through and quest B pays, so the true daily cap for an action is the SUM of caps across quests — while the model asserts it is not.
  - *Mechanism:* awardQuestAction iterates the listening quests in sorted order (lib/quests.ts:484-487). `paid` is initialised false per CALL (lib/quests.ts:493). When quest A has already hit its own stored cap, line 505 executes `continue` BEFORE `paid` is set at line 517. Control reaches quest B, whose cap is a separate counter, so B satisfies `cp = paid ? 0 : Math.min(weight, room)` at line 516 with paid still false — and pays. Effective per-action daily CP = sum over listening quests of (their weight x their cap), bounded only by the 500 ceiling. lib/cp-economics.ts:65-72 explicitly asserts the opposite in a load-bearing comment: 'NOT multiplied by a.quests.length... If this line ever grows a `* quests.length` again, the multiplier is back.' The admin worst-case model compounds it: app/admin/cp-calculator/page.tsx:48 takes `Math.max(row.cap, c)` across quests where the engine effectively sums them. tests/db/cp-economics.mts:105 tests only a single call with two listeners (first call, cap not yet exhausted) and therefore never reaches the fall-through.
  - *Consequence:* Any admin who points a second quest at an existing action silently doubles that action's daily CP cap while the CP calculator screen shows the max, not the sum. On the shipped seed each action belongs to one quest so this is latent, but the admin UI (app/actions/quests-admin.ts:20-26) exposes every action on every quest with no warning, and the calculator that exists specifically to price such a change models it wrongly.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:484, lib/quests.ts:500, lib/quests.ts:501, lib/quests.ts:505`
- **[MATERIAL]** A daily cap of 0 means UNCAPPED in the engine, and both admin write paths can set it — the CP calculator writes 0 explicitly, the quest editor drops the key entirely on a blank field.
  - *Mechanism:* lib/quests.ts:500 reads `const cap = Number((quest.dailyCaps)[actionKey] ?? 0)` and line 501 guards the count query with `if (cap > 0)`. A stored cap of 0, or an absent key, therefore skips the per-action limit entirely — the action is bounded only by the 500 ceiling. app/actions/cp-calculator.ts:79 accepts and writes any `c >= 0`, so typing 0 in the box labelled as that action's daily cap sets it to unlimited. app/actions/quests-admin.ts:23-24 only writes the cap when `c > 0`, so submitting the quest form with a blank or zero cap field DELETES the key from dailyCaps, producing the same unlimited state.
  - *Consequence:* On the one screen where a wrong number costs real money, '0' means the opposite of what it reads. A single mistyped field turns one action into a 500 CP/day single-action pipe — which is exactly the liability exposure() at lib/cp-economics.ts:119-126 exists to name, and it would be named only on the calculator page, after the fact. Note this write path requires only `requireStaff()` (app/actions/quests-admin.ts, saveQuest), not admin.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:500, lib/quests.ts:501, app/actions/cp-calculator.ts:79, app/actions/quests-admin.ts:23`
- **[MATERIAL]** The ad beacon accepts click and duration events with no session, no ownership check and no dedup — a script can inflate any advertiser's click count and any impression's viewed-duration arbitrarily.
  - *Mechanism:* The duration branch (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:41-46) updates adImpressions.durationViewedMs for ANY impression id supplied in the body, with no check that the caller owns that impression or is even signed in — only a 3,600,000ms clamp at line 43. The click branch (lines 48-52) inserts an adClicks row from `payload.ccId` with no requirement that an impression ever occurred, no session, no IP dedup and no rate limit. serveAds (lib/ads.ts:33-104) implements none of delivery counting, pacing, frequency capping or a billable cap — it reads campaigns and returns creatives, ignoring adCampaigns.budget entirely.
  - *Consequence:* Reported CTR, viewability and delivery are all client-asserted numbers. A competitor or a disgruntled gamer can drive a brand's reported click count and dwell time to anything; conversely the platform can be accused of manufacturing them and has no server-side evidence to rebut it. Combined with ad_click paying 25 CP x3 (lib/quests.ts:118), incentivised clicks are the exact 10%-CTR signature docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:231-235 identifies as 'the single most reputation-damaging line in the old table' — and it is still shipped.
  - *Exploit:* POST /api/ads/beacon {"type":"click","ccId":"<id from the public /api/ads/serve>"} in a loop, with no cookie. Each request inserts a billable click row.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:41, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:43, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:50`


#### A7 · Scale & operations (16)

- **[FATAL]** The stat-sync pipeline is hard-capped at 60 accounts/hour by a sequential loop in a single cron function — it saturates at ~30 linked accounts, and there is no queue to fix it.
  - *Mechanism:* `SYNC_BATCH = 60` (app/api/cron/sync/route.ts:8) and the cron fires hourly (vercel.json:6-9), so platform-wide sync capacity is exactly 60 accounts/hour = 1,440 accounts/day. `SYNC_INTERVAL_MIN = 30` (lib/sync.ts:11) means each account demands 48 syncs/day. Break-even is 1,440 / 48 = 30 linked accounts. Beyond that the `nextSyncAt` backlog grows without bound; `syncDueAccounts` orders by `asc(nextSyncAt)` (lib/sync.ts:232) so it drains FIFO and every account's effective freshness degrades linearly with headcount. The batch cannot simply be raised: the loop is strictly sequential — `for (const account of due) { await syncAccount(db, account) }` (lib/sync.ts:235-237) — inside one function with `maxDuration = 300` (route.ts:11), and each `syncAccount` makes 3–4 external Riot HTTP calls (lib/providers/adapters.ts:258, 273-275) plus 5–10 Neon round trips plus `scoreChallengesForAccount`. At ~2–4 s/account, one invocation physically tops out near 75–150 accounts. There is no worker pool, no queue, no fan-out: docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:4 states 'No separate backend, no queue, no websocket server.'
  - *Consequence:* At 1,000 daily gamers with one linked account each, demand is 48,000 syncs/day against 1,440 of capacity — 3% coverage; every account syncs once per ~16.7 hours instead of every 30 minutes. At 100,000 accounts, once per ~69 days. At 1,000,000 accounts, once per ~1.9 years. Challenge standings, `stat_levelup`, `play_session` and `challenge_progress` CP all derive from this pipeline (lib/sync.ts:8, 203-205), so the competition product — 'the standings ARE the product' per docs/DEPLOY.md — and 100 CP/day of the 500 CP daily mission are structurally undeliverable above ~30 users. Fixing this is not a tuning change; it is a queue, a worker fleet and a Riot production-key negotiation the company does not have.
  - *Exploit:* No exploit needed — it is a throughput ceiling. To observe: link 40 accounts, watch `linked_game_accounts.next_sync_at` fall further behind `now()` on every hourly run.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/cron/sync/route.ts:8, app/api/cron/sync/route.ts:11, app/api/cron/sync/route.ts:36, lib/sync.ts:11`
- **[FATAL]** Every serverless cold start replays 219 DDL statements — including 108 ALTER TABLEs that take ACCESS EXCLUSIVE locks and 11 full-table UPDATEs — against the live production database.
  - *Mechanism:* `getDb()` memoises on `globalThis.__clusterDb` (lib/db/index.ts:955-964), which is per-instance, so every cold Lambda runs `ensureProvisioned` (lib/db/index.ts:863). On an existing database that path unconditionally calls `runColumnMigrations(db)` (lib/db/index.ts:870) which iterates the whole `COLUMN_MIGRATIONS` array (lib/db/index.ts:26-842) — I counted 219 statements: 108 `ALTER TABLE`, 42 `CREATE TABLE`, 49 index DDL, 11 `UPDATE`, 2 `DROP INDEX`. Each is a separate `db.execute` over `neon-http`, i.e. a separate HTTPS round trip (lib/db/perf.ts:5 confirms 'one HTTPS round trip per query'). Postgres acquires AccessExclusiveLock on the relation before evaluating `IF NOT EXISTS`, so a no-op `ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS …` still serialises against every concurrent reader of `users`. `DROP INDEX IF EXISTS "pv_web_idx"` (lib/db/index.ts:465-466) runs every boot on `profile_votes`. Worse, several statements are unbounded writes with no supporting index: `UPDATE "challenges" SET "series_id" = "id" WHERE "series_id" IS NULL` (lib/db/index.ts:332), the correlated self-join `UPDATE "linked_game_accounts" a … WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM "linked_game_accounts" b …)` (lib/db/index.ts:647-654), and `UPDATE "users" SET "payout_method" = "payout_method" - 'details' WHERE "payout_method" IS NOT NULL AND "payout_method" ? 'details'` (lib/db/index.ts:838-841) — a sequential scan of the entire users table, per cold start. The `catch` in `runColumnMigrations` only swallows /already exists\|does not exist/ (lib/db/index.ts:848); a lock timeout or deadlock rethrows, `getDb()` clears the cached promise (lib/db/index.ts:959), and the next request restarts all 219 statements — a retry storm.
  - *Consequence:* Even at 5 ms RTT, 219 sequential round trips is ~1.1 s of added latency on every cold start before the first real query; at a realistic 15–20 ms it is 3–4.4 s, which alone exceeds Discord's 3-second interaction budget on a cold instance. At 100x traffic Vercel runs hundreds of concurrent instances; every one takes AccessExclusiveLock on `users`, `challenges`, `profile_votes` and `linked_game_accounts` in turn while live requests are reading them. The observable symptom is a site-wide stall on every deploy and every traffic spike, with lock-wait pileups, followed by cascading getDb() failures and full migration retries. At 1M users the single `UPDATE users … WHERE payout_method ? 'details'` seq-scans a million-row table on each of those instances.
  - *Exploit:* Trigger a deploy (invalidates all warm instances) during peak traffic. Every incoming request cold-starts and every cold start issues 108 ALTER TABLEs against the same hot tables. No attacker required.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:26, lib/db/index.ts:844, lib/db/index.ts:863, lib/db/index.ts:870`
- **[FATAL]** One awardQuestAction call is 12 sequential HTTPS round trips to Neon; at the plan's own 1M-gamer / 20-actions-per-day target that is 240 million queries a day.
  - *Mechanism:* I counted the round trips in one `awardQuestAction` for a single listening quest: (1) `SELECT *` from quests (lib/quests.ts:483 — full row including the jsonb missionsConfig/gameUi/pathPoints payloads); (2) `dailyCpCeiling` settings read (lib/quests.ts:438); (3) `cpEarnedToday` SUM (lib/quests.ts:447); (4) per-quest cap `count(*)` (lib/quests.ts:502); (5) dedup SELECT (lib/quests.ts:511); (6) INSERT quest_events (lib/quests.ts:519); (7) UPSERT user_quest_progress (lib/quests.ts:524); (8) unlockTiers progress SELECT (lib/quests.ts:564); (9) unlockTiers questTiers SELECT (lib/quests.ts:567); (10) unlockTiers held-tiers SELECT (lib/quests.ts:572); (11) maybeCompleteQuest progress SELECT (lib/quests.ts:540); (12) maybeCompleteQuest top-tier SELECT (lib/quests.ts:542). Steps 4–12 are strictly `await`ed in sequence. The driver is `neon-http`, and lib/db/perf.ts:5 states the project's own understanding: 'one HTTPS round trip per query, so the count is the latency.' Two listening quests make it 21. The web ad-impression path adds an `INSERT INTO ad_impressions` and a session read on top (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23-36), so 13 per impression.
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:15 and :204 target 1,000,000 gamers doing 20 actions/day. That is 20M awards/day × 12 = 240,000,000 queries/day for CP alone = ~2,780 QPS sustained, and gaming traffic is peaked 3–5x, so 8,000–14,000 QPS of individual HTTPS-wrapped statements. Add 15M impression inserts/day and every page render. This is not a Neon tier problem — the serverless HTTP driver has no pipelining and no connection reuse across statements, so the platform is buying 240M TLS-terminated request/response cycles a day. Per-gamer, 20 actions × 12 round trips = 240 round trips/day/gamer. At even 10ms RTT, each ad impression carries ~120ms of DB latency on the critical path of the beacon.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:479, lib/quests.ts:483, lib/quests.ts:490, lib/quests.ts:447`
- **[FATAL]** The 500 CP/day ceiling — the single number the commercial model calls 'the guarantee' — is a non-transactional check-then-write and is not enforced under concurrency.
  - *Mechanism:* `cpEarnedToday` is read once, before the loop (lib/quests.ts:490), `room` is a local variable, and the per-quest cap is a `count(*)` compared against `cap` (lib/quests.ts:502-505) followed later by an `INSERT` (lib/quests.ts:519). There is no transaction, no row lock, and no `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`; the `neon-http` driver does not support interactive transactions. N concurrent requests all read the same `already`/`count` and all pass the check. The unique index `qe_dedup_idx` on (user_id, quest_id, action_key, ref_type, ref_id) (lib/db/schema.ts:862-863) is the only DB-level guard, and it does not fire here because the ad-impression path mints a fresh `uid()` per impression and passes it as `refId` (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:22, 36) — every row is unique by construction. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:37-39 says the ceiling 'is the one number that must never be changed casually' and :429 says '500 CP a day. Fixed.'
  - *Consequence:* Cost per gamer is unbounded above the modelled $0.05/day exactly when volume is highest. At the plan's 1M-gamer scale the modelled liability is $50,000/day / $1.5M/month (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:35 scaled); a 2x concurrency overshoot is $100,000/day. It is also trivially farmable: a client firing 50 parallel POSTs to /api/ads/beacon lands 50 awards against a cap of 25 and a ceiling read once. The scale dimension makes this worse, not better — the race window is proportional to request concurrency.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Sign in. 2. `for i in {1..50}; do curl -X POST /api/ads/beacon -H 'content-type: application/json' -b "cluster_session=…" -d '{"type":"impression","ccId":"<any live cc id>"}' & done`. 3. All 50 read `count(*) = 0` before any INSERT lands; all 50 insert with cp>0. Repeat to exceed 500 CP/day arbitrarily.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:490, lib/quests.ts:502, lib/quests.ts:505, lib/quests.ts:516`
- **[FATAL]** Card rendering is hard-capped at 4,000 renders/day and 3,000 stored cards globally — the ad inventory the whole model depends on stops at roughly 270 daily gamers.
  - *Mechanism:* `DAILY_RENDER_CEILING = 4000` (lib/cards/budget.ts:23) and `CARD_STORE_CAP = 3000` (lib/cards/budget.ts:25) are module constants with no environment override and no scaling term. `getOrRenderCard` checks the budget after the cache lookup and, when exhausted, returns the previously stored URL for that key or `null` (lib/cards/cache.ts:62-72). Beyond 3,000 distinct cards the LRU eviction (lib/cards/budget.ts:92-129) means most requests are cache misses, each miss consumes render budget, the budget exhausts, and `null` sends the caller to the live `/api/card/<kind>` route which re-renders a 1200×630 PNG through satori on every single request with no cache at all.
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:120 states the whole model rests on '≥15 screens opened ⟹ ≥15 brand impressions'. If Discord cards carry those screens, 1,000 gamers need 15,000 renders/day against a ceiling of 4,000 — the ceiling binds at ~267 daily gamers. At the ladder's 1,000,000 gamers (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:204) the requirement is 15,000,000 renders/day against 4,000: a 3,750x shortfall. Worse for the buyer: when the budget trips the system serves the last good card (lib/cards/cache.ts:66), which has a *different brand's* creative baked into it, and `logCardAdImpression` still writes an impression row — brands are billed for delivery of a competitor's or a stale creative.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cards/budget.ts:23, lib/cards/budget.ts:25, lib/cards/budget.ts:27, lib/cards/cache.ts:62`
- **[FATAL]** Ad delivery has no views target, no delivered counter, no pacing and no frequency cap in code — the company cannot deliver, or bill for, the number of views it sells.
  - *Mechanism:* `adCampaigns` has `budget` (a dollar figure) and dates and nothing else (lib/db/schema.ts:504-522) — no views target, no delivered counter. I grepped the entire codebase for viewsTarget/views_target/delivered/pacing/frequencyCap: zero hits in the ad path. `serveAds` sorts by static priority then weight and slices to `maxCreativesInRotation` (lib/ads.ts:73-76), whose default is 3 (lib/db/schema.ts:474) — so the 4th and subsequent paying brands in a placement are silently invisible. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:248-252 admits this ('We cannot currently deliver a promised number of views to anybody') and lists 7 required changes; none of the 7 exist in code. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:432 nonetheless states the operating rule 'Brands we can take = monthly views ÷ 100,000. Computed, never guessed' — I grepped for 'headroom' and 'inventory' computation: no such computation exists anywhere in lib/ or app/.
  - *Consequence:* The revenue side of the model is $500 for 100,000 views (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:49). With no delivered counter there is no way to know whether a brand received 100,000 or 4,000, no way to stop at target (over-delivery is free inventory given away), and no way to prove delivery in a dispute. With `maxCreativesInRotation` defaulting to 3, selling a 4th brand into a placement takes money for zero delivery. At the ladder's ~100 brands (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:204) against ~$2.25M/month of claimed inventory value, this is an unbillable revenue line, not a scaling problem — and it gets worse with brand count, not better.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:504, lib/ads.ts:73, lib/ads.ts:76, lib/db/schema.ts:474`
- **[SEVERE]** Two boot-maintenance passes are explicitly NOT version-gated and run 17+ unindexed sequential scans — including a jsonb-cast regex over the entire users table — on every cold start.
  - *Mechanism:* `runBootMaintenance` is correctly gated by a single settings read (lib/db/seed.ts:1015-1021). `migrateGameImagesToBlob` and `rehostImagesToBlob` are not — the comment at lib/db/index.ts:875-880 says so explicitly: 'Runs EVERY boot (not version-gated)'. `migrateGameImagesToBlob` loops `IMAGE_COLUMNS` (lib/db/seed.ts:924-942, 17 entries) issuing `SELECT id, <col> FROM <tbl> WHERE <col> LIKE 'data:image/%' LIMIT 200` (lib/db/seed.ts:962) — a leading-wildcard-free LIKE on an unindexed text column, i.e. a sequential scan per column, twice over `users` (avatar_url, banner_url). `rehostImagesToBlob` then runs `db.select(...).from(schema.users).where(sql\`theme::text LIKE '%data:image%' OR theme::text ILIKE '%cloudfront.net%' OR … OR avatar_url ~* 'cloudfront\\.net\|higgsfield' …\`)` (lib/db/seed.ts:760-761) — a full scan of `users` that casts the `theme` jsonb to text and runs case-insensitive regexes on every row — followed by unbounded `SELECT *` of `games` and `quests` (lib/db/seed.ts:775, 786). The claim at lib/db/index.ts:868-869 that 'steady-state boots do no table scans' is false for these three calls.
  - *Consequence:* At 1M users the `users` table is roughly 1M rows with a jsonb theme column; casting it to text and regexing it takes seconds of CPU on the database, and it happens once per cold Lambda. During a deploy or spike with hundreds of concurrent cold starts, this is hundreds of concurrent full scans of the largest table, saturating the Neon compute's I/O and CPU. The failures are wrapped in a bare `catch {}` (lib/db/index.ts:886), so the symptom is not an error — it is unexplained database saturation with no log line.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:872, lib/db/index.ts:877, lib/db/index.ts:880, lib/db/seed.ts:1015`
- **[SEVERE]** serveAds runs 2–3 uncached queries on every Discord card render; the only ad cache is a per-Lambda-instance Map that degrades to nothing exactly when traffic is highest.
  - *Mechanism:* `serveAds` issues a placement SELECT (lib/ads.ts:34-35), a four-way join over adCampaignCreatives × adCampaigns × adCreatives × brands with no LIMIT (lib/ads.ts:45-71), and a second SELECT for fileUrls (lib/ads.ts:82-84). The web path wraps this in a 60 s in-memory `Map` (app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:13-14) — but that Map lives in one Lambda instance's heap, so with N concurrent instances the hit rate is (N-1)/N misses on the first request to each. The Discord and OG paths have **no cache at all**: `pickCardAd` calls `serveAds(db, CARD_AD_PLACEMENT, 'both')` directly on every invocation (lib/cards/ads.ts:104), and `/api/card/[kind]` calls `withCardAd` **before** consulting the render cache (app/api/card/[kind]/route.tsx:123 vs the cache lookup at :153). The join filters by `eq(adCampaigns.status,'active')` and date window with no index on those columns and no LIMIT — it materialises every live creative in the network on every call. There is no Redis, no `unstable_cache`, no ISR: I found zero uses of `unstable_cache` and 155 files declaring `export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic'`.
  - *Consequence:* Every OpenGraph fetch — Discord re-fetches embed images aggressively — pays 3 uncached joins. At 100x traffic the per-instance Map is nearly useless (each cold instance re-populates), so the ad-serve path alone contributes hundreds of QPS of an unindexed 4-table join. With the ~100 brands docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:204 projects, that join returns every live creative row on every call and is then sorted and sliced in JavaScript (lib/ads.ts:73-76).
  - *Cites:* `lib/ads.ts:33, lib/ads.ts:34, lib/ads.ts:45, lib/ads.ts:82`
- **[SEVERE]** ad_clicks has no index at all and ad_impressions has none on created_at; every dashboard, brand report and the public data room runs unindexed sequential scans over the fastest-growing tables.
  - *Mechanism:* `ad_impressions` carries exactly one index, `imp_cc_idx` on (campaign_creative_id, created_at) (lib/db/schema.ts:558); `ad_clicks` declares none at all (lib/db/schema.ts:560-568) and the generated migration confirms only the FK constraint and no CREATE INDEX for it (drizzle/0000_mighty_fenris.sql:440 is the only ad-table index in the file). Every date-only aggregate therefore seq-scans: `count(*) FROM ad_impressions WHERE created_at >= since` and the same for ad_clicks in `getAdsMasterTotals` (lib/brands.ts:218-219), in the public data room's `commercialStats` (lib/dataroom/index.ts:267-268), in admin metrics (lib/admin-metrics.ts:107-111), and — worst — `COUNT(DISTINCT hashed_ip)` over the whole table filtered only by created_at (app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx:40-41).
  - *Consequence:* At the model's 1M gamers × 15 impressions/day the table grows 15M rows/day ≈ 5.5bn rows/year. A `COUNT(DISTINCT hashed_ip)` seq-scan plus hash aggregate over billions of rows will not complete inside any HTTP timeout — the admin ad analytics page and the brand-facing totals simply stop returning. Because lib/dataroom/index.ts:267 is on the investor/partner data room, the page shown to prospective investors is the one that times out first. Even at 100,000 gamers (450M impressions/year) these are multi-second scans on every page load.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:558, lib/db/schema.ts:560, drizzle/0000_mighty_fenris.sql:440, lib/brands.ts:218`
- **[SEVERE]** The brand campaign report loads every impression ROW into the serverless function's heap and aggregates in JavaScript — it OOMs at the delivery volume the pricing model sells.
  - *Mechanism:* `getCampaignAnalytics` does `db.select({ ccId, path, country, at }).from(schema.adImpressions).where(and(inArray(ccIds), gte(createdAt, since)))` with **no aggregation and no limit** (lib/brands.ts:180-182), then the identical thing for clicks (lib/brands.ts:183-184), then loops the arrays in Node building four Maps (lib/brands.ts:191-203). This is the exact report docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:354-369 promises brands ('Reporting — per placement, not one lump').
  - *Consequence:* At the $500/100,000-views package the report pulls 100,000 rows — survivable but wasteful. At the ladder's 1,000,000 gamers, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:204 splits 450M monthly views across ~100 brands = ~4.5M rows per brand per 30-day report. 4.5M JS objects with a string path, a country and a Date is on the order of 500MB–1.5GB of heap. Vercel Node functions default to 1024MB (max 3009MB on Pro). The brand portal report — the deliverable a paying customer opens — crashes with an OOM, and it crashes for the largest customers first.
  - *Cites:* `lib/brands.ts:180, lib/brands.ts:181, lib/brands.ts:183, lib/brands.ts:191`
- **[SEVERE]** CP balance is a full-lifetime SUM over quest_events with no covering index and no materialised balance — it is on the render path of every profile card and every OG image.
  - *Mechanism:* `getTotalCp` is `SELECT COALESCE(SUM(COALESCE(cp_awarded, qp_awarded)),0) FROM quest_events WHERE user_id = $1` (lib/quests.ts:692-693) with no date bound. The only usable index is `qe_user_idx` on (user_id, created_at) (lib/db/schema.ts:862), which does not include `cp_awarded` or `qp_awarded` — so Postgres must heap-fetch every one of the user's rows. `cpWallet` = `getTotalCp` − `cpSpent` (lib/marketplace.ts:139-140); there is no materialised balance column anywhere. It is called in `profileCard` (lib/cards/data.ts:38), which backs both the Discord profile card and the page OpenGraph image, plus the wallet (lib/wallet.ts:70), the feed and the quests page.
  - *Consequence:* At 20 events/gamer/day a two-year-old account has ~14,600 rows; three years, ~22,000. Every profile card render and every OG fetch performs ~22,000 random heap reads to compute one integer. Discord re-fetches embed images on scroll, so a popular shared card multiplies this. There is no rollup table, no daily aggregate, no balance column — the cost per read grows linearly with account age forever.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:684, lib/quests.ts:692, lib/quests.ts:431, lib/db/schema.ts:862`
- **[SEVERE]** The public profile page does a read-modify-write UPDATE of a users row and fans out unbounded provider API calls on every single view.
  - *Mechanism:* Two problems on the same render. (1) `viewCount = user.profileViews ?? 0; viewCount += 1; db.update(users).set({ profileViews: viewCount })` (app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:96-99) — a read-then-write, not `profile_views + 1`. Concurrent viewers all read the same value and the last write wins, so the count silently under-reports; separately it is a row-level write on the `users` table on every page view, serialising every viewer of a popular profile on one row's lock. That number also gates a CP award (app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:103), so lost updates lose real money for the gamer. (2) `await syncUserAccountsIfStale(db, user.id)` runs before anything renders (app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:85); that function does `Promise.allSettled(syncable.map((a) => syncAccount(db, a)))` (lib/sync.ts:256) with no concurrency bound — every stale linked account fires 3–4 Riot API calls plus 5–10 DB writes, on the critical path of a public page render.
  - *Consequence:* A profile that goes viral in a Discord server is a self-inflicted DDoS: N concurrent viewers produce N row-lock-serialised UPDATEs to the same users row, and the first burst (before the 15-minute cooldown is written) produces N × accounts × 4 simultaneous Riot API calls from one page. Riot rate-limits per application key, so one popular profile can exhaust the platform-wide Riot budget and put every other gamer's sync into `rate_limited` backoff (lib/sync.ts:48-57). The page also cannot be cached — it is a write.
  - *Cites:* `app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:85, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:96, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:99, app/u/[slug]/page.tsx:103`
- **[SEVERE]** Redemption, fraud review, brand sales and delivery management are entirely manual four-button admin queues with zero automation, KYC or sanctions screening — at 1M gamers the CP liability is $1.5M/month with no staffing model in the code.
  - *Mechanism:* Redemption is a per-row human workflow: `approveRedeem`, `rejectRedeem`, `sendRedeem`, `markRedeemPaid` (app/admin/redeems/page.tsx:5) across states pending → approved → sent → paid (app/admin/redeems/page.tsx:20, 101-138). lib/payouts.ts:29-33 states the policy plainly: 'Only an admin. An owner sees what they are owed and can ask about it; the release is always ours, always attributed to a named person.' I grepped the whole codebase for kyc\|aml\|sanction\|1099\|tax — the only hits are a comment about sanctioned jurisdictions in lib/eligibility.ts:32 that defers to the payout provider, and an incidental comment at lib/eligibility.ts:133. docs/OPERATIONS.md:24-33 defines the daily ops loop as three human inboxes answered same-day. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:318 defines brand onboarding as 'Sales creates the brand in admin' — a human step per brand.
  - *Consequence:* At 1M gamers × $0.05/day the CP liability is $50,000/day = $1.5M/month (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:35, scaled). Even a 1%/month redemption rate is 10,000 redemption tickets/month; at a conservative 5 minutes of human review each that is ~833 hours ≈ 5 FTE for redemptions alone, before fraud review — and there is no fraud scoring in the redemption path, only a manual approve/reject. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:204 projects ~100 brands at 1M gamers; at a normal enterprise-adjacent cadence of 1–2 closed deals per rep-month for a $7,500/month product, reaching and retaining 100 brands implies 4–8 sellers plus account management, none of it assisted by tooling — there is no CRM, no pipeline, no headroom calculator, and no programmatic fill (grep for programmatic\|openrtb\|prebid\|adsense returned nothing outside data-room marketing copy). I am marking this UNPROVEN rather than BROKEN because it is a staffing assumption, not a code defect — but the code contains no automation that would reduce it, and no plan document in the repo states a headcount.
  - *Cites:* `app/admin/redeems/page.tsx:5, app/admin/redeems/page.tsx:20, app/admin/redeems/page.tsx:101, app/admin/redeems/page.tsx:111`
- **[MATERIAL]** quest_events and ad_impressions have no partitioning, no rollup and no retention policy — 7.3bn and 5.5bn rows/year respectively, with nothing that ever deletes one.
  - *Mechanism:* Both tables are plain (non-partitioned) pgTables (lib/db/schema.ts:843-864, 542-558) and the DDL/migration list creates them as plain tables (lib/db/index.ts:85-102). I grepped the entire codebase for `delete(schema.questEvents)`, `delete(schema.adImpressions)`, and for retention/archive/purge logic — there is none. The only eviction anywhere in the product is for stored card PNGs (lib/cards/budget.ts:92).
  - *Consequence:* At the model's 1M gamers × 20 actions/day: 20M quest_events rows/day = 7.3bn/year. Heap ~150 B/row plus two index entries (qe_user_idx ~40 B and the five-column unique qe_dedup_idx ~90 B) ≈ 300 B all-in → ~6 GB/day, ~2.2 TB/year for one table. ad_impressions at 15M rows/day ≈ 3.75 GB/day, ~1.4 TB/year. That is ~3.6 TB/year of Neon storage before any other table, plus the vacuum and index-bloat burden of a 7-billion-row table with a five-text-column unique index that must be probed on every award (lib/quests.ts:519 relies on it via onConflictDoNothing). Storage cost alone is roughly $1,300–1,500/month by end of year one at typical Neon rates and compounds annually; the operational cost — no ability to drop old partitions, autovacuum on multi-TB tables — is the real problem.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:843, lib/db/schema.ts:862, lib/db/schema.ts:542, lib/db/index.ts:85`
- **[MATERIAL]** Brand creatives fall back to being stored as inline base64 data URLs up to 8 MB in a Postgres text column, and the ad-serve join reads that column.
  - *Mechanism:* `/api/brands/upload` accepts a data URL up to `MAX_DATAURL_BYTES = 8 * 1024 * 1024` (app/api/brands/upload/route.ts:39) and returns `{ url: hosted ?? dataUrl }` (app/api/brands/upload/route.ts:46) — when the Blob upload fails or Blob is not configured, `putBuffer` returns null (lib/blob.ts:66, 74-79) and the **raw 8 MB base64 string** becomes `ad_creatives.file_url`. lib/ads.ts:40-44 documents this explicitly: 'we do NOT fetch adCreatives.fileUrl here (it can be a large inline data URL)'. The second query at lib/ads.ts:82-84 then fetches exactly those fileUrls for the served creatives, so every card render and every ad-slot miss transfers up to 8 MB per creative out of Neon. The only remediation is the un-gated boot scan at lib/db/seed.ts:962, which is itself a per-cold-start sequential scan (see the boot-maintenance finding).
  - *Consequence:* An 8 MB base64 blob in a text column is TOASTed and detoasted on every read. With 100 brands × several creatives each and a placement returning 3, a single ad-serve miss can move 24 MB out of the database. Neon bills data transfer; Vercel bills Fast Origin Transfer. There is no CDN in front of the creative — `fileUrl` is handed straight to the browser `<img src>` (components/AdSlot.tsx:141) or inlined into the PNG. At 100x traffic this is the line item that produces a surprise five-figure monthly bill.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/brands/upload/route.ts:39, app/api/brands/upload/route.ts:46, lib/blob.ts:63, lib/blob.ts:74`
- **[MATERIAL]** Impression writes that make money are fire-and-forget promises inside serverless functions, so a share of billable deliveries is silently dropped under load.
  - *Mechanism:* `logCardAdImpression` is declared `void (async () => { … })()` with its own swallowing catch (lib/cards/ads.ts:200-216) and is called from `cardRef` (lib/discord/cards.ts:121), which itself runs inside Next's `after()` callback in the interactions route (app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:363). Work that is not awaited by the `after()` callback is not guaranteed to complete before the serverless instance is frozen or reclaimed. The same pattern is used for the card cache hit counter (lib/cards/cache.ts:47-50). The code comments acknowledge the trade ('an impression that fails to write is a number slightly low on a dashboard') but the number in question is the one brands are billed on.
  - *Consequence:* Under-counted impressions understate delivery to brands, which — combined with the absence of any delivered counter (see the delivery finding) — means neither party can reconcile what was actually served. The loss rate is unbounded and grows with instance churn, i.e. it is worst exactly at peak traffic and immediately after deploys. This is a revenue-recognition and audit problem, not just a dashboard one.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cards/ads.ts:200, lib/cards/ads.ts:204, lib/cards/ads.ts:214, lib/cards/cache.ts:47`


#### A8 · Data integrity & privacy (13)

- **[FATAL]** The JWT session-signing secret has a hardcoded fallback published in this repo; if AUTH_SECRET is unset, anyone can mint a superadmin session and decrypt every stored provider token.
  - *Mechanism:* lib/auth.ts:7-9 builds the HS256 signing key as `process.env.AUTH_SECRET ?? "cluster-demo-secret-set-AUTH_SECRET-in-production"`. middleware.ts:21-23 repeats the same literal, so the admin pre-filter accepts the same forged token. There is no startup assertion anywhere that AUTH_SECRET is set — the code path silently degrades to the public literal. lib/crypto.ts:5-7 derives the AES-256-GCM key that encrypts stored provider tokens from the SAME value (with an even shorter fallback, "cluster-demo-secret"), so the one thing in the database that is encrypted at rest is encrypted under a key printed in the repository. lib/discord/install-credit.ts:36-38 makes it three call sites. `.env.example:7` ships `AUTH_SECRET=change-me-long-random-string`, so a copy-paste deploy is signed with a second known constant.
  - *Consequence:* A single missing env var converts to full platform compromise: forge `{uid:<any>, role:"superadmin"}`, pass middleware.ts and `isAdmin()` (lib/auth.ts:117), and reach every admin action — user emails, brand contact data, `regenerateBrandKey` (app/actions/admin.ts:703), invoice mutation, payout approval. It also renders the token-at-rest encryption worthless. At the stated model of $500/mo brand packages, control of `markInvoicePaid` and `setInvoiceStatus` (app/actions/billing.ts:309,325) is direct revenue theft; control of `requestRedeem`/approval is direct cash theft. This is a config-dependent, not code-dependent, catastrophe — exactly the kind an investor cannot verify from a repo, and the code offers no guard rail.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Read the literal from lib/auth.ts:8. 2. `SignJWT({uid:'<any existing user id>', role:'superadmin'}).setProtectedHeader({alg:'HS256'}).setExpirationTime('30d').sign(TextEncoder().encode(LITERAL))`. 3. Set cookie `cluster_session=<token>`. 4. Browse /admin — middleware.ts:65 verifies against the same literal and passes; the layout's live DB check reads the row for `uid`, so pick any real admin's id (or any id at all if only the middleware gate is in play for a given route). 5. For the DB-compromise variant: `createHash('sha256').update(LITERAL).digest()` is the AES key for lib/crypto.ts decryptSecret — decrypt every `linked_game_accounts.provider_data.token`.
  - *Cites:* `lib/auth.ts:7-9, middleware.ts:21-23, lib/crypto.ts:5-7, lib/discord/install-credit.ts:36-38`
- **[SEVERE]** The published privacy policy states ad impression rows are purged after 90 days. No purge code exists anywhere in the repository.
  - *Mechanism:* app/legal/privacy/page.tsx:29 tells consumers verbatim: "Raw ad impression rows are aggregated and purged after 90 days." I grepped the entire tree for `delete(schema.adImpressions)`, `retention`, `purge`, `prune` — zero hits outside unrelated marketing copy (lib/dataroom/defaults.ts:498, lib/marketplace.ts:40). lib/jobs.ts:33-64 enumerates every scheduled job (challenges, challenge-reminders, discord-ads, week-update, botlist-stats, leaderboard-feed) — none touches ad_impressions. vercel.json declares three crons, all mapping to those jobs. The table (lib/db/schema.ts:542-558) holds `userId`, `sessionId`, `hashedIp`, `geoCountry`, `geoCity`, `pagePath`, `durationViewedMs` and grows without bound and without deletion.
  - *Consequence:* A false statement of a material privacy practice in a published policy. In the US that is an FTC Act §5 deceptive-practice exposure; in the EU/UK it is GDPR Art. 5(1)(e) storage limitation plus Art. 5(1)(a) fairness/transparency, on a data set that is a per-user browsing history (page path + geo city + user id + timestamp). Fines under GDPR reach 4% of global turnover; the practical diligence consequence is that the acquirer inherits an undisclosed, unbounded behavioural-tracking database plus a written admission that it should not exist. Remediation is trivial (one cron) — which is precisely why the absence signals the privacy commitments are unaudited prose.
  - *Cites:* `app/legal/privacy/page.tsx:29-30, lib/jobs.ts:33-64, lib/db/schema.ts:542-558, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23-33`
- **[SEVERE]** The cookie-consent banner records the user's choice and nothing reads it — 'Essential only' still fires IP-hash + geo + user-id ad tracking on the very next render.
  - *Mechanism:* components/CookieConsent.tsx:13-16 `decide()` writes `localStorage.setItem("cluster-consent", v)` and hides the banner. Grepping `cluster-consent` across all .ts/.tsx yields exactly three hits: the write and read in CookieConsent.tsx (the read is only `if (!localStorage.getItem(...)) setShow(true)` — a has-it-been-dismissed check) and a mention in the cookie policy page. No component, route, or server action ever branches on the value. components/AdSlot.tsx:64-74 attaches an IntersectionObserver and POSTs to /api/ads/beacon unconditionally the moment an ad is 50% visible. app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23-33 then writes hashedIp, geoCountry, geoCity, pagePath and userId.
  - *Consequence:* This is worse than having no banner. Under GDPR Art. 7 / ePrivacy Art. 5(3) and the UK PECR, presenting a granular choice and then ignoring the refusal is the aggravating fact regulators cite when setting penalties — it converts a technical non-compliance into demonstrated bad faith, and the code is the evidence. The company operates in MENA and EU-adjacent markets per its own targeting model. Any DPA complaint from a single user is provable from a browser devtools session in under a minute.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Load any page with an ad slot. 2. Click 'Essential only'. 3. Open Network tab, scroll an ad into view. 4. Observe POST /api/ads/beacon {type:'impression'} firing, and the resulting row carrying hashed_ip, geo_country and geo_city.
  - *Cites:* `components/CookieConsent.tsx:13-16, components/AdSlot.tsx:58-77, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:18-33, app/legal/cookies/page.tsx:11-12`
- **[SEVERE]** Account deletion is a single cascading DELETE that leaves the user's Discord snowflake, geo-located browsing history, email address and payout preference behind in at least nine tables.
  - *Mechanism:* app/actions/connections.ts:254 is the entire erasure implementation: `db.delete(schema.users).where(eq(schema.users.id, me.id))`. It relies wholly on FK cascade. Multiple PII-bearing tables deliberately have NO foreign key to users and therefore survive: ad_impressions.userId is `text("user_id")` with no `.references()` (lib/db/schema.ts:545) alongside hashed_ip/geo_city/page_path; discord_command_logs stores both `userId` and the raw Discord snowflake `discordId` with no FK (lib/db/schema.ts:1080-1081); email_log.toAddress holds the plaintext email address of every message ever sent with no FK and no purge (lib/db/schema.ts:1779); profile_views keeps viewerUserId and viewerDiscordId (lib/db/schema.ts:946-947); profile_votes keeps voterUserId and voterDiscordId (lib/db/schema.ts:883-884); payout_accounts keys on `ownerId` text with no FK and retains methodPreference + country + providerRecipientId (lib/db/schema.ts:1535-1546); server_events.userId (lib/db/schema.ts:1100); discord_post_log.names is a jsonb array of display names (lib/db/schema.ts:973). Critically, tests/db/account-deletion.mts — the file that exists to prove deletion is correct — asserts only the ORDER of three source-code substrings (lines 173-184) and that the impact JSON contains no 'iban' (lines 187-192). It never inserts a row into any of these tables and never asserts a row is gone.
  - *Consequence:* GDPR Art. 17 erasure is not satisfied. A Discord snowflake plus a geo-city plus a page-path timeline is directly re-identifying, and it persists indefinitely (no retention job — see the separate finding). Every erasure request the company has answered 'done' to is a misstatement. For an acquirer this is a per-subject liability across the entire historic user base with no way to remediate retroactively, because the join key (users.id) is gone: the orphaned rows can no longer be located BY SUBJECT after the cascade runs, only by brute-force over ids the company no longer holds. That makes the defect permanently un-remediable for already-deleted accounts.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/connections.ts:254, lib/db/schema.ts:545, lib/db/schema.ts:1077-1087, lib/db/schema.ts:1776-1779`
- **[SEVERE]** The ad impression and click endpoint is completely unauthenticated and unsigned — anyone can inflate any brand's billed impression and click counts with curl.
  - *Mechanism:* app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:12 accepts an arbitrary JSON body. Line 18 checks only that `payload.type === "impression" && payload.ccId` is truthy — no session requirement, no origin check, no signature, no rate limit, and no verification that `ccId` is a live campaign_creative or that the caller ever rendered a page. It inserts straight into ad_impressions. Line 48-52 does the same for clicks. The codebase already contains exactly the tool to prevent this — `signPayload`/`verifyPayload` in lib/portal-auth.ts:56-65, whose own docstring says it exists so that nobody can 'write their OWN url with a competitor's id in it and run up that brand's click count' — and the beacon does not use it. Note the same route awards CP for `ad_impression` and `ad_click` (lines 34-37, 53-57) with a fresh `uid()` refId on impressions, so the dedup unique index at lib/db/schema.ts:863 never fires.
  - *Consequence:* The impression count is the invoiced unit — docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:49 prices a package at '$500/month = 100,000 views'. Every number a brand is billed on, and every number in the end-of-month brand report, is writable by an anonymous internet user in a shell loop. A competitor can burn a rival's package in minutes; a brand can dispute every invoice ever issued by demonstrating this endpoint; and the platform's own 'spare views' capacity alert (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:294) — the trigger for selling the next $500 package — is driven off the same poisoned table. At the stated model this is a direct, unbounded revenue-integrity defect, not a theoretical one.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Load any page with an ad, read `campaignCreativeId` from the /api/ads/serve response (that route is also unauthenticated). 2. `while true; do curl -s -X POST https://clustergg.com/api/ads/beacon -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"type":"impression","ccId":"<id>","path":"/"}'; done`. 3. Each request inserts a billable row. Repeat with `{"type":"click","ccId":"<id>"}`.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:12-59, lib/portal-auth.ts:56-65, app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx:26`
- **[SEVERE]** No age gate at signup anywhere; date of birth is collected only at first cash-out, is entirely self-asserted, and is never verified.
  - *Mechanism:* app/actions/auth.ts:11-53 (email signup) validates email format, password length and display name — nothing else. app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:236-255 (OAuth signup, the primary path) creates the user from the Discord profile with no age question at all. lib/db/schema.ts:24-32 documents the choice explicitly: 'Not collected at signup: playing has no age gate here.'
