The only gate is at redemption — lib/eligibility.ts:27 `MIN_REDEEM_AGE = 18`, enforced in app/actions/trophies.ts:90-105 against `input.birthDate`, which comes from a bare `<input type="date">` (components/RedeemStepper.tsx:284) written straight to users.birthDate with only a `Number.isNaN` check (trophies.ts:91-93). There is no document check, no third-party age-assurance call, no ID upload — grepping for KYC/verification of identity returns nothing on this path.
  - *Consequence:* Three distinct exposures, all unmitigated in code. (1) COPPA: a US under-13 can register via Discord, have their email, Discord snowflake, IP hash, geo city and browsing path collected (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23-33), with no verifiable parental consent — statutory penalty is per-child per-violation and currently exceeds $50,000; the product is explicitly marketed into Discord gaming communities, the demographic most likely to be under 13. (2) GDPR-K Art. 8: the same for EU under-16s (under-13 to under-16 depending on member state), and the ad-measurement processing has no lawful basis for a child. (3) Paying minors: the 18+ redemption gate is a typed date with zero verification, so a 15-year-old defeats it by typing 2000-01-01 — and lib/eligibility.ts:19-23's own reasoning ('a minor's contract is voidable nearly everywhere — money we may have to return and cannot get back from the payout provider') is precisely the loss the unverified field fails to prevent. The mitigation the file claims to be is a checkbox.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/auth.ts:11-53, app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:236-255, lib/db/schema.ts:24-32, lib/eligibility.ts:27`
- **[MATERIAL]** The ad impression endpoint stores the last 16 characters of the user's live session JWT in an analytics table, in plaintext, indefinitely.
  - *Mechanism:* app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:27 writes `sessionId: req.cookies.get("cluster_session")?.value?.slice(-16)`. `cluster_session` is the compact HS256 JWT minted at lib/auth.ts:17-21 — the last 16 characters are the tail of the base64url HMAC-SHA256 signature. That fragment is persisted to ad_impressions.session_id (lib/db/schema.ts:546) with no expiry, next to user_id, hashed_ip and geo_city, and is readable by any staff account with the 'ads' area grant (app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx reads this table).
  - *Consequence:* Two harms. (1) A fragment of an authentication credential is written into a business-analytics table with a much wider read audience than the auth system — a category error that violates the basic separation the rest of this codebase is careful about (lib/portal-attempts.ts:15 explicitly refuses to log portal keys 'not even hashed'). (2) It is a stable, unhashed cross-request identifier that pins every ad impression to one login session for 30 days and survives account deletion (no FK on the table). 96 bits of a 256-bit signature is not forgeable on its own — but combined with the default-AUTH_SECRET finding above, an attacker who already has the secret can use it to confirm which stored rows belong to which forged session. The policy at app/legal/privacy/page.tsx:14 lists what ad measurement records and does not mention a session-token fragment at all.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:27, lib/auth.ts:17-29, lib/db/schema.ts:546, app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx:26`
- **[MATERIAL]** Passwords are hashed with Node's default-cost scrypt, not bcrypt — SECURITY.md states bcrypt, and the code contradicts it.
  - *Mechanism:* SECURITY.md:9 asserts under 'Gamer sessions': 'bcrypt password hashes'. lib/password.ts:4-8 is the only hashing function: `scryptSync(password, salt, 64)` with NO options object — Node's defaults are N=16384, r=8, p=1. bcryptjs appears only in the VERIFY path (lib/password.ts:13-14) to accept externally seeded admin hashes; nothing in the codebase ever produces a bcrypt hash. app/actions/auth.ts:49 confirms registration calls `hashPassword`. Separately, lib/password.ts:19 calls `timingSafeEqual(candidate, Buffer.from(hash,"hex"))` — Node throws RangeError when the buffers differ in length, and app/actions/auth.ts:61 calls verifyPassword with no try/catch, so any malformed stored hash (e.g. a value not 128 hex chars) turns a login into an unhandled 500 rather than a rejection.
  - *Consequence:* N=16384 is 8x below current OWASP guidance for scrypt (N=2^17) and roughly equivalent to bcrypt cost 11 — not catastrophic, but it means an offline crack of a leaked users table is materially cheaper than the security document claims. The more important consequence for diligence is the doc-vs-code contradiction itself: SECURITY.md is the artefact an acquirer's security reviewer reads first, and its very first row is factually wrong about the primary credential store. That calibrates how much of the rest of that table can be trusted (see the portal-lockout finding, which is the next row down and also only half true).
  - *Cites:* `lib/password.ts:4-8, lib/password.ts:12-20, SECURITY.md:9, app/actions/auth.ts:49`
- **[MATERIAL]** Portal brute-force lockout is bypassed entirely by the portal server actions, and the lockout it does apply is per-portal rather than per-IP — a trivial denial-of-service against every brand and server customer.
  - *Mechanism:* Two defects. (1) BYPASS: the DB-backed lockout (`lockState`/`recordAttempt`, lib/portal-attempts.ts:29-83) is called only from app/api/portal/unlock/route.ts:82,89,97. Every portal server action instead calls `requireBrand` (app/actions/brand-portal.ts:13-23) or `requireServer` (app/actions/server-portal.ts:19-28), which do a bare `keysMatch(...)` with no throttle, no attempt row, and no lockout — and return a distinguishable success/failure to the caller (server-portal.ts:47 returns `{ok:...}` vs the catch at :47 returning an error string). So the guessing oracle is unthrottled and unlogged. (2) DoS: lib/portal-attempts.ts:35-38 scopes the lock to `(kind, portalId)` only — there is no IP dimension. Three wrong keys from anyone lock that portal for everyone for 15 minutes (LOCKOUT_MS, lib/portal-auth.ts:185). app/api/portal/search/route.ts is unauthenticated and returns brand/server name+slug for any 2-character query, so the full customer roster is enumerable. SECURITY.md:12 claims 'three misses lock the portal for 15 minutes and every attempt is a row staff can read' — true for one of two entry points.
  - *Consequence:* An attacker enumerates the customer list via /api/portal/search (2-char queries, 10 results each — the whole book in a few hundred requests), then submits three wrong keys per portal via /api/portal/unlock. Every brand and every Discord server owner is locked out of their dashboard, permanently, for the cost of a loop, with no per-IP mitigation available in the code. For a business whose entire brand-facing product IS that portal, this is a one-attacker outage of the paying-customer surface. The bypass half separately falsifies the security document's brute-force claim for the action path.
  - *Exploit:* 1. `GET /api/portal/search?kind=brand&q=aa` … iterate all 2-char pairs; collect every slug. 2. For each slug, `POST /api/portal/unlock` with kind/slug and a junk key, three times. 3. Every brand portal is now locked for 15 minutes; re-run on a timer to make it permanent. 4. Separately, to bypass the lock for guessing: call the server action `portalMarkRead(guildId, guessKey)` / `portalSendMessage(...)` directly — no throttle, no attempt row.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/brand-portal.ts:13-23, app/actions/server-portal.ts:19-28, lib/portal-attempts.ts:29-61, app/api/portal/unlock/route.ts:82-99`
- **[MATERIAL]** Open redirect on the OAuth callback: the `next` parameter is stored unvalidated and passed to NextResponse.redirect after a successful sign-in.
  - *Mechanism:* app/api/auth/[provider]/route.ts:19 reads `next` straight from the query string with no validation and stores it in the httpOnly `oauth_flow` cookie (lines 23-26). app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:208/216/231 pass it as `dest` to `finish()`, which at line 190 does `NextResponse.redirect(new URL(dest, base))`. I verified in Node that `new URL("//evil.example","https://clustergg.com").href === "https://evil.example/"` and that an absolute `https://evil.example` likewise wins over the base. There is no `startsWith('/')` check, no allow-list, nothing — grepping the auth routes for `safeNext` or `startsWith("/")` returns zero hits. The same repository demonstrates it knows better 40 lines away in app/api/portal/unlock/route.ts:44-59, which allow-lists deep-link params with a regex specifically because 'preserve whatever the caller sent on a redirect is how an open redirect is born'.
  - *Consequence:* A link of the form https://clustergg.com/api/auth/discord?next=//attacker.tld sends the victim through the real Discord consent screen on the real clustergg.com domain and lands them on the attacker's page already authenticated — the highest-credibility phishing primitive there is, and it is the pattern used to harvest the follow-on credential (a portal key, a payout confirmation). It also chains with the Discord `guilds.join` scope the app requests (lib/oauth.ts:73). Cost to fix: one line. Cost of it shipping: every user who clicks a link that looks like the company's own domain.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Send victim `https://clustergg.com/api/auth/discord?next=//attacker.tld/cluster-login`. 2. Victim sees the genuine clustergg.com origin, completes the genuine Discord OAuth. 3. callback/route.ts:216 calls finish(..., '//attacker.tld/cluster-login'); line 190 resolves that to https://attacker.tld/cluster-login and 302s there. 4. Attacker's page, reached from a real sign-in, asks for the portal key / payout confirmation.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/auth/[provider]/route.ts:19, app/api/auth/[provider]/route.ts:23-26, app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:190, app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:208`
- **[MATERIAL]** The `/api/setup` bootstrap route is public when SETUP_TOKEN is unset, and the first account created on a fresh database silently becomes superadmin.
  - *Mechanism:* app/api/setup/route.ts:19-22 gates on `const token = process.env.SETUP_TOKEN; if (token && ...)` — when the variable is UNSET the guard is skipped entirely and the route is world-callable. `.env.example:10` labels it 'Optional'. The route is idempotent against an existing schema (line 30), so on a live DB it is inert; the window is a freshly provisioned database. app/actions/auth.ts:44-45 then makes whoever registers first `superadmin` on a zero-row users table. The same optional-secret pattern repeats at app/api/cron/daily/route.ts:21-23 and /api/cron/sync — with CRON_SECRET unset, an anonymous GET runs `runAllJobs('daily')`, which fans announcements and ads out to every installed Discord guild. app/api/test-login/route.ts:15-27 mints a session for admin@clustergg.com to any anonymous POST whenever PERF_COUNT=1 is present in the environment.
  - *Consequence:* A race on deploy day: anyone who finds the URL before the founder registers owns the platform as superadmin. The CRON variant lets an anonymous caller trigger unbounded Discord fan-out — the fastest route to the bot being mass-removed or Discord-banned, which is the single-point-of-failure the whole distribution model rests on. These are config-dependent rather than code-dependent, which is why I class them RISK — but the pattern (`if (secret)` rather than `if (!secret) throw`) is a deliberate fail-open choice repeated in four places, and fail-open on a bootstrap route is not a defensible default.
  - *Exploit:* Fresh deploy, SETUP_TOKEN unset: 1. `POST /api/setup` → schema created, seeded. 2. `POST` the register action with any email → app/actions/auth.ts:45 sees count()===0 and assigns role 'superadmin'. 3. Full admin. Separately, CRON_SECRET unset: `GET /api/cron/daily` repeatedly → repeated Discord broadcasts to every guild.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/setup/route.ts:19-22, app/api/setup/route.ts:44-46, .env.example:10-11, app/actions/auth.ts:44-45`
- **[MATERIAL]** OAuth account-merge trusts an unverified provider email to attach a third-party identity to an existing Cluster account.
  - *Mechanism:* app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:220-232 is branch 3 of the sign-in flow: if the provider profile carries an email that matches an existing users.email, the identity is attached and the caller is logged in as that user. The Discord userInfo mapper (lib/oauth.ts:74-84) reads `u.email` from /users/@me and returns it — it never inspects Discord's `verified` boolean, which that same response carries. The Epic mapper (lib/oauth.ts:100-101) likewise takes `u.email` from the userinfo claim without checking an `email_verified` claim. The comment at line 224-225 acknowledges the risk for identity-ownership ('matching on email is a convenience, not a proof of ownership') and guards only the reverse direction — it does not stop the login itself.
  - *Consequence:* If any configured provider can issue an OAuth token for an account whose email is unverified or attacker-settable, this is account takeover of any Cluster account by email address — including staff and admin accounts, since branch 3 returns `finish(u.id, u.role, ...)` with the victim's stored role. I mark this UNPROVEN rather than BROKEN because whether Discord and Epic permit an OAuth authorization from an unverified-email account is a provider-behaviour question I cannot settle from this repository, and Discord in particular heavily restricts unverified accounts. The defect that IS proven from the code is that the application relies entirely on that external guarantee and checks nothing itself — a one-line `if (!profile.emailVerified) skip` is absent, and the provider's policy can change without notice.
  - *Exploit:* If a provider issues a token for an unverified email: 1. Create a provider account, set its email to victim@example.com without verifying. 2. Authorize Cluster. 3. callback/route.ts:221 matches users.email, line 226 attaches the identity, line 231 calls finish() with the victim's userId and role. Session granted as the victim.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:219-232, lib/oauth.ts:74-84, lib/oauth.ts:95-102`
- **[MINOR]** Next.js image optimiser is configured to proxy any HTTPS host on the internet, turning the deployment into an open image proxy.
  - *Mechanism:* next.config.ts:34 sets `images: { remotePatterns: [{ protocol: "https", hostname: "**" }] }`. That instructs `/\_next/image?url=<anything>` to fetch and re-serve any https URL the caller names, on the company's Vercel account and bandwidth, with a 31-day cache (line 41).
  - *Consequence:* Third parties can use clustergg.com as a free CDN and image-laundering front (bandwidth billed to the company; abuse attributed to its domain). It is also a server-side fetch primitive against any https endpoint reachable from the Vercel function — limited by the fact that only successfully-decoded images are returned, which is why I rate this minor rather than material. On the platform's own Fast Origin Transfer economics — which next.config.ts:35-40 shows the team is acutely cost-sensitive about — an abuser can run the image bill up at will.
  - *Exploit:* `GET https://clustergg.com/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fany-host%2Fbig.png&w=3840&q=100` — served and cached for 31 days at the company's expense.
  - *Cites:* `next.config.ts:34`


#### A9 · Test coverage & document truthfulness (9)

- **[FATAL]** COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md's revenue product — the $500/100,000-view package at a $5 CPM with a $4 floor — does not exist anywhere in the code; the shipped pricing engine sells a different business entirely.
  - *Mechanism:* COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.2 and §11 state the product as '$500/month = 100,000 views', '$5.00 implied CPM', and 'Hard floor $4.00 CPM — the admin must refuse to save below it'. The shipped pricing config (lib/pricing.ts:85-105) is a three-tier monthly subscription — reachBase 600, challengeBase 500, ultimateBase 400, challengePrice 250, prizePool 175 — with no view package, no view target, and no CPM field except `benchmarkCpm: 8`, which lib/pricing.ts:60-66 documents as display-only for ROAS. A repo-wide grep for CPM_FLOOR/cpmFloor/MIN_CPM/minCpm returns nothing. Invoice lines are only kind 'base', 'game', 'discount', 'addon' (lib/invoices.ts:243,253,268,277) — no impression or view line exists, so a view package cannot be billed. `ad_campaigns` (lib/db/schema.ts:504-519) has no views-target and no delivered column, so a view commitment cannot even be stored. Worse, README.md:17 and docs/BUSINESS.md:7-13 in the same commit describe a different company: '$250 buys a sponsored challenge... The $75 platform fee is the only line the business lives on' — irreconcilable with 'the business is the gap between the cents paid to gamers and the cents collected from brands' (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:20-22).
  - *Consequence:* The entire revenue model presented to the investor is unimplemented. Rule 4 of the model — 'Brands we can take = monthly views ÷ 100,000. Computed, never guessed' (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:432) — cannot be computed because neither the numerator nor the denominator is stored. Nothing prevents a salesperson selling below the stated $4 floor. The 100%-of-revenue side of the unit economics (the $2,000/month at 1,000 gamers in §0) has zero code behind it, while the cost side (CP payout, $1,500/month at 1,000 gamers) is fully implemented and running. An investor reading README/BUSINESS.md and COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md is shown two mutually exclusive businesses at the same git SHA.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:47-51, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:429-433, lib/pricing.ts:85-105, lib/invoices.ts:243`
- **[FATAL]** The 15-screen guarantee is broken in three independent ways; a gamer can bank the full 500 CP/day having opened zero screens, which makes the break-even CPM infinite.
  - *Mechanism:* §2 rests on three parts and all three fail. Part 1 — 'no action pays more than 25 CP. Therefore 500 CP is always 20 actions' — is false: top3_challenge pays 50 (lib/quests.ts:76), win_challenge 100 (:77), best_profile_award 100 (:100), connect_account 50 (:109). Part 2 — 'at most 125 CP a day may come from PASSIVE actions' — has no implementation: `awardQuestAction` (lib/quests.ts:479-534) checks only a per-quest per-action cap and one global 500 ceiling; there is no passive tally. Part 3 depends on Part 2. §2.1's closing claim, 'Every action in ACTION_CATALOG carries this flag. The mission validator and the CP ledger both read it', is false in both halves: a grep for /passive/ across lib/quests.ts and lib/cp-economics.ts returns nothing, and `missionProblems` (lib/missions.ts:149-185) validates only unknown-action, zero-weight, non-integer count, count-over-cap, duplicate action, and total≠ceiling — there is no active-task count. Summing the caps of exactly the actions §2.1 lists as passive: follower_gained 25×2=50, profile_vote_received 25×2=50, profile_views_25 25×3=75, gift_received 25×2=50, stat_levelup 25×4=100, play_session 25×3=75 — 400 CP from 16 passive events, 3.2× the stated 125 cap. Add win_challenge 100 (:77) and the day's full 500 is reached with no screen opened.
  - *Consequence:* §2 states the failure case explicitly: 'At 5 screens the break-even CPM is $10 — above the market ceiling — so that scenario is not a thin business, it is *no* business. The passive cap makes it impossible.' The cap does not exist, so that scenario is not merely possible, it is beatable — 0 screens, not 5, making break-even CPM infinite. The cost is fully real and fully automatic: $0.05/gamer/day, $1,500/month at 1,000 daily gamers, $150,000/month at 100,000, paid against zero guaranteed brand impressions. This is the single assumption A1 is built on ('15 screens per gamer per day — Guaranteed by §2', docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:414), and it is guaranteed by nothing.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Win one challenge (win_challenge, 100 CP, lib/quests.ts:77). 2. Let followers, profile votes, profile views, gifts, tracked stat rises and tracked matches accumulate — every one of these fires without the gamer opening a Cluster screen. 3. The six passive actions in §2.1 pay out 400 CP at their shipped caps. 4. Total 500 CP, the full daily ceiling, with zero brand impressions delivered. No code path anywhere refuses this.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:107-127, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:131-142, lib/quests.ts:76, lib/quests.ts:77`
- **[FATAL]** /api/ads/beacon is an unauthenticated, unsigned, unrate-limited write endpoint that mints ad impressions and ad clicks for any campaign, and pays CP for them.
  - *Mechanism:* POST /api/ads/beacon accepts a raw JSON body and takes `ccId` entirely from the caller (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:13). There is no signature, no nonce, no origin check, no CSRF token, no rate limit, and no proof the creative was ever rendered. `type:'impression'` inserts an ad_impressions row unconditionally (:23-33); `type:'click'` inserts an ad_clicks row unconditionally (:50-52). middleware.ts:84 matches only ['/admin/:path*','/u/:path*'], so nothing guards this route. lib/cards/ad-click.ts:11-14 documents a signed click URL specifically to stop 'somebody composing their own /api/ads/go?cc=<a competitor's id> and running that brand's click count — and their cost-per-click — through the floor', and app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33-35 does verify that signature — but the beacon route writes to the same ad_clicks table with no signature at all, so the defence is fully bypassed by a different URL. On the CP side, both branches call awardQuestAction with refId = a freshly minted uid() (:36, :56), so the dedup index on (user,quest,action,refType,refId) never fires; only the per-action daily caps stop it — 25 for ad_impression (lib/quests.ts:117) and 3×25 for ad_click (:118).
  - *Consequence:* Brand delivery numbers are settable to any value by anyone with curl, for any campaign, without a session. At the model's own $5 CPM each fabricated impression is worth $0.005 of billing; a single script at 100 req/s fabricates ~8.64M impressions/day, so a brand's entire 100,000-view package is consumed in roughly 17 minutes of traffic that never reached a human. Conversely a competitor can burn a rival's campaign or spike a rival's CTR. This is the exact failure §5.1 says is 'the single most reputation-damaging line in the old table' — an incentivised, unverifiable click rate — except the click count is not merely incentivised, it is forgeable. No advertiser audit, no IAB-aligned measurement, and no MRC-style viewability review survives this. Separately, a logged-in script harvests 25 CP (impressions) + 75 CP (clicks) = 100 CP/day, 20% of the daily ceiling, with zero screens opened.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Load any page carrying an ad and read `ccId` out of the rendered markup or the beacon payload — it must be exposed for the legitimate beacon to work. 2. curl -X POST https://<host>/api/ads/beacon -H 'content-type: application/json' -d '{"type":"impression","ccId":"<id>","path":"/"}' in a loop. Each call inserts one ad_impressions row. 3. Repeat with {"type":"click","ccId":"<id>"} to insert ad_clicks rows — no signature is checked on this route even though /api/ads/go requires one. 4. Attach a session cookie and the same loop also credits ad_impression and ad_click CP up to the daily caps, because refId is a fresh uid() on every call and dedup never matches.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:12-62, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23-33, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:36, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:50-56`
- **[FATAL]** Not one test in the 32-file suite exercises concurrency, and every money path is a read-then-write with no transaction — because the codebase contains zero transactions and production runs on neon-http, which cannot open one.
  - *Mechanism:* A repo-wide grep for '.transaction(' across all .ts/.tsx/.mts files returns exactly zero hits. Production selects the neon-http driver (lib/db/index.ts:910-916), which issues one HTTP request per statement and supports no interactive transaction, so the absence is structural rather than an oversight. Every money path is therefore a bare check-then-act: (a) the CP ceiling reads `cpEarnedToday` into `room` (lib/quests.ts:490-491) and inserts the paying event ~28 lines later (:516,:519) with nothing holding the window; (b) buyTrophy re-reads the wallet (lib/marketplace.ts:223), compares to price (:224), then inserts the order (:234) — the doc comment at lib/marketplace.ts:186-194 claims 'the worst case is a refused purchase rather than a negative balance', which holds sequentially and not concurrently; (c) requestRedeem selects awards WHERE status='held' (app/actions/trophies.ts:141), inserts a trophy_redeems row (:150), then updates the trophies to 'pending' with `.where(inArray(ids))` and no status guard (:154) — not even a compare-and-swap. Against that, grepping tests/db/*.mts for Promise.all\|concurrent\|race\|parallel\|simultan returns no concurrency test at all. The tests that claim to cover it do not: tests/db/gifting.mts:5-6 states its purpose as 'the property the whole gift flow rests on — that a second tab cannot spend the same points twice' and :114-117 says 'The two-tab problem', but :122-125 are two strictly sequential awaits. tests/db/cp-economics.mts:134-139 and tests/db/caps.mts:92-100 grind the ceiling in sequential for-loops.
  - *Consequence:* Three verified double-spend windows with no test between them and production. The cash-out one is the worst: two concurrent requestRedeem calls naming the same award ids both pass the held check and both write a trophy_redeems row, so the platform books and owes the same trophy value twice — real dollars out, and the only thing that catches it is a human in the redeem queue noticing. buyTrophy concurrency mints trophies for CP that was never earned, and every bought trophy is redeemable for cash (marketplace.ts award status 'held', asserted at tests/db/marketplace.mts:140). The CP ceiling race lets N concurrent awards each read the same `already` and each pay full weight, so the 500/day ceiling — the one number docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:37-39 says 'must never be changed casually' and on which the entire $0.05/gamer/day cost model rests — is not enforced under load. None of this is theoretical at scale: serverless Next.js fans requests across instances by design, and neon-http gives them no shared lock.
  - *Exploit:* Double-redemption, the highest-value one: 1. Hold one or more trophies with status 'held'. 2. Fire two requestRedeem calls with the identical awardIds array at the same instant. 3. Both execute the SELECT at app/actions/trophies.ts:141 before either reaches the UPDATE at :154, so both see status='held' and both pass the `awards.length !== ids.length` check. 4. Both INSERT a trophy_redeems row at :150 — there is no unique index on award ids and no conditional update. 5. Two pending payout requests now exist for the same trophy value. The same shape works on buyTrophy (read at lib/marketplace.ts:223, write at :234) and on the CP ceiling (read at lib/quests.ts:490, write at :519).
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:910-916, lib/quests.ts:490-491, lib/quests.ts:516, lib/quests.ts:519`
- **[FATAL]** Zero of the seven ad-serving requirements the document itself calls 'the biggest gap' are delivered except cache-keying, and none of the ad path has a single test.
  - *Mechanism:* Taking §5.3's seven numbered requirements against the code: (1) Delivery counting — ad_campaigns has no target and no delivered column (lib/db/schema.ts:504-519), serveAds applies no budget or pace filter (lib/ads.ts:61-71), nothing stops at target. (2) 'Seed on gamer + card' — the seed is `${kind}\|${key}` (lib/discord/cards.ts:115, app/api/card/[kind]/route.tsx:123), i.e. card identity only, with no viewer term; pickCardAd indexes off that hash plus the rotation window (lib/cards/ads.ts:111), so all viewers of one card see one brand and a gamer does not cycle brands. (3) Frequency cap per gamer per brand per day — absent. (4) 'No silent cutoff' — lib/ads.ts:76 still does `.slice(0, placement.maxCreativesInRotation)` with no queue and no disclosure, the exact line §5.3 flags. (5) Cache/ad separation — DONE: the pick precedes the cache and the creative id is in the cache key (lib/discord/cards.ts:116). (6) Billable-impression cap per gamer — absent; logCardAdImpression (lib/cards/ads.ts:200-216) is unconditional fire-and-forget with no per-gamer counter. (7) Category exclusivity — absent. Test coverage for the whole area: grepping tests/ for beacon\|ads/go\|adClicks\|serveAds\|pickCardAd\|logCardAdImpression returns nothing; the only file importing the ad modules is tests/db/cards.mts, whose ad assertions are box geometry (:185-186) and a regex that the string 'export const HOUSE_AD: CardAdSlot' exists in the source (:227-230).
  - *Consequence:* docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:252 states the position plainly — 'We cannot currently deliver a promised number of views to anybody' — and then §7.2 and §11 sell 100,000 views per brand per month anyway. Every $500 package is a commitment the platform has no mechanism to meter, pace, cap, or stop; over-delivery is unbounded and under-delivery is undetectable until a brand asks. Requirement 6's absence compounds finding 3: with no billable cap, an inflated impression count flows straight into whatever delivery report a brand is shown. Because requirement 2 is not merely missing but inverted, a heavy gamer re-opening the same card sees one brand repeatedly — frequency with no reach, which §5.3 item 3 itself calls 'worthless and looks fraudulent'.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:248-270, lib/ads.ts:33-104, lib/ads.ts:76, lib/cards/ads.ts:101-124`
- **[FATAL]** COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md states its own unbuilt backlog in the declarative present tense; EXECUTION_PLAN.md labels the identical items as work owed, and the only commit that added either changed no code at all.
  - *Mechanism:* HEAD (1c5c98a, 'The pivot: the commercial model becomes the product') is a docs-only commit: +433 COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md, +176 DUE_DILIGENCE_BRIEF.md, +165 EXECUTION_PLAN.md, and zero source files. The 165 EXECUTION_PLAN lines are honest — B64/B65/B66 are written as future work with explicit 'Verification owed → tests/db/ad-delivery.mts' clauses, and tests/db/ad-delivery.mts does not exist. COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md restates the same items as accomplished fact while opening with 'Nothing in here is a preference. Every number is either measured, derived from a number that is measured, or explicitly marked as an assumption' (:8-9). Verified falsehoods stated as fact: §2.1 'Every action in ACTION_CATALOG carries this flag. The mission validator and the CP ledger both read it' (:141-142) — no flag exists and missionProblems never reads one. §5.1 'ad_click drops to 0 CP' (:233) — it is 25 CP at cap 3 (lib/quests.ts:118), and all four mission templates require ad_click ×3 (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106). §5.2 'a stored reason of daily_cap_reached' (:242-244) — quest_events has id, userId, questId, actionKey, qpAwarded, cpAwarded, refType, refId, createdAt and no reason column (lib/db/schema.ts:843-857). §7.2 'Submit → admin approval required → live' (:355-356) — brand-portal.ts:141 writes the creative with status 'approved' and :133 the campaign with status 'active', direct from the brand's own portal with no human step. §7.3 'every impression is logged with pagePath: discord:card:<kind>' (:357) — the web card route deliberately logs nothing (app/api/card/[kind]/route.tsx:118-121). §10 assumption A1 is graded 'Guaranteed by §2 — if the passive cap holds' (:414); the passive cap does not exist.
  - *Consequence:* Tallying every concrete product requirement COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md states: 35 requirements, of which 5 are fully implemented (retired social actions at weight 0; past-ceiling rows written at cp 0; the caps panel wording and reset; cache/ad separation; ≥1 creative required to launch), 4 are partial (brand key/portal email, admin email templates 3-of-7, invoice-at-purchase but not at launch, Discord-only impression logging), and 26 are absent or contradicted. **5 of 35 — 14%.** Of §5.3's seven ad-serving requirements, on which the document says the revenue promise lives or dies, **1 of 7**. The §5.4 social purge is entirely undone: posts, comments and post_reactions tables all exist (lib/db/schema.ts:266,278,285), app/feed is live, and app/actions/social.ts:123,146,149,162 still calls awardQuestAction for all four retired actions. §6.1's sales cockpit, §6.2's danger zones, §3.1's 25% bonus, §1.2's $4 floor and all four §8 website pages have no code. The consequence for the deal is not a feature gap — it is that the primary document put in front of the investor describes, in the present tense and under an explicit truth warranty, a product that does not exist, while the honest version of the same list sits in EXECUTION_PLAN.md marked as unbuilt.
  - *Cites:* `docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:8-9, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:141-142, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:233, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:242-244`
- **[SEVERE]** There is no CI. No workflow, no type-check gate, no lint gate, no coverage measure — the 32 hand-rolled suites run only when a human types `npm test`.
  - *Mechanism:* There is no .github/ directory at all, and no gitlab/circle/travis/husky config anywhere in the tree. package.json defines `test: node tests/run-all.mjs` (package.json:11) and `lint: next lint` (:9) but nothing invokes either automatically. vercel.json:5 sets buildCommand to plain `next build`, so deployment gates on compilation only. tests/run-all.mjs:104 sets DEMO_DB=1 for every suite and exits non-zero on failure — a correct runner with no trigger. No coverage tool is present in dependencies or devDependencies. Separately, 14 of the 32 suites assert by regex over source text rather than over behaviour: tests/db/abuse.mts:208-214 readFile()s app/actions/auth.ts and asserts /signupVelocity\(db, \{ email \}\)/ and that one string index precedes another; tests/db/cards.mts:227-230 readFile()s lib/cards/ads.ts and asserts /export const HOUSE_AD: CardAdSlot/. Those pass on any refactor that preserves the characters and fail on any that does not, in both directions.
  - *Consequence:* Nothing stops a commit that breaks the CP ceiling, the mission arithmetic, or the invoice total from reaching production — the suite is advisory. The suite's most load-bearing fixtures (ACTION_CAP_SUM === 1330 and ceiling === 500 at tests/db/cp-economics.mts:46-47) exist precisely so a weight cannot drift silently, and with no CI they drift silently anyway. For a diligence buyer this converts every 'asserted in tests/db/…' claim in the source comments and in docs/EXECUTION_PLAN.md from a guarantee into an intention. It also means the fraction of the suite that is source-text regex is unmeasurable coverage dressed as coverage.
  - *Cites:* `package.json:5-12, tests/run-all.mjs:98-113, vercel.json:1-16, tests/db/abuse.mts:208-214`
- **[SEVERE]** Three of the four shipped mission templates breach the document's own passive cap, two deliver exactly the break-even screen count, and implementing §5.1's ad_click=0 would invalidate all four.
  - *Mechanism:* Classifying each template's tasks by §2.1's own passive/active table: 'Show up' carries follower_gained ×2 (50) + stat_levelup ×4 (100) = 150 passive; 'Get seen' carries profile_views_25 ×3 (75) + profile_vote_received ×2 (50) + play_session ×3 (75) + stat_levelup ×2 (50) = 250; 'Bring people' carries profile_views_25 ×3 + gift_received ×2 + play_session ×3 + stat_levelup ×2 = 250. Three of four exceed the stated 125 cap, two by exactly double. §2.2 says 'Two of the four shipped templates fail this today and are rebuilt' — none was rebuilt; the templates are untouched at lib/missions.ts:49-110. Counting screen-requiring tasks: 'Get seen' and 'Bring people' each contain 10, which docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:65-73 gives as exactly the $5-CPM break-even — zero margin, against a design target of 15. Separately, every one of the four templates requires ad_click ×3 (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106) worth 75 CP, 15% of the day; §5.1 orders ad_click to 0 CP, at which point missionProblems flags each as 'pays nothing, so this task can never be completed' (lib/missions.ts:160) and each total falls to 425 against a 500 ceiling (:174-183), so launchable() returns false for all four. tests/db/missions.mts asserts totals, per-quest balance, task counts, caps and editability (:40-80) and asserts nothing about active/passive or screens.
  - *Consequence:* The two flagship missions instruct gamers toward exactly the behaviour that produces zero margin at the sold price, and half their value comes from actions the document classifies as requiring no screen at all. The company cannot execute its own §5.1 remediation without simultaneously rebuilding all four missions, because the remediation makes every shipped mission unlaunchable — a dependency stated nowhere in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md or EXECUTION_PLAN.md and one a buyer would price as schedule risk on the single highest-priority fix in the document.
  - *Cites:* `lib/missions.ts:49-110, lib/missions.ts:61, lib/missions.ts:76, lib/missions.ts:91`
- **[SEVERE]** A brand pushes creatives live onto cards posted into third-party Discord servers with no human review, contradicting §7.2's stated approval gate.
  - *Mechanism:* portalLaunchCardCreative validates only that a file URL, a click URL and a CTA label are present (app/actions/brand-portal.ts:100-102), then writes the campaign with status 'active' (:133) and the creative with status 'approved' (:141). portalUploadCreative does the same at :51. serveAds requires exactly `eq(adCreatives.status,'approved')` (lib/ads.ts:67), which the brand has just set for itself. There is no pending_review state on this path, no admin queue, no notification. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:355-356 states the flow as 'Submit → **admin approval required** → live'.
  - *Consequence:* Any party holding a brand portal key can put arbitrary imagery and an arbitrary outbound link onto every card the bot draws into every opted-in Discord community, instantly and unreviewed. That is the fastest available route to the outcome the codebase elsewhere treats as existential — lib/cards/ads.ts:56-60 explains the 'Sponsored:' prefix exists because a button reading like a community endorsement 'is exactly the thing that gets a bot removed'. It also makes assumption A7 (Discord platform risk, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:420) materially worse than graded, since content policy exposure is now unbounded by any internal control. No test covers this path.
  - *Exploit:* 1. Obtain a brand portal key (issued automatically by ensureBrandKeys and emailed via the portal.key template). 2. Call portalLaunchCardCreative with any fileUrl, any https clickUrl and any CTA text. 3. The campaign is written active at app/actions/brand-portal.ts:133 and the creative approved at :141. 4. serveAds (lib/ads.ts:61-71) immediately treats it as eligible and it appears on cards in every opted-in server on the next rotation window. No human sees it first.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/brand-portal.ts:87-149, app/actions/brand-portal.ts:133, app/actions/brand-portal.ts:141, app/actions/brand-portal.ts:51`

### 9.2 Financial, legal & platform findings (Parts B & C)

*96 findings across seven research streams, each primary-sourced (URLs in §8.4 and per-stream below).*


#### B1 · Discord & game-platform ToS (A7) (13)

- **[FATAL]** Discord's Developer Policy contains a flat, unqualified prohibition on exactly what ClusterGG sells. Clause 6 bans targeting Discord users with advertising, and bans any message content unrelated to the app's function. Every card the bot renders carries a paid third-party brand creative. This is not a grey area — it is the single sentence the whole company is built against.
  - *Evidence:* Discord Developer Policy, section "Protect Discord Users", clause 6, verbatim: "Do not target users with advertisements or marketing. Messaging to Discord users from any Application or developer team should be relevant to the function of the Application and may not contain material unrelated to an Application's function or information." (https://support-dev.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/8563934450327-Discord-Developer-Policy — effective 8 July 2024, last updated 6 June 2024, content last edited 2 Aug 2024; I confirmed via the Zendesk API that this is the live current version as of 6 Aug 2026, `outdated: false`.) A brand creative for a sponsor is by construction "material unrelated to an Application's function". The Developer Policy is incorporated into the Developer Terms of Service (https://support-dev.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/8562894815383-Discord-Developer-Terms-of-Service, effective 8 July 2024) and breach of it is an "Enforcement Reason" under Section 9. Confirmed in code: lib/cards/ads.ts:101-124 `pickCardAd()` injects a `campaignCreativeId`/`imageUrl`/`clickUrl` into rendered Discord cards.
  - *Implication:* The primary revenue line — $500/month ad packages, scaling to a claimed $2.25M/month at 1M gamers — is sourced from an activity Discord prohibits in writing. There is no carve-out, no waiver process published, and no rate card under which a third party may resell Discord attention. Every dollar of the model's revenue side sits on the wrong side of one sentence.
- **[FATAL]** Paying cash-convertible points for ad clicks, ad impressions, bot-list votes and bot installs is squarely inside Discord's ban on manipulating engagement — and ClusterGG pays for all four, in shipped code.
  - *Evidence:* Developer Policy clause 13, verbatim: "Do not misrepresent or fraudulently manipulate engagement. This includes participating in, enabling, or promoting the inflation of server membership with bot or user accounts. This also includes automating messages to be sent for the purpose of maintaining activity in a Discord server." (same URL/date as above). Discord Community Guidelines rule 15, last updated 29 Aug 2025: "Do not engage with our service in an inauthentic way. This includes artificially inflating server membership, manipulating engagement metrics, and selling artificial engagement services." (https://discord.com/guidelines). Discord Terms of Service, last updated 29 Aug 2025, prohibits "selling, licensing, or otherwise commercializing content or data obtained from our services" (https://discord.com/terms). In ClusterGG code: lib/quests.ts:117-118 pays `ad_impression` 1 CP (cap 25/day) and `ad_click` 25 CP (cap 3/day); lib/quests.ts also pays `botlist_vote` 25 CP (cap 2) and `bot_added` 25 CP (cap 2) — i.e. cash for installing the bot into more servers, which is literally paid inflation of the app's server count. All four shipped daily-mission templates require `ad_click` x3 (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106). CP is convertible to real cash at 10,000 CP = $1.
  - *Implication:* 75 CP of the 500 CP daily ceiling (15%) is paid for ad clicks; a further 50 CP (10%) is paid for bot installs or bot-list votes. So 25% of the currency a gamer earns is paid for actions Discord classifies as manipulated engagement. Fixing this means deleting ad_click from all four mission templates — which makes every shipped mission uncompletable and forces a rebuild of the entire CP economy before the guarantee arithmetic in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md can even be restated. Separately, at a ~10% paid click rate the click stream is unsellable to any brand that audits it.
- **[FATAL]** Riot's API Terms prohibit using the Riot API to operate contests or promotions — which is ClusterGG's entire challenge product — and ClusterGG is running a live commercial product on a Riot DEVELOPMENT key by its own admission in source code.
  - *Evidence:* Riot Games API Terms and Conditions, prohibited-uses list, verbatim: "Using the Riot Games API for any Application to violate anyone's privacy rights (e.g. distributing unwanted commercial solicitations, such as, but not limited to, spyware and adware), to infringe on intellectual property rights (including copyright, patent and trademark rights), to sponsor or operate any contest, sweepstakes or other promotion, to harass or defame others, or to promote hatred towards any group of people." Also: "In the event You desire to resell or license any Application, charge for access to Game Information through a website or otherwise require payment for access to the Game Information, You must notify Riot and obtain Riot's prior written approval before undertaking such activities... Failure to obtain Riot's prior written permission will result in the termination of Your rights under these API Terms." And: "Riot may terminate the license at any time for any reason." (https://developer.riotgames.com/terms / https://support-developer.riotgames.com/hc/en-us/articles/22698917218323-API-Terms-and-Conditions — document dated 9 December 2013, Zendesk edit 4 Dec 2023. FLAG: the operative contract text is 13 years old and pre-dates every product in this space; it is nonetheless the live document.) Riot General Policies, last edited 29 May 2025 (https://support-developer.riotgames.com/hc/en-us/articles/22698591841939-General-Policies): "Your product cannot feature betting or gambling functionality."; "You may monetize your product as long as your product is registered on the Developer Portal and your product status is either Approved or Acknowledged"; acceptable ways to charge players include "Currencies that cannot be exchanged back into fiat"; "Tournaments must... Allot at least 70% of the entry fees to the prize pool"; and a mandatory legal boilerplate ("[Your product] isn't endorsed by Riot Games...") that must be "readily visible to players". KEY-TYPE FACTS from Riot's own portal docs (https://developer.riotgames.com/docs/portal): development keys "deactivate every 24 hours"; personal keys are limited to "20 requests every 1 second" and "100 requests every 2 minutes" and "You may not run your application for public consumption using a personal key, regardless of how long the approval process for your production key takes"; production keys get "500 requests every 10 seconds" and "30,000 requests every 10 minutes" and require an approved registered product. ClusterGG code, lib/providers/riot-verify.ts:7-8, verbatim comment: "RSO needs a PRODUCTION key with an approved product registration. We have a development key." Confirmed again at app/admin/linked-accounts/page.tsx:44 ("Riot's development key expires every 24 hours") and lib/providers/registry.ts:222. I could find NO instance of the required Riot boilerplate anywhere in the repo (grep for "isn't endorsed by Riot" returns nothing).
  - *Implication:* Three compounding failures. (1) Legal: the challenge product is a "contest or promotion" operated on the Riot API, and CP is a currency that IS exchangeable back into fiat — both on the wrong side of the terms. (2) Operational: a dev key dies every 24 hours, so League verification and stat tracking are structurally unreliable today; the product cannot be operated at any commercial scale without a production key. (3) The trap: obtaining that production key requires submitting the product to Riot's audit — at which point Riot reads the cash-out challenges and the gambling-adjacent redemption ladder. A production key is both mandatory and unlikely to be granted. Quantitatively, a production key gives 4.32M calls/day; the current dev/personal-tier budget is ~72,000/day, which at ~4 Riot calls per gamer refresh caps the platform at roughly 18,000 gamer-refreshes/day — under the 10,000-gamer rung of their own ladder if any gamer is refreshed more than twice a day, and that budget also has to cover match-v5 and spectator polling (lib/providers/riot-lol-rich.ts:107-145,224).
- **[FATAL]** VERDICT: ClusterGG is in breach of Discord's Developer Policy today, on at least two independent clauses, and the breach is inherent to the product rather than incidental to it. Probability judgement below.
  - *Evidence:* Synthesis of the above. In breach today of Developer Policy clause 6 (ads in app messaging) and clause 13 (manipulated engagement via paid clicks, paid votes, paid installs). Additionally in breach of Riot's API T&C (operating contests on the API; commercial operation on a development key without registration or written approval). All source documents fetched and verified current as of 6 August 2026: Discord Developer Policy (eff. 8 Jul 2024), Developer ToS (eff. 8 Jul 2024), Discord ToS and Community Guidelines (upd. 29 Aug 2025), Monetization Policy (eff. 5 May 2024), Ads Policy (upd. 2 Mar 2026), App Content Requirements (upd. 9 Oct 2025), Riot General Policies (upd. 29 May 2025), Riot API T&C (dated 9 Dec 2013).
  - *Implication:* PROBABILITY CURRENTLY IN BREACH OF DISCORD'S WRITTEN TERMS: ~95%. Clause 6 is not ambiguous and the product cannot be described in a way that avoids it. PROBABILITY OF ENFORCEMENT, by scale rung, assuming no product change: under 1,000 gamers / under 100 servers, 10-20% over 24 months — below the verification threshold, invisible, enforcement is complaint-driven. At 100-2,500 servers (verification required; Stripe identity verification of the team owner; App Directory review if they want discovery), 50-70% within 12 months of crossing — verification is the moment a human at Discord reads what the app does. Above 2,500 servers or once brand spend is public enough to attract a trade-press mention, 85%+ within 12 months. TIMELINE TO ENFORCEMENT IF IT SCALES: the trigger is app verification at 100 servers, not gamer count — so the clock is far shorter than the funding runway. DAY-ONE IMPACT: acquisition to zero, server-owner business to zero, ~50% of inventory gone with 100% of CP liability retained, all Discord API Data must be deleted under Section 9(b), and every sold brand contract becomes an undeliverable refund. DIVERSIFICATION: none that matters. INVESTOR CONCLUSION: A7 is not an unassessed assumption — it is a known, written, adverse answer that the company has not read. This is not a risk to be priced; it is a product that must be redesigned before it can be funded, and the redesign (ads on clustergg.com only, no cash-convertible currency for platform engagement, Riot production key with an approved registration and no cash contests) yields a materially smaller and slower business than the one in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md.
- **[SEVERE]** Discord now sells this exact product itself. Quests plus Orbs IS rewarded advertising for gamers, launched globally in 2025, sold only by Discord's own sales team — and Discord deliberately made its reward currency non-cash. ClusterGG is competing with its landlord, in the landlord's house, using the one design choice the landlord rejected.
  - *Evidence:* Discord Quests launched as a sponsored-ad format in April 2024; Video Quests on mobile announced March 2025 (https://discord.com/press-releases/announcing-quests-advertising-on-mobile); Arena Quests plus AppsFlyer and Gamesight measurement partnerships announced 2 October 2025 (https://discord.com/press-releases/discord-launches-newest-ad-format-and-partners-with-appsflyer-gamesight-for-ads-measurement), reporting "hundreds of campaigns", "millions of rewards claimed", 200M+ MAU and 1.9bn combined monthly gaming hours. Discord's ads page (https://discord.com/ads/quests) states "90M+ Daily Active Users", "90%+ Play Video Games", and critically: "Discord Ads can only be purchased through our sales team." Orbs launched globally 14 July 2025 (https://discord.com/press-releases/discord-launches-orbs-globally), described by SVP Product Peter Sellis: "By rewarding players with something they value, Discord is giving brands the ability to create an advertising experience that feels like gameplay." Discord Orbs FAQ (https://support.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/30593690165783-Discord-Orbs-FAQ, edited 26 June 2026), verbatim: "Orbs can only be used within Discord–they cannot be converted to real money." and "Orbs are attached to your account and cannot be shared or transferred between users." Discord's 29 Aug 2025 policy update (https://discord.com/safety/important-policy-updates): "We're thrilled that users and advertisers have embraced Quests, our rewards-based advertising format."
  - *Implication:* ClusterGG's pitch — pay gamers for engaging with brand creatives on Discord — is Discord's own strategic growth line, not an unserved niche. Discord's incentive to tolerate a third party arbitraging its inventory at a $5 CPM is negative and falling. Worse, Orbs is a control experiment that already answered ClusterGG's A3: Discord built the identical reward loop at 90M DAU scale and chose explicitly NOT to make the currency cash-convertible. That is either a compliance judgement (regulated-activity risk, their A8) or a unit-economics judgement — and either way it is the platform, with the data, deciding against ClusterGG's central design choice. Note also Discord does not publish a Quests rate card, so ClusterGG's "$5 CPM is the market rate" has no primary comparable from the platform it is priced against.
- **[SEVERE]** Discord's global REST rate limit puts a hard mathematical ceiling on Discord-delivered ad views that sits BELOW the top rung of ClusterGG's own scaling ladder — regardless of how many gamers they acquire.
  - *Evidence:* Discord support article "My Bot is Being Rate Limited!" (https://support-dev.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/6223003921559-My-Bot-is-Being-Rate-Limited, edited 14 July 2025), verbatim: "Global Rate Limits — Limit: 50 requests per second across most endpoints. Scope: Applies to your entire application." Also "Invalid Request Limits — Limit: 10,000 invalid requests per 10 minutes... Result: Temporary Cloudflare ban." Sharding is described as a gateway/websocket measure, not a way to raise the REST global limit: "sharding must be enabled at 2,500+ guilds". ClusterGG's own code acknowledges the exposure: lib/discord/post-queue.ts header states "parallelising would trade a slow failure for a temporary ban, which is worse."
  - *Implication:* 50 req/s x 86,400s = 4.32M Discord messages/day = ~130M/month, application-wide, forever, shared with every command response, announcement and interaction the bot makes. COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md's 1,000,000-gamer rung claims 450M views/month. Even if 100% of Discord's rate budget were spent on nothing but ad cards, Discord could supply at most ~29% of that. Realistically under 10%. The claim "Cost scales linearly with revenue — the ratio is the same at every size. Scale is not the risk" is false on Discord's own published limits: the Discord-side supply curve goes flat somewhere around the 100,000-gamer rung, while CP cost keeps scaling linearly with gamers. Margin inverts at scale, and it inverts for a reason that no amount of funding fixes.
- **[SEVERE]** Discord's Monetized Content Policy separately forbids monetizing content whose primary purpose is third-party promotion, and the App Directory content rules exclude "gambling-adjacent or addictive behavior" — closing off both the native monetization rails and the free distribution channel.
  - *Evidence:* Discord Monetization Policy (https://support.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/10575066024983-Monetization-Policy, effective 5 May 2024, last updated 1 March 2024), Monetized Content Policy item 10, verbatim: "You May Not Monetize Content Associated With Any Of The Following: ...10. Primary purpose of marketing, advertising, or promotional benefit of the app or of a third party, such as: Third party servers, Applications, Businesses, or services." Item 11 bans "Facilitation of gambling or gambling-adjacent activities"; item 15 bans "Facilitation of financial asset transfers". App Directory: App Content Requirements Policy (https://support-dev.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/9489299950487, edited 9 Oct 2025), verbatim: to be eligible for discovery "Your application's content must not contain any age-restricted content. Age-Restricted content includes, but is not limited to, content and discussion related to: ...Gambling-adjacent or addictive behavior". Discord's Ads Policy (https://support.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/25516720403223-Ads-Policy, edited 2 March 2026) states "This policy is mandatory and applies to all native and third party ads on Discord including ads in Activities" and prohibits promoting "Games or apps played for money" and "Pay to play games of chance with prizes of real-world value". Since October 7 2024 the Developer Policy's Monetization Requirements force any paid app feature through Discord's Premium Apps at price parity, with a 30% platform fee (15% growth tier on the first $1M) (https://support-dev.discord.com/hc/en-us/articles/23810643331735-Premium-Apps-Required-Support-for-Monetizing-Apps, edited 4 Oct 2025).
  - *Implication:* Three doors shut at once. (a) The server-owner revenue share on sponsored competitions cannot run on Discord's native monetization rails — item 10 is a direct description of the product. (b) A points-for-cash redemption ladder with a 100-day grind to a $5 trophy reads as "gambling-adjacent or addictive behavior" to a reviewer, which makes the app ineligible for the App Directory — removing the free organic distribution the 1,000-to-1,000,000 gamer ladder implicitly assumes and forcing paid acquisition into a model that has no CAC line. (c) If Discord ever rules that trophies are a paid app capability, the 30%/15% Premium Apps fee lands on top of a model whose claimed margin is 50%.
- **[SEVERE]** Discord can terminate with no notice, automatically, and the contract explicitly says so — and there is real 2025 precedent for mass-terminating an app with 30M users overnight.
  - *Evidence:* Developer Terms of Service Section 9(a), verbatim: "Discord reserves the right to terminate the Terms with you, or discontinue, suspend, or limit the APIs or any portion or feature or your access thereto, if there is an Enforcement Reason or at our convenience upon notice to you... remove, suspend, or limit your Application or App Content; remove any approval we have provided for your Application through App Review... require you to stop using and delete API Data; terminate, suspend, or limit your user or developer account... Enforcement actions and reviews may be automated or manual. While we may take such actions with or without notice to you, including while we investigate..." Section 9(b) requires deleting all cached API Data on termination. "Enforcement Reasons" is defined broadly, including where Discord believes the app "is negatively impacting us, the APIs or other Discord services, or our users". PRECEDENTS: (1) Shapes, Inc. — permanently terminated by Discord on 2 May 2025 along with 100,000+ user-hosted bots, reportedly 30M users, over Developer ToS violations; the developer publicly disputed the grounds and it changed nothing (https://alon-alush.github.io/ai%20world/shapesinctermination/; https://techissuestoday.com/shapes-inc-removed-from-discord-reactions/, May 2025). (2) Dank Memer removed all gambling commands because, per the developer's own post, Discord "started to limit the potential growth and monetization of bots who have any form of gambling commands" and they had to comply "in order to continue existing on the Discord platform in any meaningful capacity" (https://dankmemer.lol/blogs/gambling-removal, Dec 2022 — STALE, flagged, but it is the clearest documented instance of Discord throttling a bot's growth and monetization over a category rather than a specific violation). (3) Groovy (30 Aug 2021) and Rythm (15 Sept 2021), the two largest Discord bots of their era at 16M and 20M+ servers, both killed by cease-and-desist from an upstream platform (YouTube) — explicitly because commercialization violated the upstream ToS (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rythm_(software); https://9to5google.com/2021/09/13/youtube-nukes-popular-groovy-bot-discord-music-service-for-breaking-platforms-tos/ — STALE 2021, cited only as the canonical platform-dependency mortality case).
  - *Implication:* Enforcement is not a negotiation and is not preceded by warning. The Shapes precedent shows Discord will terminate an app 60x larger than ClusterGG's 1,000-gamer rung, at 24 hours' effective notice, with no appeal that mattered. Section 9(b) additionally compels deletion of all cached Discord API Data — so the identity graph, guild membership and audience segmentation that the ad product is priced on must be destroyed, not merely stop updating. There is no wind-down period to migrate users during.
- **[SEVERE]** There is effectively no diversification away from Discord. Discord is the identity layer, the distribution channel, the revenue-share partner, and half the ad inventory — and the code is written Discord-first by deliberate design.
  - *Evidence:* components/OAuthButtons.tsx, verbatim comment: "Discord-first third-party sign-in. Discord always leads (the universal gamer identity)... Always surface Discord as the primary identity, even before it's configured (the route shows a friendly message), so the brand is present everywhere." docs/BUSINESS.md line 3: "Cluster sells one thing: access to gamers inside Discord communities, bought by brands." Distribution is bot installs (`bot_added` is a paid quest action). Server owners — the channel partners — are Discord server owners taking a revenue share on Discord competitions. lib/discord/ contains 30 modules including a 1,941-line screen renderer. The web surfaces (feed, quests, leaderboards, marketplace, profile) exist and do carry ad rails, so post-ban the ad inventory does not go to literally zero — but user ACQUISITION does.
  - *Implication:* On the day Discord acts: acquisition goes to zero (no bot, no installs, no server-owner channel); the server-owner revenue-share business ceases to exist entirely; roughly half the promised ad inventory ("a Discord card OR a web page") disappears while 100% of the CP liability remains, since gamers keep their balances; and Section 9(b) forces deletion of the Discord-derived data. Every brand contract sold on a promised view count becomes immediately undeliverable, converting the ad book into a refund liability. The surviving web product is a gaming stats site with no distribution and an unfunded points liability. This is not a business with a Discord channel; it is a Discord business with a website.
- **[MATERIAL]** Discord's data clauses independently prohibit routing Discord-derived data anywhere near an ad system — which is what an ad server with audience segmentation does by definition.
  - *Evidence:* Developer Policy clause 17, verbatim: "Do not disclose API Data to data brokers, advertising networks or services, or any other monetization-related service." Clause 18: "Do not sell, license, or otherwise commercialize API Data or any of Discord's services (as defined in our Terms of Service, e.g., Nitro subscriptions)." Clause 15: "Do not use API Data for any purpose outside of what is necessary to provide your stated functionality." Clause 16 forbids using API Data "to profile Discord users, their identities, or their relationships with other users". Developer ToS Section 5(b): "You will not share API Data with any third party, except... (i) with a Service Provider; (ii) to the extent required under applicable laws or regulations; and (iii) when a user of your Application expressly directs you to share their API Data with the third party (and you will provide us proof thereof upon request)." In ClusterGG's favour on the narrow point: current ad targeting is thin — lib/ads.ts:55,74 targets only on `targetDevice` ("both"/device), not on Discord-derived attributes.
  - *Implication:* Today's exposure here is moderate because the ad server barely targets. But it is a growth trap: every step that raises CPM — audience segmentation, guild-level targeting, brand-safety reporting, sharing delivery data with an advertiser's measurement partner (which is exactly what Discord itself does via AppsFlyer/Gamesight, and which brands will demand) — moves Discord API Data toward "advertising networks or services" and toward a third party without per-user express direction plus proof. The $5 CPM floor is defended by targeting quality; the terms forbid building it. Any programmatic fill at the 1M-gamer rung (their own stated plan) requires passing data to an ad exchange, which is clause 17 verbatim.
- **[MATERIAL]** The Riot integration is also non-compliant on the cheap, fixable items — which is diligence-relevant because it signals the terms were never read, not that they were read and a risk was taken.
  - *Evidence:* Riot General Policies (edited 29 May 2025) require: "All products must be registered in, and audited by Riot Games through the Developer Portal"; "Any new features or changes to a product must be audited through the product's page in the Developer Portal"; and "You must post the following legal boilerplate to your product in a location that is readily visible to players: [Your product] isn't endorsed by Riot Games and doesn't reflect the views or opinions of Riot Games...". A repo-wide grep for "isn't endorsed by Riot" / "not endorsed by Riot" across .ts, .tsx and .md returns zero matches. The repo contains no evidence of a Developer Portal product registration; lib/providers/riot-verify.ts:7-8 states the opposite (dev key, no approved registration). Riot's General Policies also warn: "Failure to meet these policies may result in either suspension or cancellation of your API access or legal recourse."
  - *Implication:* Three zero-cost compliance items are unmet: the mandatory disclaimer, product registration, and audit of new features. Each is a day of work. That they are unmet on a product that has already shipped a paid economy on top of Riot data is the tell — it means nobody in the company has done the exercise this brief just did, and the investor should assume the same is true of the FTC/CFPB/state-sweepstakes questions in A8.
- **[MATERIAL]** Discord has never, to my knowledge, published an enforcement action against a bot specifically for serving paid third-party display advertising — which cuts BOTH ways and should not be read as comfort.
  - *Evidence:* I searched extensively (Discord press releases, support and developer help centres, transparency hub, trade press, developer community reports) and could NOT find a single documented case of Discord removing, banning, or de-verifying an app specifically for carrying third-party ads in bot messages. Discord's published Transparency Reports (https://discord.com/safety-transparency; most recent detailed PDF I could retrieve is Jan-Jun 2024) break out accounts and servers, not apps-by-developer-policy-clause. The precedents that DO exist (Shapes.inc, May 2025; Dank Memer gambling, Dec 2022) were data-misuse and content-category actions respectively.
  - *Implication:* The absence of precedent means ClusterGG cannot point to a survivor either — there is no known third-party ad network operating at scale inside Discord bots, which is itself evidence that this is not an overlooked opportunity but a category nobody has been allowed to build. The base rate for enforcement is unmeasurable, but the correlation between "visible enough to be worth enforcing against" and "large enough to be worth investing in" is 1.0. Enforcement risk rises precisely as the investment thesis works.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** Chess.com is the one clean integration, and Riot explicitly permits advertising on a developer's own website — so the ad model is not universally illegitimate. The problem is specifically Discord's surface and Riot's contest rule.
  - *Evidence:* Chess.com Published Data API is a public read-only API over data already public to logged-out visitors, with no stated commercial-use prohibition and unlimited serial access rate (https://support.chess.com/en/articles/9650547-published-data-api, updated 22 Apr 2026); Chess.com's User Agreement (https://www.chess.com/legal/user-agreement, last updated 25 March 2026) notes that user game activity records are publicly available and "can be accessed, used, and monetized by anyone for any number of purposes", while separately prohibiting scraping and granting only a "personal, noncommercial use only" licence over site content — the sanctioned route is the PubAPI, which is what ClusterGG uses (lib/providers/adapters.ts:52,58 hit api.chess.com/pub/player/...). Chess.com directs edge cases to legal@chess.com. On Riot: General Policies (29 May 2025) state "You must have a free tier of access for players, which may include advertising", and the API T&C say "For the sake of clarity, You may advertise through such software applications and/or websites without asking for Riot's permission, provided You are otherwise complying with these API Terms." Riot separately banned third-party ads inside Riot properties (in-game, loading screens, Riot Client) — https://x.com/RiotGamesDevRel/status/1928141776415568329, May 2025 — which does not reach ClusterGG's surfaces.
  - *Implication:* An advertising-funded gaming stats site on clustergg.com, with a free tier, is permissible under both Riot's and Chess.com's terms. That is the one durable asset here and it should be priced as the whole company, not as an accessory. It is not, however, a $2.25M/month business — it is a website with no distribution channel, because the distribution channel was the thing that breached the terms.
  - *Could not source:* 1) Any documented Discord enforcement action against a bot specifically for carrying paid third-party display advertising in its messages. I searched Discord press releases, both help centres, the transparency hub, trade press and developer-community reports and found zero cases. I am asserting the clause-level breach from the policy text, not from precedent. 2) Discord's Quests/Video Quests rate card or effective CPM. Discord publishes none ("Discord Ads can only be purchased through our sales 


#### B2 · The $5 CPM under attack (13)

- **[FATAL]** The model is negative-margin on ClusterGG's OWN assumptions before any external CPM opinion is applied: at their designed 15 views/gamer/day and their own assumed 56% fill rate, break-even CPM is $5.95 — above the $5.00 they sell at and 49% above their $4.00 'hard floor'.
  - *Evidence:* Their equation is views/gamer/day = 50 / CPM (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.3). Their cost is 500 CP = $0.05/gamer/day. Their design point is 15 screens (§2), so cost per screen = 500/15 = 33.3 CP = $0.00333, i.e. a $3.33 CPM COST at 100% fill — a 33% gross margin at a $5 sell, not the claimed 50%. But assumption A5 states fill rate is only >56%, and the doc itself says unsold inventory shows a house creative at 'zero revenue, full CP cost'. Effective revenue CPM = $5.00 x 0.56 = $2.80 against a $3.33 CPM cost. Required gross CPM to break even at 15 views and 56% fill = 50/(15x0.56) = $5.95. At their stated 10-view minimum and 56% fill it is $8.93. The '50% margin' claim silently assumes 100% fill while A5 assumes 56%; the two assumptions are never multiplied together anywhere in the document.
  - *Implication:* Gross margin at their own stated inputs is NEGATIVE 16% ($2.80 effective revenue vs $3.33 cost per 1,000 screens). To reach the claimed 50% margin they need either 100% fill (impossible — they concede it) or a $6.67 CPM (33% above their sell price). This is arithmetic, not opinion, and it fails before a single external benchmark is introduced.
- **[FATAL]** ClusterGG does not produce an IAB/MRC-countable impression on any surface. The Discord bot writes ONE 'impression' per SERVER when it posts a message, and the card renderer writes one server-side at PNG-generation time. Both are server-initiated counts, which the IAB/MRC standard explicitly rejects.
  - *Evidence:* lib/discord/ads.ts:80-84 inserts exactly one row into schema.adImpressions per guild, keyed pagePath `discord:${guildId}`, immediately after postMessage() returns ok — one row whether the server has 12 members or 120,000, and whether or not a single human opens the channel. lib/cards/ads.ts:201-213 (logCardAdImpression) inserts an impression fire-and-forget when the PNG is composited server-side, before Discord has delivered it. The web beacon (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:19-38) accepts unauthenticated POSTs with an arbitrary campaignCreativeId, no rate limit, no dedup. Against the standard: IAB and MRC 'both recommended measuring desktop and mobile display impressions on a count-on-begin-to-render basis... a buyer's ad must load and begin to render before an impression is counted as valid' — Microsoft/Xandr Industry Reference, Impression Counting, updated 2025-10-22 (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/xandr/industry-reference/impression-counting). Primary standard: MRC Desktop Display Impression Measurement Guidelines v1.1 (https://mediaratingcouncil.org/sites/default/files/Standards/Desktop-Display-Impression-Measurement-Guidelines-US%20(MMTF%20Final%20v1.1).pdf) — I could not extract its text (PDF is image-based and no PDF tooling would install in this environment); I am relying on the Xandr restatement, which is dated Oct 2025.
  - *Implication:* The unit being sold does not exist. A 'view' in the $500/100,000-views package is, on the Discord surface, a message-post event, not a human. A buyer's ad server will report zero impressions against ClusterGG's 100,000 because there is nothing for it to report. Every discrepancy reconciliation on every IO will show a 100% gap. This is not a CPM discount — it is a reason a buyer cannot write the IO at all. Correspondingly, the '450,000 views/month at 1,000 gamers' figure in the scaling ladder is not measuring what the doc says it measures.
- **[FATAL]** The 15-screen guarantee is not merely unimplemented — the shipped catalogue lets a gamer reach 425-525 CP/day with ZERO screens opened, and the three actions all four daily missions depend on most heavily are never awarded anywhere in the codebase.
  - *Evidence:* Summing only actions in lib/quests.ts that (a) exist as awardQuestAction call sites and (b) are triggered by someone or something OTHER than the earner: profile_views_25 25x3=75 (lib/identity.ts:179), follower_gained 25x2=50 (app/actions/social.ts:83), profile_vote_received 25x2=50 (lib/identity.ts:78,124), gift_received 25x2=50 (lib/marketplace.ts:275), finish_challenge 25x2=50 (lib/sync.ts:203, cron), top3_challenge 50x1=50 and win_challenge 100x1=100 (lib/sync.ts:204-205, cron) = 425 CP, plus best_profile_award 100 = 525. Separately, `grep -rn 'awardQuestAction(.*(stat_levelup|play_session|challenge_progress)'` over the whole repo returns ZERO call sites; those three keys appear only in lib/quests.ts (the catalogue) and lib/missions.ts (the templates). They are worth 100+75+100 CP in mission template A (lib/missions.ts:56-59) and can never be earned. Meanwhile ad_impression pays 1 CP (lib/quests.ts:117) — 25x worse than every other action in the catalogue — so a CP-maximising gamer rationally ignores it.
  - *Implication:* Delivered ad screens per gamer per day is a free variable between 0 and ~28, not a floor of 15. Underwriting must use the adversarial minimum: 425 passive CP + 3 ad_clicks (75 CP, lib/quests.ts:118) = 500 CP for 3 screens. At 3 screens/gamer/day, break-even CPM is 50/3 = $16.67 at 100% fill and $29.76 at 56% fill — i.e. above Discord's own premium video rate card. There is no CPM at which the business closes on that path.
- **[FATAL]** My underwritten CPM for this inventory is $0.50 (point estimate), range $0.25-$2.00, with $2.00 reserved for a founder-to-founder endemic gaming deal with zero verification demands. I would not underwrite $5.00, and I would not underwrite the $4.00 floor.
  - *Evidence:* Build-up from comparables. CEILING — Discord's own inventory: Game Quests $100K-$150K minimum at an implied $24-30 CPM, Video Quests $150K minimum at $22-28 CPM, 96% median video completion rate (Discord internal data, Dec 2025), per a Discord Quests rate card summary at https://mythos.one/me/brianswichkow/5291b2 (SECONDARY — I could not obtain a Discord-published price; discord.com/ads/quests discloses no rates). That is what Discord attention is worth when it is opt-in, full-screen, 100% viewable video with 1P data and a NON-CASH cosmetic reward. ClusterGG has none of those attributes. NEAREST FORMAT ANALOGUE — Twitch stream overlay/banner (static, endemic, gaming): $1-5 CPM on CCV; programmatic overlay/display $4-10 (https://beta-ads.no/blog/twitch-cpm-media-buying-guide-2026, 2026). VERTICAL — Playwire's 2026 State of Publisher Ad Revenue (113bn impressions, published ~5 May 2026, https://www.playwire.com/blog/publisher-revenue-optimization-by-vertical-how-to-maximize-ad-revenue-in-your-vertical): gaming is the LOW-CPM/high-volume vertical, sports CPMs run 64% ABOVE the gaming average, gaming RPS index 61 vs technology 100. MARKET FLOOR — ANA Q2 2025 Programmatic Transparency Benchmark (11 Aug 2025): open-marketplace CPM $4.41, PMP $7.15 (https://www.ana.net/content/show/id/pr-2025-08-programmatictrans, retrieved via https://www.fiducia.eco/post/the-ana-releases-q2-2025-programmatic-transparency-benchmark-report-reveals-26-8b-in-wasted-program) — but those are ads.txt-authorised, IVT-filtered, viewability-measured impressions. Long-tail remnant sits 'below $1' and open-exchange inventory below $1.50 CPM is characterised as 'almost always low-quality' (https://www.stackmatix.com/blog/programmatic-advertising-costs, 2026 — SECONDARY). ClusterGG's inventory is strictly worse than long-tail remnant on every axis a buyer prices: not countable, not viewable, not taggable, not verifiable, disclosed-incentivised, and paid-to-click. CORROBORATION FROM WITHIN — the repo's own test suite fixes FLOOR_CPM = 0.50 (tests/db/cp-economics.mts:68), which is exactly my point estimate.
  - *Implication:* At $0.50 CPM the $500/month package prices at 1,000,000 views, not 100,000 — ten times the inventory for the same money. Revenue at the 1,000-gamer rung falls from the claimed $2,250/month of inventory to roughly $225/month against $1,500/month of CP liability. Every rung of the scaling ladder divides by ten: 10,000 gamers = $2,250 not $22,500; 100,000 gamers = $22,500 not $225,000; 1,000,000 gamers = $225,000 not $2,250,000 — and the last rung is unreachable anyway because its programmatic fill is policy-barred.
- **[FATAL]** THE KILLER ARITHMETIC — at every CPM I would underwrite, the required ad screens per gamer per day exceed what the best gaming publishers in the world achieve per SESSION, on a site where the user is not being paid.
  - *Evidence:* Break-even views/gamer/day = 50/CPM on the CP cost alone ($0.05/gamer/day), before servers, salaries, Discord infrastructure, the server-owner revenue share, or any cost of sales. Table (100% fill / at their own A5 assumption of 56% fill): $8.00 -> 6.2 / 11.2. $5.95 -> 8.4 / 15.0. $5.00 -> 10.0 / 17.9. $4.00 -> 12.5 / 22.3. $3.33 -> 15.0 / 26.8. $3.00 -> 16.7 / 29.8. $2.00 -> 25.0 / 44.6. $1.00 -> 50.0 / 89.3. $0.50 -> 100.0 / 178.6. $0.25 -> 200.0 / 357.1. PLAUSIBILITY CEILING: Playwire's 2026 report finds TOP-PERFORMING gaming publishers average 14.4 impressions per SESSION (https://www.playwire.com/blog/publisher-revenue-optimization-by-vertical-how-to-maximize-ad-revenue-in-your-vertical, May 2026) — on an auto-refreshing browsing site, with no payment to the user, and that is the top of the distribution. ClusterGG requires every screen to be a deliberate user action inside Discord.
