So ~15/gamer/day is already AT the industry ceiling; 25 is implausible; 45 requires the gamer to open a ClusterGG screen every 20 waking minutes; 100+ and 179 are physically impossible and would in any case make the bot a spam engine that Discord removes.
  - *Implication:* The business is arithmetically impossible below approximately $3.33 CPM at 100% fill, and below approximately $5.95 CPM at their own 56% fill assumption. My underwritten range tops out at $2.00 and centres on $0.50. There is therefore NO overlap between the CPM the market will pay and the CPM the cost structure requires — the gap is 3x at the most generous end ($2.00 paid vs $5.95 required) and 12x at the point estimate. Execution cannot close a 12x arithmetic gap. The only fixes are structural: cut the 500 CP ceiling by roughly 10x (which the doc says would kill the product), or abandon CPM display for a CPA/offerwall model.
- **[SEVERE]** The repository contains four mutually contradictory CPMs spanning 16x, and the ad_campaigns table has no CPM field and no views-target field — so the '$500 for 100,000 views, hard floor $4.00, the admin must refuse to save below it' product cannot be represented in the database at all.
  - *Evidence:* tests/db/cp-economics.mts:68 hardcodes `const FLOOR_CPM = 0.50;` and asserts CP-per-1,000-impressions is under it 'by at least 5x'. docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.2 claims a $4.00 hard floor and a $5.00 sell price. lib/pricing.ts:102 sets `benchmarkCpm: 8` — the rate used to compute the media value and ROAS shown to paying brands. Schema check: `adCampaigns` (lib/db/schema.ts) has only `budget: doublePrecision`, `startDate`, `endDate`, `targetGeo`, `targetDevice`, `status`. There is no cpm, no rate, no viewsTarget, no delivered counter. A repo-wide grep for cpmFloor/minCpm/floorCpm returns nothing. There is no code that could refuse a below-floor save because there is no price field to save.
  - *Implication:* The commercial product described in the model document does not exist as a data model. Any diligence question of the form 'show me the campaign where a brand bought 100,000 views at $5' cannot be answered from this schema. The engineering team's own working assumption ($0.50 floor CPM, committed to the test suite) is 10x below the document's sell price and 8x below its floor — the people who wrote the code do not believe the number in the pitch.
- **[SEVERE]** The brand-facing ROAS report values a campaign at total Discord SERVER MEMBERSHIP x $8 CPM — not at delivered impressions. This is a fabricated media-value claim shown to a paying advertiser.
  - *Evidence:* lib/brand-report.ts:105-115: `mediaValue(reachMembers, clicks, cfg, entrants)` returns `(reachMembers / 1000) * cfg.benchmarkCpm + clicks * cfg.benchmarkCpc + entrants * cfg.benchmarkCpe`, with cfg.benchmarkCpm = 8 (lib/pricing.ts:102). `reachMembers` is server headcount, not viewers. lib/pricing.ts:102 also carries `impressionsPerMember: 12`, used on the public pricing page to project delivery. roasOf() (lib/brand-report.ts:117) divides that number by spend and app/api/brands/report/route.ts:73 prints 'Media value and ROAS priced at $8 CPM' into the brand's CSV export. So a bot post into a 50,000-member server is reported to the brand as $400 of media value, from one message, with zero evidence any member saw it.
  - *Implication:* Every ROAS figure ClusterGG has ever shown a brand is computed from a headcount the brand never reached, at a CPM 60% above the price the brand paid — which mathematically guarantees ROAS > 1 by construction (the exact failure mode the code comment at lib/brand-report.ts:100-104 says it is avoiding). Beyond commercial credibility, unsubstantiated performance claims to a paying advertiser are an FTC Section 5 substantiation exposure, and they are the single most likely trigger for a refund demand and a public post-mortem from the first brand that runs its own measurement.
- **[SEVERE]** Paying 25 CP per ad click with all four daily missions REQUIRING 3 clicks makes ClusterGG permanently ineligible for Google's publisher demand — which removes the programmatic backfill that the 1,000,000-gamer rung of the scaling ladder explicitly depends on.
  - *Evidence:* Google AdSense Program Policies (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/48182): 'Except for rewarded inventory, publishers may not ask others to click or view their ads or use deceptive implementation methods to obtain clicks or views.' Google's invalid-traffic definition (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/16737 and the identical AdMob text at https://support.google.com/admob/answer/3342054) lists as IVT: 'Publishers encouraging clicks on their ads (examples may include: any language encouraging users to click on ads...)', with consequences stated as 'account suspension or disablement' and 'ad serving limitations or complete disablement if traffic quality cannot be verified'. Google's rewarded-ad exception (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/9121589) permits rewarding a VIEW under disclosure and skippability rules and states rewarded ads 'must not oblige users to interact' — it does not permit rewarding a CLICK. ClusterGG pays ad_click 25 CP, cap 3/day (lib/quests.ts:118) and all four shipped mission templates require ad_click x3 (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106). Third-party corroboration that the largest DSPs treat incentivised traffic as invalid: deepsee.io, 'Rewarded Traffic', last updated 7 Nov 2024 (https://deepsee.io/blog/rewarded-traffic-incentivized-traffic-in-a-top-hat) — 'growing sentiment by the largest DSPs that this traffic is invalid, with both Google and The Trade Desk making fairly unambiguous statements'; note the MRC itself does not classify incentivised traffic as invalid, so this is a buyer-policy exclusion, not a standards exclusion.
  - *Implication:* The ladder's top rung ('~100 brands + programmatic fill') has no demand source. Google Ad Manager, AdSense and AdMob — which together are the default backfill for essentially every small publisher — are closed to this implementation by written policy, not by discretion. That means the fill-rate assumption A5 (>56%) has to be met entirely by hand-sold direct deals at every scale, which is the opposite of 'cost scales linearly with revenue'. Direct sales headcount scales with brand count, not with gamer count.
- **[SEVERE]** The reward structure manufactures a ~12% click-through rate — roughly 120-240x a normal display CTR — which is the single clearest fraud signature a verification vendor screens for, and it will terminate campaigns rather than merely reprice them.
  - *Evidence:* ad_click pays 25 CP capped at 3/day; ad_impression pays 1 CP capped at 25/day (lib/quests.ts:117-118). A click is therefore worth 25 impressions to the gamer, so a rational CP-maximiser clicks every ad up to the cap. Ceiling behaviour: 3 clicks / 25 impressions = 12.0% CTR. Benchmark display CTR sits around 0.05-0.10%. The model document itself already concedes the point — it states ad_click should drop to 0 CP because paying for clicks 'manufactures a ~10% click rate ... the signature of incentivised fraud' — and the code has not been changed. Note this is not an inference about intent: 75 of the 500 daily CP (15%) is paid for ad clicks by design in every shipped mission template.
  - *Implication:* A brand running a landing-page analytics check (not even DV/IAS — just Google Analytics) sees 12% CTR with a ~0% conversion rate and a near-100% bounce. The commercial outcome is a clawback and a terminated IO, not a lower CPM. This also poisons the case-study path: the 'results from the first cohort' that the model document says will LIFT the CPM will in fact be the evidence that destroys it.
- **[SEVERE]** A brand structurally cannot traffic its own ad tag into this inventory. The Discord creative is flattened into a server-rendered PNG and clickUrl is a bare stored string with no macro, cachebuster or click-ID support — so no third-party ad server, no frequency cap, no verification, no viewability.
  - *Evidence:* lib/cards/ads.ts composites the creative into the card PNG (video creatives are explicitly excluded at line ~107 because they 'can't be drawn into a PNG'). lib/discord/ads.ts:60-69 posts an embed with `image: { url: creative.fileUrl }` and a Discord link button pointing at `creative.clickUrl` verbatim. Schema: adCreatives has `fileUrl`, `clickUrl`, `ctaLabel` and nothing else — no third-party tag field, no impression-tracker field, no macro substitution anywhere (grep for clickUrl shows it passed through untransformed at lib/discord/ads.ts:67, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:28, lib/ads.ts:98). Against the buying standard: GroupM's global viewability standard requires 100% of a display ad's pixels in view for at least one second, verified through DoubleVerify / IAS / Moat (https://www.wppmedia.com/news/expanded-viewability-standards-2017 — announced Aug 2017; STALE as a document, but it is the last public restatement of that standard and it remains the reference point cited in trade coverage). Even Discord's own ad product markets Video Quests as a 'premium 100%-viewable video format' (https://discord.com/ads/quests, accessed Aug 2026) and has bolted on three third-party measurement partners — AppsFlyer, Kantar and Gamesight — precisely because, per Digiday (2 Oct 2025), 'advertisers don't like platforms grading their own homework' (https://digiday.com/media/discord-puts-proof-behind-its-ad-pitch-with-first-measurement-push/).
  - *Implication:* This is a workflow exclusion, not a price. An agency trafficking team cannot upload this to their ad server, cannot cap frequency, cannot verify, cannot pixel it. Anyone with a trafficking process will decline the buy at any CPM. I could not source a published percentage discount for non-MRC-accredited, non-viewability-measured, non-taggable inventory — no such benchmark is published, because the category is normally excluded rather than repriced. I am stating that absence explicitly rather than inventing a number.
- **[SEVERE]** $500/month is far below the threshold at which any media agency will do the paperwork — and above that threshold, every buyer demands measurement ClusterGG structurally cannot supply. There is no spend band in which a professional buyer can transact this inventory.
  - *Evidence:* Published minimums for salesperson-served, IO-based buying: Reddit's managed advertising requires a $30,000 minimum QUARTERLY budget (=$10,000/month), while its self-serve auction minimum is $5 (https://www.stackmatix.com/blog/reddit-ads-minimum-spend). Discord's own Quests minimum is $100,000-$150,000 per campaign (rate card summary, https://mythos.one/me/brianswichkow/5291b2). Gaming marketing agencies quote $5,000-$15,000/month for indie-studio retainers and charge 15-30% of spend (https://eakdigital.com/top-7-gaming-marketing-agencies-viral-launches-2026/, 2026). At 15-30%, a $500/month IO yields the agency $75-$150/month — under one hour of a planner's fully-loaded time, before the RFP, plan, trafficking, QA, reporting and reconciliation. On the buyer's own side, APQC benchmarks the cost to process a single purchase order at a median of about $100 (range $35.88-$506.52), with invoice processing at $10-$20 each (https://blog.hyperbots.com/what-s-the-cost-to-process-a-purchase-order-benchmarks-and-calculator; https://www.apqc.org/resource-library/resource-listing/total-cost-perform-procurement-process-group-purchase-order). Twelve monthly invoices against a $6,000 annual contract cost the buyer $120-$240 just to pay, plus the PO, plus vendor onboarding through legal, security and finance.
  - *Implication:* ClusterGG's $500/month sits at 5% of Reddit's smallest managed budget and 0.3-0.5% of Discord's minimum. No agency will transact it; the actual buyer is a founder or solo marketing lead at a small gaming brand paying by card — which is the self-serve SMB segment, and that segment benchmarks against Meta and Google, where the same $500 buys measurable, attributed, ROAS-reported clicks with no minimum and no vendor onboarding. Worse, the ladder's own escape route is a trap: the 100,000-gamer rung assumes 30 brands at $7,500/month ($90,000/year each), which IS above agency thresholds — and at $90K/year every one of those brands has an agency that will demand IAS/DV tags, MRC-countable impressions and a viewability guarantee, none of which this architecture can provide. Below ~$10K/month nobody will do the paperwork; above it nobody will accept the measurement. The ladder has no rung that a professional buyer can stand on.
- **[MATERIAL]** The comparables table in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.4 is wrong in the direction that flatters the company: gaming is the LOW-CPM vertical, and newsletter/influencer CPMs are not comparable units.
  - *Evidence:* The doc places 'Discord / community sponsorships' at $5-15 CPM as a band ClusterGG belongs in. Against that: (a) Playwire (May 2026) finds sports CPMs run 64% above the gaming average and classifies gaming, entertainment and education as 'inventory volume businesses' versus sports, news and technology as 'audience quality businesses' — gaming is explicitly the cheap end. (b) Real Discord community sponsorships are transacted as FLAT FEES per placement, not CPM: $200-$2,000 per sponsored announcement post, $1,500-$15,000/month for a sponsored channel, roughly EUR 1-3 per active member per placement (https://blog.communityone.io/discord-monetization-guide-2026/ and https://orbithq.eu/blog/how-to-actually-make-money-from-your-discord-server — both SECONDARY, no primary rate card exists publicly). (c) The doc's newsletter comparable at $20-50 CPM is real ($10-75 range, https://www.paved.com/blog/newsletter-sponsorship-rates/ and https://www.beehiiv.com/blog/newsletter-sponsorship-cost) but is priced per identified, opted-in SUBSCRIBER with open and click reporting — the opposite of an anonymous server-side impression count.
  - *Implication:* Two consequences. First, the $5 'floor of the community band' is a floor of a band ClusterGG is not in. Second, the correct commercial unit for the Discord bot-post surface is a flat sponsored-post fee ($200-$2,000/placement), not a CPM — which means the bot surface should be sold as sponsorship and the model document's entire CPM framing applies, at best, only to the web surface. Repricing the bot surface as sponsorship is a defensible business; it is just not the one being pitched, and it does not scale with gamer count.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** FAVOURABLE — a paid-to-engage audience is genuinely valuable, but on a CPA/offerwall model, not a CPM display model. Offerwall eCPMs run two to three orders of magnitude above what ClusterGG can charge for banners.
  - *Evidence:* Offerwall eCPMs are reported at $400-$530 in 2026, with some formats reaching $400-$1,500, against rewarded video at $16-$20 and standard display in the low single digits (https://torox.io/blog/offerwall-ecpm-benchmarks-by-vertical-2026-data-report/ and https://maf.ad/en/blog/rewarded-video-ads-vs-offerwalls/ — SECONDARY, vendor-published, treat as directional). Offerwall inventory is paid on completed ACTIONS, so the advertiser is buying an outcome and the incentivisation is disclosed and priced in rather than concealed — which is exactly why Google's rewarded-ad exception permits it (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/9121589) while prohibiting incentivised clicks on standard units. ClusterGG already has the hard parts: verified game-account linkage, a Discord distribution surface, and users who have demonstrated willingness to complete tasks for cents.
  - *Implication:* The pivot exists and it is one document, not one rebuild: stop selling impressions, sell verified completed actions (sponsored-challenge entrants, account links, game installs, trial starts) at CPA. The repo already prices an entrant at benchmarkCpe = $3.50 (lib/pricing.ts:102) — a single genuine entrant at $3.50 is worth 70 days of one gamer's full CP ceiling. Note this makes the CPM question moot, but it also invalidates the entire COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md: the scaling ladder, the break-even equation, the 15-screen guarantee and the $5 CPM all disappear, and the company becomes an affiliate/CPA network with a Discord front-end, competing with Freecash and Swagbucks rather than with gaming media. That is a different investment thesis and should be diligenced as one.
  - *Could not source:* 1) Any published rate card, transaction price or benchmark for a static brand image embedded in a Discord bot message. No such market is publicly priced. Everything in that direction is flat-fee sponsored-post pricing, which is a different unit. 2) A single published figure for 'the discount applied to non-MRC-accredited, non-viewability-measured inventory'. I searched for this specifically and it does not exist as a benchmark, because buyers exclude such inventory from the buy rather than repri


#### B3 · Minors & advertising standards (16)

- **[FATAL]** The code acquires COPPA 'actual knowledge' that a user is under 13 and then does nothing — it stores the birth date, tells the child to keep earning, and keeps collecting their data. This is the single worst fact in the repository on this topic.
  - *Evidence:* lib/eligibility.ts has exactly one age branch: `if (age < MIN_REDEEM_AGE)` where MIN_REDEEM_AGE = 18 (lib/eligibility.ts:27, 101-104). There is no under-13 branch anywhere in the file, and `EligibilityReason` has no under-13 state. In app/actions/trophies.ts:90-93 a submitted birthDate is WRITTEN TO THE DATABASE FIRST (`patch.birthDate = d`, then `db.update(schema.users).set(patch)`) and only then evaluated. So when an 11-year-old types their real DOB, ClusterGG persists proof of the child's age and returns the message 'You have to be 18 to cash out. Keep earning — your points and trophies are yours and they keep' (lib/eligibility.ts:103). No deletion, no parental-consent flow, no halt to collection, no flag to staff. COPPA applies to a general-audience site from the moment it has actual knowledge a user is under 13 — the FTC's own FAQ states the obligation triggers when 'a child self-identifies by age' to a responsible staff member (https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/complying-coppa-frequently-asked-questions). Personal information under 16 CFR 312.2 expressly includes persistent identifiers — 'a customer number held in a cookie, an Internet Protocol (IP) address, a processor or device serial number, or unique device identifier' (https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/16/312.2). ClusterGG collects hashed IP, geoCountry, geoCity, sessionId, deviceType and pagePath per impression (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:20-32), plus Discord ID, email and linked game-account identifiers.
  - *Implication:* This converts the best legal posture available to them (general-audience site, no actual knowledge, low risk) into the worst (operator with documented actual knowledge, continuing to collect — effectively strict liability). Every under-13 who ever enters a DOB at redemption creates a dated, self-authenticating database record that a plaintiff or the FTC can query with one SQL statement. Penalty is $53,088 per violation (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/02/ftc-publishes-inflation-adjusted-civil-penalty-amounts-2025). Fix cost is trivial (one branch, a deletion job, a consent flow); the cost of not having fixed it before the first child typed a DOB is not recoverable, because the records already exist.
- **[FATAL]** Four out of four shipped daily missions require paying users to click ads, manufacturing a ~12% click-through rate — roughly 120-240x the display norm, and the exact statistical signature that ad-fraud vendors classify as Sophisticated Invalid Traffic.
  - *Evidence:* ad_click pays 25 CP at a daily cap of 3 (lib/quests.ts:118); ad_impression pays 1 CP at a cap of 25 (lib/quests.ts:117). All four shipped mission templates include `{ action: "ad_click", count: 3 }` (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106). A gamer completing the daily loop therefore generates 3 clicks against 25 impressions = 12% CTR. Published display CTR benchmarks sit around 0.05-0.1%. The MRC classifies 'incentivized manipulation of measurements' as Sophisticated Invalid Traffic (SIVT), and MRC-accredited fraud-detection vendors must be able to identify and filter it (https://www.pixalate.com/blog/mrc-definitions-sivt-incentivized-manipulation-measurements; MRC Invalid Traffic Detection and Filtration Standards Addendum, https://mediaratingcouncil.org/sites/default/files/Standards/IVT%20Addendum%20Update%20062520.pdf). Industry treatment of this exact pattern: 'Incentivized clicks involve real people who are paid to interact with ads... commonly used in cash reward-based mobile applications... and paid-to-click websites' (https://www.humansecurity.com/learn/blog/click-fraud-101-types-of-invalid-clicks/). Note the model document itself concedes the point — it says ad_click should drop to 0 CP because paying for clicks 'manufactures a ~10% click rate ... the signature of incentivised fraud' — but the code never changed.
  - *Implication:* 25 CP × 3 = 75 CP/day, plus 25 CP of impression rewards = 100 CP, i.e. 20% of the 500 CP daily ceiling is paid for ad engagement. The cash cost is trivial ($0.01/gamer/day). The cost that matters is that the first brand to put an IAS or DoubleVerify tag on its creative, or to look at its own CTR column, sees a number that cannot occur naturally. The likely outcome is not renegotiation — it is a clawback demand on the flight, removal from the buy, and a note in the agency's vendor file. At $500/month per brand, one such incident costs more in referenceability than the contract is worth.
- **[FATAL]** Discord ad impressions are counted server-side at the moment the bot posts the message, one row per server regardless of how many humans see it. This is 'count on insertion' — the method IAB/MRC guidelines explicitly rule out — and it makes the inventory both unsellable to a sophisticated buyer and wildly overstated in the model.
  - *Evidence:* lib/discord/ads.ts:80-83 inserts exactly one adImpressions row per successful guild post: `db.insert(schema.adImpressions).values({ id: uid(), campaignCreativeId, pagePath: `discord:${g.guildId}`, deviceType: "discord", guildId })` — no userId, no client signal, fired immediately after `postMessage` returns ok. IAB/MRC guidelines require client-initiated counting on a 'begin to render' basis; server-initiated counting (counting when the content is served, a.k.a. count-on-decision or count-on-insertion) is expressly 'not acceptable for counting rendered impressions because they are the furthest away from the user actually seeing the ad' (https://www.iab.com/guidelines/desktop-display-impression-measurement-guidelines/; https://www.iab.com/news/iab-mrc-mma-propose-updates-to-ad-measurement-guidelines/). Separately, the MRC viewable-impression standard is 50% of pixels in view for 1 continuous second for display, 2 seconds for video (https://www.publift.com/blog/mrc-viewability; https://www.ana.net/content/show/id/pr-2015-measurement-guidelines) — unmeasurable inside a Discord embed, which is a static image with no JavaScript. The other Discord surface, `pickCardAd` in lib/cards/ads.ts:101-124, records nothing at all: grepping adImpressions across the repo shows inserts only in app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23 and lib/discord/ads.ts:80.
  - *Implication:* MIN_HOURS_BETWEEN_ADS = 6 (lib/discord/ads.ts:23) caps a server at 4 ad posts/day = ~120 recorded impressions/guild/month. If 1,000 gamers sit across ~30 servers, the Discord surface can record ~3,600 impressions/month — against a claimed 450,000 views/month at 1,000 gamers, a gap of roughly two orders of magnitude. The bot-rendered card surface, which the pitch describes as the core inventory ('every card the bot renders into Discord carries a brand creative'), contributes exactly zero countable impressions. The scaling ladder ($2,250 → $22,500 → $225,000 → $2.25m) is built on a view count the system cannot produce, cannot verify, and could not defend in a make-good conversation.
- **[FATAL]** A media agency's compliance lead, told the publisher pays users 25 CP to click, will classify the inventory as MFA-adjacent incentivised traffic and blocklist it — and roughly 96% of programmatic budget is now actively screened this way.
  - *Evidence:* The paragraph such a lead writes: 'This publisher operates a paid-to-click scheme. Users receive platform currency redeemable for cash, capped at three clicks per user per day, and the publisher's own quest templates require ad clicks to complete a daily mission. The resulting engagement is not indicative of user intent; the MRC classifies incentivized manipulation of measurements as Sophisticated Invalid Traffic, which our verification partners are required to identify and filter, and which is excluded from billable impressions. The Discord placements additionally cannot be measured to the MRC viewable-impression standard — they are static images in a third-party client with no client-side measurement possible — and the publisher counts them server-side on post, which is count-on-insertion and non-compliant with IAB/MRC display measurement guidelines. I am recommending we add the domain and the bot placements to the global exclusion list and decline the direct buy. Any CTR reported from this source should be excluded from creative-performance benchmarking, as inclusion would corrupt our CTR baselines across the account.' Supporting facts: the ANA/4A's/WFA/ISBA MFA definition (September 2023) keys on high ad-to-content ratio (30%+ desktop), aggressive ad refresh, generic templated content, and 'little-to-no organic audience and heavy dependence on visits sourced from clickbait' — i.e. paid rather than earned attention (https://www.ana.net/content/show/id/pr-2023-09-made-for-advertising; https://lbbonline.com/news/leading-trade-groups-define-made-for-advertising-websites). The ANA benchmark series shows MFA spend falling from 15% of programmatic dollars in 2023 to 6.2% in 2024 to ~4% latest, and the MFA domain universe shrinking from ~44,000 to 22,634 (https://www.ana.net/content/show/id/pr-2023-06-programmaticstudy; https://www.ana.net/content/show/id/pr-2024-12-programmatic; https://wfanet.org/knowledge/item/2025/01/21/ana-s-2024-programmatic-benchmark-study-progress-but-challenges-remain). Blocking is now automated and default-on: Basis Technologies' DSP uses Jounce Media's daily-updated exclusion list to auto-block MFA, and Jounce identifies MFA specifically by 'each publisher's dependence on paid traffic' (https://basis.com/news/basis-and-jounce-media-automate-blocking-of-ads-on-low-quality-websites; https://www.adexchanger.com/technology/jounce-partners-with-self-serve-buying-platform-adlib-to-block-mfa-by-default/).
  - *Implication:* Classification is effectively permanent and travels between buyers — the Jounce and DeepSee lists are shared infrastructure, so being flagged once removes the publisher from every DSP consuming those lists, not just the one that flagged it. The commercial model's stated fallback of 'programmatic fill' at the 1,000,000-gamer rung is therefore not available: programmatic is the channel most aggressively screened against exactly this pattern. That leaves 100% of revenue dependent on direct sales to brands whose own agencies apply the same screen — and the assumption A5 (fill rate above 56%) has no support at any rung of the ladder. Since unsold inventory shows a house creative at zero revenue and full CP cost, a fill-rate collapse does not merely reduce margin, it inverts it.
- **[FATAL]** Quantified maximum exposure at 100,000 users: roughly 2,000-5,000 under-13s and 10,000-15,000 under-18s, giving a statutory COPPA ceiling of $106m-$265m and a realistic settlement band of $10m-$30m — but the injunction, not the fine, is what ends the business.
  - *Evidence:* Penalty rate: $53,088 per violation, effective 17 January 2025, with no 2026 inflation increase (agencies continue using 2025 levels) (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/02/ftc-publishes-inflation-adjusted-civil-penalty-amounts-2025; https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/01/14/2026-00535/civil-monetary-penalties-2026-adjustment). Audience composition: 28% of US teens use Discord and 44% of teenage gamers do (https://thesocialshepherd.com/blog/discord-statistics); published Discord age distributions put 18-24 at ~41%, 25-34 at ~29%, 35-44 at ~15%, leaving roughly 15% split between under-18 and 45+ (https://www.demandsage.com/discord-statistics/). I could not source a primary, audited under-13 or under-18 share for gaming Discord — these are third-party aggregator figures and should be treated as indicative only. The under-13 band of 2-5% is my estimate, anchored on the ICO's finding of 'large numbers of children under 13' on Reddit despite an identical contractual ban and identical absence of verification. Statutory arithmetic: 2,000 × $53,088 = $106.2m; 5,000 × $53,088 = $265.4m. Realistic band anchored on comparables: Disney $10m, Microsoft $20m, Cognosphere $20m, Epic $275m — with FTC penalties scaling to company size, so a seed-stage company sits at the bottom or below. UK exposure separately: £17.5m or 4% of global turnover, with Imgur at £247,590 as the small-company comparator.
  - *Implication:* For an investor the fine is the less important number. Every one of the four FTC gaming settlements carried a conduct order, and the order ClusterGG would receive writes itself: implement age assurance at registration, obtain verifiable parental consent or exclude under-13s entirely, delete all data collected from children to date, and stop incentivising ad engagement by minors. Deleting under-13 accounts destroys CP balances and the linked-account graph. Adding age assurance at registration adds friction to the single Discord-OAuth step the entire acquisition funnel runs through. Stopping incentivised ad clicks removes 20% of the daily CP ceiling and requires rebuilding all four mission templates. Any of the three alone materially changes the unit economics; together they are a different company. Price the legal exposure as an operating-model risk, not a contingent liability.
- **[SEVERE]** There is no age assurance of any kind at signup — and in February 2026 the ICO fined Reddit £14.47m for precisely that, holding that self-declaration is not enough.
  - *Evidence:* The users table comments state the design intent explicitly: birthDate is 'Not collected at signup: playing has no age gate here' (lib/db/schema.ts:24-31). Nothing gates registration, linking a game account, earning CP, or being served ads. On 24 February 2026 the ICO fined Reddit £14.47m, finding no robust age assurance until July 2025 and no DPIA before January 2025, with 'large numbers of children under 13 on the platform despite terms prohibiting them'; Information Commissioner John Edwards: 'Relying on users to declare their age themselves is not enough when children may be at risk', and the ICO said it is 'focusing now on companies that primarily [rely] on self-declaration' (https://www.osborneclarke.com/insights/uk-ico-fines-online-platform-ps1447m-and-warns-age-self-declaration-not-enough-protect; https://ico.org.uk/about-the-ico/media-centre/news-and-blogs/2026/02/reddit-issued-with-1447m-fine-for-children-s-privacy-failures/). MediaLab (Imgur) was fined £247,590 in the same month for implementing no age assurance at all and having no mechanism for parental consent (https://keystonelaw.com/keynotes/childrens-privacy-enforcement-intensifies-lessons-from-icos-reddit-and-imgur-decisions/). Both decisions turned on two findings: no lawful basis for processing under-13 data, and no DPIA — and both fines could have been 'seriously reduced' had a DPIA been done. ClusterGG has no DPIA in the repository (no match for 'DPIA' anywhere in app/legal or docs). The ICO's December 2025 Children's Code strategy update reviewed age assurance across 17 platforms and signalled that 2026 is the enforcement phase.
  - *Implication:* ClusterGG is strictly worse-positioned than Imgur: Imgur merely failed to check ages; ClusterGG fails to check ages AND pays cash for engagement AND has no DPIA. UK GDPR caps fines at £17.5m or 4% of global turnover, whichever is higher. Imgur at £247,590 is the realistic comparator for a company at this stage — but the ICO's remedy is the real cost: retrofitting age assurance to a Discord-OAuth funnel adds friction at the exact top-of-funnel step the whole growth model depends on, and deleting all under-13 data means deleting accounts, CP balances and the linked-account graph that makes the profiles worth anything.
- **[SEVERE]** The privacy policy contains no children's-privacy section at all — no minimum age, no COPPA notice, no parental-consent route. On its own that is an independent COPPA violation the moment one under-13 uses the service.
  - *Evidence:* app/legal/privacy/page.tsx (last updated July 2026) has sections for What we collect, How we use it, Retention & deletion, Your rights (GDPR / CCPA), and Third-party APIs. Grepping app/legal and docs for 'child', 'under 13', 'COPPA', 'parental', 'guardian', 'age assurance' returns one hit in the whole legal directory — the string 'GDPR / CCPA' — and nothing on children. 16 CFR 312.4 requires operators to give direct notice to parents and post a complete online notice of their information practices regarding children. The FTC's Disney complaint (settled $10m, announced 10 September 2025, approved by the court 31 December 2025) charged exactly this alongside the consent failure: 'failing to provide direct notice and complete online notice to parents about its data collection practices' (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/09/disney-pay-10-million-settle-ftc-allegations-company-enabled-unlawful-collection-childrens-personal).
  - *Implication:* Notice failure is the cheapest count for a regulator to prove — it requires no forensics, only a screenshot of the published page. It also removes the 'we didn't realise' defence, because every serious US consumer service ships a children's-privacy clause as boilerplate; its absence reads as a decision rather than an oversight. This is a sub-£5,000 fix that has been left undone through a July 2026 policy revision.
- **[SEVERE]** The privacy policy promises that ad impression rows are 'purged after 90 days'. No such job exists in the codebase. Microsoft paid $20m to the FTC in a case where illegal retention of children's data was a named charge.
  - *Evidence:* app/legal/privacy/page.tsx states: 'Raw ad impression rows are aggregated and purged after 90 days.' Searching lib/, app/api/cron and scripts/ for purge / retention / 90-day / cleanup / prune returns no retention job — the only cron routes that exist are app/api/cron/{announce,daily,sync}, and the sole 'cleanup' match is a one-off image migration in lib/db/seed.ts:917. Meanwhile every impression row carries hashedIp, geoCountry, geoCity, sessionId and userId (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:23-32). The 2025 COPPA amendments added a mandatory written data-retention policy and a written information security programme, effective 23 June 2025 with full compliance required by 22 April 2026 (https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/complying-coppa-frequently-asked-questions; https://www.finnegan.com/en/insights/articles/coppas-amended-rule-is-now-in-full-effect-what-operators-need-to-know.html). The FTC's $20m Microsoft/Xbox settlement (June 2023) charged both collection without consent and illegally retaining children's personal information (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2023/06/ftc-will-require-microsoft-pay-20-million-over-charges-it-illegally-collected-personal-information).
  - *Implication:* Two distinct liabilities from one unwritten cron job. First, an unkept promise in a published privacy policy is a straightforward FTC Act §5 deceptive practice, independent of COPPA and independent of whether any child is involved. Second, the April 2026 compliance deadline for the amended COPPA retention and security-programme requirements has already passed as of August 2026 — this is a present breach, not a future risk. Any technical DD that diffs the privacy policy against the codebase finds this in about ten minutes, which makes it a credibility problem for management as much as a legal one.
- **[SEVERE]** Every comparable rewards platform sets a minimum age of 16-18 and requires parental consent for minors. ClusterGG sets none, and instead runs an earning loop for children it has already decided it will never pay.
  - *Evidence:* Freecash terms: 'This Website is offered and available to users who are at least 16 years of age or older. By using this Website, you represent and warrant that you are at least 16 years old and of legal age to form a binding contract with Almedia' (https://freecash.com/en/policies/terms). Swagbucks: minimum age 13, with parental consent required for anyone under 18 (https://help.swagbucks.com/hc/en-us/articles/360020965692-Can-Teenagers-Use-Swagbucks). Discord itself requires 13+, higher where local law sets a higher digital-consent age (https://discord.com/terms). ClusterGG's own legal page takes the opposite position: 'Playing has no age gate; being paid does' (app/legal/economy/page.tsx:73-75). Contracts with minors are voidable at the minor's election under the infancy doctrine in essentially every US state, and disaffirmance must be total (https://publications.lawschool.cornell.edu/jlpp/2024/02/26/minors-disaffirmance-of-digital-application-contracts-having-their-cake-and-eating-it-too/).
  - *Implication:* The 'earn but don't cash out' design is the worst of both worlds. It does not reduce data-protection exposure at all — COPPA and the Children's Code are about collection and profiling, not payment — while it does create a growing population of children holding non-redeemable balances, which is a consumer-protection and reputational story waiting to be written ('gaming site made my 12-year-old click ads for months, then refused to pay'). Meanwhile the 18+ gate itself is self-declared with no verification: a 15-year-old types 1999 and is paid. So the platform absorbs the disaffirmance risk anyway, since a minor who lies about age can still generally disaffirm. Setting the floor at 16 or 18 for the whole service, as Freecash does, would eliminate most of Parts 1.1-1.5 at once.
- **[SEVERE]** The read-across from FTC gaming enforcement is that the FTC pursues the party whose design choice enabled the collection, not just the party that did the collecting — and it has done so four times in the gaming/kids space since 2022.
  - *Evidence:* Epic Games: $275m COPPA civil penalty (December 2022), the largest penalty ever for violating an FTC rule, part of $520m total (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2022/12/fortnite-video-game-maker-epic-games-pay-more-half-billion-dollars-over-ftc-allegations; https://justice.gov/archives/opa/pr/epic-games-inc-developer-fortnite-video-game-agrees-275-million-penalty-and-injunction). Microsoft/Xbox: $20m (June 2023), collection from children signing up without parental notice or consent, plus illegal retention (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2023/06/ftc-will-require-microsoft-pay-20-million-over-charges-it-illegally-collected-personal-information). Cognosphere/HoYoverse (Genshin Impact): $20m (January 2025), COPPA plus deceptive loot-box practices, and banned from selling loot boxes to under-16s without parental consent (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/01/genshin-impact-game-developer-will-be-banned-selling-lootboxes-teens-under-16-without-parental). Disney: $10m (announced September 2025, court-approved 31 December 2025) — and this is the key one, because Disney did not collect anything. Disney mislabelled videos, YouTube collected the data and served the targeted ads, and Disney 'remained liable for third-party collection resulting from their misdesignation'. The FTC's own guidance blog frames the lesson as granular, content-level responsibility and pushes age assurance as the compliance pathway (https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/blog/2025/09/protecting-children-watching-youtube-videos-lessons-learned-ftcs-settlement-disney).
  - *Implication:* Disney is the direct analogue. ClusterGG's bot posts brand creatives into Discord servers whose membership it has no age signal for whatsoever (lib/discord/ads.ts:59-70 posts to a channel; nothing in the path knows or asks who is in the room), and it pays platform currency to whoever clicks. If the FTC will fine Disney $10m for a labelling error that let a third party advertise to children, a platform that actively pays children to click brand creatives is not a hard case. Note also the Cognosphere remedy — an under-16 parental-consent requirement on the monetised action — which maps almost exactly onto what a ClusterGG consent decree would look like and would gut the daily-mission loop.
- **[SEVERE]** The EU picture is worse than the US one, because the age of digital consent is 16 by default and ClusterGG has no country-conditional logic on the consent age at all.
  - *Evidence:* GDPR Article 8 sets 16 as the default age below which parental consent is required for information society services, with member states free to lower it but not below 13 (https://gdpr-info.eu/art-8-gdpr/). Implemented ages vary: Germany, Hungary, Lithuania, Luxembourg, Slovakia and the Netherlands at 16; France at 15; Austria at 14; Ireland, Spain, Denmark, Poland, Sweden, Latvia, Czechia at 13 (https://euconsent.eu/digital-age-of-consent-under-the-gdpr/). Article 8 also requires the controller to 'make reasonable efforts to verify that consent is given or authorised by the holder of parental responsibility, taking into consideration available technology'. ClusterGG's only country field is a free-text ISO-3166 code collected at redemption (lib/db/schema.ts:23, app/actions/trophies.ts:94), used solely to check the sanctions blocklist (lib/eligibility.ts:40-43, 105-108). Nothing anywhere maps country to a consent age. The BLOCKED_COUNTRIES list is six sanctioned states; there is no EU/UK branch.
  - *Implication:* The under-13 population is the COPPA problem; in the EU the exposed population is everyone under 16 in six member states plus under-15 in France, which on the estimates above is a substantially larger cohort than the under-13 band — plausibly 10,000-15,000 children at 100,000 users rather than 2,000-5,000. Because ClusterGG pays worldwide by design, it cannot argue it does not target the EU. GDPR ceiling is €20m or 4% of global annual turnover, whichever is higher. The remediation is not just an age gate but a country-conditional age gate, and the country is currently collected months after registration, at redemption — i.e. long after the unlawful processing has occurred.
- **[SEVERE]** The unauthenticated impression beacon means the advertiser-facing numbers can be inflated by anyone with curl — and because CP is paid per impression, it is also a direct cash leak.
  - *Evidence:* app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts POSTs are accepted with no authentication requirement — the session is read optionally (`const session = await getSession().catch(() => null)`, line 21) and the row is written with `userId: session?.uid ?? null`. The only input is a JSON body carrying `ccId`, and there is no rate limiting, no origin check, no nonce and no signature. When a session is present the handler additionally calls `awardQuestAction(db, session.uid, "ad_impression", ...)` (lines 34-37), paying 1 CP per accepted POST up to the daily cap of 25 (lib/quests.ts:117). The duration handler at lines 41-46 accepts an arbitrary `ms` value up to an hour against any impression id, with no ownership check on that id. Under IAB/MRC guidelines a countable impression requires a client-initiated signal tied to the ad beginning to render (https://www.iab.com/guidelines/desktop-display-impression-measurement-guidelines/); an endpoint any party can call satisfies no measurement standard, and inflating reported metrics to attract advertisers is the MRC's own worked example of incentivized manipulation as SIVT (https://www.pixalate.com/blog/mrc-definitions-sivt-incentivized-manipulation-measurements).
  - *Implication:* Three consequences. Commercially, no third-party measurement partner can certify these numbers, so any brand spending above trivial amounts will demand its own tag and immediately see the discrepancy. Financially, a scripted loop earns 25 CP/day/account free of any real ad view, and the avgViewMs figure surfaced in the admin dashboard (app/admin/ads/analytics/page.tsx:25) is attacker-controlled — so the 'viewed duration' metric that would be used to argue viewability is the least trustworthy number in the system. Legally, billing a brand against a counter that the brand's own auditor can trivially demonstrate is forgeable moves the conversation from 'measurement dispute' to 'invoice fraud'.
- **[SEVERE]** The UK Children's Code applies to ClusterGG on its face, and paying children currency to click ads runs directly at two of its named standards — nudge techniques and detrimental use of data.
  - *Evidence:* The Age Appropriate Design Code applies to information society services 'likely to be accessed by children' under 18 in the UK — a deliberately lower threshold than 'directed at children' — and sets 15 standards including data minimisation, profiling (off by default), detrimental use of data, and nudge techniques (which discourage design that nudges children into weakening their privacy or into extended engagement). I was unable to fetch ico.org.uk directly — the site returns 403 to this tool — so I am relying on the ICO's own published enforcement outcomes as reported by Osborne Clarke, Keystone and Lewis Silkin rather than on the code text itself, and the standard names above should be re-verified against the primary document before this is used in a negotiation (https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/childrens-information/childrens-code-guidance-and-resources/age-appropriate-design-a-code-of-practice-for-online-services/). What is firmly sourced is the enforcement posture: Reddit £14.47m and Imgur £247,590 in February 2026, both turning on absent age assurance and absent DPIA, and the ICO's stated 2026 focus on services relying on self-declaration (https://www.lewissilkin.com/insights/2026/02/11/the-ico-steps-up-on-protecting-children-online-102mi48; https://keystonelaw.com/keynotes/childrens-privacy-enforcement-intensifies-lessons-from-icos-reddit-and-imgur-decisions/).
  - *Implication:* 'Likely to be accessed by children' is not a close question for a Discord-native gaming product where 44% of teenage gamers already use the host platform. The daily-mission mechanic — a streak-based checklist that pays currency for 20 discrete actions per day including three ad clicks — is close to a textbook example of what the nudge-techniques standard exists to prevent, and paying a child to click an advertisement is the cleanest available illustration of detrimental use of data. Because a DPIA is mandatory here and none exists, ClusterGG currently forfeits the mitigation that both the Reddit and Imgur decisions identified as the main lever for reducing a fine. Commissioning a DPIA is the highest-return legal spend available to this company and should be a condition of any term sheet.
- **[MINOR]** The FTC Endorsement Guides and the 2024 Reviews Rule are a weaker fit here than the brief assumes, and I would not lead with either — the exposure is FTC Act §5 deception against advertisers, not endorsement-disclosure failure.
  - *Evidence:* An 'endorsement' under the Guides is 'an advertising message that consumers are likely to believe reflects the opinions or beliefs of someone other than the sponsoring advertiser'. A click is not an opinion, and the FTC's guidance does not address payments to consumers for clicking or viewing ads (https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/ftcs-endorsement-guides-what-people-are-asking). The material-connection doctrine bites when a paid consumer makes a public statement about a product; ClusterGG's users make no statement. On the 2024 Rule on the Use of Consumer Reviews and Testimonials, 16 CFR Part 465, effective 21 October 2024: the relevant provision is §465.8, Misuse of Fake Indicators of Social Media Influence, which reaches metrics generated by bots, fake accounts, accounts using someone's data without consent, hijacked accounts, or metrics that 'do not reflect a real individual's or entity's activities, opinions, findings, or experiences' (https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/consumer-reviews-testimonials-rule-questions-answers; https://www.ftc.gov/legal-library/browse/federal-register-notices/16-cfr-part-465-trade-regulation-rule-use-consumer-reviews-testimonials-final-rule). ClusterGG's clicks come from real people performing real actions, so §465.8 does not cleanly apply. Note the brief's framing referenced §465.9; the operative section is §465.8.
  - *Implication:* Report this honestly to the investor rather than overstating it — an inflated legal claim that a target's counsel can knock down damages the credibility of the findings that are solid. The real advertising-side legal exposure is plain FTC Act §5: representing to a brand that it bought 100,000 'views' at a $5 CPM, where the views are server-side counted on post, unverifiable, incentivised, and in part generated by an unauthenticated public endpoint (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts accepts anonymous POSTs with an arbitrary creative id and no rate limiting). That is a misrepresentation to a paying counterparty, and it is also straightforward commercial fraud exposure to the brands themselves, who do not need the FTC to sue.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** Favourable: the ad server does no behavioural profiling, which is the single biggest thing standing between ClusterGG and a Disney-style targeted-advertising charge.
  - *Evidence:* lib/ads.ts targets on exactly one dimension — `targetDevice` filtered to 'both' or the requesting device (lib/ads.ts:55, 74). Grepping lib/ads.ts for geo, country, segment, audience or age returns no targeting logic. Creatives are selected by priority sort and a hash-based rotation seeded on the card (lib/cards/ads.ts:107-112), and the Discord path sends the same creative to every eligible guild in a run (lib/discord/ads.ts:47-52). The COPPA 'support for the internal operations' exception permits contextual advertising and frequency capping without verifiable parental consent; it does not permit behavioural targeting. The 2025 amendments tightened this by requiring separate verifiable parental consent before disclosing children's personal information for purposes not integral to the service, which is aimed squarely at third-party behavioural advertising (https://www.ftc.gov/business-guidance/resources/complying-coppa-frequently-asked-questions; https://www.whitecase.com/insight-alert/unpacking-ftcs-coppa-amendments-what-you-need-know). The privacy policy's claim that Cluster does not sell personal data is consistent with what the code does.
  - *Implication:* This is genuinely defensible and worth preserving. Contextual-only serving keeps ClusterGG out of the worst COPPA category and out of the separate-consent requirement added in 2025. It should be documented, tested and stated in the privacy policy as a deliberate commitment rather than left as an implementation detail that a growth-minded product manager quietly reverses when a brand asks for targeting. Caveat: the impression rows still persist hashedIp, geoCity, geoCountry, sessionId and userId indefinitely (see the retention finding), so the data to build behavioural targeting is being accumulated even though it is not currently used — which is itself a data-minimisation failure under the Children's Code.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** Favourable: an 18+ redemption gate does exist, is centralised, and fails closed — the brief's premise that there is no age gate anywhere is wrong and should be corrected before this goes to the investor.
  - *Evidence:* lib/eligibility.ts:27 sets MIN_REDEEM_AGE = 18 with a documented rationale (a minor's contract is voidable, so a payout to a fifteen-year-old is money that may have to be returned and cannot be recovered from the payout provider). The rule is expressed once as a pure function, `eligibilityOf(age, country)`, so it can be tested without a database and applied to a form mid-fill (lib/eligibility.ts:87-110). The database-backed wrapper `eligibilityFor` catches and returns not-ok on any error, with the comment 'Fail CLOSED... a guard that opens when the database hiccups is not a guard' (lib/eligibility.ts:118-123). It is invoked before the payout-preference write and before any provider call in app/actions/trophies.ts:84-106, and the same constants are rendered into the public legal page (app/legal/economy/page.tsx:2,73-75) so the prose cannot drift from the code. Placeholder thresholds are flagged for counsel in comments rather than presented as settled.
  - *Implication:* Credit where due: this is better engineering and better legal instinct than most seed-stage products, and it tells you the team can build a gate correctly when it decides something matters. That makes the absence of a registration-time gate a product decision rather than a capability gap — which is both reassuring (they can fix it) and damning (they chose not to). Two residual defects: the DOB is self-declared with no verification, so the gate stops honest minors only; and because it fires only at first redemption, the platform runs unlawful collection for however many months precede a child's first cash-out attempt. Moving the same well-built check to registration is a days-not-months change.
  - *Could not source:* 1. PRIMARY REGULATORY TEXT WAS PARTLY UNREACHABLE. federalregister.gov and ecfr.gov both redirect this tool to an anti-bot interstitial, so I could not read the 2025 COPPA amendments or 16 CFR Part 312/465 in the original. I substituted ftc.gov's own FAQ and Cornell LII, which are reliable, but nobody should sign a term sheet on my paraphrase of amended §312.8 and §312.10 without counsel reading the Federal Register text. 2. ico.org.uk RETURNS 403 to this tool. The Reddit and Imgur fine amounts,


#### B4 · Money-transmission / gambling / tax (A8) (14)

- **[FATAL]** Nationwide US money transmitter licensing costs more per year than ClusterGG's entire global redemption liability at 100,000 gamers. The regulated activity is smaller than the cost of regulating it, at every scale the company plans for.
  - *Evidence:* Licensing cost, from 2026-dated secondary industry sources (I could not source an official CSBS/NMLS aggregate figure and flag these as vendor estimates, not primary): all-50-state direct cost $250k–$435k+, exceeding $1M once surety bonds and counsel are included; per-state application fees $250–$5,000; surety bonds from $10,000 (WA, WY) to $500,000 (NY, KY, MI) and CA $250,000–$7M; net worth $100k–$1M+; timeline 3–18 months per state; ongoing maintenance $225k–$280k/yr rising with volume; per-state consultants $5k–$20k. 31 states have now enacted the CSBS Money Transmission Modernization Act in whole or part, which does not remove the licence, it harmonises it. CLUSTERGG'S SIDE OF THE LEDGER, from code: lib/quests.ts:159 DEFAULT_DAILY_CP_CEILING = 500; docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md 10,000 CP = $1. Maximum a single gamer can earn is 500 CP/day = $0.05/day = $18.25/year, and that assumes a perfect 365-day streak at the ceiling. 10,000 gamers all at the ceiling = $182,500/yr of total worldwide payouts. 100,000 gamers = $1.825M/yr worldwide; the US slice at a generous 20% of users is $365k/yr. https://cornerstonelicensing.com/money-transmitter-license | https://www.brico.ai/post/how-much-do-mtls-cost | https://complyone.tech/blog/money-transmitter-license-by-state-timeline-cost-and-requirements | https://www.csbs.org/csbs-money-transmission-modernization-act-mtma | https://natlawreview.com/article/united-states-continuing-shift-modern-money-transmission-laws
  - *Implication:* At the 10,000-gamer rung of their own ladder, nationwide MTL year-one cost ($250k–$1M) is 1.4×–5.5× the ENTIRE annual worldwide payout obligation ($182,500), and recurring maintenance ($225k–$280k) alone exceeds it by 23–53%. At 100,000 gamers, maintenance is still 62–77% of all US payouts. The break-even point where licensing is a rounding error rather than a company-killer is somewhere north of 1,000,000 gamers — the top rung of a ladder whose bottom rung is unbuilt. Practical read: ClusterGG cannot afford to be a money transmitter at any scale it will plausibly reach in the next four years, so the ONLY viable strategy is to not be one. That makes finding #1 (delete gifting) existential rather than tidy.
- **[SEVERE]** Cluster Points are convertible virtual currency and ClusterGG is its 'administrator' under FinCEN's own definition — and because trophies can be GIFTED between gamers and a gifted trophy is redeemable for cash, ClusterGG allows 'transfers of value between persons', which is the exact trigger FinCEN uses to make an administrator a money transmitter.
  - *Evidence:* FinCEN FIN-2013-G001 (18 Mar 2013), p.2: 'An administrator is a person engaged as a business in issuing (putting into circulation) a virtual currency, and who has the authority to redeem (to withdraw from circulation) such virtual currency.' p.3: 'An administrator or exchanger that (1) accepts and transmits a convertible virtual currency or (2) buys or sells convertible virtual currency for any reason is a money transmitter under FinCEN's regulations, unless a limitation to or exemption from the definition applies.' p.4 (Centralized Virtual Currencies): 'The administrator of that repository will be a money transmitter to the extent that it allows transfers of value between persons or from one location to another.' CVC is defined at p.1 as virtual currency that 'either has an equivalent value in real currency, or acts as a substitute for real currency' — CP is fixed at 10,000 CP = $1. Reaffirmed by FIN-2019-G001 (9 May 2019), §1.3 and §3, and 31 CFR 1010.100(ff)(5)(i)(A): money transmission means 'the acceptance of currency, funds, or other value that substitutes for currency from one person and the transmission of currency, funds, or other value that substitutes for currency to another location or person by any means.' FIN-2019-G001 §3 expressly extends this to foreign-located entities: 'These requirements apply equally to domestic and foreign-located CVC money transmitters doing business in whole or in substantial part within the United States, even if the foreign-located entity has no physical presence in the United States.' CODE: /home/user/clustergg/lib/marketplace.ts:194-260 — buyTrophy() accepts an opts.recipientSlug, debits the BUYER's CP and writes the userTrophies row to recipientId, with the in-code comment 'The award is an ordinary held trophy: it shows on the profile and it is redeemable for cash through the flow that already exists.' Gifting is also exposed inside Discord (lib/discord/screens.ts:319-332, 898-920, lib/discord/gift-id.ts). https://www.fincen.gov/system/files/shared/FIN-2013-G001.pdf | https://www.fincen.gov/system/files/2019-05/FinCEN%20Guidance%20CVC%20FINAL%20508.pdf | https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/31/1010.100
  - *Implication:* Gamer A earns CP → buys a trophy → gifts it to Gamer B → B cashes out. Value moved from A to B through ClusterGG's books and out as real money. That is textbook third-party value transmission, not 'paying our own users'. Without gifting, ClusterGG has a defensible argument that redeeming its own liability to its own user is not transmission (no third party). WITH gifting it does not. This is one feature — roughly 60 lines across lib/marketplace.ts and lib/discord/screens.ts — and deleting it is the single highest-leverage legal act available to this company. Operating unlicensed is 18 U.S.C. § 1960: 'fined ... or imprisoned not more than 5 years, or both'. Not a civil-fine risk; a personal-liability-of-the-founder risk.
- **[SEVERE]** The two exemptions a founder would instinctively reach for — 'closed loop' and 'prepaid access' — are both expressly foreclosed by the text, and FinCEN says it reads exemptions strictly.
  - *Evidence:* 31 CFR 1010.100(kkk) defines closed loop prepaid access as 'Prepaid access to funds or the value of funds that can be used only for goods or services in transactions involving a defined merchant or location (or set of locations), such as a specific retailer or retail chain, a college campus, or a subway system.' Redeeming for cash into the user's own bank/PayPal/wallet is not 'goods or services ... involving a defined merchant'. Separately, FIN-2013-G001 p.5 kills the prepaid route outright: 'A person's acceptance and/or transmission of convertible virtual currency cannot be characterized as providing or selling prepaid access because prepaid access is limited to real currencies.' Footnote 18 adds: 'This is true even if the person holds the value accepted for a period of time before transmitting some or all of that value at the direction of the person from whom the value was originally accepted.' On the remaining candidate — the 'integral to the sale of goods or the provision of services' exemption at 31 CFR 1010.100(ff)(5)(ii)(F) — FIN-2019-G001 p.10 states: 'FinCEN interprets these exemptions strictly. Therefore, a person may not take advantage of a particular exemption if the activity it engages in does not conform fully to an exemption,' and FIN-2013-G001 p.4 rejects the analogous argument: 'this exemption does not apply when the only services being provided are money transmission services.' https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/31/1010.100 | https://www.fincen.gov/system/files/shared/FIN-2013-G001.pdf
  - *Implication:* There is no off-the-shelf carve-out. The realistic legal positions are (a) delete gifting and argue no third party is ever involved, which is a genuine and probably winnable argument but is an argument, not an exemption; or (b) register. Budget for a written FinCEN/state-counsel opinion before the first US payout: $40k–$120k is the normal range for a CVC/MSB status opinion, though I could not source a published rate card for that and am giving a market impression, not a citation.
- **[SEVERE]** Trophy gifting punches a hole straight through the tax-threshold immunity as well as the money-transmission defence — a payee's annual receipts are unbounded even though an earner's are capped at $18.25.
  - *Evidence:* lib/marketplace.ts:194+ buyTrophy() imposes no limit on how many trophies one user may gift to another, no cap on how many gifted trophies one user may receive, and no relationship check beyond 'the recipient's account is active' (status !== 'active' is the only rejection). The redemption path treats a gifted trophy identically to a won one — the in-file comment is explicit: 'it is redeemable for cash through the flow that already exists'. Meanwhile lib/eligibility.ts:135 annualPayout() and :160 annualRecipients() correctly aggregate by trophyRedeems.userId — i.e. by PAYEE — so the code already knows the payee total is the number that matters, but nothing caps it. IRS thresholds cited above are per-payer, per-payee, per-year, with no de minimis floor once crossed.
  - *Implication:* 110 gamer-years of CP consolidated into one payee is $2,000 and a 1099 obligation, plus a W-9 demand and 24% backup withholding if the TIN is not furnished. At 100,000 gamers that is 0.11% of the user base coordinating for a year — entirely achievable for a Discord server that decides to pool. It also defeats the 18+ redemption gate at lib/eligibility.ts:101: an under-18 can earn, hold and RECEIVE trophies (the age check sits only on the redeem path), so a 15-year-old accumulates and a 19-year-old friend cashes out. Every one of the three problems — money transmission, tax threshold, minor payouts — is the same feature. Gifting is the load-bearing defect in this company's legal position.
- **[SEVERE]** There is no sanctions screening of any kind in this codebase, and the blocked-country list is both over- and under-inclusive against the actual OFAC comprehensive programmes. OFAC is strict liability at $377,700 per violation — 75,540 times the value of a $5 trophy.
  - *Evidence:* CODE: a repo-wide grep for ofac|sdn|sanction|screen|watchlist|denied part returns zero compliance code — every hit is UI (fullscreen, screen readers). lib/eligibility.ts:40-43 BLOCKED_COUNTRIES = {CU, IR, KP, SY, RU, BY}. That list omits the OFAC comprehensively-sanctioned occupied regions of Ukraine (Crimea, Donetsk, Luhansk, Kherson, Zaporizhzhia), which are region- not country-coded and would never be caught by an ISO alpha-2 check, and over-blocks Russia and Belarus, which are extensively but not comprehensively embargoed. Country and date of birth are SELF-DECLARED free text collected at redemption (components/RedeemStepper.tsx:284 is an <input type='date'>; app/actions/trophies.ts:90-93 writes it unverified) with no document check, no IP/geolocation cross-check and no name screening against the SDN list. LAW: OFAC FAQ 11 — 'All U.S. persons must comply with OFAC sanctions ... Non-U.S. persons are also subject to certain sanctions prohibitions.' OFAC applies a strict liability standard for civil penalties — knowledge or intent is not required. The IEEPA maximum civil monetary penalty is $377,700 per violation or twice the transaction value, whichever is greater (2026 inflation adjustment, from $368,136 × 1.02598). https://ofac.treasury.gov/faqs/topic/1501 | https://home.treasury.gov/system/files/206/Notice-Inflation-Adjustment-to-Maximum-Civil-Monetary-Penalty.pdf | https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/01/15/2025-00786/inflation-adjustment-of-civil-monetary-penalties
  - *Implication:* One $5 payout to one name on the SDN list carries a theoretical maximum civil penalty of $377,700 — 75,540× the transaction — and the company has literally no control that would prevent it. The partial mitigant is real and deliberate: docs/PAYMENTS.md routes gamer rewards so that ClusterGG 'never learn[s] which one they picked and we never hold the destination', pushing screening onto Trolley, which does screen. But that is a DEPENDENCY, not a control: it screens the person Trolley onboards, not the ClusterGG account, and it gives ClusterGG no evidence of its own if OFAC asks. If ClusterGG is an MSB (finding #1), 31 CFR 1022.210 additionally requires a written AML programme, a designated compliance officer, training and independent testing — none of which exists in this repo. Cost to build a credible in-house programme is real but modest relative to MTL: budget $80k–$200k first year. I could not source a published benchmark for small-MSB AML programme cost and flag that figure as a market impression, not a citation.
- **[SEVERE]** The Gulf — named in the brief as a target market — is the WORST market for this product, not the best: cash prizes in UAE promotions are generally not permitted at all, and every promotion needs a government permit before it runs.
  - *Evidence:* Al Tamimi & Company (the largest UAE law firm), 'Competitions, Promotions, Prize Draws, Sweepstakes, Raffles and Instant Prizes — Sales Promotions in the UAE', dated 9 March 2016 — I FLAG THIS AS STALE and it must be re-verified with current counsel: 'Cash prizes are generally not permitted, although they may be permissible in circumstances where cash is a key aspect of the organizer's core business (such as a money exchange).' On permits: 'The Departments of Economic Development (DED) in Dubai and Abu Dhabi require permits for sales promotions ... The process for obtaining a sales promotion permit from the DED is straight-forward, and can usually be completed within a week or so,' requiring 'the nature, quantity and value of any prizes ... along with a copy of the organiser's trade licence and premises documentation.' 'Running a sales promotion without a permit can attract a penalty.' Backdrop: UAE Federal Decree-Law 31/2021 (Crimes and Penalties Law) Art. 414 — gambling punishable by up to 2 years or AED 50,000; Art. 415 — operating or organising a gambling game up to 10 years and not less than AED 100,000; Art. 416 extends the ban to promotion and advertising. The GCGRA, created in 2023, now holds exclusive federal jurisdiction over commercial gaming, and Federal Decree-Law No. 25 of 2025 (in force 1 June 2026) strips gambling and betting from the civil code ahead of the new regime. https://www.tamimi.com/law-update-articles/competitions-promotions-prize-draws-sweepstakes-raffles-and-instant-prizes-sales-promotions-in-the-u/ | https://www.mondaq.com/advertising-marketing-branding/472932/promotional-competitions-in-the-united-arab-emirates-including-dubai-and-abu-dhabi | https://uaelegislation.gov.ae/en/legislations/1529 | https://www.gcgra.gov.ae/
  - *Implication:* A per-promotion permit regime is structurally incompatible with a product that runs continuous, algorithmically-generated daily missions — you cannot file a DET application per day. And the one thing the permit regime most reliably refuses is the one thing ClusterGG's entire retention promise rests on: cash. In the Gulf the product has to become gift-card-only (see finding #12) or not launch. This should be corrected in any investor deck that lists the Gulf as a beachhead: it is a launch-last market, not a launch-first one. Egypt, which docs/PAYMENTS.md implies is the operating base ('a business operating out of Egypt or most of MENA'), is a separate unassessed exposure I did not research and flag as an open gap.
- **[MATERIAL]** The one live gambling-adjacent exposure is non-monetary consideration: twelve states treat an entrant's expenditure of time and effort as consideration, and ClusterGG's daily missions require three ad CLICKS — an act performed for the sponsor's direct commercial benefit.
  - *Evidence:* Standard US sweepstakes doctrine recognises 'monetary (typically the payment of money ...) and non-monetary consideration (an entrant's expenditure of considerable time or effort)'; the states where non-monetary consideration is expressly barred are Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Iowa, Kansas, Michigan, New Jersey, Ohio, Rhode Island, South Carolina, Texas and Wisconsin. Examples given for what tips into consideration include being required to 'generate content using the products of the sweepstakes sponsor' or 'sit through a long sales presentation'. Against that: the lead reviewer's verified code facts — ad_click pays 25 CP with a daily cap of 3 (lib/quests.ts:118), and ALL FOUR shipped daily-mission templates require ad_click x3 (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106), i.e. 75 of the 500 daily CP (15%) is paid for clicking advertisements. UK equivalent: Gambling Act 2005 Sch 2 para 2 defines paying to include 'paying money, transferring money's worth, and paying for goods or services at a price or rate which reflects the opportunity to participate'. https://www.olshanlaw.com/sweepstakes-law-basics | https://www.lexology.com/library/detail.aspx?g=003ea3f4-bd70-42b1-9409-34f7ef8ab4d3 | https://www.sweeppeasweeps.com/sweepstakes-and-contest-rules-by-state.html
  - *Implication:* Because there is no chance element, consideration alone does not create gambling — it creates a SKILL CONTEST, which is lawful in most states. So this is a second-order risk today. But it becomes first-order the moment any chance mechanic appears, and it is independently a bad look: 'we pay 25 CP per advertisement click, three per day, on every one of our four daily missions' is a sentence that will be read by a regulator, an ad buyer, and an ad network's fraud team in three different and uniformly hostile ways. Note the model document itself says ad_click should pay 0 CP because paying for clicks 'manufactures a ~10% click rate ... the signature of incentivised fraud' — the company already wrote down the correct answer and shipped the opposite.
- **[MATERIAL]** The live EU exposure is consumer law, not financial law — and it is mild because ClusterGG does not sell CP, which is exactly what the March 2025 CPC Key Principles are aimed at.
  - *Evidence:* On 21 March 2025 the Consumer Protection Cooperation (CPC) Network, led by the Netherlands ACM and the Norwegian Consumer Authority with European Commission support, adopted Key Principles on in-game virtual currencies. They require traders to 'display the price of digital content and services in real-world currency, even in cases where purchases involve in-game virtual currencies', to avoid 'practices hiding the costs of in-game digital content and services, as well as practices obliging consumers to purchase virtual currency', to respect the right of withdrawal, and to respect 'consumer vulnerabilities, in particular when it comes to children'. They are not legally binding but 'indicate how EU consumer protection authorities and associations will interpret the legal framework'. The Commission hosted stakeholder talks on their application on 3 June 2025. https://commission.europa.eu/news-and-media/news/european-commission-hosts-stakeholders-talks-application-cpc-networks-key-principles-games-virtual-2025-06-03_en | https://connectontech.bakermckenzie.com/european-consumer-protection-network-issues-new-key-principles-on-in-game-virtual-currencies-impact-for-gaming-and-gambling-entities-in-belgium-the-eu-and-beyond/
  - *Implication:* Most of the Key Principles are about consumers SPENDING money on virtual currency, which ClusterGG does not do — so the bulk is inapplicable. The two that do bite: real-world price display (ClusterGG already prices trophies in CP and in dollars, lib/marketplace.ts value field — compliant by accident) and the children limb, which is not compliant: playing has no age gate at all (lib/db/schema.ts:27 comment: 'playing has no age gate here, being PAID does'), Discord's own floor is 13, and a 13-year-old is inside a loop that pays them for clicking three advertisements a day. That is the exact 'vulnerability' the CPC principles name, and it is a much more likely first regulatory contact than any money-transmission enquiry. Low financial exposure, high headline exposure.
- **[MATERIAL]** Switching from cash to gift cards fixes most of the legal problem and is already half-built — but it also removes the only reason a gamer would grind 100 consecutive days, and the grind is already implausibly long.
  - *Evidence:* lib/payouts.ts:83 already ships 'giftcard' as a payout preference: 'Gift card / prepaid card — No bank account needed. Redeemed in your own currency.' Effect on each finding: (1) money transmission — a SINGLE-MERCHANT closed-loop card falls inside 31 CFR 1010.100(kkk) ('used only for goods or services in transactions involving a defined merchant or location'), removing the CVC characterisation; an OPEN-loop prepaid Visa does NOT, and reintroduces prepaid-access provider status once aggregate exceeds the (ff)(4) prepaid-program thresholds. (2) UAE — no longer a 'cash prize', so a DET permit becomes obtainable in principle. (3) TAX — no change; a gift card is a cash equivalent and IRS Instructions 1099-MISC box 3 covers 'prizes and awards that are not for services performed' identically, though the $2,000 threshold still makes it moot. (4) PRODUCT — the arithmetic: trophy floor 500 CP, a $5 trophy is 50,000 CP, which at the 500 CP/day ceiling (lib/quests.ts:159) is 100 consecutive perfect days. https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/31/1010.100 | https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i1099mec
  - *Implication:* The structure-around exists, is cheap, and is already in the enum — but it must be single-merchant closed loop, not an open-loop prepaid card, or it swaps one FinCEN category for another. The uncomfortable finding underneath: the redemption is ALREADY so slow that the 'REAL CASH' claim is near-theoretical. 100 days of flawless daily engagement — 20 actions a day including 3 ad clicks — for one $5 trophy. No Discord bot in the market holds a median user for 100 days. So the cash promise is doing marketing work, not economic work, which cuts both ways for the investor: it caps regulatory exposure at almost nothing, and it independently falsifies assumption A3 (that gamers want 5c/day enough to do 20 things). If nobody ever redeems, A8 is harmless and A3 is fatal. They cannot both be false in the company's favour.
- **[MATERIAL]** Bottom line — the licence stack ClusterGG actually needs is small and cheap IF it deletes trophy gifting, and unaffordable if it does not.
  - *Evidence:* Assembled from the primary sources above. US, gifting DELETED: FinCEN MSB registration arguably not required (no third-party transmission); FinCEN Form 107 registration is free if filed defensively; state MTLs arguably not required on the same reasoning; realistic cost = a written counsel opinion plus an AML-lite programme. US, gifting RETAINED: FinCEN MSB registration (31 U.S.C. §5330), AML programme under 31 CFR 1022.210, plus state MTLs at $250k–$435k+ direct / >$1M all-in / 3–18 months / $225k–$280k per year recurring, against a maximum worldwide payout obligation of $18.25 per gamer per year; 18 U.S.C. §1960 exposure of up to 5 years' imprisonment for operating without. UK: no FCA authorisation (EMRs 2011 'issued on receipt of funds' not met); no Gambling Commission operating licence (no payment to enter under Sch 2, no chance under s.14); gamers' £18.25/yr sits inside the £1,000 trading allowance so no UK reporting obligation arises for a non-UK payer. EU: no EMI, no CASP, no MiCA registration; consumer-law compliance only. UAE: DET/DED sales-promotion permit per promotion, modest official fees, but cash prizes generally refused — gift-card-only or don't launch. Saudi: no licensing route identified; recommend geo-block pending local counsel. https://www.fincen.gov/system/files/shared/FIN-2013-G001.pdf | https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/31/1010.100 | https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2005/19/section/14 | https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/HTML/?uri=CELEX:32009L0110 | https://www.litrg.org.uk/working/self-employment/trading-allowance
  - *Implication:* Three covenants convert A8 from an unassessed company-ender into a managed line item, and all three are cheap: (1) DELETE TROPHY GIFTING — kills the money-transmission trigger, the tax-threshold hole and the age-gate bypass in one commit; (2) BOARD-LEVEL PROHIBITION on any chance mechanic and on ever selling CP for money — keeps the company permanently outside AB 831, NY S5935A and the eight-state-and-growing sweepstakes regime; (3) GIFT-CARD-ONLY REDEMPTION IN THE GULF, single-merchant closed loop, with a DET permit per campaign. Total cost of the compliant path: a status opinion, an AML-lite programme, a Trolley contract they already have, and roughly 60 lines of deleted code. Total cost of the non-compliant path at their 10,000-gamer rung: more than the entire worldwide redemption liability. A8 is not unassessable. It is cheap to assess and cheaper to fix, and the fact that it is still marked UNASSESSED in docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:423 while the gifting feature ships in production is itself the diligence finding.
