- **[FAVOURABLE]** The gambling analysis is the part they were most afraid of and it is the part that is actually fine — there is no chance element and no way to buy CP, so the model fails the prize/chance/consideration test on the chance limb in every jurisdiction I checked.
  - *Evidence:* I grepped the whole repo for a monetary CP purchase path (buy/purchase/topup/checkout against CP) and for chance mechanics (random/shuffle/spin/wheel/loot/crate/raffle/draw/lottery/jackpot) in lib/. The only Math.random() uses are lib/providers/riot-verify.ts:40 (picking a summoner icon for account verification) and lib/creative-templates.ts:43 (an id generator). Every CP inflow is a deterministic quest award (lib/quests.ts); every trophy is a fixed CP price (lib/marketplace.ts priceOf). There is no CP-for-money storefront: the only Stripe/Payoneer surfaces are B2B brand INVOICES (lib/payments/stripe.ts, docs/PAYMENTS.md). Against the law: UK Gambling Act 2005 s.14(2)-(3) requires that 'persons are required to pay in order to participate' AND that prizes be 'allocated by a process which relies wholly on chance'; Schedule 2 para 5 confirms 'a reference to paying does not include a reference to incurring the expense, at a normal rate, of ... using any other method of communication'. The 2025–26 US sweepstakes-casino statutes are drafted around casino simulation, not esports: California AB 831 (approved 11 Oct 2025) reaches games that '(3) simulate[] gambling (slots, poker, lottery, bingo, sports wagering)'; NY S5935A (signed 5 Dec 2025) is likewise 'Limited to casino-style and simulated gambling games only'. UAE Federal Decree-Law 31/2021 defines gambling as agreements 'that obligate the loser to pay a predetermined amount to the winner' — nobody loses money on ClusterGG. Saudi Arabia distinguishes esports prize pools (legal, skill) from betting (illegal). https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2005/19/section/14 | https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2005/19/schedule/2 | https://leginfo.legislature.ca.gov/faces/billTextClient.xhtml?bill_id=202520260AB831 | https://www.nysenate.gov/legislation/bills/2025/S5935/amendment/A
  - *Implication:* A8's gambling half is materially LESS dangerous than the brief assumes, and this is worth saying plainly to an investor who has been primed for the opposite. The read-across from the sweepstakes-casino crackdown is weak on the current product. But it is one product decision away from being strong: add a loot box, a spin-the-wheel, a random trophy drop, or a paid CP top-up, and ClusterGG lands inside AB 831's 'dual-currency system' ('a coin, token, or other representation of value that may be purchased by a player or received through a bonus or promotion') with misdemeanour liability, $1,000–$25,000 per violation in CA and $10,000–$100,000 per violation in NY, extending to 'financial institution, payment processor, geolocation provider ... platform provider, or media affiliate'. Recommend a hard written product covenant: no chance mechanic, no CP sold for money, ever, at the board level.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** US 1099 reporting for gamers is a non-issue and will remain one — the 500 CP daily ceiling makes it arithmetically impossible for a gamer to reach the reporting threshold. This is the single strongest thing in ClusterGG's compliance posture and it happened by accident.
  - *Evidence:* IRS Instructions for Forms 1099-MISC and 1099-NEC (Rev. December 2026): 1099-NEC box 1a is filed 'for each person in the course of your business during the year to whom you have paid at least $2,000'; 1099-MISC box 3 is '$2,000 or more ... Also, enter in box 3 prizes and awards that are not for services performed.' The threshold rose from $600 to $2,000 for tax year 2026 under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (signed July 2025), indexed for inflation from 2027. Backup withholding at 24% under 31 U.S.C./IRC §3406 only bites on REPORTABLE payments where no TIN is furnished on Form W-9. Against that: maximum earn is 500 CP/day = $0.05/day = $18.25/year (lib/quests.ts:159; 10,000 CP = $1). Reaching $2,000 at the ceiling takes 109.6 years of unbroken daily play. https://www.irs.gov/instructions/i1099mec | https://www.irs.gov/businesses/small-businesses-self-employed/backup-withholding | https://www.avalara.com/blog/en/north-america/2025/07/one-big-beautiful-bill-act-1099-reporting-threshold.html
  - *Implication:* Compliance burden at 10,000 payees: ZERO 1099s, zero W-9s, zero backup withholding, zero TIN matching. At 1,000,000 payees: still zero, on earned CP alone. The brief asked me to quantify the burden and the honest answer is that it is nil — the ceiling that makes the unit economics tight is the same ceiling that makes the tax file empty. Two carve-outs: (a) SERVER OWNERS on revenue share are unbounded and will cross $2,000 — but docs/PAYMENTS.md already routes them through Trolley, which 'collects and e-files tax forms (W-9/W-8BEN)', which is the right answer; (b) lib/eligibility.ts:55 hardcodes US_REPORT_THRESHOLD = 600, which is stale for TY2026 — harmlessly conservative (it over-flags), but it is a live example of a compliance constant that nobody re-read after the law changed.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** Cluster Points are almost certainly NOT electronic money in the EU or UK, and almost certainly outside MiCA — the EU is the cleanest jurisdiction in the whole analysis, and for a reason the company can lock in permanently.
  - *Evidence:* EMD2 (Directive 2009/110/EC) Art. 2(2): "'electronic money' means electronically, including magnetically, stored monetary value as represented by a claim on the issuer which is ISSUED ON RECEIPT OF FUNDS for the purpose of making payment transactions ... and which is accepted by a natural or legal person other than the electronic money issuer." CP is never issued on receipt of funds — it is issued for completing quest actions, and I found no CP-for-money purchase path anywhere in lib/ or app/actions/. Because the definition is not met, the Art. 1(4) limited-network exclusion (which routes to PSD2 Art. 3(k)) is not even needed. MiCA (Regulation (EU) 2023/1114) Art. 3(1)(5) defines a crypto-asset as 'a digital representation of value or of rights that may be transferred and stored electronically, using distributed ledger technology or similar technology' — CP is rows in Postgres (lib/db/schema.ts), not DLT. Recital 17 provides a second, independent exclusion: 'digital assets that are accepted only by the issuer or the offeror and that are technically impossible to transfer directly to other holders should be excluded from the scope of this Regulation. An example of such digital assets includes loyalty schemes where the loyalty points can be exchanged for benefits only with the issuer or offeror of those points.' https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/HTML/?uri=CELEX:32009L0110 | https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/HTML/?uri=CELEX:32023R1114
  - *Implication:* No EMI licence, no CASP authorisation, no EU financial-services registration needed. Cost: zero, plus a memo. BUT note the trap: MiCA Recital 17's belt-and-braces exclusion turns on assets being 'technically impossible to transfer directly to other holders', and ClusterGG's trophy gifting is precisely such a transfer. The DLT limb should still hold, so this is a fallback that is broken rather than a live failure — but it is the third time in this analysis that gifting destroys a defence that would otherwise be free. The UK position tracks the EU: the Electronic Money Regulations 2011 use the same 'issued on receipt of funds' wording, so no FCA authorisation either.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** I found no evidence that any US state or federal regulator has ever taken a money-transmission enforcement action against a paid-to-engage rewards platform — and that negative result is the strongest single argument in ClusterGG's favour.
  - *Evidence:* I searched for enforcement actions, FTC settlements, state AG actions and money transmitter licensing against Prodege/Swagbucks, Mistplay and comparable rewards platforms — all of which have run cash-redeemable points economies at far greater scale and for far longer than ClusterGG, in some cases for over 15 years. I found nothing: no FTC case, no state AG action, no MTL enforcement, no FinCEN action. FinCEN maintains a public list of enforcement actions for failure to register as an MSB and no rewards platform appears in the search results for it. I am reporting this as sourced-negative rather than sourced-positive: absence of search results is weaker than a published statement, and I could not confirm whether these companies hold MTLs. https://www.fincen.gov/enforcement-actions-failure-register-money-services-business | https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prodege
  - *Implication:* Regulatory risk here is best modelled as latent rather than active: the law plausibly reaches this conduct, and no regulator has chosen to reach for it in a decade and a half of a large, visible, US-based comparable industry. For an investor that reframes A8 from 'this could end the company' to 'this is an uninsured tail risk with no observed base rate'. The honest caveat is that base rates change: the sweepstakes-casino sector also had no enforcement base rate until 2025, and then seven states acted in eighteen months. What made that sector actionable was CONSUMER HARM AT SCALE plus a clear casino analogy. ClusterGG has neither today. It acquires the first the day its unauthenticated ad beacon (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts) is discovered by a bot farm and CP starts being minted at industrial rates into cash — at which point the story stops being 'rewards app' and starts being 'we paid strangers for fraudulent ad clicks and wired them money without knowing who they were'. That, not a licensing letter, is what breaks first.
  - *Could not source:* 1) SAUDI ARABIA — I could not find any primary Saudi legal text. Every search returned gambling-affiliate SEO content (lightloom.co.uk, igamingtoday, ruthlessreviews, mentorforbes, nimbalord) which I will not cite as authority. Secondary sources consistently point to Sharia prohibition plus the Anti-Cyber Crime Law (Royal Decree No. M/17, 1428H/2007) as the enforcement hook and to CST content filtering, and consistently say esports prize pools are treated as lawful skill contests while betting i


#### B5 · Behavioural assumptions (15 screens, 5 cents) (13)

- **[FATAL]** The break-even equation the company says it runs on omits fill rate entirely, and once you put fill back in, the model is loss-making at ClusterGG's own stated assumptions. This is a one-line arithmetic error and it inverts the sign of the business.
  - *Evidence:* COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.3 states the governing equation as `minimum views per gamer per day = 50 ÷ CPM`, giving 10 views at $5 CPM, and concludes "We sell at $5, so we must deliver 10. We design for 15." That equation values every view at the full CPM — i.e. it assumes 100% fill. But the same document's own assumption A5 is "fill rate above 56%", and §3.3 states plainly that "Unsold inventory shows a house creative: zero revenue, full CP cost." The two are never multiplied together anywhere in the document. Do it: 15 views × $5/1000 × 0.56 fill = $0.042 revenue per gamer per day, against $0.05 of CP cost (500 CP ÷ 10,000 CP/$). That is a loss of $0.008 per engaged gamer per day and a gross margin of MINUS 19%. The true break-even condition is fill ≥ 50 ÷ (15 × CPM) = 66.7% at $5 CPM, 83.3% at their $4 hard floor, and 88.9% at the $3.75 Tier-3 price they plan to offer at scale (§3.2). Their stated assumption of 56% clears none of these. Sources for the cost side: DEFAULT_DAILY_CP_CEILING 500 and DEFAULT_CP_PER_DOLLAR 10,000 per lib/quests.ts and lib/marketplace.ts; §0 of the model confirms the intent ("$2,000 of revenue against $1,500 of points paid out" at 1,000 daily gamers — note $1,500 = 1,000 × $0.05 × 30, i.e. full 500 CP every day). Note also that §0's own worked example implies 4 × 100,000 ÷ 450,000 = 88.9% fill, so the document silently assumes 89% fill in one section and 56% in another.
  - *Implication:* At 1,000 DAILY gamers and 56% fill: revenue 450,000 × 0.56 × $0.005 = $1,260/month against $1,500/month of CP cost = a loss of $240/month. Because the company is correct that "cost scales linearly with revenue — the ratio is the same at every size", that negative ratio is negative at every size: -$2,400/month at 10,000 daily gamers, -$240,000/month at 1,000,000. Their own sentence "Scale is not the risk" is true and is precisely the problem — scaling a negative unit multiplies the loss. To reach break-even they must either lift fill from 56% to 66.7% (a 19% relative improvement in a market where they have zero signed deals) or cut the daily CP ceiling from 500 to 500 × 0.56/0.667 = 420 CP, or raise price above the $5 they have not yet proven anyone will pay.
- **[FATAL]** The four daily missions actually shipped in the code deliver an average of 9.75 brand-carrying screens per day, not 15 — below the company's own 10-screen break-even at $5 CPM, before any discount for engagement or fill.
  - *Evidence:* I counted the shipped templates in lib/missions.ts:51–113 against the model's own active/passive classification table (COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §2.1), which lists as PASSIVE: gain a follower, someone votes for your profile, every 25 profile views, receive a gift, a tracked stat rises, land a tracked match. Screens per template, excluding those passive actions: show-up 14, get-seen 10, give-back 17, bring-people 10 — mean 12.75. But 3 of the 20 actions in EVERY template are ad_click, and app/api/ads/go/route.ts shows an ad click is a 302 redirect to `schema.adCreatives.clickUrl`, i.e. off ClusterGG entirely: it opens no ClusterGG screen and therefore carries no ClusterGG creative. Excluding ad_click: show-up 11, get-seen 7, give-back 14, bring-people 7 — MEAN 9.75. Against §1.3's break-even of 10 views at $5 CPM. Two of the four templates deliver 7. Required CPM to break even on 9.75 screens at 100% fill = 50 ÷ 9.75 = $5.13, already above their $5 price and 28% above their $4 floor; at their stated 56% fill the required CPM is $9.16. Even on the generous count that credits ad_click as a screen (12.75), the required CPM at 56% fill is $7.00 — 40% above the price they intend to charge. Separately, three of the four templates breach the document's own 125 CP passive cap: show-up 150 CP passive, get-seen 250 CP, bring-people 250 CP. §2.2 admits "Two of the four shipped templates fail this today and are rebuilt" — it is three, and the rebuild has not happened, because as the lead reviewer established there is no active/passive flag in the code at all.
  - *Implication:* The 15-screen figure in the pitch is not a design target that the product missed; it is a number the shipped product contradicts by 35%. Every revenue figure in the ladder should be multiplied by 9.75/15 = 0.65 before any other adjustment. At 100% engagement and 100% fill — conditions that will never obtain — the shipped missions produce $0.04875 of revenue against $0.05 of cost: the product is loss-making in its best case. Two of the four templates (get-seen, bring-people) are structurally 30% below break-even and will run in half of all weeks under the ISO-week rotation in lib/missions.ts.
- **[FATAL]** The model prices its COST at 10.5% engagement and its REVENUE at 100% engagement. Applying one consistent assumption to both sides cuts the revenue ladder by 9.5× at every rung and makes the entry-level $500 product undeliverable until roughly 3,300 registered gamers.
  - *Evidence:* Cost side: lib/cp-economics.ts:51 ships DEFAULT_ASSUMPTIONS = { dailyActive: 0.35, capReach: 0.30 } — the file's own comment says "Every gamer maxes every action every day is the WORST CASE, not the forecast". Product: 0.35 × 0.30 = 0.105, i.e. the modelled cost is 10.5% of maximum. Revenue side: COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §3.3's ladder is built on 15 views per gamer per day with no engagement discount at all (1,000 → 450,000 views/month = 1,000 × 15 × 30). The two numbers describe the same population and are never reconciled. Note also what capReach=0.30 MEANS under the model's own guarantee: 30% of 500 CP is 150 CP, and since no action pays more than 25 CP (§2 Part 1), that is 6 actions — of which, at the shipped 9.75/20 screen ratio, roughly 2.9 open a screen. Applying one assumption to both sides (I ran this arithmetic in full):    ASSUMPTION SET                                 views/registered/mo   @1,000 gamers          registered gamers needed for ONE $500 package   Model as pitched (100% engage, 15 screens)     450.00                450,000 views = $2,250      222   Their own 0.35 × 0.30, 15 screens              47.25                 47,250 views = $236       2,116   Their own 0.35 × 0.30, shipped 9.75 screens    30.71                 30,712 views = $154       3,256   Realistic post-D30 (5% active × 0.30), shipped  4.39                  4,387 views = $22        22,792  The rebuilt ladder at their own assumptions: 1,000 gamers → $236 (claim $2,250); 10,000 → $2,362 (claim $22,500); 100,000 → $23,625 (claim $225,000); 1,000,000 → $236,250 (claim $2,250,000). Overstatement factor is exactly 1/(0.35 × 0.30) = 9.52× at every rung.
  - *Implication:* THIS IS THE NUMBER. The first rung of the ladder — "1,000 gamers = $2,250 inventory = 4 brands × $500" — is, on the company's own shipped engagement assumption, $236 of inventory: not four packages, not one package, less than half a package. They cannot deliver their own entry-level product until ~2,100 registered gamers (their assumptions, 15 screens), ~3,300 (their assumptions, shipped missions), or ~22,800 (realistic post-D30 activity). The pitch deck's first rung is off by between 9.5× and 103×. Any investor model built on the ladder should be re-run with views/registered-gamer/month between 4 and 47, not 450. Note the perverse consequence: the ratio-based margin is unchanged by engagement (that part of the doc is right), so the damage is entirely to SCALE — and scale is what determines whether a brand pays $5 CPM at all. A publisher offering 30,000 monthly impressions has no pricing power; the $5 CPM assumption and the engagement assumption fail together.
- **[SEVERE]** ClusterGG's effective hourly rate is $0.03–$0.45/hour. That is 0.4%–6.2% of the US federal minimum wage, 3.75% of the floor Prolific will not let a researcher pay, and below every get-paid-to platform I could find a number for — including the ones reviewers describe as exploitative.
  - *Evidence:* The offer is 20 discrete actions across ≥15 screens for 500 CP = $0.05 (10,000 CP = $1, lib/marketplace.ts). At 20 seconds per action including page load: 400s = $0.45/hr. At 30s: $0.30/hr. At 40s: $0.225/hr. But the SHIPPED missions are not UI taps — get-seen and bring-people each require play_session ×3 ("land a tracked match", lib/quests.ts) plus stat_levelup, i.e. three real ranked matches. At ~30 min per League of Legends game plus ~10 min of UI, that is ~100 minutes for the same $0.05 = $0.03/hour. Comparators, all sourced: US federal minimum wage $7.25/hr, unchanged since 24 July 2009 (https://www.dol.gov/agencies/whd/minimum-wage, accessed Aug 2026). Prolific's hard floor is "£6 / $8 per hour", recommended "£9 / $12 per hour" (https://researcher-help.prolific.com/en/articles/445230-prolific-s-payment-principles, accessed Aug 2026). Peer-reviewed crowdworking meta-analysis — Hornuf & Vrankar, Business & Information Systems Engineering 64(5):553–573, 30 Aug 2022, covering 22 studies / 105 wage observations / 76,765 data points / 22 platforms / 2009–2020 — microtask n-weighted mean $5.55/hr, and $4.07/hr once unpaid work is counted (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9425816). MTurk median ~$2/hr with only 4% of workers above $7.25/hr (Hara et al., CHI 2018, data 2014–17 — STALE, https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3173574.3174023). ILO: $4.43/hr across five microtask platforms, $3.31/hr net of unpaid work (https://gizmodo.com/un-study-finds-average-american-crowdworker-earns-less-1829233627, 2018 — STALE). Secondary-only, flagged: Freecash $2–$5/hr (https://dollarsprout.com/freecash-review/), Mistplay $1–$3/hr with a ~$550/yr cap (https://www.sidehustlenation.com/mistplay-review/), Swagbucks $1.50–$3/hr (https://sidehusl.com/swagbucks/), JustPlay $0.30–$0.80/hr with earnings that "drop sharply after the first week by design" (https://moneypantry.com/justplay-review/).
  - *Implication:* Assumption A3 ("gamers want 5c/day enough to do 20 things") is not merely untested — it asks for a rate 16× below the worst-paying serious microtask platform in the peer-reviewed literature ($4.07/hr net) and 4–25× below the worst-paying consumer rewards app I could find a figure for. The monthly ceiling is worse: $1.50/gamer/month at a PERFECT 500 CP every single day, versus $30–$80/month that casual Swagbucks and Freecash users actually report. ClusterGG's theoretical maximum is 2–5% of what a competitor's casual user earns in practice. And the first meaningful cash-out is a $5 trophy = 50,000 CP = 100 consecutive perfect days. The correct read is that ClusterGG is not competing with Swagbucks for beermoney users; at $0.03–$0.45/hr it is competing with nothing, because the rational user does literally anything else. Any investor model with a non-trivial capReach is assuming a population that is indifferent to money, in which case the CP payout is not the thing driving the behaviour and the entire cost line is being spent on nothing.
- **[SEVERE]** The cash proposition requires 100 consecutive perfect days to reach $5, against published retention where 75% of mobile games hold under 3% of users to day 28. The probability that a signup ever reaches a meaningful cash-out is well under 1%.
  - *Evidence:* Trophy floor is 500 CP (lib/marketplace.ts:102, `Math.max(500, ...)`); at 10,000 CP = $1 a $5 trophy is 50,000 CP, and at the 500 CP/day ceiling that is 100 days at 100% capReach. Retention benchmarks — GameAnalytics 2025 Mobile Gaming Benchmarks, 11,600 apps / 9 regions / 16 genres / 1.48bn MAU: D1 top-quartile 26.5–27.7% (down from 28–29% in 2023), bottom quartile 10–11.5%; D7 MEDIAN 3.42–3.94%, top quartile 7–8%, bottom quartile ~1.5%; "75% of projects have a D28 Retention lower than 3%" (https://www.gameanalytics.com/reports/2025-mobile-gaming-benchmarks, figures via https://gamedevreports.substack.com/p/gameanalytics-mobile-gaming-benchmarks). Cross-industry app benchmarks for 2025–26 run D1 25–26%, D7 11–13%, D30 5–7% (https://vmobify.com/blog/app-retention-benchmarks — secondary). Median daily playtime in mobile games is 22 minutes and median session 5–6 min across ~4 sessions/day (GameAnalytics, same source) — meaning ClusterGG's ~100-minute get-seen mission asks for 4.5× the median gamer's ENTIRE daily mobile gaming time, for five cents. For the reward-app category specifically: incent-heavy networks showed D30 retention of 1.1% against 2.4–4.4% for gaming apps generally (https://www.appsflyer.com/blog/mobile-marketing/rewarded-advertising-good-bad-ugly/ — published 16 May 2017, STALE and predating SKAdNetwork; directionally corroborated by JustPlay's documented decaying-payout design but not by any recent primary figure I could find).
  - *Implication:* Compounding a generous 5% D30 survival with the requirement of 100 CONSECUTIVE days at 100% capReach — a condition the shipped missions make structurally unreachable (see next finding) — puts the share of signups who ever redeem $5 at well below 1%. That is favourable for the CP liability (assumption A4, redemption below 100%, is safe and then some) and catastrophic for the acquisition story: the product's headline promise is one that essentially no user will ever experience, which is the profile that draws FTC deceptive-earnings-claim attention (cf. the $100m Walmart Spark judgment, Feb 2026, https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2026/02/walmart-agrees-100-million-judgment-settle-ftc-states-charges-over-deceptive-earnings-claims-related) and, more immediately, guarantees the word-of-mouth that kills a rewards platform.
- **[SEVERE]** capReach = 1.0 is not merely optimistic, it is unreachable by construction: every shipped mission contains tasks the gamer cannot perform unilaterally, and two of four require adding the bot to two servers you own, every single day.
  - *Evidence:* Reading lib/missions.ts:51–113 against lib/quests.ts weights and caps: get-seen requires profile_views_25 ×3 (75 other people must view your profile, in one UTC day) and profile_vote_received ×2; bring-people requires profile_views_25 ×3 and gift_received ×2; show-up requires follower_gained ×2. These are other people's actions, not the gamer's. get-seen and give-back each require bot_added ×2 — and the code's own comment at lib/quests.ts admits the constraint: "Self-limiting by nature, since you run out of servers you own." A gamer who administers one Discord server can never complete two of the four templates, on any day, ever. show-up and bring-people require botlist_vote ×2 against a 12-hour cooldown that the codebase itself documents (lib/botlists.ts: `VOTE_COOLDOWN_HOURS = 12`, "A vote can be cast every twelve hours on most lists"; corroborated externally — the standard top.gg cooldown is 12 hours, https://top.gg/bot/422087909634736160/vote), and `voteLinks()` only returns lists whose admin status is "live", so if fewer than two lists are live the second vote is impossible without a second session 12 hours later. Finally get-seen and bring-people require play_session ×3 plus stat_levelup ×2 — three completed tracked matches with a successful stat sync.
  - *Implication:* The mission mechanics are "explicitly designed to drive 100%" capReach, as the brief puts it, but the design cannot deliver it: on a conservative count, between 4 and 7 of the 20 required actions in each template are outside the gamer's unilateral control. This resolves the capReach question raised in the brief — 0.30 is closer to right than 1.0, and not because gamers are lazy but because the product forbids 1.0. It also means the distribution is not smooth: full-mission completion is gated by a conjunction of independent low-probability events (75 profile views AND 2 votes AND 3 matches AND 2 new servers), so it will be near-zero for the median user and concentrated in a tiny colluding tail — pairs of accounts gifting and following each other, which lib/quests.ts already flags as "an arbitrage a pair of accounts can farm". My defensible distribution for FULL daily-mission completion as a share of SIGNUPS: D1 ~0.75–1.25% (15–25% D1 active × ~5% full-cap reach, with day-1 one-off actions like connect_account and join_planet available); D7 ~0.25–0.5% (5–10% active × ~5%); D30 ~0.2–0.75% (2–5% active × 10–15%, higher conditional rate from survivor selection). The 5% full-cap-reach figure is an inference, not a benchmark — I could source no published daily-quest completion rate (see what_i_could_not_source).
- **[SEVERE]** Two of the four shipped missions require redeeming trophies, which costs more CP than the mission can possibly pay. A gamer who completes the daily mission runs a permanently negative CP balance and can never accumulate toward a cash-out.
  - *Evidence:* redeem_trophy pays 25 CP with a cap of 2/day (lib/quests.ts). The trophy floor price is 500 CP (lib/marketplace.ts:102, `return Math.max(500, Math.ceil(raw / 100) * 100)`). So one redemption is +25 CP earned and −500 CP spent: net −475 CP. The show-up template requires redeem_trophy ×1; the give-back template requires redeem_trophy ×2 (lib/missions.ts:56, 106). The daily earning ceiling is 500 CP (DEFAULT_DAILY_CP_CEILING). On a give-back day, a gamer who completes the mission earns at most 500 CP and must spend at least 1,000 CP to have done so — net −500 CP against a day whose maximum gross is +500. On a show-up day the net is −475 at best.
  - *Implication:* On the two templates that require redemption — half the rotation — completing the daily mission makes the gamer's CP balance monotonically non-increasing. The advertised path ("earn CP, buy trophies, redeem for REAL CASH") and the designed path ("complete the daily mission, keep the streak") are mutually exclusive: doing what the product asks you to do every day guarantees you never accumulate the 50,000 CP a $5 trophy needs. This is not a rounding problem, it is a sign error in the core loop, and it means either (a) the missions are uncompletable in practice, which caps views at well under 9.75/day, or (b) they are completable and the cash promise is false. Both readings are bad; they cannot both be avoided.
- **[MATERIAL]** The scaling ladder is ambiguous between registered and daily-active gamers, and the document uses both readings in the same argument. Under either consistent reading the top rung requires 3–5× more signups than stated.
  - *Evidence:* COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §0 says "At 1,000 DAILY gamers that is 450,000 views a month" (my emphasis) — the daily-active reading. §3.3's ladder column is headed simply "Gamers" and runs to 1,000,000, which in any investor deck reads as registered. The two cannot both hold: under the daily-active reading, 15 views/DAU/day requires capReach = 1.0, contradicting lib/cp-economics.ts:51's 0.30; under the registered reading it additionally requires dailyActive = 1.0, contradicting the same line's 0.35. Note that dailyActive = 0.35 is itself aggressive: it is DAU/registered, and published guidance puts world-class DAU/MAU at 20–30% (https://linkrunner.io/blog/metrics-that-matter-mobile-gaming-edition — secondary), with DAU/registered strictly lower than DAU/MAU. My plausible figures: views per DAILY-ACTIVE gamer per day = capReach 0.30 × 9.75 shipped screens ≈ 2.9 (range 2–4); views per REGISTERED gamer per day = × dailyActive 0.35 ≈ 1.02, or ≈ 0.15 at a realistic post-D30 5% activity rate. Against the model's assumed 15: an overstatement of 3.3× on the daily-active reading and 14.7× on the registered reading.
  - *Implication:* If "gamers" means daily-active, the 1,000,000-gamer rung needs ~3.3–5m registered accounts at a world-class 20–30% DAU/MAU — so the ladder's top rung is a 5m-signup business described as a 1m-gamer business. If it means registered, the ladder overstates inventory by 9.5× at their own assumptions (see Finding 3). The investor should require the company to restate every rung with the denominator named and the engagement multiplier shown explicitly, and should treat the current ladder as unusable. Concretely: to deliver one $500 package (100,000 views/month) requires ~2,100 registered gamers on the company's own assumptions with 15 screens, ~3,300 with the shipped 9.75 screens, and ~22,800 at a realistic post-D30 activity rate — against the 222 the pitched ladder implies.
- **[MATERIAL]** By the Media Rating Council's own definitions, 100% of ClusterGG's inventory is 'purchased traffic' and its paid ad_click is 'incentivized human invalid activity' — a named SIVT category. Nothing in the repository discloses this to brands.
  - *Evidence:* MRC Invalid Traffic Detection and Filtration Standards Addendum (June 2020 update; I decoded the PDF text directly from https://mediaratingcouncil.org/sites/default/files/Standards/IVT%20Addendum%20Update%20062520.pdf). Verbatim: SIVT key examples include "Incentivized human invalid activity: self-directed activity to benefit self or harm others and directed activity" and "Automated browsing from a non-dedicated device: ... as well as incentivized automated activity". On traffic sourcing, verbatim: "Purchased traffic represents traffic redirected from properties other than those owned by the entity acquiring the traffic or otherwise incentivized activity (users compensated for visiting)." The addendum's sourced-traffic table marks Purchased as "Yes for IVT and Audience" on required reporting segmentation — the only category so marked — and states "Additional filtration should be considered where unknown to buyers or not declared and must be filtered when otherwise obfuscated or where exhibiting robotic characteristics." ClusterGG's users are, by construction, compensated for visiting. I grepped the entire repo (docs/, lib/, app/) for "incentiv" and found no brand-facing disclosure anywhere: the only hits are internal fraud-risk commentary in EXECUTION_PLAN.md and COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md's own assumption A7. The brand-facing rate card (app/pricing/page.tsx, app/brands/) contains no incentivisation notice.
  - *Implication:* Any brand that buys through an agency with MRC-accredited verification, or that runs IAS/DoubleVerify/HUMAN on its creatives, will have ClusterGG's inventory segmented as purchased traffic at minimum and filtered as SIVT at worst — and "not declared" is explicitly the trigger for additional filtration. That is not a $5 CPM; that is a zero. The addendum's carve-out ("excludes cases where the entity paying for the incentive is the entity being promoted") does not obviously rescue ClusterGG, because the entity paying the CP is ClusterGG, not the brand. This should be reclassified from assumption A7's vague "platform risk" into a hard commercial gate: the company must be able to name one buyer who will pay $5 CPM for inventory disclosed as incentivised, and they cannot today. Caveat on staleness: the operative addendum is dated June 2020; I checked the 2024 IVT Interim Updates (https://mediaratingcouncil.org/sites/default/files/Standards/2024_IVT_Interim_Updates_FINAL.pdf) and decoded its full text — it does not mention incentivised traffic, so the 2020 language stands unamended.
- **[MATERIAL]** Paying 25 CP for an ad click manufactures a 12–20% CTR against a 0.46% display benchmark — a 26–43× anomaly the company's own document identifies as fraud, and which it ships anyway.
  - *Evidence:* All four shipped templates require ad_click ×3 (lib/missions.ts:61, 76, 91, 106), paying 25 CP each = 75 CP, i.e. 15% of the 500 CP daily ceiling is paid for clicking ads. ad_impression pays 1 CP capped at 25/day (lib/quests.ts:117), so a fully engaged gamer generates 3 clicks against 15–25 impressions = 12–20% CTR. Benchmark: display ads average ~0.46% CTR globally, with 0.5–1% considered good (https://focus-digital.co/average-display-ad-ctr-2025-benchmarks/ — secondary; corroborated by https://www.wordstream.com/blog/2025-google-ads-benchmarks). ClusterGG's own EXECUTION_PLAN.md:3611 states the case against itself: "against a real 0.05–0.35%; that is the signature of incentivised fraud", and COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:235 says it "gets us discounted to nothing by buyers and delisted by" [networks]. The model document says ad_click drops to 0 CP; the code does not (lib/quests.ts:118, still 25 CP, cap 3). On audience quality generally: incent-heavy networks showed D30 retention of 1.1% versus 2.4–4.4% for gaming apps (AppsFlyer, 16 May 2017 — STALE). The most-cited academic defence of incentivised advertising, Chiong, Yang & Chen, "Understanding the Effect of Incentivized Advertising along the Conversion Funnel" (arXiv:1709.00197), was WITHDRAWN at v3 on 2 Feb 2021 because "Section 5 of this draft contains an error that renders the estimation results incorrect" (https://arxiv.org/abs/1709.00197). And a vendor that sells rewarded traffic for a living, asked directly whether rewarded traffic is high quality, published an article containing zero comparative data — only generic industry retention figures it labels "not universal targets" (https://www.gamelight.io/post/is-rewarded-traffic-high-quality-what-advertisers-should-actually-measure, May 2026).
  - *Implication:* There is no body of evidence that paying users cash improves audience quality for advertisers; the one paper that argued a positive funnel effect is retracted, the retention evidence points the other way, and even sell-side vendors cannot produce numbers. Practically: the 75 CP/day spent on ad_click is 15% of the CP cost line buying inventory that does not exist — app/api/ads/go/route.ts redirects the gamer OFF ClusterGG to the brand's URL, so a paid click generates no ClusterGG screen and no ClusterGG creative, while terminating the session that would have produced further views. Removing ad_click from the missions (as the model document says has already happened) would cut CP cost by 15% and, per Finding 2, leave screens at 9.75 — improving unit economics by more than it costs. That this fix is written down, costed, and not shipped is the clearest available signal about the gap between the company's documents and its product.
- **[MATERIAL]** The headline '50% margin' is a markup on cost, not a margin on revenue. The correct figure at their own best-case inputs is 33%, and it is negative once fill is applied.
  - *Evidence:* COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.3: "We sell at $5, so we must deliver 10. We design for 15. At 15 the margin is 50%". The 50% is (15−10)/10 — markup on the break-even volume. The gross margin at 15 views and $5 CPM is: revenue 15 × $0.005 = $0.075, cost $0.05, margin (0.075−0.05)/0.075 = 33.3%. Applying the document's own 56% fill assumption takes it to −19.0% (Finding 1); applying the shipped 9.75 screens as well takes it to −83.2%.
  - *Implication:* A 50%→33% correction is survivable on its own; the point is what it reveals about how the model's numbers were assembled. Combined with the omitted fill multiplier (Finding 1), the 9.5× engagement inconsistency (Finding 3) and the 15→9.75 screen shortfall (Finding 2), this is a document where the four largest arithmetic decisions all err in the same direction. Independent errors do not do that. The investor should treat every unverified number in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md as directionally overstated until re-derived, and should specifically require the company to restate §1.3 as `revenue/gamer/day = screens × CPM/1000 × fill` with all three terms named.
- **[MINOR]** dailyActive = 0.35 in the shipped cost model is itself above world-class, so even the 'conservative' cost assumption is optimistic — and the cost model is the ONLY place the company has written down a realistic-looking number.
  - *Evidence:* lib/cp-economics.ts:51 sets dailyActive: 0.35, i.e. 35% of REGISTERED users active on a given day. Published guidance treats DAU/MAU above 20% as healthy and above 30% as world-class (https://linkrunner.io/blog/metrics-that-matter-mobile-gaming-edition — secondary), and DAU/registered is necessarily lower than DAU/MAU because the registered base includes all lapsed users. Against retention benchmarks (GameAnalytics 2025: D7 median 3.4–3.9%; D28 under 3% for 75% of projects), a mature ClusterGG cohort would show DAU/registered in the low single digits, not 35%. Even the generous cross-industry D30 of 5–7% is 5–7× below the shipped assumption.
  - *Implication:* Cuts both ways, and the investor should understand which. On the COST side this is comforting: real CP liability will be 5–7× lower than the shipped model projects, so the company will not be bankrupted by payouts. On the REVENUE side it is fatal, because inventory scales with the same term: at a realistic 5% active × 0.30 capReach × 9.75 shipped screens, views per registered gamer per month is 4.39, and one $500 package needs ~22,800 registered gamers. The company has, without noticing, written down a number that protects its balance sheet and destroys its revenue line, and has then declined to apply it to the revenue line.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** Three things in the model are correct and should be preserved in whatever gets rebuilt.
  - *Evidence:* (1) "Fill rate is the risk" — correct, and stronger than they state: the break-even condition reduces to fill ≥ 50 ÷ (screens × CPM), which is independent of population size, so the ratio genuinely is the same at every scale as §3.3 claims. (2) "CP is a liability, not a cash cost, until it is redeemed" (§1.1) — correct accounting, and Finding 5 shows redemption will be far below 100%, so assumption A4 is safe with wide margin. (3) The 25 CP per-action ceiling (lib/quests.ts) does genuinely bound the worst case at 20 actions for 500 CP; the arithmetic in §2 Part 1 holds. Separately, lib/cp-economics.ts's insistence on reporting worst case and forecast as two numbers rather than one is good practice and is the reason this contradiction was findable at all.
  - *Implication:* The framework is not the problem; three of its inputs are, and all three are measurable within one cohort. The company should be asked to instrument (a) actual screens rendered per gamer per day, (b) actual fill rate against a real signed brand, and (c) time-on-task for the daily mission — none of which is instrumented today. Those three numbers convert this from a model dispute into a fact. Until they exist, the correct posture is that ClusterGG has not measured the single quantity — views delivered — that its entire revenue line is denominated in.
  - *Could not source:* 1. PUBLISHED CHURN/RETENTION FOR GPT PLATFORMS. I could not source a single primary retention or churn figure for Swagbucks, InboxDollars, Freecash, ySense, Cash'em All or any GPT platform. Prodege (Swagbucks/InboxDollars/MyPoints) is private and publishes cumulative payout totals ($2bn+ lifetime) but no cohort data. Mistplay publishes a benchmarks blog about OTHER games, not about itself. This data does not appear to exist publicly. Anyone claiming a GPT churn benchmark to this investor is maki


#### B6 · Fill rate, sales effort & the ladder (13)

- **[FATAL]** ClusterGG's own break-even fill rate is 66.7%, but their stated assumption A5 is "fill rate above 56%" — they have written down a target that is BELOW the level at which the company loses money on every gamer.
  - *Evidence:* Model's own inputs: 15 views/gamer/day x 30 days = 450 views/gamer/month; at $5 CPM that is $2.25 revenue per gamer per month. CP cost is 500 CP/day = $0.05/day = $1.50/gamer/month, and §1.1 explicitly says "Worst case — 100% redemption at full value — is the number above, and it is the number to plan against." Break-even fill = 1.50 / 2.25 = 66.7%. Their own equation in §1.3 (min views = 50/CPM) says the same thing: at $5 CPM they need 10 of the 15 views sold = 66.7%. At the A5 figure of 56% fill, revenue is $1.26 against $1.50 cost — a gross margin of MINUS 19%. Required CPM as a function of fill: 100% -> $3.33, 75% -> $4.44, 66.7% -> $5.00, 56% -> $5.95, 40% -> $8.33, 25% -> $13.33, 10% -> $33.33. At their assumed 56% fill they must sell at $5.95 CPM — 19% above their list price and 49% above their own $4.00 hard floor. Model doc: /home/user/clustergg/docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.1, §1.3, §10 (A5).
  - *Implication:* A5 is not a conservative assumption, it is an arithmetic error. Every scenario the company has planned against is loss-making. At 1M gamers, 56% fill = -$240,000/month; 40% fill = -$600,000/month; 25% fill = -$937,500/month. There is no rung of the ladder at which 56% fill produces a profit.
- **[FATAL]** The ladder's own tier prices push break-even fill to 83–89%, i.e. the company must sell essentially ALL its inventory, forever, to make zero profit before paying a single salary.
  - *Evidence:* §3.2 sets Tier 2 at $2,000 = 500,000 views ($4.00 CPM) and Tier 3 at $7,500 = 2,000,000 views ($3.75 CPM). Recomputing break-even fill at each rung's own price: rung 1 ($5.00 CPM) 66.7%; rung 2 ($4.00) 83.3%; rung 3 ($3.75) 88.9%. Annual gross margin at 100% fill: rung 1 $9,000 (33%); rung 2 $36,000 (17%); rung 3 $225,000 (11%). Also note Tier 3's $3.75 CPM directly breaches the model's own §1.2 "Hard floor $4.00 CPM — the admin must refuse to save below it". I grepped the repo for any CPM floor enforcement: `grep -rn "cpm|CPM" lib app` returns only reporting/benchmark uses (lib/brand-report.ts, lib/pricing.ts) — there is NO floor check anywhere in the code.
  - *Implication:* The volume-discount ladder is self-defeating: every step up in scale lowers the CPM and therefore RAISES the fill rate required to break even. The business gets harder, not easier, as it grows — the exact opposite of the claim in §3 that "Scale is not the risk." And the $4 floor that is supposed to protect this is a sentence in a document, not a line of code.
- **[FATAL]** At 1,000 gamers the entire inventory is worth $27,000 a year. One fully loaded ad salesperson costs $105,000–$250,000. A direct-sold model is impossible at the bottom of the ladder by a factor of 4–9x on revenue and 12–28x on gross margin.
  - *Evidence:* Fully loaded seller cost: US BLS Occupational Outlook Handbook gives a median annual wage for advertising sales agents of $61,460 in May 2024 (https://www.bls.gov/ooh/sales/advertising-sales-agents.htm), 103,700 jobs, employment projected to DECLINE 6% 2024–2034. At a standard 1.3x loading for employer taxes, benefits, tooling and management allocation that is ~$80,000, and ~$105,000 once CRM/travel/recruiting amortisation is included. A seller capable of selling a novel unproven format to brands is a senior digital AE: The Bridge Group's 2024 SaaS AE Metrics & Compensation benchmark reports median OTE $190K at a 53:47 base:variable split, median quota ~$800K ACV, 51% of AEs hitting quota, 60-day median sales cycle (https://blog.bridgegroupinc.com/2024-ae-metrics-compensation-benchmark) — ~$250,000 fully loaded. UK equivalent: Glassdoor UK puts Media Sales Executive at ~£35,000 average (range £23k–£50k) (https://www.glassdoor.co.uk/Salaries/media-sales-executive-salary-SRCH_KO0,21.htm); with employer NIC at 15% above a £5,000 secondary threshold from 2025/26 plus auto-enrolment pension and tooling, ~£48,000–£55,000 loaded for a junior and £125,000–£165,000 for a senior. Rung-by-rung, assuming quota at 4.2x OTE (Bridge Group ratio) and the model's own deal sizes: 1,000 gamers, $27,000/yr revenue, $9,000 GM, 4 deals of $500/mo — one junior US rep = $105,000 = 389% of revenue and 1,167% of gross margin; one senior = $250,000 = 926% of revenue, 2,778% of GM. 10,000 gamers, $240,000/yr (10 x $2,000/mo), $60,000 GM — one junior rep = $105,000 = 175% of gross margin. 100,000 gamers, $2.7M/yr, $900,000 GM — 4 senior reps at quota = $1,000,000 = 111% of GM (8 junior reps = $840,000 = 93% of GM), before any sales manager, ad-ops or campaign-management headcount. 1,000,000 gamers, $27M/yr, $9M GM — 34 senior reps = $8.5M = 94% of GM.
  - *Implication:* The sales cost exceeds the gross margin at EVERY rung, including the top one, before a single engineer, server, lawyer or server-owner revenue share is paid. The crossover is not somewhere up the ladder — there is no rung where a direct-sold sales team fits inside the margin. Rung 1 must be founder-sold (4 deals a year is a founder's job, not a hire), which is fine — but it means there is no proof of a repeatable sales motion at the point where the company would be raising on the strength of one.
- **[FATAL]** "Cost scales linearly with revenue — the ratio is the same at every size" is false. CP cost scales linearly; sales cost is a step function with a minimum indivisible unit of one human that is 4x total revenue at rung 1.
  - *Evidence:* §3.3 of COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md asserts linear cost scaling and concludes "Scale is not the risk. Fill rate is." The model only counts CP payout as cost. But fill rate is not an exogenous variable — it is the output of sales effort, and sales effort is the largest cost in a direct-sold ad business and the one that does not scale linearly. Ratio of fully loaded sales cost to annual revenue at each rung, from the computation above: 389% / 44% / 31% / 31%. The ratio is emphatically NOT the same at every size. Additionally, the sales MOTION changes character three times up the ladder: $500/month is a self-serve/inside-sales price point; $7,500/month ($90k/yr) is mid-market direct; $270,000/yr average (which rung 4 requires — see below) is agency/holding-company enterprise sales requiring MRC-accredited viewability measurement, IAS/DoubleVerify pre-bid verification and third-party tag support. Benchmark for the middle of that range: B2B win rate ~20-21% overall and ~31% for SMB, 19% in 2025 down from 29% in 2024 (Ebsta x Pavilion, cited in Gradient Works' 2025 B2B Sales Performance Benchmarks, https://www.gradient.works/blog/2025-b2b-sales-performance-benchmarks); marketing & advertising sellers specifically show ~24% win rates on ~58-day cycles at ~$15,200 average deals (secondary benchmark aggregation, https://optif.ai/learn/questions/sales-cycle-length-benchmark/); SMB deals under $15K ACV close in 14–30 days.
  - *Implication:* At a 24% win rate a rep must work ~4.2 qualified opportunities to close one $500/month deal worth $6,000/yr. Rung 1's entire book is 4 deals = ~17 qualified opportunities per year. No hire is justifiable. Rung 3 requires 30 concurrent mid-market brand relationships renewed annually — roughly 125 qualified opportunities a year at a 24% win rate — which is 4–8 people, i.e. the whole gross margin.
- **[FATAL]** The programmatic fallback does not exist. Google's publisher policies prohibit exactly what ClusterGG has shipped, by name.
  - *Evidence:* Google AdSense's "Top invalid traffic and policy violations that lead to account closure" (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/2660562) contains, under the heading "Using an incentivized traffic source": "Publishers should not use third-party services that generate clicks or impressions such as paid-to-click, paid-to-surf, autosurf and click-exchange programs." And under "Encouraging users to support your site, YouTube channel, or app through ad interaction": "Publishers may not ask others to refresh or click their ads. This includes asking for users to support your site, YouTube channel, or app, offering rewards to users for viewing or clicking on ads, or performing searches and promising to raise money for third parties for such behavior." Google's invalid-traffic definition (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/16737) is "any clicks or impressions that may artificially inflate an advertiser's costs or a publisher's earnings", and the consequence is stated as account suspension or closure. ClusterGG's shipped code pays 1 CP per ad_impression capped at 25/day and 25 CP per ad_click capped at 3/day (lib/quests.ts:117-118), and ALL FOUR shipped daily-mission templates require ad_click x3 (lib/missions.ts:61,76,91,106) — 75 of the 500 daily CP, 15% of a gamer's income, is paid for clicking ads. This is a literal, self-documented paid-to-click program. Access is also gated on scale ClusterGG does not have: AdX is generally described as requiring ~5 million monthly page views for consideration (secondary: https://www.publisher-collective.com/blog/google-adx-vs-google-adsense) plus a clean policy history and a valid ads.txt. And AdSense requires that "you must own the site" you monetise.
  - *Implication:* The "Programmatic / affiliate fill — the remainder" product in §4 is not a lower-yield fallback; it is an application that would be rejected, and if somehow accepted would be terminated on the first invalid-traffic review. Worse, the paid-click mechanic contaminates the DIRECT business too: the model document itself concedes at §5.1 that a manufactured ~10% CTR "gets us discounted to nothing by buyers and delisted by networks" — yet the code still pays 25 CP per click. The fix identified in the doc has not been made.
- **[FATAL]** Roughly 60% of the claimed inventory is Discord in-message PNGs, which cannot be sold programmatically by anyone, ever — not at a low CPM, not at any CPM.
  - *Evidence:* From ClusterGG's own illustrative brand report in §7.3: Discord placements 18,400 + 12,900 + 9,100 + 4,200 = 44,600 views (59.5%); web placements 21,000 + 9,400 = 30,400 views (40.5%). The Discord half is structurally excluded from programmatic for four independent reasons. (1) Domain identity: ads.txt and sellers.json are domain-scoped — the IAB Tech Lab ads.txt spec authorises sellers for a publisher's own domain (https://iabtechlab.com/ads-txt/, spec v1.1 Aug 2022), and sellers.json exists to resolve the final seller of a bid request (https://iabtechlab.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/07/Sellers.json_Final.pdf). ClusterGG does not own discord.com and cannot publish an ads.txt for it. I checked: clustergg.com/ads.txt, /app-ads.txt and /sellers.json all return a 308 redirect with no file (curl, Aug 2026) — there is no ads.txt today even for the web half. (2) No tag: the creative is composited into a PNG server-side (lib/cards/ads.ts pickCardAd) and posted as a Discord embed image (lib/discord/ads.ts) — a bid response is HTML/JS/VAST, none of which Discord will execute. (3) No measurement: the MRC Viewable Ad Impression Measurement Guidelines require 50% of pixels in view for one continuous second for display (https://mediaratingcouncil.org/sites/default/files/Standards/081815%20Viewable%20Ad%20Impression%20Guideline_v2.0_Final.pdf), which is unmeasurable inside a Discord client; the model concedes this at §1.4 ("we cannot prove viewability inside Discord"). (4) Platform prohibition: Discord's Developer Policy (effective 1 Oct 2022, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/discord/discord-api-docs/48d3c2d324796d8236355262ace3b22c1d29acb0/docs/policies_and_agreements/Developer_Policy.md) states "Do not target users with advertisements or marketing", "Do not disclose API data to any advertising network, data broker, or other advertising or monetization related service", and "Do not attempt to manipulate engagement". Note also that the Discord posting path (lib/discord/ads.ts:80) logs ONE impression per guild per post, not one per member who saw it — the Discord "view" count is a post count, not an audience count.
  - *Implication:* "Programmatic fill" can address at most ~40% of the claimed inventory, and only if the web half can pass an AdSense/AdX policy review it cannot pass (see previous finding). The Discord half — the majority of the inventory and the entire differentiation story — is 100% direct-sold or worthless. There is no floor under it.
- **[FATAL]** Rung 4 of the ladder requires programmatic remnant to clear at a $6.00 CPM — 60% above ClusterGG's own tier-3 direct rate card. Realistic value is 1.4%–13.5% of the modelled number.
  - *Evidence:* §3.3 rung 4: 1,000,000 gamers, 450,000,000 views/month, $2,250,000 inventory value, "~100 brands + programmatic fill". At the model's own tier-3 package ($7,500 for 2,000,000 views), 100 brands = $750,000/month and consumes 200,000,000 views (44%). The residual $1,500,000 must therefore come from the remaining 250,000,000 views — an implied programmatic CPM of $6.00, versus a tier-3 direct price of $3.75. Remnant inventory sold blind through an exchange is worth a fraction of direct, never a multiple. What it is actually worth: the ANA Programmatic Media Supply Chain Transparency Study (December 2023, https://www.adslot.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/ana-programmatic-media-supply-chain-transparency-study.pdf) found that of $1,000 entering a DSP, "$710 reaches the seller as revenue", with "transaction costs: primarily DSP and SSP costs, accounting for 29 percent of the original ad dollar" and that "only 36 cents of every ad dollar that enters a DSP effectively reaches the consumer" — explicitly EXCLUDING agency fees, so the true figure is lower. The ANA's Q2 2025 Programmatic Transparency Benchmark (released 11 Aug 2025) puts the TrueCPM index at 36.5%, down from 37.8%, with $26.8bn of global media value unrealised — so the tax has not improved. On the gross side, Digiday reported average open-marketplace CPMs for mid-to-large publishers of $1.45 (Jan 2020), $1.41 (Jan 2021), $1.41 (Jan 2022), $1.21 (Jan 2023), with publisher RPMs down 20–55% YoY (https://digiday.com/media/the-programmatic-open-marketplace-is-faltering-but-publishers-see-a-bright-spot-in-private-programmatic-deals/, 1 Feb 2023 — STALE, flagged). Gaming is among the lowest-yielding verticals. AdSense pays publishers 68% of Google Ads display revenue / 80% after the advertiser platform fee (https://support.google.com/adsense/answer/180195). Modelling the web-eligible 40.5% of the residual at net CPMs of $0.20/$0.50/$1.00/$2.00 yields $20,250 / $50,625 / $101,250 / $202,500 per month against $1,500,000 modelled — 1.4% to 13.5%.
  - *Implication:* Rung 4 is short by $1.30M–$1.48M per month, or $15.6M–$17.8M per year, at 1,000,000 gamers. Since CP cost at that scale is $1.5M/month, the shortfall alone converts a claimed $750,000/month profit into a $750,000/month loss. The top of the ladder is where the model is most wrong, not least.
- **[FATAL]** Even taking every one of ClusterGG's own assumptions at face value, the top of the ladder yields a 33% gross margin on $27M of revenue that a 34-person sales organisation consumes 94% of.
  - *Evidence:* Full sensitivity at the model's own 15 views/gamer/day and $5 CPM (monthly revenue / gross margin / margin %): 1,000 gamers — 100% fill $2,250/$750/33%, 75% $1,688/$188/11%, 66.7% $1,500/$0/0%, 56% $1,260/-$240/-19%, 40% $900/-$600/-67%, 25% $563/-$938/-167%, 10% $225/-$1,275/-567%. 10,000 gamers — 100% $22,500/$7,500/33%, 75% $16,875/$1,875/11%, 56% $12,600/-$2,400/-19%, 40% $9,000/-$6,000/-67%, 25% $5,625/-$9,375/-167%, 10% $2,250/-$12,750/-567%. 100,000 gamers — 100% $225,000/$75,000/33%, 75% $168,750/$18,750/11%, 56% $126,000/-$24,000/-19%, 40% $90,000/-$60,000/-67%, 25% $56,250/-$93,750/-167%, 10% $22,500/-$127,500/-567%. 1,000,000 gamers — 100% $2,250,000/$750,000/33%, 75% $1,687,500/$187,500/11%, 56% $1,260,000/-$240,000/-19%, 40% $900,000/-$600,000/-67%, 25% $562,500/-$937,500/-167%, 10% $225,000/-$1,275,000/-567%. At a realistic blended $2.00 CPM (a small direct book at $4–5 plus a programmatic remainder at $0.20–1.00 net) the business is loss-making at 100% fill at every rung: -$600 / -$6,000 / -$60,000 / -$600,000 per month, a -67% margin, because break-even fill would need to be 166.7%. At the Digiday open-marketplace $1.21 CPM it is -175% at 100% fill (break-even fill 275.5%).
  - *Implication:* The margin is a knife-edge that only exists in a narrow band: fill above ~85% AND CPM at or above $5 AND 15 views delivered AND redemption at 100% assumed. Miss any one and it inverts. The honest headline number for an investor is: at the model's own most optimistic settings, 1,000,000 gamers produce $9,000,000 of annual gross margin, of which a 34-rep sales team consumes $8,500,000, leaving $500,000 to fund all engineering, infrastructure, legal, compliance, payments, fraud and the server-owner revenue share.
- **[SEVERE]** The ladder is arithmetically oversold at rungs 2 and 3 on ClusterGG's own package definitions — by 11% and 33% respectively.
  - *Evidence:* Rung 2: 10,000 gamers = 4,500,000 views/month. The stated shape is "10 brands x $2,000", and §3.2 defines Tier 2 as $2,000 = 500,000 views. 10 x 500,000 = 5,000,000 views promised against 4,500,000 available — oversold by 500,000 views (11%), and revenue is $20,000/month, not the $22,500 in the "inventory value" column. Rung 3: 100,000 gamers = 45,000,000 views/month. Stated shape "30 brands x $7,500"; Tier 3 = $7,500 = 2,000,000 views. 30 x 2,000,000 = 60,000,000 views promised against 45,000,000 available — oversold by 33%. The $225,000 revenue figure only matches the inventory column because the inventory is valued at $5 CPM while the packages sell at $3.75 CPM; the two columns of the same table describe different quantities of inventory. Source: /home/user/clustergg/docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §3.2, §3.3.
  - *Implication:* The ladder table cannot be reproduced from the model's own package definitions. An investor doing this arithmetic in a diligence meeting will conclude either that the numbers were never checked or that the inventory column is decorative. Either conclusion is fatal to the credibility of the whole document. Note also that the controls meant to prevent overselling — §6.1 "Live inventory / Headroom / The alert / The brake" and §6.2 "Danger zones" — do not exist: `grep -rni "fillRate|viewsTarget|deliveredViews|headroom|oversold|pacing"` across the repo returns zero relevant hits.
- **[SEVERE]** The precedent is unambiguous and it is a graveyard: every business that has paid consumers for engagement funded by DISPLAY advertising has failed, and the category is on the SEC's investor-alert list.
  - *Evidence:* AllAdvantage: launched 31 March 1999 on "Get paid to surf the Web", raised nearly $200 million, exceeded 10 million members in 18 months, delivered more than 4 billion ads in November 1999 alone, paid out over $160 million to members, and shut down in February 2001 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AllAdvantage). Widely reported (secondary, https://paleoadtech.com/2023/12/10/45-jim-jorgensen-the-amazing-dot-com-adventure-of-alladvantage/) that in a single quarter it paid $40 million to users against $9 million of advertiser revenue — a 4.4:1 cost-to-revenue ratio. Note the scale comparison: ~4 billion ads to ~4 million members is ~1,000 impressions per member per month, i.e. ~33 views/member/day — more than DOUBLE ClusterGG's designed 15 — and it still could not cover a $12.50/month payout cap. AGLOCO revived the model in 2007 and was in TechCrunch's deadpool within a year. The category's second act was outright fraud: SEC v. Rex Venture Group / ZeekRewards, a $600 million online pyramid and Ponzi scheme shut down in 2012 where "the company's revenues would soon fall below the level of its cash payouts" (https://www.sec.gov/newsroom/press-releases/2012-2012-160htm); SEC v. Traffic Monsoon, 162,000 investors and ~$207 million of "AdPacks" with over 99% of distributions funded by newer investors. The SEC now publishes a standing Investor Alert, "Beware of Paid-To-Click (PTC) Schemes" (7 November 2017, https://www.investor.gov/introduction-investing/general-resources/news-alerts/alerts-bulletins/investor-alerts/investor-21), whose red flags include "Easy money — be skeptical if you are offered high returns in exchange for merely purchasing products or for trivial tasks such as clicking on a certain number of online ads" and "No revenue from genuine products or services".
  - *Implication:* ClusterGG is not novel; it is the third generation of a model with a 25-year unbroken failure record, and the failure mode is precisely the one its own arithmetic already exhibits: payouts outrunning advertiser revenue. An investor should assume the SEC alert language will be quoted back at them by a journalist, a payment processor's risk team, or a regulator. The company also has no defence prepared: A8 ("paying gamers is not a regulated activity") is marked UNASSESSED.
- **[SEVERE]** Discord is simultaneously the platform, the competitor and the regulator of this inventory — and it launched a directly competing rewarded-ads product with its own points currency while ClusterGG was building.
  - *Evidence:* Discord launched Quests (users earn rewards for watching in-app video ads) in 2024, expanded Video Quests to mobile in June 2025 as its first mobile ad format (https://discord.com/press-releases/announcing-quests-advertising-on-mobile), launched Arena Quests in October 2025 rewarding time spent playing sponsored games (https://variety.com/2025/gaming/news/discord-arena-quests-ad-sponsored-games-1236536780/), and globally launched Orbs, its own virtual reward currency redeemable in the Discord Shop and for Nitro (https://www.marketingdive.com/news/discord-strengthens-pitch-to-advertisers-with-new-orbs-virtual-reward/753332/). Meanwhile the Discord Developer Policy binding ClusterGG's bot says "Do not target users with advertisements or marketing" and "Do not attempt to manipulate engagement", including "mass creation of user accounts to redeem rewards".
  - *Implication:* The brand budget ClusterGG intends to capture is being sold by Discord directly, with first-party measurement partners, at scale, to the same gaming advertisers. ClusterGG's pitch is therefore "buy an unmeasurable PNG in someone else's client" against "buy a measured, rewarded video from the platform owner". A2 ($5 CPM achievable) is not merely unsigned — it is being competed against by the landlord. This makes A7 (Discord permits incentivised bot engagement at scale) worse than unmitigated: Discord now has a commercial motive to enforce the clause.
- **[SEVERE]** Fill rate cannot be measured, managed or defended, because none of the machinery described in §6 exists in the codebase.
  - *Evidence:* §6.1 specifies a sales cockpit with live inventory, headroom, an alert at 100,000 spare views, a brake on overselling, per-brand delivery pacing and a break-even-CPM readout; §6.2 specifies danger-zone panels for oversold, views-per-gamer below 10, fill below 50%, and any campaign under the $4 CPM floor. `grep -rni "fillRate|fill_rate|viewsTarget|views_target|deliveredViews|headroom|oversold|pacing"` across /home/user/clustergg returns no matching implementation. `grep -rn "cpm|CPM"` returns only benchmark/reporting uses. lib/ads.ts:33-104 serves by priority-then-weight sort with no budget, no delivered counter and no stop-at-target; lib/cards/ads.ts:101-124 selects by hash rotation. The model itself concedes at §5.3: "We cannot currently deliver a promised number of views to anybody."
  - *Implication:* Fill rate is the single risk the model names ("Scale is not the risk. Fill rate is.") and it is the one number the product cannot compute. A brand cannot be shown a delivery report that reconciles, sales cannot be told how much is left to sell, and the investor cannot be shown a fill-rate series. Every fill figure in any future deck will be an estimate produced by hand.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** The one version of this model that DOES work in 2025-26 is funded by performance budgets (CPI/CPA), not display CPM — and ClusterGG has chosen the single funding source that has never worked.
  - *Evidence:* Prodege (Swagbucks, InboxDollars) announced it expected to deliver over $250 million of revenue to mobile gaming clients in 2025 through REWARDED USER ACQUISITION, with 20m+ MAU, 300+ gaming studio clients, gaming revenue doubled year on year, and $2.4bn awarded to members since 2005 (https://www.prodege.com/resources/press-release/gaming/prodege-to-deliver-over-250-million-in-revenue-to-mobile-gaming-clients-in-2025-through-rewarded-user-acquisition/, 30 Jan 2025). Mistplay operates the same way. The economics are visible in the eCPM gap: rewarded video clears ~$16–20 eCPM in the US and offerwall placements clear $400–$500+ eCPM (secondary aggregations: https://blog.playio.co/rewarded-ad-benchmarks-2026, https://torox.io/blog/offerwall-ecpm-benchmarks-by-vertical-2026-data-report/) — 3–100x ClusterGG's $5 — precisely BECAUSE the advertiser is paying per install or per completed action out of a user-acquisition budget, not per impression out of a brand-awareness budget. Discord itself has taken this route: Video Quests and Arena Quests reward users with Orbs for opt-in ad experiences, with an AppsFlyer/Gamesight measurement partnership, ~70 campaigns in the first year, a reported ~10% acceptance rate, and a cited 30% reduction in cost-per-install for one advertiser (https://discord.com/press-releases/discord-launches-newest-ad-format-and-partners-with-appsflyer-gamesight-for-ads-measurement, Oct 2025).
  - *Implication:* ClusterGG's own "lead product" — sponsored challenges measured in entrants and completions — is the CPA product that could actually clear the $1.50/gamer/month cost, because a gaming CPE runs in dollars not fractions of a cent. The $5 CPM display layer is the part that cannot, and it is the part the entire model document is built on. The strategic recommendation is to invert the model: price on entrants/completions and treat impressions as a free value-add. That also removes the Google policy exposure, because there is no ad network in the loop.
  - *Could not source:* (1) A published, primary Discord-community advertising rate card. Every CPM figure I found for Discord sponsorships ($15-30 per 1,000 engaged members, $200-500 per announcement for a 10k-member server) comes from SEO-driven monetisation blogs with no methodology, sample or date discipline. The model's §1.4 comparables table ('Discord / community sponsorships $5-$15') is therefore UNSOURCEABLE in either direction — I can neither confirm nor refute it, and neither can they. A2 remains genuinely un


#### B7 · CP liability, breakage & redemption (14)

- **[FATAL]** A 50,000 CP floor for the first $5 is a 100-day time-to-first-reward, which is roughly 800x worse than the direct comparables and makes the program a non-payer by construction.
  - *Evidence:* lib/marketplace.ts DEFAULT_CP_PER_DOLLAR = 10000 and priceOf() -> a $5 bronze trophy is 50,000 CP; lib/quests.ts DEFAULT_DAILY_CP_CEILING = 500. 50,000/500 = 100 days at a perfect daily maximum, and the code's own comment concedes it: "A hundred days at the ceiling: far, expensive, and reachable". Comparables: Mistplay's minimum is $5 for gift cards / $10 PayPal with users typically reaching $10 "in three to four hours of focused play"; Swagbucks cashes out from $3-$5 (secondary aggregation, 2026: https://www.eneba.com/hub/play-to-earn/mistplay-how-to-earn-money/ ; https://visu.network/blog/mistplay-vs-swagbucks/ ; https://unstar.app/blog/does-mistplay-justplay-cash-giraffe-copper-actually-pay-reward-game-apps-2026). Against the SHIPPED assumption in lib/cp-economics.ts:51 (dailyActive 0.35 x capReach 0.30), a gamer earns 52.5 CP per calendar day, so the first $5 arrives after 952 calendar days = 2.6 years.
  - *Implication:* The product's headline promise — "redeemable for REAL CASH" — is, on the company's own shipped engagement assumption, a 2.6-year promise. Every competitor pays in hours. This is not a pricing nuance; it is the single largest determinant of both retention and legal exposure, and it is set by one constant that no one has stress-tested against a comparable.
- **[FATAL]** Under plausible retention the probability that a signup ever reaches the first cash-out is 0.9%-2.8%, implying 97-99% breakage — far outside every published loyalty benchmark and outside the range any auditor would accept as a going concern for a rewards product.
  - *Evidence:* Gaming-app D30 retention benchmarks for 2025-2026 sit at 4-8% (aggregators citing AppsFlyer State of App Marketing 2025 and Adjust Mobile App Trends 2026: https://apsteq.com/blog/app-retention-benchmarks/ ; https://uxcam.com/blog/mobile-app-retention-benchmarks/ ; https://segwise.ai/blog/mobile-gaming-app-user-retention-strategies). Fitting a standard power-law survival curve to D1=27%, D30=5% gives S(t)=0.27*t^-0.496, hence S(100)=2.75%, S(365)=1.45%, and ~9.95 active days per install in year one. Monte-Carlo over 120,000 simulated signups accumulating CP until they hit 50,000: (a) a user who maxes 500 CP on every retained day reaches the first cash-out 2.78% of the time; (b) at 70% active x 60% of cap, 1.81%; (c) at the shipped 35% x 30% assumption, 0.94%. Published loyalty breakage benchmarks: retail 15-30%, hospitality 15-25%, airline 40%+, with "most healthy programs 5-30%" and "above 40-50% usually means your rewards are not compelling or redemption is too difficult" (https://www.voucherify.io/glossary/loyalty-breakage ; https://www.leat.com/resources/insights/loyalty-program-breakage ; the widely-cited 30%/41% aggregate figures trace to Bond Brand Loyalty and are secondary — I could not obtain the primary report).
  - *Implication:* Implied breakage is 97-99%. That is roughly double the level the loyalty industry uses as its "program is broken" threshold. The A4 assumption is technically satisfied (redemption is indeed below 100%) but for the reason that destroys the business rather than the reason that saves it: nobody gets paid, so nobody stays, so nobody generates the views that are the entire revenue line.
- **[FATAL]** The breakage trap is not a trade-off the company can optimise out — at ClusterGG's parameters both horns are loss-making, and the horn they are currently on is the one that kills the revenue line, not the cost line.
  - *Evidence:* Model arithmetic per gamer-day at the doc's own numbers: revenue = 15 views x $5 CPM / 1000 = $0.0750; CP face = 500/10,000 = $0.0500; CP is 66.7% of gross revenue. HORN 1 (high breakage, current design): cash payout is trivial ($1,807/yr at 10,000 plan-rate gamers at 99% breakage) but retention collapses. To hold a steady-state DAU with ~9.95 active days per install requires 1,005 new signups/day (30,148/month) at 10,000 DAU and 10,049/day (301,481/month) at 100,000 DAU. At even $0.50 CPI that is $15,000/mo and $150,000/mo of acquisition spend — against gross revenue of $22,500/mo and $225,000/mo, and acquisition appears NOWHERE in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md. HORN 2 (fix breakage by making the reward reachable, i.e. cut the trophy floor ~10x to a Mistplay-comparable): breakage falls toward the retail 15-30% band and cash cost becomes 70-85% of $0.05/gamer/day = $0.035-$0.0425 against $0.075 of revenue at 100% fill — a 43-53% gross margin BEFORE the company's own assumed fill rate. At the doc's A5 floor of 56% fill, revenue is $0.042/gamer-day against $0.05 of CP face: gross margin is MINUS 19%. Separately, the doc's own break-even equation (50/CPM = 10 views at $5 CPM, against 15 designed) puts break-even fill at 10/15 = 66.7%, which is 10.7 points ABOVE the 56% floor A5 asserts — an internal inconsistency I could not reconcile from the document.
  - *Implication:* At 10,000 gamers the choice is between $180,000/yr of unpaid, accruing, on-demand liability plus ~$180,000/yr of unmodelled acquisition spend (horn 1), or ~$153,000/yr of real cash payouts against ~$184,000/yr of revenue at the assumed 56% fill, i.e. negative gross margin (horn 2). At 100,000 gamers multiply both by ten: $1.8M/yr of accrued liability plus ~$1.8M/yr of acquisition, or ~$1.53M/yr of cash payouts against $1.84M of revenue. There is no parameter setting in the current design where the CP economics and the retention economics are simultaneously positive.
- **[FATAL]** The plan cost number and the shipped cost number differ by 9.5x, and an investor must underwrite the HIGH cost with the LOW revenue — the two numbers are not alternatives, they are the two ends of a scissor.
  - *Evidence:* COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md §1.1 charges the full 500 CP/day ceiling to every daily gamer: $0.05/day, $1.50/gamer/month. lib/cp-economics.ts:51 ships `DEFAULT_ASSUMPTIONS = { dailyActive: 0.35, capReach: 0.30 }` and `expectedDailyCost()` multiplies by both, giving an effective 10.5% of the ceiling. Computed: 1,000 gamers $1,500/mo plan vs $158/mo shipped; 10,000 $15,000 vs $1,575; 100,000 $150,000 vs $15,750; 1,000,000 $1,500,000 vs $157,500 — a constant 9.52x gap. But views are produced by the same actions that mint CP, so the same 10.5% multiplier applies to the revenue side: at 10,000 gamers the ladder's 4,500,000 views/mo ($22,500) becomes 472,500 views/mo ($2,362); at 100,000, 45,000,000 ($225,000) becomes 4,725,000 ($23,625). The doc's own claim "Cost scales linearly with revenue — the ratio is the same at every size. Scale is not the risk" is exactly why this cuts both ways.
  - *Implication:* The correct underwriting position is asymmetric and unfavourable in both directions. Revenue must be underwritten at the SHIPPED engagement assumption, which is one tenth of the ladder: the 100,000-gamer rung is worth $23,625/month, not $225,000/month — it does not clear a single $7,500 brand deal thirty times over, it clears three. Cost must be underwritten at the PLAN ceiling, because the 15-screen guarantee and the 125 CP passive cap that were supposed to bound the CP-per-view ratio are unimplemented (no active/passive flag exists in lib/quests.ts) and ad_click still mints 25 CP x 3/day for a click, so CP can be earned faster than screens are opened. The gap between the number the deck shows and the number the code assumes is a factor of 9.5 in one direction on cost and a factor of 9.5 in the other direction on revenue.
- **[SEVERE]** "CP is a liability, not a cash cost, until it is redeemed" is not a defensible accounting policy under either IFRS or US GAAP, and no auditor would sign it.
  - *Evidence:* Under ASC 606-10-55-46 to 55-49 and IFRS 15, an obligation to deliver something in exchange for points is a contract liability recognised when the points are ISSUED; breakage is a reduction of that liability recognised in proportion to the pattern of actual redemptions, never at issuance and never at grant (https://www.revenuehub.org/article/unexercised-rights ; https://viewpoint.pwc.com/dt/us/en/pwc/accounting_guides/revenue_from_contrac/revenue_from_contrac_US/chapter_7_options_to_US/74unexercised_rights_US.html ; ASC 606-10-55-48 as summarised at https://legalclarity.org/accounting-for-accounting-for-breakage-revenue-under-asc-606/ and https://dart.deloitte.com/USDART/home/codification/revenue/asc606-10/roadmap-revenue-recognition/chapter-8-step-5-determine-when/8-8-customers-unexercised-rights-breakage). Starbucks — the closest widely-disclosed analogue — records loaded value as deferred revenue at load and recognises breakage "proportional to stored value card redemptions, based on historical redemption rates", carrying ~$1.6bn of stored-value liability (secondary summary of the SBUX 10-K, https://www.restaurantdive.com/news/strategic-organizing-center-asks-sec-to-look-at-starbucks-gift-cards/636853/). Delta's FY2025 10-K defers SkyMiles revenue and adjusts equivalent ticket value "for Points that are not expected to be redeemed (breakage)" — i.e. the liability exists at issuance and breakage only reduces it (https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/27904/000002790426000013/dal-20251231.htm, filed Feb 2026). Grep of the entire repo for `breakage|escheat|deferred revenue|IFRS|ASC 606|liabilit` returns ZERO hits in lib/finance.ts, lib/admin-metrics.ts, lib/cp-economics.ts or any doc — there is no outstanding-CP metric anywhere in the product. The only place the word appears correctly is a code comment in lib/payments/tremendous.ts ("the liability is still ours until they do"), which directly contradicts docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md line 41.
  - *Implication:* Every margin figure in COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md is computed on a cash-basis policy that fails audit. On an accrual basis at the plan issuance rate, the P&L carries $15,000/mo of CP expense at 10,000 gamers and $150,000/mo at 100,000 — against claimed revenue of $22,500/mo and $225,000/mo. The claimed "50% margin" is in fact a 50% mark-up = 33% gross margin before the liability is even discounted for breakage, and there is no ledger in the product from which an auditor could compute the accrual. Expect a going-concern-adjacent audit finding at the first real close, and a restatement risk if the company has been reporting the cash number to investors.
- **[SEVERE]** ClusterGG cannot claim breakage credit at all, because ASC 606 requires historical redemption experience it does not have, and the variable-consideration constraint forces the zero-breakage path.
  - *Evidence:* ASC 606-10-55-48 requires an entity to apply the constraint on variable consideration (606-10-32-11 through 32-13) before it may assume it is entitled to any breakage; if it cannot, 55-48 forces the alternative path — recognise only "when the likelihood of the customer exercising its remaining rights becomes remote" (https://www.revenuehub.org/article/unexercised-rights).
