Practitioner guidance dated 26 May 2026 states auditors expect "at least two years of historical redemption data, preferably three to five" for a defensible breakage model, and that "recognizing breakage income in advance of the redemption pattern it represents is a common audit flag", with the SEC having issued comment letters where breakage assumptions diverged from redemption trends (https://blog.brandmovers.com/what-cfos-need-to-know-about-loyalty-program-liability-in-2026). ClusterGG has zero redemption history — lib/stuck-money.ts and app/actions/trophies.ts show the redeem flow exists but there is no historical redemption dataset, and the trophy floor of 50,000 CP means no user can have completed a cycle in under 100 days of perfect play.
  - *Implication:* For the first 2-3 years the company must carry CP at 100% of face value with no breakage offset. That is precisely the "worst case" number the model calls unrealistic, imposed as the mandatory accounting base case: $180,000 of accrued CP expense in year one at 10,000 plan-rate gamers, $1.83M at 100,000. Assumption A4 ("redemption is below 100%") is described in the doc as "conservative direction" — it is the opposite: it is the assumption the accounting standard refuses to let them take.
- **[SEVERE]** There is no reserve, no escrow, no float and no segregated account anywhere in the product; the payout rail hard-fails at a zero balance, so a redemption rush is a hard stop, not a cash-flow squeeze.
  - *Evidence:* lib/payments/tremendous.ts line 105 sends orders with `payment: { funding_source_id: process.env.TREMENDOUS_FUNDING_SOURCE_ID || "balance" }`. Tremendous's own documentation: "The most common funding model is to maintain a balance, and use that to pay for orders placed via API" and "When an account's prefund balance is negative, orders cannot be placed until the account is brought back into good standing" (https://developers.tremendous.com/docs/paying-for-orders ; https://help.tremendous.com/hc/en-us/articles/41472293589267-Account-balance-prefunding — the help article itself 403s to automated fetch; the quoted rule is from the search index of that page and from https://developers.tremendous.com/reference/funding-sources). Credit cards cannot fund API orders. lib/prepay.ts is brand-side invoicing only — it invoices brands at campaign purchase and blocks new publishing on non-payment; it segregates nothing and it explicitly states "Prizes already won are always honoured" without any funding mechanism behind that promise. lib/stuck-money.ts is a read-only admin report. lib/payments/vendors.ts:3 states plainly "Cluster does not hold funds". The only throttle on a rush is app/actions/trophies.ts approveRedeem(), a manual staff button.
  - *Implication:* Rush cash requirement after 12 months of operation, computed as an invariant (CP face is 66.7% of gross revenue by construction, so the ratio holds at any engagement level): 7.9 months of gross revenue if 1% has been redeemed, 5.6 months if 30% has. In absolute terms at 12 months: $180,675 at 10,000 plan-rate gamers, $1,806,750 at 100,000 plan-rate gamers, $18,971 / $189,709 at the shipped engagement rate. That money must land in a Tremendous prefund balance by ACH or wire before a single payout clears. The company has no reserve line item and no cash-flow model containing one. In a shutdown or press-cycle scenario the practical outcome is that staff stop clicking approve — which is exactly the conduct the regulators have named.
- **[SEVERE]** Withholding or slow-walking earned redemptions in a rush is the specific fact pattern the FTC has already sued over and the CFPB has already circularised — and ClusterGG's Terms of Service contain nothing to fall back on.
  - *Evidence:* FTC v. Tapjoy (7 Jan 2021): a mobile ad platform "promised gamers in-app rewards for completing advertising offers made by its partners, but then often didn't deliver"; the FTC charged deception under Section 5(a) and the order requires monitoring, complaint investigation and clear disclosure of reward conditions (https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2021/01/ftc-requires-mobile-advertising-company-stop-misleading-users-about-game-rewards). NOTE: this is a 2021 action — flagged as pre-2024 and therefore stale as a statement of current enforcement priorities, though the consent order stands. CFPB Circular 2024-07 (18 Dec 2024) holds that rewards operators risk unfair or deceptive acts when they materially reduce the value of rewards consumers have already earned ("bait-and-switch"), when systems failures cause consumers to lose points on redemption, and when rewards are revoked "based on buried or vague conditions", including catch-all language like "gaming" or "abuse" left to operator discretion (https://www.consumerfinance.gov/compliance/circulars/consumer-financial-protection-circular-2024-07-design-marketing-and-administration-of-credit-card-rewards-programs/). The Circular survived the CFPB's 12 May 2025 mass withdrawal of 67 guidance documents — Morgan Lewis confirms it "has not been withdrawn or rescinded" (https://www.morganlewis.com/pubs/2025/06/cfpb-revokes-guidance-in-sweeping-rollback-of-agency-policies-and-priorities, 4 Jun 2025; withdrawal notice at https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2025/05/12/2025-08286/). Meanwhile app/legal/terms/page.tsx is 42 lines, "Last updated: July 2026", and contains NO mention of Cluster Points, trophies, redemption, cash value, expiry, forfeiture, eligibility, or governing law — greps for `expire|forfeit|no cash value|jurisdiction|governing law` return nothing, and grep for `cluster point` returns nothing.
  - *Implication:* The company operates a cash-convertible virtual currency with zero contractual terms governing it. It has no expiry clause (so the liability is perpetual), no forfeiture clause (so lib/stuck-money.ts's UNCLAIMED_HOLD_DAYS=90 forfeiture of a dead user's cash-valued trophy has no contractual basis at all), and no admin-discretion clause it could even try to hide behind. Circular 2024-07's scope is credit-card issuers, so it does not bind ClusterGG directly — but it is the clearest current statement of how a consumer regulator reads exactly this conduct, and Tapjoy shows the FTC will use Section 5 against a non-financial rewards platform. Litigation and enforcement risk here is unpriced and unreserved.
- **[MATERIAL]** Cluster Points are almost certainly NOT exempt from US unclaimed-property law, because the statutory carve-outs for loyalty points and in-game currency switch off precisely when the balance is monetizable — which is ClusterGG's entire pitch.
  - *Evidence:* Illinois RUUPA 765 ILCS 1026/15-102 defines "Loyalty card" as a record given without direct monetary consideration "which may be used or redeemed only to obtain goods or services or a discount on goods or services" and states "The term does not include a record that may be redeemed for money or otherwise monetized by the issuer." "Game-related digital content" is content existing only in a game or platform and "does not include an item that the issuer permits to be redeemed for use outside a game or platform for money... or otherwise monetizes for use outside a game or platform" (https://www.ilga.gov/documents/legislation/ilcs/documents/076510260K15-102.htm ; https://codes.findlaw.com/il/chapter-765-property/il-st-sect-765-1026-15-102/). Wisconsin's RUUPA carries the identical carve-outs at §177.01(11d) and §177.01(7b) (https://docs.legis.wisconsin.gov/statutes/statutes/177/i/01). These exclusions derive from the ULC's 2016 Revised Uniform Unclaimed Property Act, under which "stored value cards are escheatable where they are redeemable for money or may be monetized by the owner, whereas loyalty cards and game-related digital content are excluded" (https://www.lexology.com/library/detail.aspx?g=3b1091a2-95a2-41bc-a207-15dae0eed167). ClusterGG's chain is CP -> trophy with a stated dollar `value` -> real cash via Tremendous; app/actions/trophies.ts and the marketing copy both describe it as money. Dormancy for gift certificates/stored value is 3 years in ~12 states and 5 years in Delaware, DC, Georgia, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, Oklahoma and Utah, at FULL face value in most (Missouri 60%, North Carolina 60% where an expiration date exists); exempting states include AZ, CT, FL, IN, KS, MD, ND, OH, OR, RI, SC, WA, WI (https://unclaimed.org/property-type-gift-certificates/, NAUPA). New York requires escheatment of the full remaining face value with no balance retention (https://sovos.com/tax-reporting/unclaimed-property-laws-by-state/new-york/).
  - *Implication:* A held trophy with a dollar `value` in `userTrophies.status = 'held'`, and an `approved`-but-unpaid row in `trophyRedeems`, are textbook "fixed and certain interest in intangible property held... in the course of a holder's business". At the plan issuance rate and 98% breakage, roughly $1.79M of face value per year at 100,000 gamers goes unredeemed and begins entering dormancy 3-5 years later. Because ClusterGG collects country only at first redemption and has no address for the overwhelming majority of users, the second priority rule from Texas v. New Jersey, 379 U.S. 674 (1965) sends the whole pool to the holder's state of incorporation (https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/379/674/ ; https://www.govinfo.gov/app/details/USREPORTS-379/USREPORTS-379-674/context). If that is Delaware — the most aggressive audit state, 5-year dormancy, full face value — the exposure is the entire unredeemed balance plus interest and estimation-based assessment for the years with no records. I could not determine ClusterGG's legal entity or state of incorporation from the repo: there is no entity name, company number or governing-law clause anywhere in app/legal/.
- **[MATERIAL]** ASC 606-10-55-49 destroys the breakage argument in exactly the jurisdictions where escheatment bites: the unredeemed amount becomes a liability to the state instead of revenue.
  - *Evidence:* "if breakage amounts will be owed to the state, then a company should follow the normal process of accounting for breakage amounts with one major difference: when the entity would normally recognize revenue, a liability for breakage amounts payable to the government should be recognized instead" — ASC 606-10-55-49, as set out at https://www.revenuehub.org/article/unexercised-rights. The two-path structure and the escheat carve-out are also described at https://viewpoint.pwc.com/dt/us/en/pwc/accounting_guides/revenue_from_contrac/revenue_from_contrac_US/chapter_7_options_to_US/74unexercised_rights_US.html and https://dart.deloitte.com/USDART/home/codification/revenue/asc606-10/roadmap-revenue-recognition/chapter-8-step-5-determine-when/8-8-customers-unexercised-rights-breakage.
  - *Implication:* The entire commercial thesis rests on breakage being the company's to keep. For the US share of the user base sitting in non-exempting states, it is not: the unredeemed balance converts from "free margin" into a payable to a state treasury. There is no scenario in the model where the company keeps 97-99% of issued CP as profit. Whatever share of users is in the 3-year and 5-year states is a straight deduction from the only source of margin the design actually produces.
- **[MATERIAL]** lib/stuck-money.ts forfeits a departed user's cash-valued trophy after 90 days — a private extinguishment of a monetizable balance that no US state's dormancy regime recognises and no ClusterGG contract authorises.
  - *Evidence:* lib/stuck-money.ts:30 `export const UNCLAIMED_HOLD_DAYS = 90;` and the `owner_gone` branch (lines 124-156) sets the recommended action to "Forfeit it — the hold has run and there is nobody to pay." State dormancy periods for the analogous property class are 3 years (AK, AR, IA, KY, LA, MI, MT, SD, TX, VT, WV, WY) or 5 years (DE, DC, GA, MS, NJ, NM, NY, OK, UT) — https://unclaimed.org/property-type-gift-certificates/. New York permits no balance retention at all (https://sovos.com/tax-reporting/unclaimed-property-laws-by-state/new-york/). app/legal/terms/page.tsx contains no forfeiture, expiry or dormancy clause of any kind. Note that stuckTotal() also deliberately EXCLUDES `no_payout_preference` balances from "the one number: dollars currently in limbo" on the reasoning that "nothing is owed and nothing expires" — so the admin dashboard's headline stuck-money figure systematically understates what is actually owed to users.
  - *Implication:* A 90-day forfeiture of property whose statutory dormancy is 3-5 years, unsupported by any term of service, is both an unclaimed-property compliance failure and a UDAP exposure of the exact type CFPB Circular 2024-07 describes (revoking earned rewards on conditions not disclosed to the consumer). It also means the single admin number intended to answer "is any money stuck" is defined so as to exclude a category of genuine obligation.
- **[MATERIAL]** Redemption approval is a manual human action with no funding check, so the system will happily approve payouts it cannot fund and record them as failures against the user.
  - *Evidence:* app/actions/trophies.ts: `approveRedeem()` at line 217 sets status to "approved" and emails the user "Your $X payout is approved" with no query against the Tremendous balance; the actual `payer("rewards")` call happens later at line 260 in a separate release step, and `releaseRedeem` requires status === "approved" first. lib/stuck-money.ts's `failed_payout` branch exists precisely to surface rows where "a redemption failed at the provider", storing `failedReason`. Tremendous rejects orders when the prefund balance is negative (https://developers.tremendous.com/docs/paying-for-orders ; https://developers.tremendous.com/reference/funding-sources).
  - *Implication:* Under a rush the sequence is: approve, email the user a promise, attempt the order, get rejected for insufficient prefund, write a failure row. The user has a written confirmation of an approved cash payout that did not arrive. That is the highest-quality evidence a plaintiff or a regulator could ask for, generated automatically by the product, at scale, at the exact moment the company is least able to respond. A balance pre-check before approval is a one-day fix and its absence is the clearest single indicator that no one has modelled the failure mode.
- **[MINOR]** Redeeming a trophy itself pays 25 CP, twice a day — the payout event mints new liability.
  - *Evidence:* lib/quests.ts action catalog: `{ key: "redeem_trophy", label: "Redeem a trophy", group: "ascension", defaultWeight: 25, defaultCap: 2 }`. There is no offsetting debit; lib/marketplace.ts is explicit that "Balance is EARNED minus SPENT, computed from two separate places" and that `getTotalCp` "only ever goes up".
  - *Implication:* Small in absolute terms (50 CP = $0.005/day) but structurally wrong: the act of discharging a liability creates a new one. In a redemption rush this is mildly reflexive — every payout adds a fraction back to the outstanding balance. More importantly it shows the CP catalogue was designed without any concept of a liability ledger at all, which is the same root cause as the missing accrual, the missing reserve and the missing outstanding-CP metric.
- **[FAVOURABLE]** The one favourable finding: the exchange rate itself is defensible, and the code author documented the derivation honestly.
  - *Evidence:* lib/marketplace.ts DEFAULT_CP_PER_DOLLAR was repriced from 1,000 to 10,000 with the reasoning written out: at the old rate "a gamer doing everything the product asks, every day, cost $1.26 a day... At a million gamers that is $1.26M/day. The number was never survivable; it only looked survivable because nobody had multiplied it out." lib/quests.ts documents the ad_impression weight being cut from 5 CP to 1 CP because at 5 CP the payout was "$0.50 per 1,000 impressions against a floor CPM of $0.50 — every cent of the ad revenue, straight back out", and credits a test suite (tests/db/cp-economics.mts) with catching it pre-ship. lib/cp-economics.ts separates worst case from forecast by design and refuses to report them as one number.
  - *Implication:* The unit rate is not the problem and the team demonstrably can do this arithmetic when they look at it. The problem is that the same discipline was never applied one level up — to the accrual, the reserve, the escheat exposure or the time-to-first-reward. That is a fixable management gap rather than an inability, and it is the reason the trophy-floor question is worth putting to the founders directly before writing off the model.
  - *Could not source:* 1. THE CENTRAL ACCOUNTING QUESTION FOR THIS EXACT FACT PATTERN. Every source I found on loyalty accounting assumes points are issued to a customer in connection with a revenue contract, which is what makes them a "material right" and a separate performance obligation under IFRS 15 / ASC 606. ClusterGG's gamers pay nothing — the paying customers are the brands. I could NOT source an authoritative pronouncement on how to account for points issued free to non-paying users who can convert them to ca

### 9.3 What was tested and held (clean bills)

The most valuable part of a DD report is what the reviewer tried to break and could not. These are the code paths a skeptic attacked and found sound. Where a favourable point is offset by a nearby defect, that is noted.

*47 items where an attack was attempted and the code held.*


**Ad delivery:**

- **Claim tested:** The Discord sponsor-button click count can be forged by a third party against a competitor.
  - *Attack:* Attempted to construct a /api/ads/go?cc=<victim ccId> request without a valid signature, and looked for a bypass in the verification path.
  - *Why it held:* lib/cards/ad-click.ts:31 signs `${ccId}|${guildId}` with signPayload, and app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33 rejects any request failing verifyClick before touching the database — it redirects home rather than inserting. safeTarget (lib/cards/ad-click.ts:55-66) also correctly blocks non-http(s) schemes and re-reads the destination from the DB rather than the query string (app/api/ads/go/route.ts:40-46), so this is not an open redirect either. This one path is done properly. Note it does NOT protect the web click beacon, which is unsigned (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48-58) — that is reported as a finding.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cards/ad-click.ts:31, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:38-40, app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33-46, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:55-66`
- **Claim tested:** A cache HIT on the Discord card path re-serves a stale brand while skipping the impression log (the failure docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:264-265 asserts).
  - *Attack:* Traced the full cardRef → withCardAd → getOrRenderCard → logCardAdImpression sequence looking for a branch where the cached URL is returned without an impression write, or where the cached PNG's baked-in brand differs from the logged one.
  - *Why it held:* The ad is picked BEFORE the cache is consulted and its id is part of the cache key (lib/discord/cards.ts:115-116), so a hit can only return the same creative's own render. logCardAdImpression is called at lib/discord/cards.ts:120-121, outside and after the getOrRenderCard call, on every path where an ad was picked — hit, miss, and null alike. The doc is wrong about its own code here. (The genuine drift is on the live-route fallback, which I report separately.)
  - *Cites:* `lib/discord/cards.ts:115-116, lib/discord/cards.ts:120-125, lib/cards/cache.ts:35-52`
- **Claim tested:** The challenge-delivery ledger double-counts reach when an announcement cron retries or staff re-publish.
  - *Attack:* Looked for a path where recordDeliveries could insert a second row for the same (challenge, guild) and inflate the summed member count, and checked whether the ending/result posts are added to launch reach.
  - *Why it held:* schema.challengeDeliveries carries uniqueIndex("cdel_once_idx") on (challengeId, guildId, kind) (lib/db/schema.ts:1273) and the insert uses onConflictDoNothing (lib/challenge-delivery.ts:56). deliveryTotalsFor additionally filters to kind = "launch" only (lib/challenge-delivery.ts:98), so the ending and result fan-outs cannot triple the number. The idempotency and the launch-only rule are both correct. The number they protect is the wrong number (guild membership, not views) — that is finding #2 — but the ledger mechanics themselves are sound.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:1272-1275, lib/challenge-delivery.ts:56, lib/challenge-delivery.ts:96-99`
- **Claim tested:** Replacing a creative destroys a brand's accumulated impression history.
  - *Attack:* Checked whether adCampaignCreatives rows are deleted on replacement, which would cascade-delete adImpressions (lib/db/schema.ts:544 has onDelete cascade).
  - *Why it held:* The retirement pattern is implemented: rows are marked retiredAt rather than deleted (app/actions/brand-portal.ts:59-62, lib/db/schema.ts:538-539), serveAds excludes retired rows via isNull (lib/ads.ts:65), and the brand portal deliberately sums impressions across retired rows too so the placement total does not drop (lib/brands.ts:78-81, 112-116). This was a real bug once and it is genuinely fixed.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:521-540, lib/ads.ts:65, app/actions/brand-portal.ts:59-62, lib/brands.ts:78-81`
- **Claim tested:** The /api/ads/serve in-memory cache causes brands to be served after their campaign ends or before it starts.
  - *Attack:* Checked whether the 60-second TTL cache (app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:12-33) can outlive a campaign's endDate boundary in a way that materially over-delivers.
  - *Why it held:* TTL is 60s (line 14) and the browser Cache-Control is max-age=30 / s-maxage=60 / SWR=120 (line 37), so worst-case over-serve past endDate is on the order of three minutes. That is immaterial next to the fact that there is no volume target to over-serve against at all. Not a finding on its own.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:12-14, app/api/ads/serve/route.ts:37, lib/ads.ts:69-70`


**Impression integrity:**

- **Claim tested:** The Discord sponsor-button click path can be forged to run a competitor's click count.
  - *Attack:* Tried to construct /api/ads/go?cc=<victim campaignCreativeId> and drive their click count and CPC.
  - *Why it held:* app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33 rejects the request unless verifyClick passes, and lib/cards/ad-click.ts:38 checks an HMAC over `${campaignCreativeId}|${guildId}` minted server-side at button-build time (lib/cards/ad-click.ts:31). The redirect target is also re-read from the DB rather than taken from the query string (app/api/ads/go/route.ts:40-46) with safeTarget rejecting non-http(s) schemes (lib/cards/ad-click.ts:55-66), so it is not an open redirect either. This one path is done correctly — which is precisely why the unsigned web click branch at app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:48 is a defect rather than an oversight.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/ads/go/route.ts:33, app/api/ads/go/route.ts:40, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:31, lib/cards/ad-click.ts:38`
- **Claim tested:** The demo seeder's fabricated 30-day impression series leaks into production and inflates the counts brands and investors see.
  - *Attack:* seedAdDelivery (lib/db/seed-activity.ts:516-556) writes ~30 days of synthetic impressions with hashedIp 'demo-N' and durationViewedMs 800-9800 into the real ad_impressions table, guarded only by a count>0 check (line 517) — and ad_impressions has no is_demo/source column to distinguish them afterward. I tried to establish it runs against a real database.
  - *Why it held:* seedDemoActivity is invoked only from the PGlite in-memory branch of createDb (lib/db/index.ts:949-950), which is reached only when process.env.DATABASE_URL is unset (lib/db/index.ts:911). The DATABASE_URL path calls ensureProvisioned -> seed(db, {demo:false}) (lib/db/index.ts:902) and never touches seed-activity. There is also a second guard: seedDemoActivity returns early unless the five demo users exist (lib/db/seed-activity.ts:62). It holds — CONDITIONAL on the production deployment actually setting DATABASE_URL. I could not verify that from the repo, and if clustergg.com runs without it, every impression, invoice and payout number on the live site and in the data room is seeded fiction (see notes).
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/seed-activity.ts:516, lib/db/seed-activity.ts:517, lib/db/seed-activity.ts:62, lib/db/index.ts:902`
- **Claim tested:** A server owner can inflate impressions to increase their own payout.
  - *Attack:* Traced whether guild-attributed impressions (ad_impressions.guild_id, lib/db/schema.ts:556; counted per guild at lib/discord/guilds.ts:431) feed any money path. A malicious owner CAN mint unlimited impressions by spamming /cluster commands — every render calls cardRef -> logCardAdImpression (lib/discord/cards.ts:121) and there is no per-user, per-guild or per-command cooldown on interactions (the only cooldowns, lib/discord/audience.ts:110-145, apply to bot announcements, not to command renders).
  - *Why it held:* It does not pay them — today. lib/server-earnings.ts computes owner earnings purely from the sponsored-challenge platform fee apportioned by entrants (lines 4-31, 42, 50); no code multiplies guild impressions by anything. So this is not a self-enrichment vector. It IS still a pure delivery-fraud vector against the advertiser: unlimited free impressions charged to a brand's contracted 100,000 views, at the cost of typing a slash command, with no billable cap (docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:267 requires one; none exists). The moment the promised ad-revenue share (lib/discord/ads.ts:64) is actually implemented, this becomes a direct money-printing bug.
  - *Cites:* `lib/discord/cards.ts:121, lib/discord/audience.ts:110, lib/server-earnings.ts:42, lib/db/schema.ts:556`
- **Claim tested:** The web ad_impression quest reward is large enough to be the primary self-inflation incentive.
  - *Attack:* Cross-referenced lib/quests.ts:117 (ad_impression, defaultWeight 1, defaultCap 25) against the beacon's award call (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:36) and the exchange rate (10,000 CP = $1, tests/db/cp-economics.mts:58).
  - *Why it held:* Maxing ad_impression pays 25 CP = $0.0025/day. That is genuinely too small to motivate a human, and the comment at lib/quests.ts:112-116 shows the team caught and priced this deliberately against a $0.50 floor CPM. Credit where due. But it does not clean the count: the award is irrelevant to the fraud because the beacon needs no session at all (app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:19) — an attacker with no account and no CP motive inflates the count for free. The incentive that IS live and damaging is ad_click at 25 CP x cap 3 (lib/quests.ts:118), giving a structural ~12% CTR. Also note the quest dedup on (quest, action, refType, refId) at lib/quests.ts:509-513 is vacuous for impressions, since refId is a freshly-minted uid per row — only the daily cap binds.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:112, lib/quests.ts:117, lib/quests.ts:118, lib/quests.ts:509`
- **Claim tested:** There is a test suite protecting impression measurement integrity.
  - *Attack:* Searched all 33 files in tests/db/ for any coverage of ad_impressions, the beacon endpoint, dedup, viewability or delivery counting.
  - *Why it held:* It does not hold — there is none. Only two test files mention ads at all (tests/db/cp-economics.mts, tests/db/missions.mts) and both test CP pricing arithmetic against the ACTION_CATALOG, not measurement. tests/db/cp-economics.mts:66-72 checks that CP paid per 1,000 impressions stays under a $0.50 floor CPM — it validates the cost side of an impression and never validates that an impression is real. There is no test asserting the beacon rejects unauthenticated writes, no test asserting dedup, no test asserting a viewability threshold. The measurement layer that the entire revenue model rests on has zero test coverage.
  - *Cites:* `tests/db/cp-economics.mts:66, tests/db/cp-economics.mts:70`


**CP ledger:**

- **Claim tested:** The dedup unique index actually exists and is not defeated by Postgres NULL semantics.
  - *Attack:* I expected the classic hole: a unique index over nullable columns where NULLs compare as distinct, making dedup a no-op. quest_events.ref_type and ref_id ARE nullable (lib/db/schema.ts:858-859), and qe_dedup_idx is declared without NULLS NOT DISTINCT (lib/db/index.ts:102), so the hole is real at the schema level. I then checked whether awardQuestAction can ever write NULLs into those columns.
  - *Why it held:* It cannot. lib/quests.ts:509-510 coerces both to the empty string before use — `const refType = ref?.refType ?? ""; const refId = ref?.refId ?? "";` — and line 520 writes those coerced values. Empty strings compare equal, so the unique index does bite for every award the engine writes, including the no-ref case. A caller passing no ref at all therefore gets exactly one lifetime award for that (user, quest, action), which is the intended behaviour for a once-ever action and is not a global no-op. Sequential retries genuinely cannot double-pay. The index is the one piece of real enforcement in this file — which is also why finding 12's swallowed CREATE INDEX matters.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:509, lib/quests.ts:510, lib/quests.ts:520, lib/db/index.ts:102`
- **Claim tested:** Within a single awardQuestAction call, two quests listening to the same action cannot both be paid.
  - *Attack:* I traced the loop at lib/quests.ts:496-532 looking for a path where the `paid` flag fails to suppress the second listener — an early `continue` that skips the assignment, an exception between iterations, or a reset.
  - *Why it held:* `let paid = false` is declared once outside the loop (495) and `paid = true` at 517 executes unconditionally on the same line as the payment computation at 516, before any await. Every `continue` in the loop body (498, 505, 515) occurs strictly before line 516, so no path can pay twice without setting the flag. The stable sort at 486-487 also makes 'which quest gets paid' deterministic across environments. This part of B34.2 is correctly implemented. It is worth being precise that this holds only WITHIN one call — it provides no protection whatever across concurrent calls, which is finding 1.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:486, lib/quests.ts:495, lib/quests.ts:498, lib/quests.ts:505`
- **Claim tested:** The spendable wallet balance is derived from the ledger, not from quest progress, so the wallet itself is not inflated by the progress multiplier.
  - *Attack:* Having found that QuestView.totalCp double-counts (finding 10), I checked whether that inflated number leaks into anything spendable — cpWallet, the marketplace affordability check, or the account-deletion payout figure.
  - *Why it held:* It does not. cpWallet calls getTotalCp (lib/marketplace.ts:139), which sums CP_PAID_SQL over quest_events (lib/quests.ts:692-693) and never touches user_quest_progress. marketplaceCatalog's `affordable` flag reads wallet.balance (lib/marketplace.ts:176) and buyTrophy re-reads it server-side (223). lib/wallet.ts:209 and lib/account-deletion.ts:101 both derive from the same wallet. So the double-count is a display defect, not a spendable one — the inflated number is a lie to the gamer rather than a hole in the till. The till has its own hole (finding 3), by a different mechanism.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:139, lib/marketplace.ts:176, lib/marketplace.ts:223, lib/quests.ts:692`
- **Claim tested:** The botlist vote webhook cannot be used to mint CP by an outsider.
  - *Attack:* This is an unauthenticated public POST endpoint that awards 25 CP, so I attacked it directly: no secret, wrong secret, replay of the same delivery, and a forged voter id.
  - *Why it held:* Three gates hold. The route 503s when no webhook secret is configured and 401s on any mismatch (app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:65-72) — it never falls open. An unknown Discord snowflake is acknowledged but explicitly not credited (91-106), so webhook traffic cannot conjure users. And the dedup ref is an hour bucket, `new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 13)` (110), scoped by `refType: botlist:${service}` (112), so a list retrying a delivery lands on the same key and the unique index rejects it. This is the only award path in the codebase with a genuinely well-chosen dedup key. It is still subject to the concurrency race of finding 2 if the list itself delivers in parallel, but that is not attacker-controllable without the secret.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:65, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:70, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:91, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:110`
- **Claim tested:** CP_PAID_SQL correctly distinguishes 'progress only' rows from 'never migrated' rows.
  - *Attack:* COALESCE(cp_awarded, qp_awarded) looked like a trap: if the progress-only rows (which must pay nothing) were written with cp_awarded NULL rather than 0, the coalesce would fall back to qp_awarded and pay the full weight — silently doubling every award to a second listening quest.
  - *Why it held:* They are written as an explicit integer. lib/quests.ts:516 computes `const cp = paid ? 0 : Math.min(weight, room)` and line 520 writes `cpAwarded: cp` — so a progress-only row carries literal 0, not NULL, and COALESCE returns 0. NULL is reserved for pre-B34 rows exactly as documented (lib/db/schema.ts:849-856), and the migration deliberately adds the column without a backfill (lib/db/index.ts:95-100) for the stated reason. The reasoning here is sound and the implementation matches it.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:516, lib/quests.ts:520, lib/db/schema.ts:857, lib/db/index.ts:100`
- **Claim tested:** UTC day-boundary handling is consistent between what is enforced and what is disclosed.
  - *Attack:* I looked for the classic double-pay across a cap reset: an enforcement boundary computed differently from the display boundary, or a local-time helper mixed in with a UTC one.
  - *Why it held:* One helper, `startOfUtcDay()` (lib/quests.ts:417), is used by cpEarnedToday (449), the per-action cap count (504), and capsToday's grouped read and reset time (726, 737). missions.ts:193 derives its dayKey from toISOString, the same boundary. Server clock skew is a theoretical concern but Vercel and Neon are both NTP-disciplined and the window is milliseconds, which is far smaller than the race window in finding 1 — so the boundary is not where this ledger loses money. I could not construct a double-pay from the reset itself.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:417, lib/quests.ts:449, lib/quests.ts:504, lib/quests.ts:726`


**Cash-out path:**

- **Claim tested:** The gift loop (gift_sent + gift_received) is a net-positive CP pump that two colluding accounts can farm.
  - *Attack:* Computed the full two-account arithmetic. gift_sent pays 25 CP at cap 2/day and gift_received pays 25 CP at cap 2/day (lib/quests.ts:97-98), so each account's maximum daily yield from gifting is 2×25 (sent) + 2×25 (received) = 100 CP. To collect the 50 CP of gift_sent an account must send two gifts, and the cheapest possible gift is the 500 CP price floor for a value-0 trophy (lib/marketplace.ts:102) — so 1,000 CP out for at most 100 CP in. Net −900 CP per account per day, a 10:1 burn. I also checked for an asymmetry between the two sides that could be arbitraged and there is none: both are weight 25, cap 2.
  - *Why it held:* The loop is decisively NET NEGATIVE and is not an exploit. Worth one correction to the team's stated reasoning, though: the comment at lib/marketplace.ts:267-268 credits 'the buyer's daily cap' with stopping the loop. The daily cap is not what stops it — 100 CP/day of yield against a 500 CP minimum ticket is stopped by the PRICE FLOOR. If anyone ever lowers the floor below ~50 CP or raises the gift caps, the loop flips positive and the comment will not warn them.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:97-98, lib/marketplace.ts:102, lib/marketplace.ts:262-276`
- **Claim tested:** A value-0 trophy bought at the 500 CP floor can be laundered into cash.
  - *Attack:* Bought-trophy awards are inserted at status 'held' with the trophy's value (lib/marketplace.ts:244-247), so I traced whether a $0 trophy could reach a payout. `requestRedeem` sums live `trophies.value` and returns 'These trophies have no redeemable value yet' when the total is ≤ 0 (app/actions/trophies.ts:146-147).
  - *Why it held:* The zero-value case is correctly refused before any redeem row is written. The floor is decoration pricing only. Note the residual risk: the check is on the SUM, so a $0 trophy bundled with a $5 one passes — but it adds nothing to the amount, so no value is created.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:244-247, app/actions/trophies.ts:141-147`
- **Claim tested:** A gamer can redeem another gamer's trophy, or redeem a trophy that is already pending or already redeemed, by passing its award id directly to the server action.
  - *Attack:* `requestRedeem` takes raw `awardIds` from the client, so I attacked the ownership and state predicates. The SELECT at app/actions/trophies.ts:141-144 filters on `inArray(id, ids) AND userId = user.id AND status = 'held'`, and :145 rejects the whole request unless every requested id came back.
  - *Why it held:* Ownership and the held-state precondition are both enforced server-side in the same query, and the fail-closed count check at :145 means a partially-valid list is refused rather than partially honoured. The sequential double-redeem is genuinely blocked. It is only the CONCURRENT case that breaks it, which I have reported separately as a fatal finding — the predicate is right, the isolation is absent.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/trophies.ts:141-145`
- **Claim tested:** Closing a challenge twice, or reopening and re-closing it, mints duplicate trophies for the same winner.
  - *Attack:* `reopenChallenge` (app/actions/admin.ts:637-651) explicitly leaves awarded trophies in place and lets the challenge be closed again, and `awardChallengeTrophies` relies on `.onConflictDoNothing()` (lib/trophies.ts:102-104) — which is inert without a matching constraint, exactly as it is in buyTrophy. I went looking for the constraint, since schema.ts:705-713 declares none.
  - *Why it held:* The constraint is real but lives in the runtime migration list, not the Drizzle schema: `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS "ut_user_trophy_chal_idx" ON "user_trophies" ("user_id","trophy_id","challenge_id")` at lib/db/index.ts:128. Challenge awards always carry a non-null challengeId, so the index binds and re-closing is genuinely idempotent. Two caveats the team should know: (a) the index is invisible in lib/db/schema.ts, so a future reader has no reason to believe it exists; (b) Postgres treats NULLs as distinct, so the same index provides zero protection for marketplace purchases (challengeId is null) — which is why the buyTrophy race is unconstrained. If reopen/re-close changes the podium, the new winner is awarded and the old winner keeps theirs, which inflates liability without duplicating a row.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:128, lib/trophies.ts:102-104, app/actions/admin.ts:637-651, lib/db/schema.ts:705-713`
- **Claim tested:** The database stores payment instruments (bank accounts, routing numbers, cards) that could be exfiltrated.
  - *Attack:* Inspected the `trophy_redeems` schema (lib/db/schema.ts:730-763) and the redemption write path, looking for anything that lands a destination. Also checked what the Tremendous adapter sends and keeps.
  - *Why it held:* This one is genuinely well built and I could not break it. `details` is documented as dead and permanently empty (:738-739), `method` is a preference word, `collectUrl` is a provider-hosted link, and the Tremendous adapter deliberately uses `delivery: { method: 'LINK' }` rather than EMAIL so the redemption cannot be collected from an inbox (lib/payments/tremendous.ts:92-95). tests/db/money.mts:44-57 asserts this structurally by scanning information_schema for banned column names — a real test of a real property. The destination genuinely never enters this system.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:730-763, lib/payments/tremendous.ts:86-124, tests/db/money.mts:44-57`


**15-screen guarantee:**

- **Claim tested:** Pointing two quests at the same action doubles the CP payout (the pre-B34.2 multiplier).
  - *Attack:* I looked for an ordering of the listening-quests loop in awardQuestAction where the `paid` flag could fail to suppress the second payment — in particular whether a clamped-to-zero first payment (room exhausted) would leave `paid` false and let the next quest pay.
  - *Why it held:* lib/quests.ts:517 sets paid = true unconditionally on the line after cp is computed, before any continue can be reached, and the only paths that skip it (cap hit at line 505, dedup at line 515) skip the insert too. The sort at lines 486-487 makes the winning quest deterministic across environments. I could not construct a double payment.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:486, lib/quests.ts:495, lib/quests.ts:516, lib/quests.ts:517`
- **Claim tested:** Two colluding accounts can arbitrage the gift give/receive spread.
  - *Attack:* Compared gift_sent and gift_received weights and caps looking for a differential a pair of accounts could pump.
  - *Why it held:* Both are 25 CP at cap 2 (lib/quests.ts:96-97) and the test at tests/db/cp-economics.mts asserts the pair stays identical. There is no spread to farm. (The pair still mints 100 CP/day across two accounts for zero screens — that is counted in the passive total, not as an arbitrage.)
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:96, lib/quests.ts:97`
- **Claim tested:** bot_added can be farmed by removing and re-adding the bot to the same server.
  - *Attack:* Traced creditInstall looking for a per-(user, guild) dedup that a re-install would reset.
  - *Why it held:* The dedup is per GUILD across all gamers (lib/discord/install-credit.ts:100-118): once discord_guilds.installedByUserId is set for a guildId it returns already_credited regardless of who asks, and the row survives an uninstall. The install state is also HMAC-signed with a 60-minute TTL and verified with timingSafeEqual (lines 56-71). This one is built properly.
  - *Cites:* `lib/discord/install-credit.ts:56, lib/discord/install-credit.ts:100, lib/discord/install-credit.ts:118`
- **Claim tested:** The botlist_vote webhook is a free CP faucet (25 CP x 2/day) for anyone who can find the URL.
  - *Attack:* Read the route looking for a missing signature check or a replayable dedup key — the same shape of hole the ads beacon has.
  - *Why it held:* The route requires an exact match on a per-list shared secret in the Authorization header (app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:69), 503s rather than 401s when unconfigured, refuses unknown services, and uses an hour-bucket refId (line 113) so a retry lands on the same dedup key. Without the secret I could not mint a vote. Note this makes the CONTRAST with /api/ads/beacon starker, not softer: the team knows how to secure a webhook and did not apply it to the money-bearing one.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:69, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:111, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:113`
- **Claim tested:** The global daily ceiling fails to clamp a single sequential gamer.
  - *Attack:* Traced a serial sequence of awards past 500 to see whether room could go negative or wrap.
  - *Why it held:* room is clamped at max(0, ...) on line 491 and decremented by the actual cp paid on line 518, and Math.min(weight, room) at line 516 pays the remainder rather than refusing. Serially, a gamer cannot exceed 500. It only fails under concurrency, which I have filed separately.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:491, lib/quests.ts:516, lib/quests.ts:518`


**Abuse surface:**

- **Claim tested:** Money cannot leave the platform without a human approving it.
  - *Attack:* Traced the full CP→cash path looking for any automated payout. requestRedeem (app/actions/trophies.ts:152-156) writes trophyRedeems with status 'pending' and flips the trophies to 'pending'; nothing in that action calls a payment provider. Server-side payouts (app/actions/payouts.ts:189-245) separate 'open' from 'release' and require an admin, with an audit row at line 235. lib/abuse.ts:129-153 holds a guild's FIRST payout for 30 days and payoutHoldFor (lib/abuse.ts:171-184) fails CLOSED on a DB error.
  - *Why it held:* Every exit is gated on a manual staff action. A farm therefore converts into a manual-review burden rather than an automatic cash drain — genuinely the strongest control in the codebase. Its limit is that it is entirely human: at farm scale the queue is the failure mode.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/trophies.ts:152, app/actions/payouts.ts:189, lib/abuse.ts:129, lib/abuse.ts:171`
- **Claim tested:** The bot-list vote webhook cannot be forged for CP.
  - *Attack:* Attempted to find a path to mint botlist_vote (25 CP x2) without the list's cooperation. The route requires a stored per-list secret (app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:64-72), returns 503 rather than crediting when unconfigured, refuses on any Authorization mismatch, and requires the payload's Discord snowflake to already resolve to an oauthIdentities row (lines 84-106). Retries are deduped on an hour bucket (line 110-116).
  - *Why it held:* Without the shared secret there is no forgery path. The only weakness is that the comparison at line 70 is a non-constant-time `!==`, which is a theoretical timing oracle, not a practical one over HTTP. This is the one action on the abuse surface that is genuinely defended.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:64, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:70, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:84, app/api/botlist/vote/[service]/route.ts:110`
- **Claim tested:** Game stats are not self-reported, so stat-based CP is not directly spoofable.
  - *Attack:* Looked for any path where a client supplies a metric value. syncAccount pulls exclusively from adapter.fetchStats (lib/sync.ts:35-40) and writes statCurrent/statSnapshots from that result only (lib/sync.ts:68-91). Challenge points derive from the delta against a stored baseline (lib/sync.ts:126-169). Linking requires adapter.verify to succeed (lib/link-account.ts:46-47), and one game account is held by one gamer (lib/link-account.ts:64-67, lib/account-ownership.ts:161-190).
  - *Why it held:* There is no client-writable stat path. Note the finding is inverted here: stat_levelup and play_session have no emitter at all, so there is nothing to spoof AND nothing to earn. Also note lib/sync.ts:93-99 deliberately refuses to set `verified` on sync, so 'account exists' is not conflated with 'this person owns it' — that distinction is correctly implemented.
  - *Cites:* `lib/sync.ts:35, lib/sync.ts:68, lib/sync.ts:93, lib/link-account.ts:46`
- **Claim tested:** The gift loop is not a CP mint.
  - *Attack:* Tested whether A↔B gifting nets positive CP. Buying costs priceOf(trophy) with a hard floor of 500 CP (lib/marketplace.ts:99); gifting pays 25 to each side (lib/marketplace.ts:274-275); the recipient receives a redeemable trophy at trophy.value. At the shipped 10,000 CP/$ rate a 500-CP trophy is worth $0.05, so the trophy value transferred equals the CP spent.
  - *Why it held:* Trophy value is conserved across the pair, so the loop is not self-funding. It still nets +50 CP per gift pair per day (25 each side, cap 2) with no real activity, which I have counted in the collusion-ring finding — but it is a leak, not a mint. The 500-CP floor at lib/marketplace.ts:99 is what prevents it becoming one.
  - *Cites:* `lib/marketplace.ts:99, lib/marketplace.ts:274, lib/marketplace.ts:275`
- **Claim tested:** The quest-event dedup index is real and correctly shaped.
  - *Attack:* Looked for double-award via retry or replay. questEvents carries uniqueIndex qe_dedup_idx on (user_id, quest_id, action_key, ref_type, ref_id) (lib/db/schema.ts:863) and the insert uses onConflictDoNothing (lib/quests.ts:519-522). awardQuestAction always passes '' rather than null for a missing ref (lib/quests.ts:508-509), which matters because Postgres unique indexes do not dedup NULLs.
  - *Why it held:* Replaying an identical (user, quest, action, ref) is genuinely idempotent, and the empty-string default closes the NULL hole. It is defeated only where the caller mints a fresh ref per request — which is precisely what the ad beacon does at app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:22, and is why the ceiling race in my first finding is exploitable.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:863, lib/quests.ts:508, lib/quests.ts:519, app/api/ads/beacon/route.ts:22`


**Scale & ops:**

- **Claim tested:** The Discord bot violates the 3-second interaction budget by rendering a PNG inline.
  - *Attack:* I traced every interaction entry point looking for synchronous card work before the ACK: the command path, the button path, all six modal submits, and the autocomplete path.
  - *Why it held:* Every non-modal, non-autocomplete path defers correctly. `command()` returns `DeferredChannelMessageWithSource` immediately and does all DB and card work inside `after()` (app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:363-386); `componentPress()` returns `DeferredUpdateMessage` and defers (app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:508-539); every modal submit follows the same shape (e.g. :126-164, :169-224, :565-651). Modals are returned synchronously with no DB work, which is exactly what Discord requires (:397-425, :459-497). Autocomplete answers synchronously but does one indexed catalog read (:336-348). The 3-second budget is respected. Card latency is a *user-experience* problem inside the webhook edit window, not an ACK failure — and rendering is separately bounded by CARD_IMAGE_BUDGET_MS = 3200 (lib/cards/img.ts:192) and a 2200 ms per-image timeout (lib/cards/img.ts:36).
  - *Cites:* `app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:363, app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:382, app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:508, app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:539`
- **Claim tested:** `sharp` is imported at runtime but is not a declared dependency, so image transcoding will fail in production.
  - *Attack:* package.json lists no `sharp`, yet lib/cards/render.tsx:2469 and lib/cards/img.ts:58 both `await import('sharp')`. I parsed package-lock.json to find who declares it.
  - *Why it held:* `sharp` is an optionalDependency of `next` itself (`node_modules/next` → optionalDependencies.sharp ^0.34.3), and `node_modules/sharp` is present in the lockfile. npm installs optionalDependencies by default, so it resolves on Vercel. The code also guards both call sites with try/catch and a documented degradation path (lib/cards/img.ts:54-62, lib/cards/render.tsx:2469-2476). The comment at lib/cards/img.ts:51-53 is accurate. Not a defect.
  - *Cites:* `package.json:14, lib/cards/render.tsx:2469, lib/cards/img.ts:54, lib/cards/img.ts:58`
- **Claim tested:** `cpEarnedToday` — a SUM run on every award — is unindexed and will table-scan.
  - *Attack:* I checked whether `qe_user_idx` supports the predicate `user_id = $1 AND created_at >= startOfUtcDay()`.
  - *Why it held:* The index is on (user_id, created_at) (lib/db/schema.ts:862 and lib/db/index.ts:101), which is exactly the right leading-column order for that predicate — it is an index range scan, and each user has at most ~40 rows in a UTC day. This specific query is fine. The unbounded lifetime variant `getTotalCp` is NOT fine (see findings) because it has no date bound and the index does not cover cp_awarded, but the daily-ceiling read the task asked about is correctly indexed.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:447, lib/db/schema.ts:862, lib/db/index.ts:101`
- **Claim tested:** The DDL bootstrap array will corrupt or drop production data because ALTERs precede their CREATEs.
  - *Attack:* I read the full 219-statement COLUMN_MIGRATIONS array looking for an ALTER that references a table created later in the same array, and for destructive statements without guards.
  - *Why it held:* The ordering hazard is real and the authors are aware of it — they call it out at lib/db/index.ts:241-242 and :373-376 and have placed the relevant ALTERs after their CREATEs. Every ALTER uses `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS`, every CREATE uses `IF NOT EXISTS`, and the destructive statements (lib/db/index.ts:835-841) are intentional, documented, and idempotent. The array is *operationally* dangerous for the lock and latency reasons in my findings, but I could not find a correctness bug in the ordering or in the idempotency.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:241, lib/db/index.ts:373, lib/db/index.ts:822, lib/db/index.ts:835`
- **Claim tested:** Rendered cards are re-drawn on every Discord message, blowing up cost.
  - *Attack:* I traced getOrRenderCard to see whether repeat requests actually hit the stored Blob URL.
  - *Why it held:* The cache is real and content-hash keyed (lib/cards/cache.ts:27-29, 42-52), the card route redirects to the hosted PNG with CDN headers (app/api/card/[kind]/route.tsx:153-167, 186-188), and the sponsor id is folded into the cache key so brand rotation does not poison it (lib/discord/cards.ts:113-116). The design is sound. What breaks is not the cache — it is the 3,000-card store cap and 4,000/day render ceiling above it, which is a separate finding.
  - *Cites:* `lib/cards/cache.ts:35, lib/cards/cache.ts:45, app/api/card/[kind]/route.tsx:153, lib/discord/cards.ts:116`


**Privacy & data:**

- **Claim tested:** OAuth access and refresh tokens for Discord / Steam / Epic / Battle.net are NOT stored — the columns exist but nothing ever writes them. A database compromise does not let an attacker act as users on Discord or the game platforms.
  - *Attack:* This was my primary attack thesis. lib/db/schema.ts:89-90 defines `accessToken`/`refreshToken` on oauth_identities, which looks damning. I grepped every reference to `oauthIdentities` across the tree (14 files) and read every INSERT: app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:171-173 writes only {id, userId, provider, providerUserId}; lib/discord/identity.ts:84-86 writes the same four; lib/db/seed.ts:238 adds only `scopes`. I then grepped `accessToken|refreshToken|access_token|refresh_token` across lib/ and app/ — the only live token handling is in-memory: callback/route.ts:47 holds the Discord token in a local variable for the `guilds.join` call and the comment at line 45-46 says 'it is only usable here — we never store it'.
  - *Why it held:* The claim is accurate. The two token columns are dead weight, not a liability — the correct criticism is that dead credential columns should be dropped so a future contributor does not start populating them, not that they are populated. The one provider secret that IS stored (Mobile Legends login token, app/actions/connections.ts:75) goes through AES-256-GCM at lib/crypto.ts:9-15 — though see the AUTH_SECRET finding, which undermines that key.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:89-90, app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:45-47, app/api/auth/[provider]/callback/route.ts:171-173, lib/discord/identity.ts:84-86`
- **Claim tested:** No bank details, IBANs, routing numbers, card numbers or wallet addresses are stored anywhere, and a migration actively strips them from historic rows.
  - *Attack:* Grepped iban/paypal/bank/routing/accountNumber/sortCode/last4/card across the whole tree. Read the money section of the schema (lib/db/schema.ts:1510-1660) in full, plus trophy_redeems (730-763) and users.payoutMethod (50-59). Checked whether the deprecated `details` column is genuinely emptied rather than merely documented as such.
  - *Why it held:* It holds. lib/db/index.ts:835 runs `UPDATE "trophy_redeems" SET "details" = '{}'` and lines 838-839 run `UPDATE "users" SET "payout_method" = "payout_method" - 'details'` — real DDL, not a comment. payout_accounts (schema.ts:1531-1553) stores only an opaque `providerRecipientId`, a method word, a country and a currency. This is the strongest privacy decision in the codebase and it is genuinely implemented, not just claimed. Caveat worth stating: the migration cleans the live table, not Neon's WAL or point-in-time backups, so any pre-migration snapshot still contains the old values.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/schema.ts:1510-1521, lib/db/schema.ts:1531-1553, lib/db/schema.ts:715-739, lib/db/index.ts:835`
- **Claim tested:** No secrets are committed to the repository.
  - *Attack:* Ran a regex sweep for sk_live/sk_test/pk_live/AKIA[A-Z0-9]{16}/ghp_/xox[baprs]-/vercel_blob_rw_/postgres:// connection strings/re_ (Resend)/AIza/-----BEGIN across all .ts, .tsx, .json, .md, .mjs excluding node_modules and package-lock. Checked .gitignore, `git ls-files | grep env`, next.config.ts and vercel.json.
  - *Why it held:* Exactly one hit — lib/blob.ts:9, which is a prefix comparison (`v.startsWith("vercel_blob_rw_")`), not a key. .gitignore covers .env/.env.local/.env*.local/.vercel; only .env.example is tracked and every value in it is blank or a placeholder. next.config.ts and vercel.json contain no credentials. SECURITY.md:17's 'Secrets: Env only. Nothing committed.' is the one row of that table I could fully confirm.
  - *Cites:* `lib/blob.ts:9, .gitignore:1-9, .env.example:1-60, next.config.ts:1-50`
- **Claim tested:** Admin server actions are comprehensively guarded — I could not find an unguarded admin mutation.
  - *Attack:* This was the vulnerability class I most expected to find. I enumerated every `export async function` in all 36 files in app/actions/ and cross-referenced against guard calls. My first pass flagged billing.ts (11 exports, 0 hits), brand-portal.ts, offers.ts, shots.ts, payments.ts, admin-email.ts and botlists.ts as unguarded. All were false positives from too narrow a grep: they use `requireSystemFor()` (lib/departments.ts:160-169) or `requireArea()` (lib/permissions.ts:27-36) rather than requireAdmin/requireStaff. I then read the guard implementations themselves to confirm they throw rather than warn, and read all 52 exports in admin.ts line by line — including the 15 brand/campaign/creative functions my grep had missed — checking the first statement of each.
  - *Why it held:* Every mutating admin action in app/actions/ calls one of requireAdmin / requireStaff / requireArea / requireSystemFor as its first statement. requireArea (lib/permissions.ts:29,35) and requireSystemFor (lib/departments.ts:162,166) both throw FORBIDDEN or notFound() on failure — they fail closed. The parallel-rendering leak that would otherwise expose admin pages is closed at middleware.ts before render, with the layout's live DB check as a documented second layer. Two smaller notes rather than findings: SECURITY.md:10's phrasing ('every mutating path calls requireStaff()/requireAdmin()') understates the actual mechanism, and app/actions/profile-week-admin.ts:37-39 swallows the throw into a null check — correct here, but a pattern that fails open if a future author forgets the `if (!me) return`.
  - *Cites:* `lib/permissions.ts:27-36, lib/departments.ts:160-169, app/actions/billing.ts:29-32, app/actions/admin.ts:655-919`
- **Claim tested:** Cross-tenant isolation between brand portals and between server portals holds — one brand cannot read another brand's data by manipulating parameters.
  - *Attack:* Attempted the standard IDOR: every brand-portal action takes `(brandId, key, ...)` from the client, which is the classic shape for a tenant-confusion bug. Traced whether the key is ever validated against a different brand than the one whose data is returned, and whether the portal session cookie can be replayed across tenants.
  - *Why it held:* app/actions/brand-portal.ts:13-23 loads the brand BY the supplied brandId and then validates the key against that same row — there is no path where brand A's key authorises brand B's id. The session cookie name embeds the portal id (lib/portal-auth.ts:79-83, 'so a session for one server can never be replayed against another') and the cookie VALUE is an HMAC over `kind:id`, so a stolen cookie is useless elsewhere. Comparison is HMAC-then-timingSafeEqual (lib/portal-auth.ts:68-73), which also avoids the length leak a naive timingSafeEqual would have. app/actions/server-portal.ts:19-28 is the same shape. The separate brute-force and DoS defects I filed are about rate limiting, not isolation — the tenant boundary itself is sound.
  - *Cites:* `app/actions/brand-portal.ts:13-23, app/actions/server-portal.ts:19-28, lib/portal-auth.ts:68-83, lib/portal-auth.ts:122-131`
- **Claim tested:** Discord bot interactions are genuinely Ed25519-verified, and the portal key generator is not guessable.
  - *Attack:* Checked whether the SECURITY.md:13 signature claim is real or aspirational, and computed the actual entropy of both key generators after their post-processing (which is where these usually go wrong).
  - *Why it held:* app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:2 imports and uses verifyInteraction before any handler runs, with the 401-on-invalid contract stated at lines 36-38. On the keys: newPortalKey (lib/portal-auth.ts:213-222) draws 12 chars from a 32-char confusable-free alphabet via randomBytes — ~60 bits, correctly noted in its own comment. newAccessKey (lib/brands.ts:8-11) is lossier than it looks (base64url uppercased, `-`/`_` stripped, so the effective alphabet collapses from 64 to ~36 and the string can come out 11 chars instead of 12), but still lands around 60 bits. Neither is brute-forceable online. I tried to make the entropy loss into a finding and it does not reach materiality.
  - *Cites:* `app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:2, app/api/discord/interactions/route.ts:33-38, lib/portal-auth.ts:213-222, lib/brands.ts:8-11`


**Tests & doc truth:**

- **Claim tested:** The tests are real database tests, not an in-memory shim — a passing DB suite does exercise genuine Postgres semantics.
  - *Attack:* I expected DEMO_DB=1 to select a fake/stub driver and invalidate every DB-level assertion, which would have been the single largest finding in Part 1. I grepped DEMO_DB across the whole repo, read lib/db/index.ts:900-966, and traced the driver selection.
  - *Why it held:* DEMO_DB is a red herring — it appears only in test headers and comments and is read nowhere in lib/db. The real switch is `isDemoMode = !process.env.DATABASE_URL` (lib/db/index.ts:12), and the demo branch instantiates `new PGlite()` (lib/db/index.ts:921-923), which is genuine Postgres compiled to WASM, running the same DDL (lib/db/index.ts:928) and the same idempotent column migrations (:930) as production. Constraints, unique indexes, foreign keys and SQL semantics are real. The DB-level assertions in tests/db are therefore valid as far as they go. The caveat that matters is narrower and belongs to the concurrency finding: PGlite is single-connection and in-process, so it cannot express a race even if a test tried — and none does. It also cannot reproduce the one production-specific property that breaks these paths, namely neon-http's inability to hold a transaction.
  - *Cites:* `lib/db/index.ts:12, lib/db/index.ts:910-916, lib/db/index.ts:920-930, tests/run-all.mjs:104`
- **Claim tested:** The CP ledger is internally coherent, and 'CP once, progress everywhere' genuinely closes the double-payment hole it was written to close.
  - *Attack:* I looked for a way to make one action pay twice by pointing two quests at the same action key — the multiplier B34.2 claims to have removed — and separately for a way to make the wallet disagree with the ledger.
  - *Why it held:* awardQuestAction sorts listening quests deterministically and sets `paid` on the first credit, so every subsequent listener writes cpAwarded 0 while still advancing its own QP (lib/quests.ts:487-522). getTotalCp sums COALESCE(cp_awarded, qp_awarded) from quest_events only, so progress rows cannot inflate a balance. This is asserted end-to-end rather than by inspection at tests/db/cp-economics.mts:88-116 and reconciled against the wallet in tests/db/wallet.mts. The clamping of the last award to the remainder (lib/quests.ts:516) is also real and is asserted at tests/db/cp-economics.mts:143-151. Sequentially, this subsystem is correct and well tested. It fails only under concurrency, which is a separate finding.
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:487-522, lib/quests.ts:516, tests/db/cp-economics.mts:88-116, tests/db/cp-economics.mts:143-151`
- **Claim tested:** The money/invoice layer and the abuse-hold layer are the strongest tested parts of the codebase, and the structural 'we store no payment details' property is genuinely enforced.
  - *Attack:* I went looking for a stored payment instrument, a stored invoice total that could drift from its lines, and a way to release a payout twice or to clear an abuse hold by opening and cancelling a draft payout.
  - *Why it held:* tests/db/money.mts asserts structurally over the schema that no column could hold an account, card or routing number, and asserts that an invoice total is recomputed from its lines rather than stored, that a paid payout cannot be paid again, and that payout preferences store only a short method word. tests/db/abuse.mts:118-130 specifically proves a draft payout and a cancelled payout do not count as a track record while a paid one does, and that the tier-unlock stamp is one-way (:91-102). tests/db/stuck-money.mts covers every terminal state where a prize has nowhere to go, one assertion per row of the decision table. tests/db/prepay.mts proves the invoice is priced from the campaign rather than typed, and that a blocked brand's already-won prizes are still payable. This is disciplined, adversarial work and I could not break any of it by reading. Two caveats: none of it is concurrency-tested, and 2 of abuse.mts's assertions are regex over app/actions/auth.ts source text (tests/db/abuse.mts:208-214) rather than over behaviour.
  - *Cites:* `tests/db/money.mts, tests/db/abuse.mts:91-130, tests/db/abuse.mts:208-214, tests/db/stuck-money.mts`
- **Claim tested:** EXECUTION_PLAN.md is an honest document — the dishonesty is confined to COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md.
  - *Attack:* I was told to distrust both and went in expecting the plan's Amendments table and its shipped-behaviour claims to overstate what exists. I read the Amendments table and the 165 lines the pivot commit added.
  - *Why it held:* The Amendments table is a genuine decision log that records where the team was wrong and what superseded it (B16/B17 superseded by B34, B30 by B44, B31 by B43). More to the point, the B64/B65/B66 sections added by the pivot commit describe the passive cap, ad_click at 0, delivery counting, the sales cockpit and the danger zones as work still owed, with explicit 'Verification owed →' clauses naming test files that do not exist yet (tests/db/ad-delivery.mts). Read alone, EXECUTION_PLAN.md would not mislead a buyer. COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md takes the identical list and renders it in the present indicative under a truth warranty at :8-9. The two documents were committed together in 1c5c98a; the buyer is being pointed at the one that lies.
  - *Cites:* `docs/EXECUTION_PLAN.md:3751, docs/COMMERCIAL_MODEL.md:8-9`
- **Claim tested:** §5.2's ceiling behaviour is genuinely shipped: past 500 CP an action still succeeds, is still logged at 0 CP, and the gamer is shown the figure and the reset time.
  - *Attack:* I tried to find the humane-disclosure half of the cap claim missing like the rest of §5, since the neighbouring claims in the same section are false.
  - *Why it held:* It is real. awardQuestAction writes the row with cp 0 rather than refusing (lib/quests.ts:516,519), tests/db/cp-economics.mts:143-145 asserts rows with cpAwarded 0 exist past the ceiling, tests/db/caps.mts:97-100 asserts awarding past the ceiling neither throws nor credits, and the UI copy 'daily maximum reached' with a UTC-midnight reset exists at components/CapsToday.tsx:48,70 and is asserted at tests/db/caps.mts:54-57. Only the sub-claim of a stored `daily_cap_reached` reason string is false — quest_events has no reason column (lib/db/schema.ts:843-857).
  - *Cites:* `lib/quests.ts:516, tests/db/cp-economics.mts:143-145, tests/db/caps.mts:54-57, tests/db/caps.mts:97-100`
